# Majmu' al-Fatawa - Tome 26

## Page 12781

VOLUME TWENTY-SIX
THE BOOK OF JURISPRUDENCE
PART SIX:
HAJJ

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Bestower of Mercy.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him and be pleased with him—was asked:

“Is ‘umrah (the minor pilgrimage) obligatory? If so, what is the evidence for this?”

He answered:

Section

Scholars hold two opinions on whether ‘umrah is obligatory. Both opinions are found in the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad, though the well-known position reported from both is that it is obligatory. The other opinion is that it is not obligatory; this is the position of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik.

The latter opinion is stronger, for Allah made only Hajj obligatory when He said:

“Pilgrimage to the House is a duty people owe to Allah.”

He did not make ‘umrah obligatory; rather, He required the completion of both. Thus, He required those who begin either pilgrimage to complete it, but He made only Hajj obligatory to undertake in the first place. Likewise, all the authentic hadiths prescribe only Hajj as obligatory. Moreover, ‘umrah contains no kind of rite that is not already included in Hajj: it consists of entering and leaving the state of ritual consecration, circumambulating the House, and passing between

---

## Page 12782

al-Safā and al-Marwah, all of which is included in Hajj.

Since this is so, Allah has not prescribed any of the rites of Hajj twice: He has not prescribed two appointed times, two circumambulations, or two passages between al-Safā and al-Marwah, nor has He made Hajj obligatory twice. The farewell circumambulation is not a pillar; rather, it is obligatory, but it is not part of completing Hajj. Instead, everyone leaving Mecca must perform a farewell circumambulation. Thus, according to the correct view, anyone who remains in Mecca does not perform it. Its obligation ensures that the departing person’s last encounter is with the House, just as entering Mecca in a state of ritual consecration is obligatory according to one of the two scholarly views. This obligation arises from a contingent circumstance, rather than being an obligation imposed by Islam itself, as Hajj is.

Another reason is that the Companions residing in Mecca did not perform ‘umrah there, either during the Prophet’s lifetime—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—or during the time of his caliphs. Indeed, no one performed ‘umrah in Mecca during the Prophet’s lifetime—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—except ‘Ā’ishah alone, owing to a particular circumstance. We have discussed this at length elsewhere.

He was also asked:

“If someone performs Hajj without performing ‘umrah, omitting it either deliberately or forgetfully, is it waived

---

## Page 12783

for him by performing Hajj, or not? Has anyone reported any disagreement on this matter, or not?”

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds.

Scholars hold two well-known views on whether ‘umrah is obligatory. Both are attributed to al-Shāfi‘ī and transmitted from Ahmad. The well-known position among their followers is that it is obligatory. However, the majority, including Mālik and Abū Hanīfah, hold that it is not obligatory. Both views are reported from some of the Companions. The stronger view is that ‘umrah is not obligatory, and that someone who performs Hajj without performing ‘umrah owes nothing, whether he omitted ‘umrah deliberately or forgetfully.

This is because Allah has prescribed in His Book only pilgrimage to the House, saying:

“Pilgrimage to the House is a duty owed to Allah by people.”

The word Hajj in the Quran does not include ‘umrah. Rather, when He—glorified be He—intends ‘umrah, He mentions it alongside Hajj, as in His words:

“Complete Hajj and ‘umrah for Allah.”

And His words:

“Whoever performs Hajj to the House or performs ‘umrah, there is no blame upon him for passing between them.”

Thus, when He commanded completion, He commanded the completion of both Hajj and ‘umrah. By common agreement, this verse was revealed in the year of al-Hudaybiyyah, year 6. The verse in Āl ‘Imrān was revealed later, in year 9 or 10, and it establishes the obligation of Hajj. Hence, the more correct of the two views is that Hajj became obligatory at that later time.

Those who say that it became obligatory in year 6 rely on the verse commanding completion. This is mistaken, for the verse only commands those who have begun these rites to complete them; it does not command them to undertake Hajj or ‘umrah in the first place. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—undertook the ‘umrah of al-Hudaybiyyah before this

---

## Page 12784

verse was revealed, when neither Hajj nor ‘umrah had yet been made obligatory for him. Then, when the polytheists prevented him from proceeding, Allah revealed this verse, commanding the completion of Hajj and ‘umrah and explaining the ruling concerning someone prevented from completing them. Thus, the imams agree that Hajj and ‘umrah become binding once undertaken and must therefore be completed. They disagree, however, concerning fasting, prayer, and devotional retreat.

Furthermore, ‘umrah contains no kind of act that is not also part of Hajj. It consists of entering ihrām, circumambulation, walking between Safā and Marwah, and leaving ihrām—all of which are found in Hajj. Allah has made Hajj obligatory only once, not twice, nor has He required any of its obligatory rites twice. He has not required two ritual standings or two circumambulations. Rather, the obligatory circumambulation is tawāf al-ifādah. As for the farewell circumambulation, it is not part of Hajj; it is required only of someone intending to leave Mecca. Hence, someone who remains in Mecca does not perform it. Nor is it obligatory for everyone, since it is waived for a menstruating woman. Were someone to omit it, a sacrificial offering would suffice, and omitting it would not invalidate the Hajj, unlike omitting the obligatory circumambulation or the standing.

Likewise, walking between Safā and Marwah is required only once. Stoning on the Day of Sacrifice is required only once, and stoning each pillar on each day is required only once. Similarly, shaving or shortening the hair is required only once. Since ‘umrah contains no act beyond the acts of Hajj, and Allah has made the acts of Hajj obligatory only once, not twice, it follows that Allah has not made ‘umrah obligatory.

---

## Page 12785

Some who hold ‘umrah to be obligatory have cited the transmitted hadith stating that “‘Umrah is the lesser pilgrimage” as evidence. Yet it actually indicates that ‘umrah is not obligatory, for this hadith points to two kinds of pilgrimage: greater and lesser. The Quran likewise indicates this in His words, “the day of the greater pilgrimage.”

If that is so, declaring ‘umrah obligatory would mean making two pilgrimages obligatory: a greater and a lesser one. Allah Most High, however, has not prescribed two pilgrimages; He has made only one obligatory. When Hajj is mentioned without qualification, it means the greater pilgrimage. This is what Allah has prescribed for His servants and assigned a specified time outside which it cannot be performed, as He says, “the day of the greater pilgrimage.” ‘Umrah, by contrast, is not restricted to any particular time; it may be performed in any month of the year.

Moreover, ‘umrah in relation to Hajj is like ablution in relation to the ritual bath. According to the majority of scholars, someone bathing to remove major ritual impurity need only perform the ritual bath and is not required to perform ablution as well. The same applies to Hajj: Hajj and ‘umrah are two acts of worship of the same kind, one lesser and one greater. Once someone performs the greater, performing the lesser is not obligatory. Nevertheless, performing the lesser as well is better and more complete, just as performing ablution along with the ritual bath is better and more complete.

This is what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions did. He did, however, instruct them to perform tamattu‘, saying:

“‘Umrah has become part of Hajj until the Day of Resurrection.”

This has been explained at length elsewhere. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12786

He was asked:

A woman performed her obligatory Hajj but had not performed ‘umrah. In the second year, she intended to perform Hajj on behalf of her daughter. The first time, she had entered the state of ritual consecration for both Hajj and ‘umrah. Is she required to perform another ‘umrah?

He replied:

No further ‘umrah is required of her on account of what has already passed. If, however, she performs ‘umrah this year on her own behalf, separately from the ‘umrah on behalf of her daughter, that is permissible.

He was also asked, may Allah have mercy on him:

What do the learned say of a man
Whom the Lord of the Throne has granted wealth, who has performed Hajj and ‘umrah?

Joyful longing for the Chosen One has stirred his heart—
Do you deem Hajj better, or giving his wealth to the poor?

Or is performing Hajj on his father’s behalf better?
Or what, my masters, has become clear to you?

---

## Page 12787

So give your ruling to one who loves you—I would give my life for you—
Who constantly speaks of you, whether absent or present.

He replied, may Allah be pleased with him:

We say that Hajj is better
Than giving charity and providing for the poor.

Performing Hajj on behalf of one’s parents is dutifulness to them,
And the mother takes precedence in such dutiful care.

But if the obligation concerns the father alone,
Then he takes precedence, so that harm may be averted.

As when the father is in need of support,
While the Creator of mankind has sufficiently provided for the mother.

Here is your answer, good sir, in matching verse,
Though the one giving you this ruling is not numbered among the poets.

---

## Page 12788

He, may Allah have mercy on him, was asked:

“A woman possesses more than approximately one thousand dirhams and intends to give her clothes to her daughter. Is it better for her to keep her clothing for her daughter or to use it to perform Hajj?”

He replied:

“Praise be to Allah. Yes, she should perform Hajj with this money—one thousand dirhams or thereabouts—and use the remainder to arrange her daughter’s marriage, if she wishes. Hajj is an obligation incumbent upon her if she has the means to undertake it, and a woman who possesses this amount has the means.”

He was also asked:

“An elderly man’s limbs have grown feeble; he cannot eat or drink, nor can he move. May he hire someone to perform the obligatory Hajj on his behalf?”

He replied:

“As for Hajj, if he cannot ride a mount, he should appoint someone to perform Hajj on his behalf.”

---

## Page 12789

He was also asked:

“May a woman perform Hajj without a mahram (a male relative whom she is permanently forbidden to marry)?”

He replied:

“If she is an elderly woman who does not menstruate, no longer expects to marry, and has no mahram, then, according to one of the two scholarly views, she may perform Hajj with someone she trusts. This is one of the two positions reported from Ahmad and is the position of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī.”

He, may Allah have mercy on him, said:

Section

The scholars agree that a woman may perform Hajj on behalf of another woman, whether or not she is the other woman’s daughter. Likewise, according to the four imams and the majority of scholars, a woman may perform Hajj on behalf of a man, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed the woman from Khathʿam to perform Hajj on behalf of her father when she said:

“O Messenger of Allah, the obligation of

---

## Page 12790

Hajj that Allah has imposed upon His servants became incumbent upon my father when he was already an elderly man.” The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed her to perform Hajj on behalf of her father, even though a man’s ihrām (state of pilgrim consecration) is more complete than hers. And Allah knows best.

He, may Allah have mercy on him, said:

Section

On performing Hajj on behalf of a deceased or physically incapacitated person using money received either to cover expenses—which is permissible by agreement—or as payment under a contract of hire or as a stipulated reward, concerning which the jurists disagree. This applies whether the money used for Hajj is bequeathed to a particular person or as an asset without further qualification, donated, or taken directly from the estate. Some followers of al-Shāfi‘ī considered this recommended, saying that it is among the most wholesome ways of earning a living, since one performs a righteous deed and eats from lawful earnings.

Ahmad is explicitly reported to have said:

“I know of no one among the early generations who used to do this.”

He regarded it as an innovation and disapproved of it. His exact wording is recorded elsewhere. His disapproval concerned only contracts of hire and stipulated rewards.

I say:

The truth of the matter is that undertaking such a Hajj is recommended when the pilgrim intends one of two things: benefiting the person on whose behalf he performs Hajj, or the actual performance of

---

## Page 12791

Hajj for his own benefit. If Hajj was obligatory upon the deceased, it remains an obligation owed by him. Performing Hajj on his behalf is therefore an act of kindness that discharges his obligation, just like repaying his debt. Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to the woman of Khath‘am:

“Tell me, if your father owed a debt and you repaid it, would that discharge his obligation?”

She replied, “Yes.”

He said, “Allah has a greater right to be repaid.”

He expressed this meaning in several hadiths, explaining that Allah, in His mercy and generosity, has even more reason to accept the discharge of a debt on someone’s behalf. If the pilgrim’s purpose is to discharge this obligatory debt on that person’s behalf, he is showing him kindness, and Allah loves those who do good. This is therefore recommended. Such kindness is usually prompted by some particular reason, such as kinship, affection and friendship, or a kindness previously received from that person which he wishes to repay. He takes only enough money to help him perform Hajj on that person’s behalf. A sign of this intention is that he asks only for enough to cover his Hajj expenses. This is why we have permitted the payment of Hajj expenses without dispute. The same applies if the deceased left instructions in his will for a voluntary Hajj, and the pilgrim wishes to convey its reward to him.

The second case is when a man wishes to perform Hajj out of love for it and longing for its sacred sites, but lacks the means. He therefore uses the money provided for Hajj to enable him to perform it. He may be given money to perform Hajj without doing so on anyone else’s behalf, just as a fighter is given money to go forth to fight. There is no doubt about the permissibility of this. The pilgrim receives the reward for performing Hajj in person, while the donor receives the reward for supporting Hajj with his wealth, just as in jihad: “Whoever equips a warrior has himself gone forth to fight.” He may also be given

---

## Page 12792

money to perform Hajj on someone else’s behalf. The giver’s purpose is for Hajj to be performed on behalf of that person, while the pilgrim seeks the reward of performing Hajj itself, rather than the reward of showing kindness to another.

This accords with Abū Hanīfah’s principle, for he said: “The Hajj counts for the pilgrim, while the giver receives the reward for spending, just as in jihad.” According to our principle, someone who prays, fasts, gives charity or performs Hajj on another’s behalf has a righteous intention both in performing the act itself and in performing it on another’s behalf.

Given that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The trustworthy treasurer who willingly gives what he has been instructed to give, in full and without deduction, is one of the two givers of charity,”

thereby granting the agent the same reward in charity as the principal, even though he acts as a representative—and that he also said:

“When a woman spends from her husband’s household without wastefulness, she receives her reward for what she spends, her husband receives his reward for what he earns, and the servant receives a similar reward,”

so too, someone acting on another’s behalf in Hajj or any other act that admits of representation receives a reward, as does the person who appoints him. This pilgrim likewise takes only what he spends on Hajj, just as someone going forth to fight takes only what he spends on that expedition.

These, then, are two recommended cases: the two permissible forms of accepting money for Hajj expenses and returning the surplus. But if his purpose is to earn money—that is, to retain a financial surplus—this constitutes hiring or a reward-based contract. The correct view is that this is not recommended, even if it is held to be permissible, because an act performed for worldly gain is not, in itself, a righteous deed if it is undertaken with no intention

---

## Page 12793

other than obtaining money; it therefore falls within the category of permissible acts. Whoever seeks worldly gain through deeds intended for the Hereafter will have no share in the Hereafter. When we permit hiring and reward-based contracts for righteous acts whose performers must be qualified to undertake acts of devotion, we regard those acts, in these circumstances, as merely permissible; we do not classify them as “acts of devotion.” For there are three possibilities: a person may be punished for performing the act with this intention, rewarded for it, or neither rewarded nor punished.

Likewise, accepting the money may be prohibited, recommended, or permissible. Each case has its corresponding ruling, and Allah knows best. However, hiring may be preferable to . . . (1) if the person needs that money to meet his expenses during Hajj, to support himself after his return, or to repay his debt, and thus intends to meet his expenses and discharge the debt he is obliged to repay.

Here, then, there are three possibilities: he may intend only to perform Hajj and show kindness to another; he may intend only to meet his legitimate expenses; or he may intend both. If he intends the first, that is good. If he intends both together, that too is good, Allah willing, for both are righteous aims. But if he intends only to earn money to meet his expenses, this requires further consideration. This issue is explained in detail elsewhere.
