## Page 12794

He was asked:
“May a woman who has already performed Hajj perform Hajj on behalf of a deceased woman for payment?”

He replied:
“There is agreement that she may perform Hajj on behalf of the deceased with funds received for acting as a proxy. As for doing so under a contract of hire, scholars hold two views, both of which are reported from Ahmad. The first permits it; this is the view of al-Shāfiʿī. The second does not permit it; this is the position of Abū Hanīfa.

“If this woman’s intention in performing Hajj on behalf of the deceased is to perform Hajj or to benefit the deceased, she will receive reward and recompense for that. But if her sole intention is to receive payment, she will have no share in the Hereafter.”

He was asked about someone who performed Hajj on behalf of another in order to repay his debt.

---

## Page 12795

He replied:

“Scholars have differed over whether it is better for someone to perform Hajj on another’s behalf in order to repay his debt or to refrain. The more correct view is that refraining is better.

“Performing Hajj in order to retain some surplus from the funds provided for expenses was not the practice of the early generations. Indeed, Imam Ahmad said: ‘I do not know of anyone who used to perform Hajj on another’s behalf for payment.’ Had this been a righteous deed, they would have hastened to do it. Earning a livelihood through righteous deeds is not the way of the righteous.

“By this I mean when a person’s sole purpose in doing the deed is to earn money. It is better for this debtor to receive enough zakat to repay his debt than to set out to perform Hajj in order to obtain money with which to repay it. Accepting money to perform Hajj on another’s behalf is recommended only for one of two types of people:

“Either someone who loves Hajj and wishes to see its sacred sites but is unable to do so, and therefore accepts enough to fulfil this worthy desire while also discharging his brother’s obligation of Hajj; or someone who wishes to discharge a deceased person’s obligation of Hajj, whether because of a bond between them, a general compassion for the believers, or something similar, and therefore accepts funds to accomplish that.

“The guiding principle is that what is recommended is to accept money in order to perform Hajj, not to perform Hajj in order to obtain money. This applies to all stipends received for righteous work. Whoever accepts support in order to learn, teach, or engage in jihad does well.

“As it is reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

‘Those of my community who go out to fight and receive their pay are like the mother of Moses, who nursed her son and received her wages.’

“He likened them to someone who performs an act

---

## Page 12796

out of a desire to do so, just as Moses’ mother desired to nurse her son, unlike a hired wet nurse who is unrelated to the child. As for someone who engages in the outward form of righteous work merely to earn a living, this is a worldly pursuit. There is a difference between someone whose aim is religion and who uses worldly means to serve it, and someone whose aim is worldly gain and who uses religion as a means to obtain it. The more likely view is that the latter will have no share in the Hereafter, as indicated by scriptural texts which this is not the place to discuss.”

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man owes a debt to someone who is away in Baghdad, while the debtor lives in Egypt and is unable to pay. Another person wishes to take him on Hajj at his own expense. May he perform Hajj while still owing this debt?”

He replied:

“Yes, a debtor who is unable to pay may perform Hajj at someone else’s expense, provided this does not compromise the creditor’s right to repayment—whether because the debtor is unable to earn or because the creditor is absent and cannot be paid from the debtor’s earnings. And Allah knows best.”

---

## Page 12797

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

“A man set out for Hajj to Allah’s Sacred House with provisions and a mount, but died on the way. Is his obligation thereby discharged or not?”

He replied:

“All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds. His obligation is not thereby discharged. If he set out for Hajj when it became obligatory upon him, without neglecting it, he died without being sinful. If, however, he neglected it after it became obligatory, he died in a state of disobedience. Hajj is to be performed on his behalf, starting from the place he reached. If he left any wealth, the expenses must be paid from it, according to the stronger of the two scholarly opinions.

“The details are as follows:

“If a person has the means to perform Hajj, possessing provisions and a mount, Hajj becomes obligatory upon him by scholarly consensus. If he then sets out for Hajj as soon as he is able and dies on the way, Allah has guaranteed his reward. He dies without being sinful and receives the reward for his intention and purpose. If, however, he neglected it, then later set out and died before performing Hajj, he died in a state of disobedience and sin, though he receives the reward for what he did. His obligation is not thereby discharged; rather, Hajj remains a liability upon him, and it is to be performed on his behalf, starting from the place he reached. And Allah knows best.”

---

## Page 12798

Chapter on Ihrām (Ritual Consecration)

Shaykh al-Islām was asked:

“The scholars of our school—may Allah have mercy on them—have reported differing views concerning whether ihrām is an essential pillar of Hajj. Elsewhere, however, they state that ihrām means the intention to perform Hajj. How, then, can there be disagreement concerning intention, when a legally valid Hajj is inconceivable without it? Please clarify this for us; may you be richly rewarded.”

He replied:

“All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds. This question can be answered in two ways: generally and in detail.

“As for the general answer, we say: Regarding the intention to perform Hajj or ʿumrah, there is no disagreement among the scholars of our school or the rest of the Muslims that Hajj is valid only with intention, whether that of the pilgrim himself or of the person taking him on Hajj, as when a guardian takes a child on Hajj. If a man were to perform the rites of Hajj without intending it, his Hajj would not be valid, just as prayer and fasting are not valid without intention. This holds whether one maintains that Hajj is initiated by intention alone, or that it requires both intention and some further utterance or action, such as reciting the pilgrim’s invocation or garlanding a sacrificial animal—a matter of disagreement

---

## Page 12799

well known among the scholars on this issue. This holds whether we consider ihrām an essential pillar or not. The necessity of intention admits of no disagreement, for acts intended as worship cannot qualify as the prescribed acts of worship without intention. Whether ihrām takes effect through intention alone, however, is disputed both within our school and elsewhere, as we shall explain, if Allah Most High wills.

There is a distinction between the intention required for Hajj and the intention by which one enters ihrām. A man may intend to perform Hajj from the moment he leaves his home, as commonly happens, and perform the standing and circumambulation while maintaining that intention, both consciously and in legal effect, even though he neither intends to enter ihrām nor even thinks of it.

The basis of this distinction is that intention, as understood in acts of worship, comprises two elements: intending the act of worship and directing one’s intention to the One worshipped. Directing one’s intention to the One worshipped is the foundation indicated by His words, glorified be He:

“They were commanded only to worship Allah, devoting their religion sincerely to Him.”

And by the words of the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“Whoever’s migration is to Allah and His Messenger, his migration is to Allah and His Messenger. But whoever’s migration is for worldly gain or a woman to marry, his migration is to that for which he migrated.”

He (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) thus distinguished between one intended goal and another, and this

---

## Page 12800

intended goal is, in general, indispensable to every voluntary act. The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“The truest names are Hārith (one who works) and Hammām (one who resolves).”

For every human being—indeed, every animal—must have resolve, meaning will, and engage in work, meaning action, since moving by its own will is an essential characteristic of an animal. Whatever it intends is its goal, even though another intention may subsequently arise. Souls find tranquillity only when they attain their intended goal.

As for intending the act of worship, this means intending the particular deed. Someone who seeks Allah and the Hereafter through his deeds may seek Him through prayer or through Hajj. Likewise, someone who intends to obey Him by complying with His command has obeyed Him in this act. He may also intend to obey Him through this particular act. This second kind of intention is like intending prayer rather than fasting, then the noon prayer rather than the afternoon prayer, then an obligatory prayer rather than a voluntary one. This is the intention generally discussed in later works of jurisprudence. Both kinds of intention are obligatory in general.

As for the first, it distinguishes those who worship Allah, devoting their religion sincerely to Him, from those who worship false objects of worship or associate others in the worship of their Lord. It also distinguishes those who seek the harvest of the Hereafter from those who seek the harvest of this world. This is the religion devoted sincerely to Allah, which all revealed laws share and concerning which the prophets were forbidden to become divided. As Allah Most High says:

“He has ordained for you of religion what He enjoined upon Noah, and that which We have revealed to you, and what We enjoined upon Abraham and Moses

---

## Page 12801

and Jesus—to establish the religion and not be divided therein.”

For this reason, the religion of the prophets was one, even though their revealed laws differed. Allah Most High says:

“And ask those of Our messengers whom We sent before you: Have We appointed besides the Most Merciful any gods to be worshipped?”

Allah Most High also says:

“And We sent no messenger before you except that We revealed to him: There is no deity except Me, so worship Me.”

And He says:

“We certainly sent a messenger to every nation, saying: Worship Allah and shun false gods.”

And He says:

“I did not create jinn and mankind except to worship Me.”

And He says:

“O mankind, worship your Lord.”

As for the second kind of intention, it distinguishes the various types of worship and categories of prescribed observances. It distinguishes someone performing prayer from someone performing Hajj or fasting; someone performing the noon prayer and fasting to make up missed days of Ramadan from someone performing the afternoon prayer and fasting some days of Shawwāl; and someone giving charity as zakat on his wealth from someone giving it to fulfil a binding vow or as an expiation.

The forms of worship are among the matters in which revealed laws differ, for religion cannot subsist without revealed law: the deeds of the heart are not complete without the deeds of the body, just as the soul cannot subsist without the body—I mean, so long as it is in this world. Likewise, the meanings conveyed in speech are not fully expressed without words, and speech is constituted by words and meaning together, even though meaning does not differ from one nation to another, while the wording

---

## Page 12802

varies from one nation to another. Moreover, the language of some nations may convey meaning more fully than that of others, and some expressions within a language convey meaning more fully than others.

The religion common to all concerns the intention of the heart, but bodily action is indispensable for bringing that intention to completion and perfection. Bodily deeds have therefore varied accordingly; their variety also reflects the requirements of Allah’s will, His mercy towards His servants, and His wisdom in what He commands. Both kinds of intention are obligatory because Allah has required us to uphold His religion according to the revealed law with which He sent His Messenger Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He does not accept that we worship Him according to any other revealed law.

The prescribed acts of worship consist of specific words and deeds, for which particular times, places, and characteristics may be stipulated. Whenever we are obliged to worship Allah and to perform that worship in a particular manner, we are obliged to intend Him with the intention by which we become His worshippers, and to have the intention by which we worship Him through the very deed He has commanded.

Know, then, that intentions may be present in general terms or in detail, and they may arise by implication. Intentions may also differ in merit, such that the obligation is discharged by the least of them, while greater merit belongs to whoever fulfils the highest. Something may be intended through the second intention without the first. A person may then be conscious of the second intention while being heedless of the first. For a person, in

---

## Page 12803

intending worship, may seek the Face of Allah in general terms, or seek to obey Him, worship Him, or draw near to Him. He may seek His reward without having any particular reward in mind, or hope for a particular reward in the Hereafter, in this world, or in both; or he may fear punishment, whether conceived of generally or in detail. The particulars of these intentions constitute a vast subject.

From this perspective, he may have no preference for one kind of bodily deed over another, except by considering how this compares with the intention for a particular kind of deed. Someone who intends to perform Hajj may have only a general conception of it—namely, that it involves going to a particular place—and thus intend what he has conceived, without knowing or intending its individual rites: standing, circumambulation, refraining from prohibited acts, and so forth. Rather, the individual rites of Hajj become objects of his intention only when he becomes conscious of them. He may know the kinds of rites involved in Hajj—that they include standing, circumambulation, and the like—because they have been described to him, even though he does not know the exact place or how circumambulation is performed; he then intends those rites. Or he may know all these details and intend what he knows.

Likewise, when a disbeliever embraces Islam and we tell him, “Prayer has now become obligatory for you,” he commits himself to it and intends to perform it because he has a general conception of it, even though he does not yet know how it is performed. Indeed, everyone who has firm faith in the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, has a faith that entails believing what the Messenger reports and obeying what he commands, even if he neither knows nor intends the various kinds of

---

## Page 12804

reports and deeds. Then, upon learning the details, he either believes and obeys, thus becoming one of those who believe and do righteous deeds, or acts otherwise, becoming a hypocrite, a sinful transgressor, or something else.

This makes clear to you that there are three categories:

A man intends to worship and obey Allah but does not intend the specific deed commanded. For example, a man possesses wealth from which he gives to those who ask and those in need, seeking thereby the Face of Allah, without giving any thought to zakat, expiation, or distributing it among all eight categories rather than only some of them. He is rewarded for what he does for Allah, glorified be He, but the obligatory duties remain incumbent upon him.

Another man may intend the specific deed without intending to obey and worship Allah. For example, he pays the zakat on his wealth to the ruler lest the ruler behead him, diminish his standing, or seize his wealth; or he stands to pray out of fear for his life, wealth, or honour. This is the general condition of the hypocrites, and the condition of those who show off in particular deeds.

As Allah Most High says:

“When they stand for prayer, they stand lazily, showing off before people.”

And He says:

“So woe to those who pray,
who are heedless of their prayer,
who show off,
and withhold even small kindnesses.”

And Allah Most High says:

“They come to prayer only lazily and spend only reluctantly.”

The third category is one who intends to perform the particular deed he has been commanded to perform

---

## Page 12805

for Allah, glorified be He. The jurists agree that intending the particular kind of obligatory act is necessary in principle: one must intend prayer, Hajj, or fasting. They have detailed discussions and disagreements concerning subsidiary questions, but this is not the place for them.

They differed concerning the first intention, namely, the intention to dedicate the act to Allah Most High. Some scholars of our school said that this intention is not obligatory. Others distinguished between acts of worship intended for their own sake, such as prayer, Hajj, and fasting, and those not intended for their own sake, such as ritual purification and dry ablution.” Likewise, according to the sounder of their two views, the scholars of al-Shāfiʿī’s school did not require an intention to dedicate the act to Allah Most High. This is because the very intention to perform an act of worship implicitly includes dedicating it to Him, just as it includes the number of units of prayer. Prayer is prescribed only for Allah Most High, just as the noon prayer, when one is not travelling, can only consist of four units. For this reason, the intention to dedicate the act to Him was not obligatory.

Furthermore, an intention that remains in force takes the place of an intention consciously kept in mind, although consciously maintaining the intention is more complete and better. Thus, if a servant intends the noon prayer at the outset, it suffices for that intention to remain in force. Likewise, the believing servant into whose heart faith has entered has already formed a general intention that his acts of worship are for Allah alone and no one else; otherwise, he would be a hypocrite. When he intends a particular act of worship, such as prayer or fasting, he maintains the continuing effect of that

---

## Page 12806

general intention, which encompasses all forms of worship. Likewise, when he intends to bow and prostrate during prayer, he maintains the continuing effect of his intention to perform the noon or afternoon prayer, which encompasses all the acts of that prayer. Then, if he does something that negates his awareness of that intention, he invalidates it, thereby revoking it, just as if he revoked his intention to pray during the prayer itself. Thus, if he stands to pray to avoid being beaten or having his property seized, or pays zakat to avoid being beaten, he has revoked that intention arising from faith.

For this reason, the correct view in our school and among most scholars is that such worship is invalid: an act performed with this intention does not discharge the obligation. We have also held that obligatory acts of worship performed for show are invalid, and that when the ruler takes zakat from someone who refuses to pay it, this does not discharge that person’s obligation inwardly, according to the sounder of the two views.

However, most Muslims are born to Muslim parents and thus acquire Muslim status by legal judgment, without themselves having actual faith. When they reach maturity, some are granted actual faith and fulfil their obligations, while others do what they do purely out of habit and in imitation of their relatives, the people of their town, and the like. For example, someone may pay zakat because it is customary for the ruler to collect levies, without any awareness, either general or detailed, that zakat is obligatory upon him. To him, there is no difference between newly instituted levies and the zakat prescribed by the religion. Another example is a resident of Mecca who goes out to Arafāt every year because that is the prevailing custom, without any awareness, either general or detailed, that this is an act of worship devoted to Allah; or someone who fights the disbelievers

---

## Page 12807

because his people fought them, and he fought merely in following his people, and so on.

The worship of such people is unquestionably invalid. Indeed, the texts of the Quran and Sunnah and the consensus of the Muslim community establish that these acts do not discharge the obligation. Let no one therefore suppose that those jurists who said, “The intention to dedicate the act to Allah is not obligatory,” meant people such as these. Rather, they considered the continuing effect of the intention sufficient in this regard, as we explained above. They thus distinguished between someone who does not seek Allah through his deeds, either generally or specifically, and someone who seeks Him generally but is heedless of specifically seeking Him through the particular deed.

No one in the Muslim community would say that the former is worshipping Allah or fulfilling what He has commanded in any way. This is clear.

Some scholars of our school required this intention when performing the particular act. They said: “The intention required in prayer is to intend to perform the particular prayer that Allah has enjoined upon one and to comply with His binding command, without showing off or seeking a reputation.” Some expressed it as “following His command and devoting the deed sincerely to Him.”

The statements of most of them indicate this, for they cite, as evidence that intention is required in purification, prayer, and similar acts, His words:

“They were commanded only to worship Allah, devoting their religion sincerely to Him.”

They said: “Devoting one’s religion sincerely to Allah is intention.” Whoever bathes to cool himself or to become clean has not devoted his worship sincerely to Allah. They also cite His words:

“Whoever desires the harvest of the Hereafter, We increase his harvest for him; and whoever desires the harvest of this world, We give him some of it, but he will have no share in the Hereafter.”

They said that whoever bathes to cool himself and to become clean has not sought the harvest of the Hereafter

---

## Page 12808

and so it follows that he has not devoted his worship sincerely to Him. Clearly, these two verses indicate the obligation to act for Allah and the Hereafter more emphatically than they indicate the obligation to intend the particular act.

However, someone who supports the first view might say: Intending the specific kind necessarily entails intending the general category. Whoever intends the particular act has thereby intended to act for Allah by virtue of his faith, as explained above.

Someone who supports the second view says: The obligatory intention cannot precede the act by twenty years. Rather, it may precede it only by a short interval, or from the beginning of the time when it becomes due, according to the two differing views.

Moreover, the apparent purport of the evidence and analogical reasoning require the intention to be consciously present throughout the act of worship. Maintaining that conscious awareness during worship has been excused only because of the hardship involved. Yet there is no hardship in intending to worship Allah when undertaking each act of worship.

Furthermore, most people are Muslims by outward legal status; Islam only enters their hearts later, if it enters at all. If this intention is not made obligatory for them, they will not form it, and their hearts will remain devoid of it. They will thus become hypocrites, performing deeds merely out of habit and conformity, as is indeed the case with many people.

---

## Page 12809

Shaykh al-Islām Abū al-ʿAbbās Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah be pleased with him and grant him contentment—was asked:

“Which is preferable: tamattuʿ (performing ʿumrah and Hajj with a break in ritual consecration between them) or qirān (combining them in a single state of ritual consecration)?”

He replied:

All praise belongs to Allah. We seek His help and forgiveness, and we seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whoever Allah guides, none can misguide; and whoever He leaves astray, none can guide. We bear witness that there is no god worthy of worship except Allah alone, with no partner, and we bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bless him and his family and grant him abundant peace.

There is no disagreement within Ahmad’s school that, if a person arrives during the months of Hajj without bringing sacrificial animals, tamattuʿ in its specific sense is preferable for him. This means performing ʿumrah and leaving ihrām (ritual consecration) after circumambulating the House and going between al-Safā and al-Marwah, then entering ihrām for Hajj.

If, however, he brings sacrificial animals, al-Marwadhī reported from Ahmad that qirān is preferable. Some of our fellow scholars treated this as a second position reported from Ahmad. Thus, for someone who brings sacrificial animals, they presented two reported positions on whether tamattuʿ or qirān is preferable. This is the approach of the later scholars who said that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—

---

## Page 12810

performed Hajj as tamattuʿ. According to this view, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—performed tamattuʿ, brought sacrificial animals, and instructed his Companions to perform tamattuʿ. There would therefore be no grounds for preferring qirān.

However, Ahmad’s explicit position, followed by the leading early scholars of his school, is that the Prophet performed Hajj as qirān, but instructed those of his Companions who had not brought sacrificial animals to perform tamattuʿ: they were to leave ihrām and make their pilgrimage tamattuʿ. He said:

“Had I known beforehand what I now know, I would not have brought sacrificial animals, and I would have made it ʿumrah.”

According to this view, the issue concerns unqualified and qualified statements. Ahmad did not explicitly state that tamattuʿ is preferable for someone who brings sacrificial animals. Rather, he preferred tamattuʿ because the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—instructed his Companions to perform it, and because he said:

“Had I known beforehand what I now know, I would not have brought sacrificial animals, and I would have made it ʿumrah.”

The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—only instructed those who had not brought sacrificial animals to leave ihrām. He only expressed a preference for making it ʿumrah; someone who has not chosen to make it ʿumrah while bringing sacrificial animals does not leave ihrām.

Furthermore, Ahmad did not say that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—performed Hajj as tamattuʿ in its specific sense. Rather, he explicitly stated that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—performed Hajj as qirān. He said:

“I have no doubt that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—performed qirān, but tamattuʿ is preferable to me, because it was the later of the two instructions from the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”

---

## Page 12811

for he said:

“Had I known beforehand what I now know, I would not have brought sacrificial animals, and I would have made it ʿumrah.”

His discussion therefore concerned only which is preferable: to bring sacrificial animals and perform qirān, or to perform tamattuʿ without bringing sacrificial animals. For someone who brings sacrificial animals may not leave ihrām. This is a matter on which scholarly judgment may differ. When the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Had I known beforehand what I now know, I would not have brought sacrificial animals, and I would have made it ʿumrah,”

was this because leaving ihrām after ʿumrah is preferable to qirān? Or was it not for that reason, but rather to join his Companions, who had found it difficult when he instructed them to leave ihrām? This is a matter open to scholarly judgment. Ahmad’s statements, however, consistently maintain that tamattuʿ is preferable for someone who has not brought sacrificial animals and arrives during the months of Hajj.

Furthermore, if someone brings sacrificial animals and arrives during the first ten days of Dhū al-Hijjah, then according to Ahmad, Abū Hanīfah and others, he may not leave ihrām until he slaughters the sacrificial animals on the Day of Sacrifice, whether he is performing tamattuʿ in its specific sense or qirān.

In that case, according to Ahmad, there are only two differences between someone performing tamattuʿ and someone performing qirān.

The first is that someone performing qirān will have entered ihrām for Hajj before circumambulating the Kaaba, whether he entered ihrām for Hajj and ʿumrah together, or entered ihrām for ʿumrah and then added Hajj to it. In either case, he is performing qirān, by agreement of the imams.

As for someone performing tamattuʿ in its specific sense, he delays entering ihrām for Hajj until after

---

## Page 12812

completing ʿumrah. It is clear, then, that entering ihrām for Hajj earlier is preferable to delaying it. Thus, qirān is preferable for someone who has brought sacrificial animals.

The second difference is that, according to Ahmad, someone performing qirān performs saʿy—the ritual walking between Safā and Marwah—only once, just like someone performing Hajj alone. For someone performing tamattuʿ, however, he prefers that saʿy be performed twice, although he explicitly states that performing it once suffices, just as it does for someone performing Hajj alone or qirān. On this basis, tamattuʿ would be distinguished by an additional, recommended saʿy. Yet he also recommends that someone performing tamattuʿ first perform the arrival circumambulation after returning from ʿArafah. Thus, someone performing tamattuʿ would circumambulate twice after ʿArafah and perform saʿy a second time.

Someone performing qirān, by contrast, performs the same rites as someone performing Hajj alone. All of this, however, is disputed. Another position within Ahmad’s school holds that a second saʿy is obligatory for someone performing tamattuʿ.

Another position holds that someone performing qirān must circumambulate twice and perform saʿy twice, as in the school of Abū Hanīfah.

Yet another position holds that the arrival circumambulation is not recommended for someone performing tamattuʿ. This is the correct view. Indeed, a second saʿy is not recommended for him either, for the Companions who performed Hajj with the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—performed saʿy only once. This makes clear why performing qirān while bringing sacrificial animals is preferable to performing tamattuʿ without bringing them. If, however, the rites of someone performing tamattuʿ include an additional obligatory or recommended saʿy

---

## Page 12813

or an additional recommended circumambulation, it may be said that it is preferable in this respect. Yet this is contrary to the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Moreover, even if it were conceded that this is recommended, it would not follow that doing more is always better. Rather, what is preferable may be easier: tamattuʿ is preferable to performing Hajj alone, yet it is easier; not fasting while travelling is preferable, yet it is easier; and shortening the prayers is likewise preferable to praying four units, yet it is easier.

Another argument for preferring someone performing tamattuʿ is that his first circumambulation is obligatory, since it is the circumambulation of ʿumrah, whereas the first circumambulation of someone performing qirān is the arrival circumambulation, which is not obligatory; and what is obligatory is preferable. This claim, however, is not conceded. Merit depends on the extent of the benefit in an act, whereas obligation entails harm in leaving it undone.

Ahmad’s statements consistently maintain that tamattuʿ is preferable for someone who has not brought sacrificial animals and arrives during the months of Hajj. This is because the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—commanded everyone who performed Hajj with him to leave ihrām and make their pilgrimage an ʿumrah, except those who had brought sacrificial animals.

Ahmad’s position is also that if someone makes one journey for Hajj alone and another for ʿumrah, performing them separately in this way is preferable for him to tamattuʿ. He explicitly stated this in more than one place. Jurists of his school also mention it, including al-Qādī Abū Yaʿlā in his commentary, and others. This is likewise

---

## Page 12814

the position of all the other scholars, even the followers of Abū Hanīfah. They expressly stated that the Kūfan ʿumrah is preferable to qirān, even though they otherwise regard qirān as preferable. However, the qirān performed by the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—is not the qirān described by Abū Hanīfah, for the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—performed only one circumambulation and one saʿy. Abū Hanīfah’s position is that someone performing qirān first performs circumambulation and saʿy for ʿumrah, then circumambulation and saʿy for Hajj. If he commits an act prohibited in ihrām, he owes two compensatory penalties, one for Hajj and one for ʿumrah.

This has also been reported as one position transmitted from Ahmad: that someone performing qirān must perform two circumambulations and two performances of saʿy, as Abū Hanīfah maintains. However, Ahmad’s well-known position, explicitly stated on several occasions, agrees with that of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī and others: someone performing qirān undertakes no additional rites beyond those undertaken by someone performing Hajj alone.

Indeed, the matter goes further: two positions are transmitted from Ahmad concerning whether someone performing tamattuʿ can suffice with the initial saʿy accompanying the circumambulation of ʿumrah, or needs a second saʿy after the circumambulation of ifādah or another circumambulation. The second is the well-known position among his followers, though he also explicitly stated the first.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ahmad said: I asked my father, “Does someone performing tamattuʿ perform saʿy between al-Safā and al-Marwah?” He replied:

---

## Page 12815

“If he performs sa'y twice, that is better; but if he performs it once, there is no harm.” He said: “Performing it twice is preferable to me,” and cited the hadith of Jābir in support. Ibn Mansūr likewise transmitted this from him. His position on this differed only because the hadiths concerning it differ. In Sahīh Muslim, Jābir said:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and his Companions performed sa'y between al-Safā and al-Marwah only once”—his initial sa'y. This was despite their performing tamattu'.

Ahmad narrated: Al-Walīd ibn Muslim narrated to us: Al-Awzā'ī narrated to us, from 'Atā', from Ibn 'Abbās, that he used to say: “For someone performing qirān, tamattu', or Hajj alone, one circumambulation of the House and one sa'y between al-Safā and al-Marwah suffice.”

In the two Sahīhs, 'Ā'ishah said:

“We set out with the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—in the year of the Farewell Pilgrimage and entered ihrām for 'umrah. Then the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said, ‘Whoever has brought a sacrificial animal should enter ihrām for both Hajj and 'umrah, and must not leave ihrām until he completes both.’” The narration continues until she said: “Those who had entered ihrām for 'umrah circumambulated the House and performed sa'y between al-Safā and al-Marwah, then left ihrām. After returning from Minā, they performed another circumambulation for their Hajj. As for those who had combined Hajj and 'umrah,

---

## Page 12816

they performed only one circumambulation of the House.”

I say:

By “another circumambulation,” she meant circumambulation of the House together with sa'y between al-Safā and al-Marwah, as she mentioned at the beginning of the hadith. Moreover, those who combined Hajj and 'umrah must perform tawāf al-ifādah (the obligatory circumambulation). Thus, we know that she was denying an additional circumambulation accompanied by sa'y between al-Safā and al-Marwah, not a circumambulation of the House on its own. What she denied for the pilgrim performing qirān, she affirmed for the pilgrim performing tamattu' who entered ihrām for 'umrah without incorporating Hajj into it.

According to some reports of his position, Ahmad understood this to mean that they performed an arrival circumambulation of the House without sa'y. He therefore recommended that a pilgrim performing tamattu', upon returning from Minā, should first perform an arrival circumambulation and then the obligatory circumambulation. Anyone who rejects Ahmad’s argument on the grounds that the circumambulation meant here is the obligatory one is mistaken. The obligatory circumambulation is common to those performing tamattu', Hajj alone, and qirān, whereas 'Ā'ishah affirmed for those performing tamattu' something she denied for those performing qirān.

Rather, this hadith refers to circumambulation of the House together with sa'y between al-Safā and al-Marwah—unless she meant circumambulation of the House, since she herself circumambulated the House only once because of her menstruation. This, however, is opposed by Jābir’s authentic hadith:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and his Companions whom he instructed to leave ihrām and make their pilgrimage an 'umrah performed sa'y between al-Safā and al-Marwah only on the first occasion.”

And this

---

## Page 12817

contradicts what was understood from the hadith of 'Ā'ishah. For if they did not perform sa'y after the obligatory circumambulation, there is all the more reason to conclude that they did not perform an arrival circumambulation before it. There is much to discuss concerning which of the two hadiths should be given precedence, but this is not the place to elaborate. Indeed, meticulous hadith scholars know that this addition to 'Ā'ishah’s hadith consists of al-Zuhrī’s words, not those of 'Ā'ishah, and therefore does not contradict the authentic hadith. Al-Bukhārī also cited a report from Ibn 'Abbās, without its full chain of transmission, similar to the hadith of 'Ā'ishah. That report, too, has a defect.

Al-Shāfi'ī sometimes favored tamattu' and sometimes favored performing Hajj alone—and whoever says that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, entered ihrām without specifying the form of pilgrimage is mistaken. Al-Shāfi'ī’s statements concerning the Prophet’s ihrām varied among these three views.

Mālik favors performing Hajj alone, although it has been said that, when doing so, it is recommended to delay 'umrah until Muharram. As for setting out from Mecca to perform 'umrah immediately after Hajj, as many people do today, this was not known in the time of the early generations. No one has reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or any of those who performed Hajj with him did this, except 'Ā'ishah, may Allah be pleased with her. She had arrived intending to perform tamattu', but then began menstruating, so the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed her to enter ihrām for Hajj and set aside her 'umrah.

---

## Page 12818

The position of Ahmad, Mālik, and al-Shāfi'ī is that she had entered qirān—combining Hajj and 'umrah in one ihrām—and was not obliged to make up that 'umrah. However, according to one of the two reports from Ahmad, he held that she was obliged to make it up: according to the well-known report from him, 'umrah is obligatory, and, according to one of the two reports from him, neither the 'umrah included in qirān nor an 'umrah begun at the nearest point outside the Sacred Precinct discharges that obligation.

They give the same ruling for anyone intending tamattu' who runs short of time and cannot perform the circumambulation before the standing at 'Arafah. They instruct him to add Hajj to his 'umrah, thereby entering qirān. He is then like someone performing Hajj alone who arrives with little time remaining: he stands at 'Arafah first, without performing the circumambulation beforehand. This is also what pilgrims from Iraq do when they arrive late: they reach 'Arafah on the day of the standing and stand there without having performed the circumambulation beforehand.

Abū Hanīfah’s position is that 'Ā'ishah abandoned her 'umrah and entered ihrām for Hajj, thus becoming someone performing Hajj alone. According to him, she was obliged to make up the 'umrah she had abandoned. He based this on his principle that someone performing qirān must perform two circumambulations and two sa'ys; thus, qirān would have offered her no benefit.

The majority, however, based their position on their principle that the rites required of someone performing qirān are no more than those required of someone performing Hajj alone. They said that “the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, only arranged for 'Ā'ishah to perform 'umrah

---

## Page 12819

to put her mind at ease, for she said: ‘My companions will return having performed Hajj and 'umrah, while I shall return having performed only Hajj.’ The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, told her: ‘Your circumambulation suffices for your Hajj and 'umrah.’ In the version reported in the Sunan collections: ‘Your circumambulation of the House and your passage between al-Safā and al-Marwah suffice for your Hajj and 'umrah.’”

When she persisted, he arranged for her to perform 'umrah to put her mind at ease. In the report transmitted by al-Athram and others, Ahmad said that neither the 'umrah included in qirān nor an 'umrah undertaken from Mecca discharges the obligation of 'umrah. He cited as evidence the hadith of 'Ā'ishah, in which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, arranged for her to perform 'umrah: she had performed qirān, yet he arranged for her to perform 'umrah afterwards. Thus, in this report, Ahmad regarded this 'umrah as obligatory, as did Abū Hanīfah. They differed, however, as to the precise basis of the ruling.

During the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, no one undertook 'umrah from Mecca except 'Ā'ishah, owing to this particular circumstance. As for the Prophet’s 'umrahs, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he performed them only when travelling to Mecca. In the year of al-Hudaybiyah, he entered ihrām for 'umrah at Dhū al-Hulayfah and left ihrām at al-Hudaybiyah when he was prevented from proceeding and the polytheists barred him from the House. Al-Hudaybiyah lies west of Mount al-Tan'īm; it was there that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, received his Companions’ pledge beneath the tree and the polytheists concluded a treaty with him.

Mount al-Tan'īm is the mountain beside the mosques known as the Mosques of 'Ā'ishah, on your right as you enter Mecca. Those mosques are built in al-Tan'īm.

---

## Page 12820

and these mosques did not exist in the time of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. . . . (1)

For the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed her to perform 'umrah from al-Tan'īm. Al-Tan'īm is the nearest place outside the Sacred Precinct to Mecca. Someone performing 'umrah from Mecca goes outside the Sacred Precinct so that his pilgrimage encompasses both the area outside it and the Sacred Precinct itself. This differs from someone performing Hajj from Mecca, who goes out to 'Arafah, which lies outside the Sacred Precinct.

Then, in the following year, he performed the make-up 'umrah, entering ihrām at Dhū al-Hulayfah. Later, after encountering Hawāzin in the valley of Hunayn and defeating them, he went to al-Tā'if and besieged them. He then returned to al-Ji'rānah and distributed the spoils of Hunayn there, before proceeding into Mecca to perform 'umrah. Hunayn, al-Ji'rānah and al-Tā'if all lie to the east of 'Arafāt. The closest of them to 'Arafah is al-Ji'rānah, followed by the valley of Hunayn, then al-Tā'if.

Neither he nor his Companions would go out from Mecca to perform 'umrah, apart from the instance already mentioned in the hadith of 'Ā'ishah. For this reason, Ahmad explicitly stated on more than one occasion that 'umrah is not obligatory for the people of Mecca. Ahmad also narrated that Ibn 'Abbās said:

“O people of Mecca, 'umrah is not obligatory for you. Your 'umrah is simply circumambulating the House. Whoever nevertheless insists on performing 'umrah should put a valley between himself and Mecca.”

This is because the Companions residing in Mecca during the lifetime of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not undertake 'umrah from Mecca.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 205):
> The shaykh, may Allah have mercy on him, gave a similar answer elsewhere in this volume (26:102), saying: “Al-Tan‘īm is the nearest point outside the Sacred Precinct to Mecca. There stand today the mosques called ‘the Mosques of ‘Ā’ishah.’ These did not exist during the lifetime of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace; rather, they were built later to mark the place where ‘Ā’ishah entered ihrām (ritual consecration).” Perhaps, then, the blank here should contain the words, “Rather, they were built later to mark the place where ‘Ā’ishah entered ihrām,” or something similar. And Allah Most High knows best.

---

## Page 12821

‘Umrah is obligatory according to the better-known of the two positions reported from Ahmad. Some of his followers therefore treated this as a third reported position, saying that three positions are reported on this question: that it is obligatory; that it is not obligatory; and that a distinction is made between Meccans and others. This is the approach of our grandfather Abū al-Barakāt and others.

Others said that the people of Mecca are exempt, and ‘umrah is not obligatory for them, with no differing position reported from Ahmad on this point. This is the approach of Shaykh Abū Muhammad, and it is the more correct view.

Some jurists recommended that someone undertaking ‘umrah from Mecca enter ihrām at al-Hudaybiyah or al-Ji‘rānah, citing the ‘umrah of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as evidence. This is mistaken. Al-Hudaybiyah was where he left ihrām when he was prevented from proceeding, not where he entered it. As for al-Ji‘rānah, he entered ihrām there while on his way into Mecca, because that was where he began his ‘umrah.

For this reason, the more correct of the two views held by the scholars of our school—and the position explicitly stated by Ahmad—is that frequent ‘umrah is not recommended, whether from Mecca or elsewhere. Rather, one should leave an interval between two ‘umrahs, even if only long enough for one’s hair to grow back sufficiently to be shaved. This applies to someone who goes out to the designated ihrām point for his region and performs ‘umrah.

For someone staying in Mecca, however, frequent circumambulation of the House is better than undertaking ‘umrah from Mecca. This was the practice of the Companions: when staying in Mecca, they would circumambulate the House frequently rather than undertake ‘umrah from Mecca. Thus, the Companions who recommended performing Hajj separately

---

## Page 12822

such as ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb and others, only recommended making another journey for ‘umrah, so that Hajj would have its own journey and ‘umrah its own journey. Ahmad, Abū Hanīfah and others followed the Companions in this, preferring this form of separate Hajj to tamattu‘ (‘umrah followed by Hajj, with release from ihrām between them) and qirān (combining ‘umrah and Hajj in a single state of ihrām).

Abū Bakr al-Athram said:

Abū ‘Abd Allāh was asked, “Which manner of performing ‘umrah do you consider best?”

He replied, “In my view, the best ‘umrah is one performed outside the months of Hajj, as ‘Umar said: ‘It is more complete for your Hajj and more complete for your ‘umrah that you perform it outside the months of Hajj.’”

Abū ‘Abd Allāh was asked, “Do you then enjoin tamattu‘ while saying that ‘umrah outside the months of Hajj is better?”

He replied, “I was only asked about the most complete ‘umrah, and I said that it is outside the months of Hajj. I also said that tamattu‘ suffices for one’s ‘umrah. Thus, the most complete ‘umrah is one performed outside the months of Hajj.”

‘Alī said, “Part of making ‘umrah complete is to come from your own home.” Sufyān ibn ‘Uyaynah explained this as setting out on a journey specifically for it, not entering ihrām at home before reaching the designated ihrām point.

‘Umar said concerning ‘umrah, “From your own home.”

Abū ‘Abd Allāh was asked, “Should one then make a separate journey for Hajj and a separate journey for ‘umrah?”

He replied, “Yes.”

I said to him, “If someone performs ‘umrah outside the months of Hajj, then stays in Mecca until he performs Hajj, would he thereby have

---

## Page 12823

made a separate journey for ‘umrah and a separate journey for Hajj?”

He replied, “No—not until he returns home and then performs Hajj. Thus, one sets out from one’s home for ‘umrah and sets out from one’s home for Hajj. That is what it means.”

Abū ‘Abd Allāh was asked, “People report that you say tamattu‘ is better than the other forms of Hajj.”

He replied, “There is no doubt that it is better than Hajj alone.” He then said, “Which is better: to perform ‘umrah and Hajj, or to perform Hajj alone? It is better than performing Hajj alone.”

I said to him, “And it is better than qirān, because one performs each separately, which is better than combining them.”

He replied, “Yes, and better than qirān.” He then said something similar to what I had said.

Al-Athram said:

I heard Abū ‘Abd Allāh say, “Tamattu‘ is preferable to me. It was the later of the Messenger of Allah’s two instructions, for he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Had I known beforehand what I know now, I would have done as you have done.’ He also told his Companions, ‘Leave ihrām.’ There are also the hadiths reported concerning it.”

He also said:

Abū ‘Abd Allāh was asked, “Do you favour tamattu‘?”

He replied, “It is preferable to me and better. That is because we hold that ‘umrah is obligatory. Allah Most High says:

‘And complete Hajj and ‘umrah for Allah.’”

He then said, “This is clear.”

---

## Page 12824

Ibn ‘Abbās and Ibn ‘Umar regarded it as obligatory. Ibn ‘Abbās said, “By Allah, it is indeed paired with Hajj in the Book of Allah.” A number of scholars said, “The lesser Hajj is ‘umrah.” Since the term Hajj applies to it, this indicates that it is obligatory. Thus, someone who sets out to perform tamattu‘ has fulfilled both his Hajj and his ‘umrah obligations, having performed a separate ‘umrah and a separate Hajj. As for ‘umrah performed in Muharram, I do not consider it sufficient to discharge his obligation. Nor is it a complete ‘umrah: it begins only four miles away.

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to ‘Ā’ishah:

“Your reward is only in proportion to your exertion and expenditure.”

What is meant by ‘umrah in Muharram is that they used to leave Mecca during Muharram and go to the nearest point outside the sacred precincts to perform ‘umrah. What, then, of someone who performs ‘umrah from Mecca in Dhū al-Hijjah immediately after Hajj? This was not a practice of the early generations.

Once it is clear that, by scholarly consensus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, never combined Hajj with an ‘umrah undertaken from Mecca immediately afterward, that none of the Companions did so except ‘Ā’ishah, and that his rightly guided caliphs did not practise it, this cannot be the preferable course.

As for any jurist who says, “Ifrād means performing Hajj and then performing ‘umrah from Mecca immediately afterward,” he is mistaken according to scholarly consensus. For there is no disagreement among them that someone who performs ‘umrah before the months of Hajj and returns home, then performs Hajj, or remains in Mecca until he performs Hajj

---

## Page 12825

in that same year, is performing Hajj alone. Likewise, someone who performs ‘umrah after Hajj on a separate journey is, by agreement, performing Hajj alone. This is the form of ifrād that the Companions recommended, and Ahmad and others also recommend it. Performing ‘umrah in Ramadan and remaining until Hajj is better than tamattu‘, although returning home and then travelling again for Hajj is better still. Tamattu‘ is permissible by scholarly agreement; only a group among the Umayyads and others disliked it.

It has been said that those who disliked it only disliked converting Hajj into tamattu‘. People would arrive from distant regions and enter ihram for Hajj; those who permitted this conversion allowed them to perform tamattu‘, while those who prohibited it prevented them from doing so.

There are three well-known opinions concerning this conversion:

One holds that it is obligatory. This is the view of Ibn ‘Abbās and his followers, the Zāhirīs, and the Shī‘ah.

Another holds that it is forbidden. This is the view of Mu‘āwiyah, Ibn al-Zubayr, and those who followed them, such as Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, and al-Shāfi‘ī.

The third holds that it is permissible and recommended. This is the position of the jurists of the hadith tradition, including Ahmad and others. Instructions to do so are reported from several Companions and Successors.

---

## Page 12826

And for this reason, Ibn ‘Umar and Ibn ‘Abbās used to instruct people to perform tamattu‘.

Ahmad said:

‘Abd al-Razzāq informed us: Ma‘mar narrated to us, from al-Zuhrī, from Sālim, who said: Ibn ‘Umar was asked about tamattu‘ in Hajj, and he instructed people to perform it. He was told, “You are contradicting your father.” He replied, “‘Umar did not say what you claim. He merely said that Hajj should be performed separately from ‘umrah, as this makes ‘umrah more complete—or that ‘umrah is not complete during the months of Hajj unless a sacrificial offering is made. He wanted the House to be visited outside the months of Hajj. Yet you have made tamattu‘ forbidden and punished people for it, although Allah has permitted it and the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, practised it.” When they persisted in arguing with him, he would say, “Which is more deserving of being followed: the Book of Allah or ‘Umar?”

Ibn ‘Abbās also used to instruct people to perform it. They would say, “Abū Bakr and ‘Umar did not perform it.” He would reply, “Stones may soon rain down upon you from heaven! I tell you, ‘The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said,’ and you say, ‘Abū Bakr and ‘Umar said’!”

‘Urwah ibn al-Zubayr used to debate Ibn ‘Abbās about it. He said, “Abū Bakr and ‘Umar knew the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, better than you.” Ibn ‘Abbās replied, “‘Urayyah, ask your mother.” He meant that she would tell him that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had instructed his Companions to leave ihram; Asmā’ was among those who had done so. This dispute arose only because Ibn ‘Abbās held tamattu‘ to be obligatory

---

## Page 12827

—indeed, he held that converting one’s Hajj into ‘umrah was obligatory. He would say:

“Anyone who has circumambulated the House and passed between al-Safā and al-Marwah, without having brought a sacrificial animal, has thereby left ihram.”

He cited as evidence the Prophet’s instruction to his Companions to leave ihram during the Farewell Pilgrimage, and the words of Allah Most High:

“Then their place of sacrifice is at the Ancient House.”

The view that tamattu‘ is obligatory is held by a group of hadith scholars and Zāhirīs, such as Ibn Hazm and others. It is also the position of the Shia. However, the overwhelming majority of the Companions, the four imams, and others hold that tamattu‘, ifrād, and qirān are all permissible. Nevertheless, the people of Mecca, Banū Hāshim, and the hadith scholars consider tamattu‘ preferable. Thus, those versed in his Sunnah, the adherents of his Sunnah, and the people of his city, near which the pilgrimage rites take place, all consider it preferable. These three groups are the people most closely connected to him. This is also one of al-Shāfi‘ī’s two positions. Abū Yūsuf considers tamattu‘ and qirān equal in merit.

The majority permit all three because it is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to his Companions:

“Whoever among you wishes to enter ihram for ‘umrah may do so; whoever wishes to enter ihram for Hajj may do so; and whoever wishes to enter ihram for both Hajj and ‘umrah may do so.”

As for his subsequent instruction to his Companions to leave ihram and make their pilgrimage an ‘umrah—except for those who had brought sacrificial animals—the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, wanted them to combine Hajj and ‘umrah, rather than perform an ‘umrah from Mecca, even if they undertook a journey

---

## Page 12828

on another occasion for ‘umrah. Anyone in this situation should perform tamattu‘. Thus, tamattu‘ was the necessary choice for the Companions if they wished to do what was best for them. Initially, they had been permitted to convert their Hajj into ‘umrah, but had not been commanded to do so. This applies especially if ‘umrah is held to be obligatory: tamattu‘ then becomes obligatory for anyone who does not undertake another journey or perform ‘umrah from Mecca after Hajj.

The ‘umrah performed as part of tamattu‘ is comparable to ablution performed as part of a full ritual bath. When someone bathing to remove major ritual impurity performs ablution, that ablution forms part of the complete bath. Likewise, according to Ahmad, the ‘umrah of someone performing tamattu‘ forms part of his complete Hajj. For this reason, Ahmad permits such a pilgrim to fast the three days from the moment he enters ihram for ‘umrah. Allah Most High says:

“Then a fast of three days during Hajj.”

Thus, from the moment he enters ihram for ‘umrah, he has begun his Hajj, just as someone taking a ritual bath has begun that bath when he performs ablution. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever performs Hajj to this House, neither indulging in obscenity nor committing sin, will return free of sins, as on the day his mother bore him.”

This is recorded in both Sahih collections. It includes the pilgrim performing tamattu‘ from the moment he enters ihram for ‘umrah.

For this reason, Ahmad rejected the claim that the Hajj of someone performing tamattu‘ is a Hajj undertaken from Mecca.

Al-Athram said: “I heard Abū ‘Abd Allāh say: ‘Ibn al-Mubārak, they say, used to advocate tamattu‘. He was told, “In that case, his journey would have been for ‘umrah.” He replied, “Tell me, what if

---

## Page 12829

a man went out intending to offer the noon prayer in congregation, offered four units of voluntary prayer beforehand, and then prayed the noon prayer—would that increase the good he gained or diminish it?”’

Then Ahmad said, ‘How well he put it!’ Abū ‘Abd Allāh then said, ‘He is asking whether his purpose in coming was the noon prayer or the voluntary prayer.’ That is, his purpose in coming was the noon prayer. Abū ‘Abd Allāh said, ‘This is a newly introduced view’—meaning their description of it as ‘a Hajj undertaken from Mecca.’

He said: ‘On another occasion, I heard Abū ‘Abd Allāh mention Ibn al-Mubārak’s statement and say, “It is a newly introduced view”—meaning their description of it as “a Hajj undertaken from Mecca.”

‘Abū ‘Abd Allāh was asked, “Is ‘Abd Allāh’s statement a newly introduced view?” He replied angrily, “Yes, by Allah, it is a newly introduced view! I do not know what to make of it. How could it not be newly introduced, when the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, knew of this practice and instructed his Companions to perform it?” He spoke sternly on the matter.’

He said: ‘On yet another occasion, I heard Abū ‘Abd Allāh being asked, “What of someone who calls it a Hajj undertaken from Mecca?”

‘He replied, “This is a newly introduced view.” He was asked, “From whom is it reported?”

‘He answered, “From al-Sha‘bī and Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr.”’”

Section

The evidence for this is that reports have been transmitted through numerous independent chains from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

---

## Page 12830

that during the Farewell Pilgrimage, after his Companions had circumambulated the House and gone between al-Safā and al-Marwah, he instructed them to leave their state of ihrām (ritual consecration) and make their pilgrimage an ʿumrah—except for those who had brought sacrificial animals, whom he instructed to remain in ihrām until the animals reached their appointed place of sacrifice.

Thus, when Salamah ibn Shabīb said to Ahmad, “Abū ʿAbd Allāh, you have emboldened the Rāfidah by advising the people of Khurāsān to perform tamattuʿ (ʿumrah followed by Hajj, with release from ihrām between them),” he replied:

“Salamah, I used to hear that you were a fool, and I would defend you. Now it has become clear to me that you are indeed a fool! I have eleven authentic hadiths from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Am I to abandon them on your say-so?”

Ahmad thus made clear that reports transmitted through numerous independent chains establish that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded all his Companions who had not brought sacrificial animals to perform tamattuʿ—even those who had intended Hajj alone or Hajj and ʿumrah together. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would not move them from a superior practice to an inferior one; rather, he would only command them to do what was better for them.

For this reason, Ahmad considered converting Hajj into tamattuʿ recommended. He did not regard scholarly disagreement over the permissibility of this conversion as grounds for cautiously refraining from it. Such caution is prescribed only when the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, has not become clear. Once the Sunnah is clear, following it takes precedence, even if some scholars have said that this is not permissible—especially since other scholars, both early and later, have held this conversion to be obligatory. Taking precautions to avoid disagreeing with the former is therefore no more warranted than avoiding disagreement with the latter.

---

## Page 12831

Those who prohibited converting Hajj into ʿumrah, or prohibited tamattuʿ altogether, said: “This was exclusively for the Companions of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.” They argued that the people of the pre-Islamic era disliked performing ʿumrah during the months of Hajj and would say: “When the camels’ saddle sores have healed, the tracks have faded, and Safar has passed, ʿumrah becomes lawful for whoever performs it.” They said: “The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, therefore commanded his Companions to perform ʿumrah to demonstrate that it was permissible during the months of Hajj.”

Ahmad and others considered this view mistaken for several reasons.

First, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had already performed his three earlier ʿumrahs during the months of Hajj. He performed his first ʿumrah, that of al-Hudaybiyah, in Dhū al-Qaʿdah; his make-up ʿumrah in Dhū al-Qaʿdah; and his ʿumrah from al-Jiʿrānah in Dhū al-Qaʿdah. It is established in the authentic hadith collection that ʿĀ’ishah was told:

“Ibn ʿUmar says that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed ʿumrah in Rajab.”

She replied: “May Allah forgive Abū ʿAbd al-Rahmān! The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, never performed ʿumrah in Rajab, and he never performed an ʿumrah without Ibn ʿUmar accompanying him.”

The scholars agree with ʿĀ’ishah’s statement that all his ʿumrahs took place in Dhū al-Qaʿdah, the middle month of the months of Hajj. How, then, can it be said that the Companions did not know that ʿumrah was permissible during the months of Hajj until he commanded them to convert their Hajj into ʿumrah, when he had already performed it three times?

Second, it is established in the two authentic hadith collections, on the authority of ʿĀ’ishah, that he told them at the mīqāt (designated boundary for entering ihrām):

“Whoever wishes to enter ihrām for both ʿumrah and Hajj may do so.”

Thus he made clear to them the permissibility of

---

## Page 12832

performing ʿumrah during the months of Hajj, explaining this at the mīqāt while the general body of Muslims were with him. How, then, could they not have known this?

Third:

He commanded those who had not brought sacrificial animals to leave ihrām, while commanding those who had brought them to remain in ihrām until the animals reached their place of sacrifice. He thus distinguished between two people in ihrām. This shows that what prevents a person from leaving ihrām is having brought a sacrificial animal, not the initial ihrām itself. The reason he mentioned also applies to the person who brought a sacrificial animal . . .
(1)

“He commanded us to have intercourse with our wives, so that we would come to ʿArafah with semen dripping from our private parts. He said: The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, then stood among us and said, ‘Do you not know that I am the most mindful of Allah among you, the most truthful, and the most righteous? Were it not for my sacrificial animal, I would leave ihrām as you are doing. Had I known beforehand what I know now, I would not have brought the sacrificial animal. So leave ihrām.’ We therefore left ihrām, listened, and obeyed.

“ʿAlī then arrived from his alms-collecting duties, and the Prophet asked him, ‘What did you enter ihrām for?’

“He replied, ‘Whatever the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, entered ihrām for.’

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Then offer a sacrificial animal and remain in ihrām.’ He said: ʿAlī brought him a sacrificial offering. Surāqah ibn Mālik ibn Juʿshum asked, ‘Is this for this year of ours or for all time?’

“He replied, ‘Rather, for all time.’”

In al-Bukhārī’s narration:

“Surāqah ibn Mālik ibn Juʿshum met the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, at al-ʿAqabah while he was stoning it. Juʿshum asked, ‘Is this exclusively for you, O Messenger of Allah?’

“He replied, ‘No, rather, it is for all time.’”

He thus made clear that this ʿumrah, into which some had converted their Hajj, was valid for all time

> Second: I found nothing in Shaykh al-Islām’s published writings, may Allah have mercy on him, that fills this gap. I therefore searched Ibn al-Qayyim’s discussion of Hajj, may Allah have mercy on him, hoping to find it there, since he frequently quotes his teacher. I found it, if not in the same wording, then in substance. When compared, the arguments he presents closely resemble the Shaykh’s texts, indicating that he was quoting from him. I shall reproduce his discussion from the “third argument” through to Jābir’s hadith in the “fifth argument,” from Zād al-Maʿād, 2/197–199. He said, may Allah have mercy on him:
> 
> “Third: He commanded those who had not brought sacrificial animals to leave ihrām, and those who had brought them to remain in ihrām until the animals reached their place of sacrifice. Thus, he distinguished between one person in ihrām and another. This shows that bringing sacrificial animals is what prevents one from leaving ihrām, not merely the initial entry into ihrām. The rationale they mention does not apply to one person in ihrām rather than another. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, therefore made permission to leave ihrām depend on whether sacrificial animals had been brought, not on anything else.
> 
> “Fourth: It may be said that if the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, intended to differ from the polytheists, this would establish that faskh (converting Hajj into ʿumrah) is preferable for that very reason. For if he commanded them to do so specifically to differ from the polytheists, that would establish that this conversion remains prescribed until the Day of Resurrection, whether as an obligation or a recommendation. Whatever the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did and prescribed for his community in the pilgrimage rites to differ from the polytheists’ practice remains prescribed until the Day of Resurrection, whether as an obligation or a recommendation.
> 
> “The polytheists used to depart from ʿArafah before sunset, and would not depart from Muzdalifah until sunrise. They would say, ‘Shine, Thabīr, so that we may set off!’ The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, differed from them and said, ‘Our practice differs from that of the polytheists.’ Thus, we did not depart from ʿArafah until the sun had set. This departure from their practice is either an essential pillar, according to Mālik; an obligation whose omission is compensated for by a sacrificial offering, according to Ahmad, Abū Hanīfah, and one of al-Shāfiʿī’s two opinions; or a sunnah, according to al-Shāfiʿī’s other opinion. Departing from Muzdalifah before sunrise is a sunnah by the agreement of the Muslims.
> 
> “Likewise, Quraysh did not stand at ʿArafah, but departed from Jamʿ. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, differed from them: he stood at ʿArafāt and departed from there. Concerning this, Allah Most High revealed: ‘Then depart from where the people depart.’ This departure from their practice is one of the essential pillars of Hajj by the agreement of the Muslims. How, then, could anything in it be prohibited? How can it be said that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded his Companions to perform a pilgrimage rite differing from that of the polytheists, while what he forbade them was supposedly better than what he commanded them to do? Or that someone who performs Hajj as the polytheists did, without performing tamattuʿ, has a better Hajj than that performed by the earliest forerunners among the Emigrants and Helpers at the command of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace?
> 
> “Fifth: It is established in the two Sahīh collections that he said, ‘ʿUmrah has become incorporated into Hajj until the Day of Resurrection.’ He was asked, ‘Is this ʿumrah of ours for this year alone, or forever?’ He replied, ‘No, rather, forever and ever. ʿUmrah has become incorporated into Hajj until the Day of Resurrection.’ Their question concerned the ʿumrah resulting from converting Hajj, as is explicitly stated in Jābir’s lengthy hadith. He said:
> 
> “‘When his final circuit ended at al-Marwah, he said, “Had I known beforehand what I know now, I would not have brought the sacrificial animals, and I would have made it ʿumrah. Whoever among you has no sacrificial animal should therefore leave ihrām and make it ʿumrah.” Surāqah ibn Mālik stood up and asked, “O Messenger of Allah, is this for this year alone, or forever?” The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, interlaced his fingers and said twice, “ʿUmrah has become incorporated into Hajj,” then said, “No, rather, forever and ever.”’
> 
> “In another version: ‘The Messenger of Allah arrived on the morning of the fourth of Dhū al-Hijjah and commanded us to leave ihrām. We said: When only five days remained before ʿArafah, he commanded us to have intercourse with our wives, so that we would come to ʿArafah with semen dripping from our private parts…’”

---

## Page 12833

and that ʿumrah has become part of Hajj until the Day of Resurrection. This shows that the ʿumrah of tamattuʿ—performing ʿumrah followed by Hajj with release from ihrām between them—is part of Hajj. When the questioner asked, “Is this ʿumrah of ours for this year alone, or for all time?” he did not mean that it discharged the obligation in that year. For if ʿumrah is obligatory, it is obligatory only once. Moreover, had that been his meaning, the Prophet would not have replied, “Rather, for all time,” since “for all time” cannot apply to a particular group alone, but applies to all Muslims. Nor would he have said:

“ʿUmrah has become part of Hajj until the Day of Resurrection.”

If it is asked, “Did he mean by his statement, ‘ʿUmrah has become part of Hajj,’ that performing ʿumrah during the months of Hajj is permissible?” the answer is: Yes, and this includes the ʿumrah of someone who converts his Hajj into ʿumrah, since that was the occasion for this statement. The occasion that prompted a general statement cannot be excluded from its scope. It is thus clear that his statement, “ʿUmrah has become part of Hajj,” includes the ʿumrah of someone who converts his Hajj into ʿumrah, and that it has become part of Hajj until the Day of Resurrection.

Fourth:

Converting Hajj into tamattuʿ accords with analogy based on established principles; it does not contradict it. If a person in ihrām undertakes more than was already binding upon him, that is permissible by agreement of the imams. Thus, if he enters ihrām for ʿumrah and then adds Hajj to it, that is permissible without dispute. However, if he enters ihrām for Hajj and then adds ʿumrah to it, that is not permissible according to the majority. This is the position of Ahmad and Mālik, and the apparent position of al-Shāfiʿī.

Abū Hanīfah, however, permits it because the person thereby becomes one who combines Hajj and ʿumrah in a single ihrām. In his view, such a person must perform two circumambulations and two passages between al-Safā and al-Marwah. This is also what follows by analogy from the view reported from Ahmad concerning one who combines Hajj and ʿumrah in a single ihrām.

---

## Page 12834

That being so, someone who enters ihrām for Hajj is bound to perform Hajj alone. If he then undertakes tamattuʿ, he commits himself to both ʿumrah and Hajj. By converting his Hajj, he thus undertakes more than was previously binding upon him. This is therefore permissible; and since it is preferable, it is recommended. This presents a difficulty only to someone who imagines that he has converted Hajj into ʿumrah alone. That is not the case: if he intended to convert Hajj into an independent ʿumrah, that would be impermissible without dispute. Such a conversion is permissible only for someone who intends to perform Hajj after ʿumrah.

We have already explained that someone performing tamattuʿ has embarked upon Hajj from the moment he enters ihrām for ʿumrah, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“ʿUmrah has become part of Hajj.”

For this reason, he may fast the three days from that point onward. His entering ihrām specifically for Hajj only afterward is comparable to the Prophet’s practice, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: “When he performed the full ritual bath following major ritual impurity, he would begin with ablution.” Likewise, he told the women washing his daughter’s body: “Begin with her right side and the parts washed in ablution.” Washing those parts thus constituted ablution while also forming part of the full ritual bath.

If it is said, “The sacrifice offered by someone performing tamattuʿ is a compensatory sacrifice, and a pilgrimage requiring no compensation is better than one requiring compensation,” the answer is:

This is incorrect for two reasons. First, it is established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ate from his sacrificial offering. He ordered that a piece be taken from each sacrificial camel and placed in a pot, then ate some of the meat and drank some of the broth. It is also established that he was performing tamattuʿ in the broader sense, for

---

## Page 12835

one who combines Hajj and ʿumrah in a single ihrām falls within the designation of someone performing tamattuʿ, as we shall explain. This indicates that eating from the sacrificial offering of someone performing tamattuʿ is recommended, whereas this is not the case with a compensatory sacrifice. It is also established in the two Sahīh collections, on the authority of ʿĀʾishah, may Allah be pleased with her:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, fed his wives from the sacrificial offerings he slaughtered on their behalf, and they were performing tamattuʿ.”

This is among the evidence cited by Imam Ahmad.

Second, the act that necessitates compensation is, in principle, prohibited, such as invalidating Hajj through intercourse, committing prohibited acts, or omitting obligatory acts. A person may not invalidate his Hajj, nor may he commit a prohibited act or omit an obligatory act without a valid excuse. Tamattuʿ, however, is permissible without qualification. If its sacrifice were compensatory, tamattuʿ would not be permissible without qualification. Thus, we know that it is a ritual sacrifice and an offering, and that it is among the ways Allah has granted ease to the Muslims. He has permitted them to leave ihrām for an interval, with the sacrificial offering taking the place of remaining continuously in ihrām, because of the hardship that continuous ihrām entails.

It is therefore comparable to shortening the prayer when travelling, a traveller’s breaking the fast, or someone wearing leather socks wiping over them. For someone wearing them, wiping is preferable to removing them and washing his feet, according to the apparent position of Ahmad’s school. This is because, when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was wearing leather socks that he had put on in a state of ritual purity, he would wipe over them rather than remove them and wash his feet. By contrast, when he was not wearing leather socks, he would wash his feet.

It is established in the Sahīh that he would say in his sermon:

“The best speech is the speech of Allah, and the best guidance is the guidance of Muhammad.”

---

## Page 12836

And Muhammad’s guidance for someone whose feet are uncovered is to wash them, not deliberately to put on leather socks in order to wipe over them. For someone already wearing leather socks, his guidance is to wipe over them, not to remove them and wash his feet—even though wiping over leather socks is a substitute. The same applies to the sacrificial offering. Even if it serves as a substitute in return for the ease of being spared one of the two journeys, combining Hajj and ʿumrah is preferable, for someone who has arrived during the months of Hajj, to performing Hajj alone and following it with ʿumrah.

A substitute may even be obligatory, as with Friday prayer: although it is a substitute for the noon prayer, it is obligatory.
