Likewise, someone unable to use water is obliged to perform dry ablution, even though it is a substitute. If a substitute can be obligatory, then it can all the more readily be recommended. Thus, it is recommended for a traveller to refrain from fasting and make up the fast later, although making it up is a substitute for observing it at its appointed time. Likewise, a sick person who finds fasting difficult refrains from fasting and makes it up later, although making it up is a substitute.

An intervening release from ihrām does not prevent the whole from being regarded as a single act of worship. The obligatory circumambulation, for example, is part of completing Hajj by the consensus of the Muslims, yet it is performed only after the first release from ihrām. Stoning the pillars during the days at Minā is also part of completing Hajj. If a person has performed the circumambulation before that, he stones the pillars during the days at Minā after having fully left ihrām. This is the Sunnah, as practised by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. And during the month of Ramadan, fasting by day is interspersed with

---

## Page 12837

breaking the fast at night. This is the prescribed fast mentioned in Allah’s words:

“Fasting has been prescribed for you as it was prescribed for those before you, so that you may become righteous,”

up to His words: “The month of Ramadan.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “Whoever fasts Ramadan with faith and seeking Allah’s reward will have his past sins forgiven.” Yet this fasting is interrupted every night by breaking the fast. The same applies to his saying: “Whoever performs Hajj to this House without engaging in sexual relations or committing sin will return free of sin as on the day his mother bore him.” The verse also encompasses one who performs Hajj as tamattuʿ (ʿumrah followed by Hajj), even though this state of ihrām may be interrupted by a release from it. From the moment he enters ihrām for Hajj, he has begun Hajj, just as fasting the first day marks the beginning of his fasting the month of Ramadan.

Likewise, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “Whoever stands in prayer during Ramadan with faith and seeking Allah’s reward will have his past sins forgiven.” Yet this prayer is interrupted by the salām after every two units of prayer. The same applies to Witr offered as three units in separate sets.

Section: “Description of the Farewell Hajj”

No scholar has disputed that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded his Companions, once they had circumambulated the House and gone between al-Safā and al-Marwah, to leave ihrām and make their pilgrimage an ʿumrah. This is established by mass-transmitted hadiths,

---

## Page 12838

and they also agree that neither the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor any of the Companions except ʿĀ’ishah performed ʿumrah after Hajj. All this is agreed upon: the transmitted reports do not differ concerning it, nor has any scholar disputed it.

They did, however, disagree over whether he performed tamattuʿ, Hajj alone, or Hajj combined with ʿumrah—or whether he entered ihrām without specifying the form of pilgrimage. They found the hadiths concerning this confusing, although, praise be to Allah, these reports do not contradict one another for anyone who understands what the Companions meant by them.

Imam Ahmad’s explicitly recorded position is that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, combined ʿumrah and Hajj. Indeed, he said: “I have no doubt that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, combined them.” This is also the position of the leading hadith scholars, such as Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh and others. It is undoubtedly correct. Abū Muhammad ibn Hazm wrote a work on the Farewell Hajj in which he collected the transmitted reports and established this conclusion.

Ahmad preferred tamattuʿ only because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded his Companions to perform it—not because he believed that the Prophet himself performed tamattuʿ in its specific sense. Thus, in the report transmitted by al-Marwadhī, he said: “If one brings the sacrificial animals, combining Hajj and ʿumrah is preferable.” Unless Ahmad believed that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had combined Hajj and ʿumrah and brought the sacrificial animals, this position would have no basis. For had he believed that the Prophet performed tamattuʿ, then the Prophet would have both practised it and commanded it; there would then be no basis for preferring the combination of Hajj and ʿumrah for someone who brings the sacrificial animals.

---

## Page 12839

None of Ahmad’s early followers said that the Prophet performed tamattu in its specific sense. As far as we know, the first of Ahmad’s followers to claim this was Judge Abū Ya‘lā. In his commentary, he cited this line of argument as evidence for the superiority of tamattu. He noted that the first approach—arguing from the Prophet’s command rather than his practice, and from his statement, “Had I known beforehand what I came to know later”—was the approach of Ahmad’s followers, just as their imam, Ahmad, had argued.

Those among Ahmad’s followers who maintained that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed tamattu held two views.

The first was that he left ihrām despite having brought the sacrificial animals. They interpreted the report stating that tamattu was exclusively for them to mean that they alone were permitted to leave ihrām despite having brought sacrificial animals, unlike those Companions who brought sacrificial animals. This was the approach of the judge and those who followed him. The overwhelming majority of scholars rejected this view. Among those who criticized the judge for it were Shaykh Abū al-Barakāt and others. They said: “Anyone who carefully considers the widely transmitted hadiths will see clearly that neither the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor anyone else who brought sacrificial animals left ihrām.”

The second view was that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed tamattu in the sense that he entered ihrām for umrah, did not leave ihrām because he had brought the sacrificial animals, and entered ihrām for Hajj

---

## Page 12840

after performing the circumambulation and sa‘i for umrah. This was the approach of Shaykh Abū Muhammad and others. These scholars describe such a pilgrim as performing tamattu, though they may also describe him as performing qirān because he entered ihrām for Hajj before leaving ihrām for umrah. However, qirān as generally understood means entering ihrām for umrah before circumambulating the House, so that the circumambulation counts for both umrah and Hajj.

The distinction between someone performing qirān and someone performing tamattu who has brought sacrificial animals is evident in two respects. The first concerns entering ihrām for Hajj before circumambulation. The second concerns the sa‘i following tawāf al-ifādah: someone performing qirān is not required to perform a second sa‘i, just as someone performing Hajj alone is not required to do so. For someone performing tamattu, however, this sa‘i is obligatory according to most scholars. Two views on this are reported from Ahmad.

As for al-Shāfi‘ī, his statements concerning the Prophet’s Hajj, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, differed. At one point he said that the Prophet performed Hajj alone; at another, that he performed tamattu; and at another, that he entered ihrām without specifying the form of pilgrimage.

In his Compendium of Hajj, he said: “I prefer that one perform Hajj alone, because what is established according to us is that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed Hajj alone.”

In Divergent Hadiths, he stated that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Had I known beforehand what I came to know later, I would not have brought the sacrificial animals, and I would have made it umrah.”

He said: “Anyone who said that he performed Hajj alone may well have said so on the basis of what he knew from the scholars he had met—not from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—that

---

## Page 12841

no one would remain engaged in Hajj unless he had initially entered ihrām for Hajj.”

He said: “I think that when ‘Urwah related that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, entered ihrām for Hajj, he understood in this sense what he had heard ‘Ā’ishah say about the Prophet’s actions during his Hajj.”

Here al-Shāfi‘ī clearly states that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed tamattu. He explains that those who said that he performed Hajj alone assumed this because they considered that anyone who remained in ihrām must be performing Hajj. Since the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, remained in ihrām, they assumed that he had been performing Hajj.

He also said, concerning the differing reports about the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, when he set out:

“No discrepancy is clearer than this, although error in this matter is objectionable, since it allows a choice. For the Book, then the Sunnah, and then the absence of any disagreement known to me indicate that tamattu through umrah followed by Hajj, performing Hajj alone, and qirān are all permissible.”

He said:

“It is established that he set out awaiting a divine ruling. That ruling came down to him while he was between al-Safā and al-Marwah. He instructed those of his Companions who had entered ihrām without bringing sacrificial animals to make it umrah, and said:

‘Had I known beforehand what I came to know later, I would not have brought the sacrificial animals, and I would have made it umrah.’”

He said:

“If someone asks, ‘On what grounds are the reports of ‘Ā’ishah, Jābir, Ibn ‘Umar, and Tāwūs accepted in preference to the report of those who said that he performed qirān?’

---

## Page 12842

The answer is:

“Because of Jābir’s long-standing companionship with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his clear account of the report from beginning to end; because of ‘Ā’ishah’s narration from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and her exceptional retention of what she learned from him; and because of Ibn ‘Umar’s closeness to him.”

He said:

“Also, whoever described the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as awaiting a divine ruling would seem to have remembered more accurately. He had not performed Hajj from Medina since Hajj became obligatory, and he sought the best choice among the forms of Hajj and umrah that Allah had permitted. A case involving two spouses invoking Allah’s curse upon one another had been brought before him, and he awaited a divine ruling. Likewise, he was reported to have awaited a divine ruling concerning Hajj.”

Al-Muzanī said:

“If Anas’s report that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed qirān is established, such that it stands in opposition to the other hadiths, then al-Shāfi‘ī’s underlying principle is that umrah is obligatory, and fulfilling this obligation at the time of Hajj is better than fulfilling just one obligation. For the more a person does for Allah, the greater his reward from Allah.”

I say:

The correct view in this matter is that the hadiths agree, differing only in minor ways such as occur elsewhere. It is authentically reported from the Companions that he performed tamattu, and in their usage tamattu includes qirān. Those who are reported to have said that he performed Hajj alone are also reported to have said that he performed tamattu.

As for the first point, the two Sahīh collections relate from Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyab that he said:

“‘Alī and ‘Uthmān met. ‘Uthmān would forbid tamattu or umrah, so ‘Alī said: ‘What

---

## Page 12843

does he seek but to forbid something that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did?’ ‘Uthmān replied, ‘Leave us alone.’ ‘Alī said, ‘I cannot leave you be.’ When ‘Alī saw this, he proclaimed his intention to perform both together.” This is Muslim’s wording. Al-Bukhārī omits the exchange from “Leave us alone” through “leave you be.”

Al-Bukhārī alone also related it through Marwān ibn al-Hakam, who said:

“I witnessed ‘Uthmān forbidding tamattu and combining Hajj with umrah. When ‘Alī saw this, he proclaimed his intention to perform both: ‘Here I am, answering Your call for an umrah and a Hajj.’ He said, ‘I would not abandon the Sunnah of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, for anyone’s opinion.’”

This makes clear that, in their usage, someone who combined the two was performing tamattu, and that this was what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had done. It was the Prophet’s Sunnah that ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib followed. ‘Uthmān agreed with him that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had done this. Their disagreement, however, concerned whether it is preferable for us, and whether changing from Hajj to tamattu is prescribed for us—questions over which the jurists likewise disagreed.

In the Sahīh, ‘Abd Allāh ibn Shaqīq reported:

“‘Uthmān used to forbid tamattu, while ‘Alī would enjoin it. ‘Uthmān made a remark to ‘Alī, who replied, ‘You certainly know that we performed tamattu with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.’ He answered, ‘Yes, but we were afraid.’”

Thus, ‘Uthmān and ‘Alī agreed that they had performed tamattu with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. As for ‘Uthmān’s statement, “We were afraid,” they were indeed afraid during the compensatory umrah

---

## Page 12844

and had performed umrah during the months of Hajj. Anyone who performed umrah during the months of Hajj was also described as performing tamattu, because those who forbade tamattu prohibited umrah during the months of Hajj altogether.

This is supported by the report in the Sahīh that, when Sa‘d ibn Abī Waqqās learned that Mu‘āwiya had forbidden tamattu, he said:

“We performed it with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, while this man was still an unbeliever in the dwellings of Mecca.”

He meant Mu‘āwiya. It is well known that Mu‘āwiya was a Muslim during the Farewell Pilgrimage—indeed, during the umrah from al-Ji‘rāna in the year of the conquest, or even earlier. During the compensatory umrah, however, he was still an unbeliever in the dwellings of Mecca. Thus, Sa‘d called the compensatory umrah tamattu. Perhaps ‘Uthmān meant their fear in the year of the compensatory umrah. They were also afraid in the year of the conquest. During the Farewell Pilgrimage, however, they were secure: no polytheist remained, for Allah had banished polytheism and its adherents.

Hence they said:

“We prayed two rak‘ahs with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, when people were at their safest.”

Perhaps he confused their circumstances in one of these years with their circumstances in the other. A similar confusion affected the person who reported that he prohibited temporary marriage during the Farewell Pilgrimage, whereas the prohibition occurred during the campaign of the conquest. Likewise, some people suppose that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, entered the Kaaba during a Hajj or an umrah. In fact, he entered the Kaaba in the year of the conquest, when he conquered Mecca. No one said that he entered it during a Hajj or an umrah. Rather, in the two Sahīhs, Ismā‘īl ibn Abī Khālid said:

“I said to ‘Abd Allāh ibn Abī Awfā, one of the Prophet’s Companions,

---

## Page 12845

may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ‘Did the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, enter the House during his umrah?’ He replied, ‘No.’”

In the two Sahīhs, Mutarrif ibn al-Shikhkhīr reported:

“‘Imrān ibn Husayn said to me: ‘I shall relate a hadith to you through which Allah may benefit you: the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, combined his Hajj and umrah. He never prohibited this before he died, nor was any Quranic revelation sent down forbidding it.’”

In another version, he said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed tamattu, and we performed it with him.”

Thus ‘Imrān, one of the most eminent of the earliest Muslims, reported that he performed tamattu and combined Hajj and umrah. In Sahīh Muslim, Ghunaym ibn Qays reported:

“I asked Sa‘d ibn Abī Waqqās about tamattu in Hajj. He said: ‘We performed it while this man was still an unbeliever in the dwellings’—meaning the houses of Mecca. By ‘this man’ he meant Mu‘āwiya.”

Sa‘d meant only the compensatory umrah, for Mu‘āwiya had not yet embraced Islam at that time. By the Farewell Pilgrimage, however, he had embraced Islam, as he had by the umrah from al-Ji‘rāna. Sa‘d therefore called performing umrah during the months of Hajj tamattu. Some Syrians used to forbid performing umrah during the months of Hajj, so the Companions related the Sunnah concerning this in rebuttal of those who prohibited it. In their terminology, therefore, someone combining Hajj and umrah was performing tamattu. For this reason, he was required to offer a sacrificial animal and was included in the words of the Most High:

“Whoever performs umrah followed by Hajj must offer whatever sacrificial animal he can readily afford.”

In Sahīh al-Bukhārī and elsewhere, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb reported:

“I heard the Messenger of

---

## Page 12846

Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say while he was in the valley of al-‘Aqīq: ‘A visitor from my Lord came to me tonight and said: “Pray in this blessed valley and say: ‘An umrah combined with Hajj.’”’”

Thus, it is related through the most authentic chains of transmission from these Rightly Guided Caliphs—‘Umar, ‘Uthmān and ‘Alī—and from others besides the caliphs, such as ‘Imrān ibn Husayn, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, combined umrah and Hajj. They used to call this tamattu.

In the two Sahīhs, Bakr ibn ‘Abd Allāh al-Muzanī reported:

“Anas ibn Mālik said: ‘I heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, reciting the talbiya for Hajj and umrah.’ I related this to Ibn ‘Umar, who said: ‘He recited the talbiya for Hajj alone.’ I then met Anas and told him, and he said: ‘They think we are nothing but children! I heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say: “Here I am, for umrah and Hajj.”’”

Here, then, Anas reports that he heard the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, reciting the talbiya for both Hajj and umrah. As for what Bakr related from Ibn ‘Umar concerning him, the answer is that trustworthy narrators who were more reliable than Bakr in transmitting from Ibn ‘Umar—such as his son Sālim—reported that he said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed tamattu, combining umrah with Hajj.”

These narrators were more reliable than Bakr in transmitting from Ibn ‘Umar. It is more reasonable to attribute an error to Bakr in reporting from Ibn ‘Umar than to attribute errors both to his son Sālim in reporting from him and to Ibn ‘Umar himself in reporting from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

A likely explanation is that Ibn ‘Umar told him, “He performed Hajj alone,” and he thought that he had said, “He recited the talbiya for Hajj.” For they used the expression “performing Hajj alone” to mean performing a single set of the rites of Hajj. And this

---

## Page 12847

refutes the claim of those who say that he combined umrah and Hajj but performed two sets of circumambulation and two sets of walking between Safā and Marwa, as well as those who say that he came out of ihrām. Thus, the reports of the Companions who related that he performed Hajj alone refute these claims.

This is clarified by what Muslim narrated in his Sahīh from Nāfi‘, from Ibn ‘Umar, who said:

“We entered ihrām for Hajj with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who was performing Hajj alone.”

Another version reads: “He entered ihrām for Hajj alone.”

Thus, they reported only that Ibn ‘Umar said, “He performed Hajj alone,” not that he said, “He recited the talbiya for Hajj.” In the Sunan collections, al-Barā’ ibn ‘Āzib reported:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to ‘Alī: ‘I have brought the sacrificial animals and combined umrah and Hajj.’”

In the two Sahīhs, al-Zuhrī narrated from Sālim, from his father, from ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed tamattu during the Farewell Pilgrimage, combining umrah with Hajj. He brought sacrificial animals with him, driving them from Dhū al-Hulayfa. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed umrah, entering ihrām for umrah and then for Hajj. The people likewise performed tamattu with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, combining umrah with Hajj. Some brought sacrificial animals with them, while others did not.

“When the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, arrived in Mecca, he said to the people: ‘Those of you who have brought sacrificial animals must not resume anything forbidden to them in ihrām until they have completed their Hajj. Those of you who have not brought sacrificial animals should circumambulate the House, pass between Safā and Marwa, shorten their hair and come out of ihrām. Then they should enter ihrām for Hajj and offer sacrificial animals. Whoever cannot find a sacrificial animal must fast three days during Hajj and seven

---

## Page 12848

when he returns to his family.’ When the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, arrived in Mecca, he circumambulated the House, first touching the Corner, then jogging for three of the seven circuits and walking the remaining four. After completing his circumambulation of the House, he went back and prayed two rak‘ahs at the Station of Abraham. He then concluded his prayer with the salutation, left, and went to Safā, making seven passages between Safā and Marwa. He remained bound by all the restrictions of ihrām until he had completed his Hajj, slaughtered his sacrificial animals on the Day of Sacrifice, and returned to circumambulate the House. Then everything previously forbidden to him became permissible. Those who had brought sacrificial animals with them did as the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had done.”

Al-Zuhrī said:

“‘Urwa also related to me from ‘Ā’isha a report like that of Sālim from his father.”

This is the most authentic hadith on the face of the earth. It is transmitted by al-Zuhrī—the most knowledgeable person of his time concerning the Sunnah—from Sālim, from Ibn ‘Umar, and is more authentic than the report of Ibn ‘Umar. It is also transmitted by ‘Urwa from ‘Ā’isha, and is more authentic than the report of ‘Ā’isha. It is authentically reported from her in the two Sahīhs:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed four umrahs, the fourth together with his Hajj.”

By the agreement of the Muslims, he performed no umrah after that. It therefore follows necessarily that he combined umrah and Hajj. And he said: “This is what the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did.” She likewise reported that those who combined Hajj and umrah performed only one circumambulation.

As for those who were reported to have said that he performed Hajj alone, they are three: ‘Ā’isha and Ibn

---

## Page 12849

‘Umar and Jābir. All three are also reported to have said that he combined umrah with Hajj. The reports of ‘Ā’isha and Ibn ‘Umar that he combined umrah with Hajj are more authentic than their reports that he performed Hajj alone. Whatever is authentically reported from them to the latter effect refers to performing a single set of Hajj rites.

In the two Sahīhs, Hafsa reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed his wives to come out of ihrām in the year of the Farewell Hajj. Hafsa asked, “What prevents you from coming out of ihrām?”

He replied, “I have matted my hair and garlanded my sacrificial animals, so I shall not come out of ihrām until I slaughter my sacrificial animals.”

In another version, she asked, “Why have the people come out of ihrām while you have not come out of the ihrām of your umrah?”

He replied, “I have matted my hair and garlanded my sacrificial animals, so I shall not come out of ihrām until I slaughter the sacrificial animals.”

This indicates that he was performing umrah; it does not indicate that he was not also performing Hajj along with his umrah. Further clarification comes from the report of Anas in the two Sahīhs:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed four umrahs, all in Dhū al-Qi‘da except the one performed with his Hajj: the umrah of al-Hudaybiya in Dhū al-Qi‘da, an umrah the following year in Dhū al-Qi‘da, an umrah from al-Ji‘rāna in Dhū al-Qi‘da, and an umrah with his Hajj.”

Also in the two Sahīhs, Mujāhid said:

“‘Urwa ibn al-Zubayr and I entered the mosque and found ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar sitting beside ‘Ā’isha’s chamber. ‘Urwa asked him, ‘Abū ‘Abd al-Rahmān, how many umrahs did the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, perform?’

“He replied, ‘Four umrahs, one of them in Rajab.’

“‘Urwa then said, ‘Mother of the Believers, are you not listening to

---

## Page 12850

what Abū ‘Abd al-Rahmān is saying?’

She asked, ‘What does he say?’

He replied, ‘He says that Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed four umrahs, one of them in Rajab.’

She said, ‘May Allah have mercy on Abū ‘Abd al-Rahmān! Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, never performed umrah without him accompanying him, and he never performed umrah in Rajab.’”

Thus, ‘Ā’isha denied that he had performed umrah in Rajab; she did not deny that he had performed four umrahs. ‘Ā’isha and Ibn ‘Umar therefore agreed that he performed four umrahs, as was also reported from Anas. It is established by universal agreement that he did not perform umrah after Hajj. It is also established that Ibn ‘Umar and ‘Ā’isha reported that he performed umrah together with Hajj. This is tamattu‘ (combining umrah and Hajj) in its broader sense, which includes qirān (combining both in a single state of ihrām) and entails a sacrificial offering. It is therefore clear that the many established reports from Ibn ‘Umar and ‘Ā’isha accord with the practice of the other Companions: he performed tamattu‘ in its broader sense.

Those who say that he entered ihrām without specifying the form of pilgrimage rely on a report with a disconnected chain of transmission. Such a report cannot be used to contradict authentic hadiths.

It is therefore clear that anyone who says he performed Hajj alone, and claims that he performed umrah after Hajj—as some students of jurisprudence suppose—is mistaken by scholarly consensus. As for the claim that he performed Hajj alone in the sense that he did not perform umrah along with his Hajj, some scholars have held this view, but it is mistaken and has not been established from any of the Companions.

---

## Page 12851

Anyone who says that he entered ihrām without specifying the form of pilgrimage is mistaken; this has not been reported from any of the Companions. Anyone who says that he performed tamattuʿ in the sense that he did not enter ihrām for Hajj until after circumambulating the Kaaba and traversing between Safā and Marwa is likewise mistaken; this has not been reported from any of the Companions.

Anyone who says that he performed tamattuʿ in the sense that he left ihrām is also mistaken, by the agreement of scholars versed in hadith. Anyone who says that he performed qirān in the sense that he performed two tawāfs (circumambulations) and two saʿys (traversals between Safā and Marwa) is likewise mistaken. None of the Companions reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did this. The errors in this matter thus arose among those after the Companions, who misunderstood their words. The Companions’ reports, however, are consistent with one another.

Among the evidence showing that neither he nor his Companions performed two tawāfs or two saʿys is the report in the two Sahīhs, through ʿUrwa, from ʿĀ’isha, who said:

“We set out with Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and he said, ‘Whoever has a sacrificial animal with him should enter ihrām for Hajj together with umrah, and must not leave ihrām until he has completed both.’”

In the same report, she said:

“Those who had entered ihrām for umrah circumambulated the House and traversed between Safā and Marwa, then left ihrām. After returning from Minā, they performed another tawāf for their Hajj. As for those who combined Hajj and umrah, they performed only one tawāf.”

---

## Page 12852

In Sahīh Muslim, Tāwūs relates from ʿĀ’isha:

“She entered ihrām for umrah, but after arriving, her menses began before she had circumambulated the House. She performed all the rites, having entered ihrām for Hajj. On the day of departure, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to her, ‘Your tawāf suffices for both your Hajj and your umrah.’ But she declined, so he sent her with ʿAbd al-Rahmān to al-Tanʿīm, and she performed umrah after Hajj.”

Muslim also relates through Mujāhid, from ʿĀ’isha:

“Her menses began at Sarif, and she became pure at ʿArafa. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to her, ‘Your traversal between Safā and Marwa suffices for both your Hajj and your umrah.’”

In Sunan Abī Dāwūd, ʿAtā’ relates from ʿĀ’isha:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to her, ‘Your circumambulation of the House and traversal between Safā and Marwa suffice for both your Hajj and your umrah.’”

In the two Sahīhs, Jābir said:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, entered upon ʿĀ’isha and found her weeping. She said, ‘My menses have begun. The people have left ihrām, but I have not, and I have not circumambulated the House.’ He said, ‘Bathe, then enter ihrām for Hajj.’ She did so and stood at all the prescribed stations. Once she became pure, she circumambulated the Kaaba and traversed between Safā and Marwa. He then said, ‘You have now left ihrām for both your Hajj and your umrah.’

“She said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, I feel troubled that I did not circumambulate the House when I performed Hajj.’ He said, ‘Take her, ʿAbd al-Rahmān, and have her perform umrah from al-Tanʿīm.’ This was on the night of al-Hasba.”

Thus, in the authentic hadith, ʿĀ’isha stated that those who combined Hajj and umrah performed no circumambulation of the House or traversal between Safā and Marwa other than the initial ones performed by

---

## Page 12853

those performing umrah before Hajj with a break in ihrām between them, at the outset. Moreover, the authentic hadiths concerning her case establish that:

“When she circumambulated the House and traversed between Safā and Marwa on the Day of Sacrifice, he said to her, ‘You have now left ihrām,’ and, ‘Your circumambulation suffices for your Hajj and your umrah.’”

They also establish that she was not required to make up that umrah. This demonstrates that a single circumambulation of the House and a single traversal between Safā and Marwa suffice for someone combining Hajj and umrah, just as they suffice for someone performing Hajj alone. This is especially so since ʿĀ’isha performed no circumambulation except the arrival circumambulation; rather, she did not circumambulate until after standing at ʿArafa, and she also performed the traversal. If the principal Hajj circumambulation and the traversal following it suffice for someone combining Hajj and umrah, then the arrival circumambulation together with the principal Hajj circumambulation, and a single traversal accompanying either of them, must suffice all the more.

Further evidence is that all the Companions who related the account of the pilgrimage of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, reported that, once the Companions had circumambulated the House and traversed between Safā and Marwa, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed them to leave ihrām, except those who had brought sacrificial animals: they were not to leave ihrām until the Day of Sacrifice. None of them reported that any Companion circumambulated and traversed, then circumambulated and traversed again. It is well known that an occurrence of this kind would have given people ample reason and incentive to report it. Since none of the Companions reported it, we know that it did not occur.

Those who hold that view rely chiefly on a report transmitted by the Kufans from ʿAlī and another report from Ibn Masʿūd. Yet Jaʿfar ibn Muhammad related from his father, Muhammad ibn ʿAlī, that he had preserved a report from ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib prescribing a single traversal between Safā and Marwa for someone combining Hajj and umrah, contrary to what is preserved by the people of

---

## Page 12854

Iraq. Some reports transmitted by the Iraqis have broken chains of transmission, while others contain narrators who are unknown or whose reliability has been impugned.

For this reason, hadith scholars criticized these reports, to the point that Ibn Hazm said: “Not a single word of what has been related from the Companions on this matter is authentic.” Reports on this matter have also been attributed to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that are undoubtedly fabricated.

Furthermore, the two Sahīh collections relate that Ibn ʿUmar said to those with him:

“Bear witness that I have undertaken Hajj together with my umrah.” He then set out, reciting the talbiyah for both, until he reached Mecca. He circumambulated the House and traversed between Safā and Marwa, doing no more than that. He neither shaved nor shortened his hair, nor resumed anything forbidden to him in ihrām, until the Day of Sacrifice, when he shaved his head and slaughtered his sacrificial animal. He considered that his initial circumambulation had fulfilled the circumambulation required for Hajj and umrah. He then said: “This is what Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did.”

Moreover, it is authentically established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Umrah has become part of Hajj until the Day of Resurrection, and since it has become part of it, it requires no rites beyond those of Hajj.”

Sufyān al-Thawrī also related from Salama ibn Kuhayl, who said: “Tāwūs swore to me that none of the Companions of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed more than one circumambulation for his Hajj and umrah.” The same is authentically established from Ibn ʿUmar, Ibn ʿAbbās, Jābir and others. They were among those most knowledgeable about the pilgrimage of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and would not act contrary to it.

---

## Page 12855

Thus, these firmly established, authentic hadiths from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, show that he performed only one circumambulation of the House and one traversal between Safā and Marwa.

It is therefore clear that the hadiths support the position of the leading hadith scholars, such as Ahmad and others: the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, combined Hajj and umrah in a single state of ihrām and performed only one circumambulation of the House and one traversal between Safā and Marwa. However, he brought his sacrificial animal with him. For anyone who brings his sacrificial animal, combining Hajj and umrah in a single state of ihrām is preferable to performing umrah followed by Hajj with a release from ihrām between them. For anyone who does not bring his sacrificial animal, the latter is preferable, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed his Companions. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12856

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“Regarding the Hajj of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: did he perform Hajj alone, combine Hajj and umrah in a single state of ihrām, or perform umrah followed by Hajj with a release from ihrām between them? Which is best for someone performing Hajj? People have discussed this at great length, adding and omitting much, and the aim is to bring the truth about these matters to light. What of some people’s claim that one of the Companions performed an umrah starting from Mecca? And is the hadith they narrate authentic or not:

‘An umrah in Ramadan is equivalent to such-and-such a number of Hajj pilgrimages’?”

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. Regarding the Hajj of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the correct view is that he combined Hajj and umrah in a single state of ihrām and brought his sacrificial animal with him. Upon arriving, he performed only one circumambulation of the House and one traversal between Safā and Marwa. In addition to these two rites, however, he performed tawāf al-ifādah (the main Hajj circumambulation).

What we have stated is the correct, firmly established conclusion among those versed in hadith who have collected their various chains of transmission and understood their purport. Abū Muhammad ibn Hazm compiled a fine book on the Farewell Pilgrimage addressing this subject.

Imam Ahmad said:

“I have no doubt that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was

---

## Page 12857

combining Hajj and umrah in a single state of ihrām. Yet I prefer performing umrah followed by Hajj with a release from ihrām between them, since this was the later of the two directives.”

He means the words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, after he had circumambulated the House, traversed between Safā and Marwa, and instructed his Companions to leave ihrām. When they found this difficult, he said:

“Had I known beforehand what I came to know afterward, I would not have brought the sacrificial animal, and I would have made it an umrah.”

This only implies that he would have performed umrah followed by Hajj without bringing a sacrificial animal. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had, however, brought his sacrificial animal.

For this reason, Ahmad said, as reported by al-Marwadhī:

“If a person brings his sacrificial animal, combining Hajj and umrah in a single state of ihrām is preferable, because that is what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did.”

Our conclusion that he performed Hajj and umrah together becomes clear to anyone who reflects on the hadiths and understands their meaning. A detailed discussion is not possible here, but we shall mention a few brief points.

One is that those who transmitted the actual words of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—such as his wording of the pilgrimage invocation, his statements about himself, and his accounts of what Allah had commanded him—mentioned only the combination of Hajj and umrah. For example, Anas said, as recorded in the two Sahīh collections:

“I heard him say, ‘Here I am for umrah and Hajj.’” Anas was beneath the Prophet’s she-camel.

Another example is the hadith of ʿUmar recorded in the Sahīh, in which the Prophet said:

“A messenger from my Lord came to me in this blessed valley and said, ‘Say: an umrah combined with Hajj.’”

There is also his statement in the hadith of al-Barāʾ ibn ʿĀzib . . .
(*)

As for those who said that he performed umrah followed by Hajj, their hearts remained set on something other than

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd said (p. 210):
> The text of the hadith of al-Barāʾ ibn ʿĀzib has been omitted here. As narrated from him by Abū Dāwūd (1797) and others, it reads:
> 
> “I was with ʿAlī when the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, appointed him governor of Yemen. While with him, I acquired some ūqiyyas (units of weight) of gold. When ʿAlī returned from Yemen to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he found that Fātima, may Allah be pleased with her, had put on dyed garments and sprinkled the house with perfume. She said, ‘What is the matter with you? The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, has instructed his Companions, and they have left ihrām.’”
> 
> ʿAlī said: “I told her, ‘I have entered ihrām for the same rites as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.’ Then I went to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and he asked me, ‘What did you do?’ I replied, ‘I entered ihrām for the same rites as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.’ He said, ‘I have brought the sacrificial animals and combined Hajj and umrah.’” … The hadith continues.

---

## Page 12858

combining Hajj and umrah in a single state of ihrām, for they included this combination under the designation of performing umrah in conjunction with Hajj. This is explained in the two Sahīh collections: ʿUthmān used to prohibit this, whereas ʿAlī advocated it. When ʿAlī saw this, he entered ihrām for both together. Accordingly, the imams held that someone combining the two must offer a sacrificial animal, on the basis of Allah’s words:

“Whoever benefits from performing umrah in conjunction with Hajj must offer whatever sacrificial animal he can afford.”

The essential meaning of tamattuʿ is to perform umrah during the months of Hajj and perform Hajj in the same year, thereby enjoying the convenience of dispensing with one of the two journeys. This applies whether one leaves ihrām after umrah and then enters ihrām for Hajj, enters ihrām for Hajj and umrah together, or adds Hajj to an umrah already begun. One thus performs both umrah and Hajj during the months of Hajj without travelling between them, enjoying the convenience of dispensing with one of the two journeys. All of this falls within the designation of tamattuʿ. Moreover, these narrators did not transmit the actual words of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

The same applies to those who said, “He performed Hajj alone.” This expression was intended to refute those who said that he performed umrah followed by Hajj and left his ihrām, as well as those who said that he performed two circumambulations and twice traversed between Safā and Marwa. His Companions left ihrām because they had not brought sacrificial animals; thus they remained in ihrām, as someone performing Hajj alone does, and performed no rites beyond those of someone performing Hajj alone.

These narrators therefore explained that he performed only the rites of Hajj: he neither left ihrām nor added any further rites. This also establishes that he performed umrah four times, one of them in conjunction with his Hajj. There is no disagreement among the scholars that he did not perform umrah

---

## Page 12859

after the Hajj, nor did anyone who accompanied him on the Farewell Hajj, with the sole exception of ʿĀ’isha. He sent her to perform umrah with her brother ʿAbd al-Rahmān because she had menstruated. Mosques were subsequently built there and named “the Mosques of ʿĀ’isha,” since she had entered ihrām for umrah from that location. It is the nearest point outside the sacred precinct to Mecca, because the boundary of the sacred precinct on that side is closer to Mecca than on any other side. He had performed umrah in conjunction with his Hajj and had not performed it afterwards. It is therefore clear that his umrah formed part of his Hajj and preceded it; thus, he had performed umrah in conjunction with Hajj.

This is further clarified by the fact that most of those reported to have said that he performed Hajj alone, such as ʿĀ’isha and Ibn ʿUmar, are also reported to have said that he performed umrah in conjunction with Hajj. This is established in the two Sahīh collections through reports from Ibn ʿUmar, ʿĀ’isha and others.

It has also become clear that the claim that he performed umrah in conjunction with Hajj and then left ihrām is mistaken. Some followers of Ahmad, such as al-Qādī and others, maintained this, claiming that it was a privilege granted to him alone, unlike others who performed umrah in conjunction with Hajj and brought sacrificial animals. Likewise, the view attributed to some followers of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī—that he performed Hajj alone and then performed umrah afterwards—is mistaken. Both views contradict the consensus of scholars versed in the transmitted reports.

The same applies to the claim that he performed two circumambulations and twice traversed between Safā and Marwa, as the followers of Abū Hanīfa maintain: it contradicts the authentic hadiths which

---

## Page 12860

show that he circumambulated the House and traversed between Safā and Marwa only once.

As for those followers of Ahmad who say that he performed tamattuʿ (umrah followed by Hajj) but did not leave ihrām because he had brought sacrificial animals—as maintained by Abū Muhammad and others—the well-known position among them is that tamattuʿ requires traversing between Safā and Marwa for Hajj after the circumambulation of ifādah, just as one initially traversed between them for umrah. Yet the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not traverse between them after the circumambulation of ifādah. How, then, could he have performed tamattuʿ according to this view? There is, however, another report from Ahmad that someone performing tamattuʿ does not need to traverse between them a second time; rather, the first traversal suffices, just as it suffices for someone performing Hajj alone or combining Hajj and umrah.

The reason for these two differing reports from Ahmad is that Jābir’s hadith states:

“They circumambulated the House and traversed between Safā and Marwa only on the first occasion.”

Whereas ʿĀ’isha’s hadith states:

“They circumambulated after standing at ʿArafāt.”

Under this latter report from Ahmad, the objection does not apply. However, no distinction then remains between someone combining Hajj and umrah and someone performing tamattuʿ who brought sacrificial animals—and therefore did not leave ihrām—except that the former entered ihrām for Hajj before circumambulating and traversing between Safā and Marwa, while the latter entered ihrām for Hajj afterwards. If adding Hajj to umrah after circumambulating and traversing between Safā and Marwa, like adding it before doing so, does not require a second traversal, then there would be no distinction whatsoever between someone combining Hajj and umrah and someone performing tamattuʿ who did not leave ihrām. Accordingly, entering ihrām for Hajj before circumambulating and traversing between Safā and Marwa is better than

---

## Page 12861

entering ihrām for it after circumambulating and traversing between Safā and Marwa. It is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, entered ihrām for both together and said, “At Your service for umrah and Hajj.” Someone who enters ihrām for Hajj only after circumambulating and traversing between Safā and Marwa does not say this.

As for those followers of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī who say that he performed Hajj alone without performing umrah alongside it, this view is refuted by the authentic hadiths showing that he performed umrah alongside his Hajj and that he performed four umrahs: the umrah of al-Hudaybiya, the make-up umrah, the umrah of al-Jiʿrāna, and the umrah accompanying his Hajj. It is likewise refuted by Hafsa’s words in the hadith recorded by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim: “Why have the people left ihrām, while you have not left ihrām for your umrah?” He replied, “I have matted my hair and garlanded my sacrificial animals, so I will not leave ihrām until I slaughter them.”

As for the question, “Which is better?”, the precise answer is that performing Hajj on one journey and umrah on another is better than combining Hajj and umrah, or performing tamattuʿ in its specific sense, on a single journey. Ahmad and Abū Hanīfa explicitly stated this, as did Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and others. This is the separate performance of Hajj practiced by Abū Bakr and ʿUmar. ʿUmar preferred it for the people, as did ʿAlī, may Allah be pleased with him. Regarding Allah’s words:

“Complete Hajj and umrah for Allah,”

ʿUmar and ʿAlī said, “Completing them means entering ihrām for them from your family home.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to ʿĀ’isha concerning her umrah: “Your reward is in proportion to

---

## Page 12862

your exertion.”

If a pilgrim returns to his family home and sets out from there for umrah, or performs umrah before the months of Hajj and stays until he performs Hajj, or performs umrah during those months, returns home, and then sets out for Hajj, he has undertaken each of the two rites from his family home. He has thus performed both in the fullest manner, which is better than the alternatives.

However, if he performs Hajj alone and then performs umrah from the nearest point outside the Sacred Precinct, this manner of performing Hajj separately was not practised by the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or by any of his Companions who performed Hajj with him, nor indeed by anyone else. How, then, could it be better than what they did with him at his command? Indeed, no one other than ʿĀ’isha is known to have set out for umrah from Mecca during the time of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—whether during the Farewell Pilgrimage, before it, or after it. Moreover, according to one of the two reports from Ahmad, such an umrah does not fulfil the obligation of umrah. Some scholars consider it tamattuʿ.

A group of scholars considers performing umrah in Dhū al-Hijja disliked. Indeed, whenever ʿĀ’isha performed Hajj, she would wait until Muharram began and then enter ihrām from al-Juhfa. Thus, she would perform umrah neither from the nearest point outside the Sacred Precinct nor during Dhū al-Hijja.

As for someone who wishes to combine the two rites in a single journey, arrives in Mecca during the months of Hajj, and has not brought sacrificial animals with him, tamattuʿ is better for him than performing Hajj and then performing umrah from outside the Sacred Precinct, because the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

---

## Page 12863

who performed Hajj with him and had not brought sacrificial animals were all commanded by him to perform Hajj in this manner:

He commanded them, once they had circumambulated the House and passed between al-Safā and al-Marwa, to leave ihrām and make their pilgrimage tamattuʿ. When the Day of Tarwiya came, he commanded them to enter ihrām for Hajj. Mass-transmitted reports establish that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded them to do this and that they performed Hajj with him in this manner.

It is well known that they are the best of the Muslim community after him. No Hajj could be better than that performed by the best of this community, alongside the best of creation and at his command. How, then, could the Hajj of someone who performs Hajj separately and follows it with umrah, or combines Hajj and umrah without bringing sacrificial animals, be better than the Hajj these Companions performed with him at his command? How could he direct them away from what is better towards what is inferior? His command carries greater weight than his own practice.

Furthermore, someone who enters ihrām for umrah has already intended Hajj, since he intends to perform tamattuʿ, proceeding from umrah to Hajj. This is like someone performing a full ritual bath who begins with ablution, intending that ablution to form part of the bath. Thus, the pilgrim enters ihrām twice and leaves it twice, just as someone performing the two rites separately leaves ihrām twice and enters it twice. He also offers a sacrificial animal, just as someone combining Hajj and umrah in a single ihrām does.

This sacrifice is a ritual offering, not a compensatory sacrifice. The act necessitating a compensatory sacrifice—omitting an obligation or committing something prohibited—is permissible only with a valid excuse. A pilgrim may not omit any obligation of Hajj without an excuse, or commit any of its prohibited acts without an excuse, and then offer a sacrifice. Yet he may perform tamattuʿ without an excuse and offer the sacrificial animal. This establishes that it is a ritual sacrifice. The Sunnah also establishes that he may eat from it, just as did

---

## Page 12864

the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, from his sacrificial offering while combining Hajj and umrah in a single ihrām. Likewise, he slaughtered a cow on behalf of his wives and fed them some of its meat while they were performing tamattuʿ.

Moreover, someone performing two acts of worship of the same kind that can be combined may begin with the lesser before the greater, just as someone taking a full ritual bath first performs ablution and then completes the bath. He likewise instructed that this be done when washing the deceased. Performing umrah and then Hajj follows this pattern. By contrast, someone who has performed Hajj has already completed the greater rite. If he performs umrah immediately afterward, his umrah adds no new ritual action.

If someone enters ihrām for umrah and then incorporates Hajj into it, this is permissible by consensus, because he has undertaken more than he was previously obliged to perform. But if he enters ihrām for Hajj and then incorporates umrah into it, this is not permissible according to the correct view, because he undertakes nothing additional. Abū Hanīfa permitted it only on the basis of his principle that someone combining Hajj and umrah performs additional rites beyond those performed by someone undertaking Hajj alone.

If someone makes a journey during which he performs umrah, then wishes to make another journey for Hajj, performing tamattuʿ is still better for him than performing Hajj alone. Many of the Companions who performed Hajj with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had previously performed umrah. Nevertheless, he commanded them to perform tamattuʿ, not Hajj alone. Moreover, this combines two umrahs, one Hajj and a sacrificial offering, which is better than one umrah and one Hajj.

---

## Page 12865

Likewise, if he performs tamattuʿ and then travels from his home to perform tamattuʿ again, this is better than making one journey for umrah and another for Hajj alone. Performing Hajj alone in this latter manner is better than making a single journey for tamattuʿ. However, if he wishes to combine both rites in one journey and bring sacrificial animals with him, combining Hajj and umrah in a single ihrām is preferable, following the example of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who combined them and brought sacrificial animals with him.

If someone says, “Even when he brings sacrificial animals with him, tamattuʿ is preferable for him,” the response is:

Besides this being contrary to the Sunnah, if he enters ihrām for Hajj before circumambulating the Kaʿba and walking between Safā and Marwa, he will have entered ihrām earlier, and those rites will count for both Hajj and umrah. If he enters ihrām after them, they will count only for umrah. Having these acts count for Hajj together with umrah is better than having them count for an umrah from which he does not leave ihrām until he performs Hajj.

He might, however, say, “If he delays entering ihrām for Hajj, he must walk between Safā and Marwa a second time, and this is an additional act of worship.” But this is disputed, as explained earlier. Nor can he cite as evidence the Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Had I known at the outset what I came to know later, I would not have brought the sacrificial animals, and I would have made it umrah.”

For he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not say, “I would have performed tamattuʿ while bringing sacrificial animals.” Rather, he said:

“I would not have brought the sacrificial animals, and I would have made it umrah.”

Thus, he indicated that what was desired was tamattuʿ without bringing sacrificial animals, and this

---

## Page 12866

is further evidence that one who brings sacrificial animals should combine Hajj and umrah in a single ihrām rather than perform tamattuʿ. If combining Hajj and umrah and performing tamattuʿ are equally preferable when sacrificial animals are brought, then the disagreement falls away.

If it is asked, “Which is better: to bring sacrificial animals and combine Hajj and umrah, or to perform tamattuʿ without bringing sacrificial animals and leave ihrām?”

The answer is:

This is a matter for scholarly judgment, since two pieces of scriptural evidence appear to conflict.

The first is that he combined Hajj and umrah and brought sacrificial animals during the Farewell Pilgrimage. Allah would not have chosen for His Prophet what was less preferable over what was more preferable, for the best guidance is the guidance of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

The second is that his statement implies that, had he been entering ihrām at that point, he would have entered it for umrah and would not have brought sacrificial animals, as indicated by his words:

“Had I known at the outset what I came to know later.”

What lay behind him was what he had already done: it had passed and was now behind him. What lay ahead of him was what he had not yet done and was still before him. Thus, it becomes clear that, had he once again been facing the step he had already taken—namely, entering ihrām—he would have entered ihrām for umrah without bringing sacrificial animals. He would not choose to move from what is more preferable to what is less preferable; rather, he would choose only what is more preferable. This indicates that it had then become clear to him that performing tamattuʿ without sacrificial animals was better for him. However, those who support the first view answer this by saying that he did not say this because

---

## Page 12867

what he had done was less preferable, but because his Companions found it difficult to leave ihrām while he remained in it. He would therefore have preferred to join them so that they would carry out what they had been commanded gladly and willingly. He may leave a better course for a less preferable one for the sake of harmony and unity of hearts, as when he said to ʿĀ’ishah:

“Were it not that your people had only recently left the pre-Islamic state of ignorance, I would have demolished the Kaaba and given it two doors.”

Here, he left what was preferable for the sake of harmony and conciliation, which were of lesser merit than that preferable course. Likewise, he chose tamattuʿ without bringing sacrificial animals.

On this understanding, Allah would have enabled him to do what was best while also granting him the merit of his willingness to join them. Thus, he would receive both rewards. This is what befits his standing, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The following makes this clear:

Bringing sacrificial animals is better than not bringing them, and he brought a hundred sacrificial camels. How, then, could foregoing that be better in itself merely because one leaves ihrām and then enters it again? Bringing sacrificial animals honours the sacred rites of Allah in a way that repeatedly leaving and entering ihrām does not. This is further illustrated by the fact that a pilgrim performing tamattuʿ who brings sacrificial animals should have greater merit than anyone who does not bring them, while a pilgrim who combines Hajj and umrah in a single ihrām and brings sacrificial animals has greater merit than both.

Furthermore, both the pilgrim who combines Hajj and umrah in a single ihrām and the pilgrim performing tamattuʿ must offer a sacrificial animal. It is well known that the sacrificial animals a pilgrim brings

---

## Page 12868

from outside the Sanctuary are, by the consensus of the Muslims, better than those he buys within the Sanctuary. Indeed, according to one of the two scholarly opinions, an animal only qualifies as a sacrificial offering if it is brought from outside the Sanctuary into it. Accordingly, bringing it from the mīqāt (the boundary for entering ihrām) is better than bringing it from the nearest point outside the Sanctuary. How, then, could a sacrificial animal that was not brought along be considered better than one that was? This and other considerations show that bringing sacrificial animals when performing tamattuʿ or qirān (combining Hajj and umrah in a single ihrām) is better than performing tamattuʿ without bringing them.

As for the questioner’s inquiry about whether any of the Companions performed umrah starting from Mecca: during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, no one did so except ʿĀ’ishah alone. Indeed, when ʿĀ’ishah herself performed Hajj, she would remain until the beginning of Muharram, then go out to al-Juhfah and enter ihrām there for umrah.

As for his statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“An umrah in Ramadan is equivalent to a Hajj”—and in another wording, “is equivalent to a Hajj with me”—

and the report in which he said:

“Hajj is in the cause of Allah,”

he explained to her that performing umrah in Ramadan would take the place of the Hajj she had missed. That Hajj was from Medina, and the umrah was also from Medina. This is because Ramadan is the month of fasting and precedes the months of Hajj. Whoever performs Hajj in that same year has greater merit than a pilgrim performing tamattuʿ. A pilgrim performing tamattuʿ must perform umrah during the months of Hajj, although he could have entered ihrām for Hajj. Thus, when he turned away from entering ihrām

---

## Page 12869

for Hajj to entering ihrām for umrah, he enjoyed the ease of being spared one of the two journeys. The sacrificial offering was therefore prescribed in return for that ease. For this reason, some jurists thought that the sacrificial offering required for tamattuʿ was compensatory. They prohibited the pilgrim from eating any of it and regarded its obligatory nature as evidence that tamattuʿ was less preferable, since a pilgrimage requiring no compensation is better than one requiring it.

Others replied:

One may not, without an excuse, commit an act that necessitates a compensatory sacrifice. Yet tamattuʿ is permissible without any need, so this cannot be a compensatory sacrifice. It may, however, be said that tamattuʿ is a concession, and a concession may be preferable. Thus, according to the scholars, shortening the prayer is preferable to praying four units, as established by the mass-transmitted Sunnah and the agreement of the early generations. Likewise, according to the stronger of the two scholarly opinions, not fasting while travelling and wiping over leather socks are preferable.

Not fasting was the later of the Prophet’s two practices, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Scholars disagreed over whether it is obligatory and whether fasting while travelling fulfils the obligation. A group of scholars, both early and later, held that anyone who fasts while travelling must make up those days. Muslims unanimously agree that not fasting while travelling is permissible, because it was the later of the Prophet’s two practices, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Muslims agree on its permissibility, and it is preferable; they did not dispute its permissibility. Moreover, it is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“It is not righteousness to fast while travelling.”

It is also established in Sahīh Muslim “that Hamzah

---

## Page 12870

ibn ʿAmr said to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ‘I am a man who fasts frequently. Should I fast while travelling?’ He replied, ‘If you do not fast, that is good; and if you fast, there is no harm.’” He thus commended not fasting and stated that there was no harm in fasting.

The same applies to “wiping over leather socks.” No one has reported that, after putting on leather socks while in a state of ritual purity and subsequently breaking his ablution, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would remove them and wash his feet. Rather, he would wipe over them. This is the point at issue. If someone is not wearing leather socks, his duty is to wash his feet; it is not prescribed for him to put them on merely in order to wipe over them. Rather, the question concerns someone who has put them on because he needs them: is it preferable to wipe over them, to remove them, or are both options equally preferable? There are three opinions. The correct view is that wiping is preferable, in keeping with the Sunnah.

Furthermore, someone who performs Hajj as tamattuʿ has done what is prescribed according to the agreement of the recognized scholars, whereas the pilgrimage of someone who does not perform tamattuʿ is disputed. It is established that Ibn ʿAbbās and a group of the early generations held that tamattuʿ is obligatory, and that anyone who has performed tawāf (circumambulation) and saʿy (walking between Safā and Marwah), without having brought a sacrificial animal, leaves the state of ihrām whether or not he intended to do so. According to them, no one may perform Hajj except as tamattuʿ. This is the position of Ibn Hazm and other Zāhirī scholars. It is also the position of the Shia, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed his Companions to do this during the Farewell Pilgrimage. Thus, if tamattuʿ is

---

## Page 12871

disputed as to its obligation but agreed upon as permissible, while the other forms are neither obligatory nor unanimously accepted as permissible, then the form of Hajj whose permissibility is agreed upon is preferable. This is not contradicted by the fact that some earlier scholars forbade tamattuʿ and some rulers would have people beaten for performing it. For it has been said of the scholars who held this position that they did not regard tamattuʿ as unlawful; rather, they preferred people to perform ʿumrah outside the months of Hajj, so that the House would always be frequented by pilgrims performing Hajj and ʿumrah. If anyone supposes that the prohibition amounted to declaring it unlawful, then this position contradicts both the established Sunnah of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the Book of Allah, and is therefore to be disregarded.

As for the scholars’ disagreement over whether those performing Hajj alone or combining Hajj and ʿumrah may change their pilgrimage to tamattuʿ, some scholars said that this had been abrogated and that it had been exclusive to those who performed Hajj with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Some explained that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, wanted to teach them that performing ʿumrah during the months of Hajj was permissible.

Others said: This is a very weak position. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed ʿumrah during the months of Hajj more than once; indeed, all his ʿumrahs took place during those months. The ʿumrah of al-Hudaybiyyah was in Dhū al-Qaʿdah, the make-up ʿumrah the following year was in Dhū al-Qaʿdah, and the ʿumrah of al-Jiʿrānah was in Dhū al-Qaʿdah. Was this not sufficient to demonstrate the permissibility of performing ʿumrah during the months of Hajj?

Moreover, it is established in the two Sahīh collections: “When they were at Dhū al-Hulayfah, he said, ‘Whoever wishes to enter ihrām for ʿumrah and Hajj may do so, and whoever wishes to enter ihrām for Hajj

---

## Page 12872

may do so, and whoever wishes to enter ihrām for ʿumrah may do so.’” He thus explicitly told them that all three forms were permissible, clearly demonstrating the permissibility of performing ʿumrah during the months of Hajj.

Moreover, the pilgrimage of those who performed tamattuʿ with him itself demonstrated its permissibility. He therefore could not have ordered everyone who performed Hajj with him to leave ihrām and make their pilgrimage tamattuʿ merely to demonstrate that it was permissible. Nor would he have moved them from a superior form to an inferior one. It is therefore clear that he moved them only to what was preferable. The following report is also established: “The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was asked, ‘Is this ʿumrah of ours for this year only, or for all time?’ He replied, ‘Rather, for all time. ʿUmrah has become part of Hajj until the Day of Resurrection.’”

Furthermore, since the unbelievers neither performed tamattuʿ nor performed ʿumrah during the months of Hajj, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, intended to differ from them, this would be among the Sunnah practices of Hajj. This is just as he did when standing at ʿArafah and Muzdalifah: the polytheists would hasten their departure from ʿArafah before sunset and delay their departure from Jamʿ until sunrise. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, differed from them, saying:

“Our practice differs from that of the polytheists.”

He therefore delayed his departure from ʿArafah until the sun had set and hastened his departure from Jamʿ before sunrise. This is the Sunnah for Muslims, by their unanimous agreement. The same applies to what he did regarding tamattuʿ and changing one’s pilgrimage to it: if he intended thereby to differ from the polytheists, then this is the Sunnah; and if he did so because it is preferable, it is likewise a Sunnah. On either understanding, changing one’s pilgrimage to tamattuʿ is preferable, in accordance with what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded his Companions to do. And Allah, glorified is He, knows best.

---

## Page 12873

Shaykh al-Islām, may Allah have mercy on him, said:

As for the Yemeni Corner, it is not to be kissed according to the correct view. As for the other sides of the House, the two northern corners, and the Station of Abraham, they are neither to be kissed nor rubbed with the hands, by agreement of the Muslims who follow the mass-transmitted Sunnah of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Since rubbing these places and kissing them is not recommended, it is all the more fitting that places of lesser standing should neither be kissed nor rubbed.

The scholars agree that someone greeting the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, at his grave is not recommended to kiss the chamber or rub his hands over it, lest the dwelling of a created being be made to resemble the House of the Creator. Moreover, he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“O Allah, do not make my grave an idol that is worshipped.”

He also said:

“Do not make my grave a site of recurring observance.”

And he said:

“Those before you used to take graves as places of worship. Beware! Do not take graves as places of worship, for I forbid you to do so.”

If this is the Muslims’ religious practice regarding the grave of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who is the foremost of Adam’s descendants, then it is all the more fitting that anyone else’s grave should neither be kissed nor touched. Some scholars have reported a less sound, dissenting view on this matter. However, I know of no disagreement about it among the imams whose teachings are followed and the early generations who preceded us. And Allah, glorified is He, knows best.

---

## Page 12874

Shaykh al-Islām Abū al-ʿAbbās Ahmad ibn Shihāb al-Dīn ʿAbd al-Halīm ibn Imām Majd al-Dīn ʿAbd al-Salām ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Taymiyya—may Allah be pleased with him—also said:

All praise belongs to Allah. We praise Him, seek His help and guidance, and ask His forgiveness. We seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whomever Allah guides, none can lead astray, and whomever He leads astray, none can guide. I bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped but Allah alone, without partner, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bestow blessings and abundant peace upon him, his family, and his Companions.

To proceed:

Many Muslims have repeatedly asked me to write an explanation of the rites of Hajj covering what most pilgrims need on most occasions. Earlier in my life, I wrote a manual of pilgrimage rites in which I included many supplications and followed the legal rulings of the scholars who preceded me. In this work, I have set out briefly and clearly what has become evident to me of the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. There is no power nor strength except through Allah.

---

## Page 12875

Section

The first thing a person intending Hajj or ʿumrah must do when he wishes to begin either is to enter ihrām, the state of consecration, for it. Before this, he merely intends Hajj or ʿumrah and has not yet begun either. He is like someone setting out for the Friday prayer: he receives the reward for making his way there, but has not begun the prayer until he formally enters it. Upon reaching the mīqāt (designated entry point), he must enter ihrām.

There are five entry points: Dhū al-Hulayfa, al-Juhfa, Qarn al-Manāzil, Yalamlam, and Dhāt ʿIrq.

{When the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, designated these entry points, he said: “These are for their inhabitants and for those from elsewhere who pass through them intending Hajj or ʿumrah. Whoever lives nearer to Mecca than these points enters ihrām from his home; even the people of Mecca enter ihrām from Mecca.”}

Dhū al-Hulayfa is the farthest of these entry points from Mecca. The distance between it and Mecca is ten stages of travel, or somewhat less or more, depending on the route, since several routes lead from it to Mecca. It is also called Wādī al-ʿAqīq, and its mosque is called the Mosque of the Tree. There is a well there which the ignorant among the common people call “ʿAlī’s Well,” believing that ʿAlī fought the jinn there. This is false, for none of the Companions fought the jinn, and ʿAlī is far too exalted

---

## Page 12876

in rank for the jinn to stand their ground against him in battle. This well has neither any special merit nor anything blameworthy about it, and throwing a stone or anything else into it is not recommended.

As for al-Juhfa, it lies about three stages of travel from Mecca. It was an old, inhabited village called Mahyaʿa, but it is now in ruins. For this reason, people have come to enter ihrām before reaching it, at the place called Rābigh. This is the designated entry point for those coming for Hajj from the west, such as the people of Syria, Egypt, and the other western lands. However, if they pass through the Prophet’s city of Medina—as they do nowadays—they enter ihrām at the entry point designated for the people of Medina. There is scholarly agreement that this is recommended for them. If they delay entering ihrām until al-Juhfa, however, there is disagreement concerning this.

Each of the other three entry points lies about two stages of travel from Mecca. No one intending Hajj or ʿumrah may pass the designated entry point without entering ihrām. Someone travelling to Mecca for trade or a visit should also enter ihrām, though there is disagreement over whether this is obligatory.

Anyone reaching the designated entry point during the months of Hajj may choose between three forms of pilgrimage, known as tamattuʿ, ifrād, and qirān. If he wishes, he may enter ihrām for ʿumrah and, after leaving ihrām upon completing it, enter ihrām for Hajj. This is what is specifically called tamattuʿ. Alternatively, he may enter ihrām for both together, or enter ihrām for ʿumrah and then include Hajj in it before circumambulating the Kaaba

---

## Page 12877

—this is qirān, which is included under the term tamattuʿ in the Quran, the Sunnah, and the words of the Companions. Alternatively, he may enter ihrām for Hajj alone; this is ifrād.

Section: Which of These Is Preferable

The correct conclusion is that the preferable form varies according to the pilgrim’s circumstances. If he makes one journey for ʿumrah and another for Hajj, or travels to Mecca before the months of Hajj, performs ʿumrah, and remains there until he performs Hajj, then ifrād is preferable for him, by agreement of the four imams. Entering ihrām for Hajj before its months is not a Sunnah practice; rather, it is disliked. If someone does so, there is disagreement over whether he thereby enters ihrām for ʿumrah or for Hajj.

If, however, he does what most people do—combining ʿumrah and Hajj in a single journey and arriving in Mecca during the months of Hajj, namely Shawwāl, Dhū al-Qaʿda, and the first ten days of Dhū al-Hijja—then qirān is preferable for him if he brings sacrificial animals with him. If he does not bring sacrificial animals, it is preferable for him to leave ihrām by performing ʿumrah. For this is established through widely transmitted reports whose authenticity is undisputed among scholars

---

## Page 12878

of hadith—that when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed the Farewell Hajj with his Companions, he instructed them all to conclude their ihrām with an ʿumrah, except those who had brought sacrificial animals. He instructed those pilgrims to remain in ihrām until the sacrificial animals reached their appointed place of sacrifice on the Day of Sacrifice.

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and a group of his Companions had brought sacrificial animals. He combined ʿumrah and Hajj, saying: ‘Here I am, answering Your call for ʿumrah and Hajj.’”

None of those accompanying the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed ʿumrah after Hajj except ʿĀʾishah alone. She had begun menstruating and had therefore been unable to circumambulate the Kaʿbah, for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A menstruating woman performs all the pilgrimage rites except circumambulation of the House.”

He therefore instructed her to enter ihrām for Hajj and refrain from performing the rites of ʿumrah, since she had intended tamattuʿ. She later asked the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to enable her to perform ʿumrah. He sent her with her brother ʿAbd al-Rahmān, and she entered ihrām for ʿumrah from al-Tanʿīm.

Al-Tanʿīm is the nearest point outside the Sacred Precinct to Mecca. Today it contains the mosques known as the “Mosques of ʿĀʾishah.” These did not exist during the Prophet’s lifetime, may Allah bless him and grant him peace; they were built later to mark the place where ʿĀʾishah entered ihrām. For someone passing them while in ihrām, neither entering these mosques nor praying in them is obligatory or a Sunnah practice. Rather, deliberately seeking to do so and believing it to be recommended is a reprehensible innovation. However, if someone leaves Mecca to perform ʿumrah and enters one of these mosques and prays there for the purpose of entering ihrām,

---

## Page 12879

there is nothing wrong with that.
