In the time of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Rightly Guided Caliphs, no one would leave Mecca to perform ʿumrah except for a valid reason, whether in Ramadan or at any other time. Of those who performed Hajj with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, none undertook an ʿumrah from Mecca after Hajj except ʿĀʾishah, as already mentioned. Nor was this the practice of the Rightly Guided Caliphs. Those Companions who considered it preferable to perform Hajj separately from ʿumrah meant performing Hajj on one journey and ʿumrah on another. They did not consider it preferable to perform Hajj and then immediately undertake an ʿumrah from Mecca. Indeed, they never did this, save perhaps on rare occasions.

The early generations differed over this: would such a person be considered to be performing tamattuʿ and thus required to offer a sacrifice, or not? Would this ʿumrah fulfil the obligation of ʿumrah required in Islam, or not?

After his migration, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed four ʿumrahs:

The ʿumrah of al-Hudaybiyah: he reached al-Hudaybiyah, which lies beyond the mountain at al-Tanʿīm, near the Mosques of ʿĀʾishah, on your right as you enter Mecca. The polytheists prevented him from reaching the House, so he concluded a peace agreement with them, left ihrām, and departed.

The ʿumrah known as al-Qadiyyah, which he performed the following year.

The ʿumrah from al-Jiʿrānah: he had fought the polytheists at Hunayn, and Hunayn lies toward

---

## Page 12880

the east, in the direction of al-Tāʾif. Badr, by contrast, lies between Medina and Mecca. The two battles were six years apart, but they are mentioned together because Allah Most High sent down angels in both to support the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the believers in battle. He then went and besieged the polytheists at al-Tāʾif, before returning and distributing the spoils of Hunayn at al-Jiʿrānah. After distributing the spoils of Hunayn, he performed ʿumrah from al-Jiʿrānah, entering Mecca rather than leaving it to enter ihrām.

His fourth ʿumrah accompanied his Hajj. He combined ʿumrah and Hajj in a single ihrām, as agreed by those knowledgeable about his Sunnah and by the Companions. No Companion is reported to have said that he performed tamattuʿ in which he left ihrām between the two rites. Rather, they used the term tamattuʿ for qirān—combining the two in a single ihrām. Nor is any Companion reported to have said that, when he combined them, he performed two tawāfs (circumambulations) and two saʿīs (rites of walking between al-Safā and al-Marwah).

The accounts transmitted from the Companions describing his Hajj are generally consistent. They have caused confusion only among those who did not understand what the Companions meant. All the Companions reported to have said that he performed Hajj alone—such as ʿĀʾishah, Ibn ʿUmar and Jābir—also said that he combined ʿumrah with Hajj as tamattuʿ. Indeed, this is established in the two Sahīhs from ʿĀʾishah and Ibn ʿUmar through a chain of transmission more authentic than that of the report that he performed Hajj alone. By tamattuʿ they meant qirān, as is also established in the authentic hadith collections.

When a person wishes to enter ihrām, if he is combining ʿumrah and Hajj in a single ihrām, he says: “I answer Your call for ʿumrah and Hajj.” If he is performing tamattuʿ, he says: “I answer Your call for ʿumrah, to be followed by Hajj as tamattuʿ.” If he is performing Hajj alone, he says: “I answer Your call for Hajj.”

---

## Page 12881

or say: “O Allah, I have committed myself to ʿumrah and Hajj,” or “I have committed myself to ʿumrah, to be followed by Hajj as tamattuʿ,” or “I have committed myself to Hajj,” or “I intend to perform Hajj,” or “I intend to perform both,” or “I intend to perform ʿumrah followed by Hajj as tamattuʿ.” Whichever of these he says suffices, by agreement of the imams. There is no prescribed wording for this, nor is any of these formulations obligatory, by agreement of the imams, just as verbalizing one’s intention for ritual purification, prayer or fasting is not obligatory, by agreement of the imams. Rather, whenever a person recites the talbiyah intending to enter ihrām, his ihrām takes effect, by agreement of the Muslims. He need not say anything before the talbiyah.

The scholars have, however, differed over whether saying such words is recommended, just as they have differed over whether verbalizing one’s intention for prayer is recommended. The definitively correct view is that none of this is recommended. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not prescribe any of it for the Muslims. Neither he nor his Companions would utter any words expressing their intention before the opening takbīr. Rather:

{When he instructed Dubāʿah bint al-Zubayr to stipulate a condition, she asked: “What should I say?” He replied: “Say: ‘I answer Your call, O Allah, I answer Your call. My place of release from ihrām shall be wherever You prevent me from continuing.’” This was narrated by the authors of the Sunan collections and authenticated by al-Tirmidhī. Al-Nasāʾī’s wording is: “I intend to perform Hajj; what should I say?” He replied: “Say: ‘I answer Your call, O Allah, I answer Your call. My place of release from ihrām shall be wherever You prevent me from continuing,’ for you are entitled, with your Lord, to the exception you have stipulated.”}

The hadith concerning stipulating a condition is in the two Sahīhs. The point of citing this wording, however, is that he instructed her to stipulate the condition within the talbiyah, and did not

---

## Page 12882

instruct her to say anything before the talbiyah, whether a condition or anything else.

In his talbiyah, he would say: “I answer Your call for ʿumrah and Hajj.” He would ask individual Companions: “For which rite have you recited the talbiyah?”

Concerning the designated places for entering ihrām, he said: “The people of Medina enter ihrām at Dhū al-Hulayfah; the people of Syria at al-Juhfah; the people of Yemen at Yalamlam; the people of Najd at Qarn al-Manāzil; and the people of Iraq at Dhāt ʿIrq. Whoever lives nearer than these places enters ihrām from his home.”

Ihlāl means reciting the talbiyah. This, then, is what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prescribed for Muslims to say at the beginning of Hajj and ʿumrah, although it is also prescribed thereafter—just as the opening takbīr is prescribed for prayer, and further takbīrs are prescribed when changing positions.

If someone enters ihrām without specifying its form, that is valid. Thus, if he enters ihrām with a general intention to perform Hajj, without knowing these distinctions, that is valid.

Likewise, if he begins reciting the talbiyah as people do, intending to perform the pilgrimage rites, but neither names a particular form aloud nor intends in his heart tamattuʿ, ifrād or qirān, his Hajj is still valid, and he may perform any one of the three.

If he does what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed his Companions to do, that is good. If, fearing an impediment, he stipulates a condition with his Lord, saying, “If anything prevents me from continuing, my place of release from ihrām shall be wherever You prevent me from continuing,” that is good. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed his cousin Dubāʿah bint al-Zubayr ibn ʿAbd al-Muttalib to stipulate a condition with her Lord when she was unwell, because he feared that illness might prevent her from reaching the House, and he did not

---

## Page 12883

instruct everyone who performed Hajj to do so.

Likewise, if someone entering ihrām wishes to apply perfume to his body, that is good. Yet he is not instructed to do so before entering ihrām, for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did this himself but did not instruct people to do it. Nor did the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instruct anyone to use a particular formula. Rather, one simply says, “He pronounced the talbiyah for Hajj,” or “He pronounced the talbiyah for ʿumrah,” whether using the verb ahalla or labbā. This is the meaning of Allah’s words:

“Hajj is during well-known months. Whoever undertakes Hajj during them must refrain from sexual intimacy, sinful conduct, and disputing during Hajj.”

It is also authentically reported from him in the two Sahīh collections that he said:

“Whoever performs Hajj to this House without engaging in sexual intimacy or sinful conduct emerges free of his sins, as on the day his mother gave birth to him.”

This accords with the recitation in which “no sexual intimacy and no sinful conduct” is read with nominative endings. Here, “sexual intimacy” encompasses intercourse and talk of it, while “sinful conduct” encompasses all acts of disobedience. According to this recitation, “disputing” means controversy over the rites of Hajj. Allah has made these clear, explained them, and put an end to the controversy over them, unlike the disputes people used to have about their rulings in the pre-Islamic era.

The other recitation may also be interpreted in this sense. It has also been interpreted as meaning that the pilgrim must not argue with anyone. The first interpretation is more correct, for Allah has not forbidden disputation without qualification, whether for someone in ihrām or anyone else. Rather, disputation may be obligatory or recommended, as Allah Most High says:

“And argue with them in the best manner.”

Disputation may also be forbidden, both during Hajj and otherwise, such as arguing without knowledge or disputing the truth after it has become clear.

---

## Page 12884

The term “sinful conduct” encompasses everything Allah Most High has forbidden; it is not limited to verbal abuse. Although reviling a Muslim is sinful conduct, the term encompasses this and other acts as well. “Sexual intimacy” means intercourse. Among the prohibited acts, only acts of sexual intercourse invalidate Hajj; hence the distinction drawn between sexual intimacy and sinful conduct.

As for the other prohibited acts, such as wearing prohibited clothing and applying perfume, although one sins by committing them, they do not invalidate Hajj according to any of the well-known imams. A person in ihrām should speak only of matters that concern him. When Shurayh entered ihrām, he would become like a deaf snake.

A person does not enter ihrām merely by having the intention and resolve to perform Hajj in his heart, for that resolve has remained in his heart ever since he left his hometown. Rather, there must be some utterance or action by which he enters ihrām. This is the correct one of the two views.

Removing prohibited clothing is obligatory in ihrām, but it is not a condition for its validity. If someone enters ihrām while still wearing such clothing, his ihrām is valid, as established by the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and by the agreement of the leading scholars. He must, however, remove the prohibited clothing.

Section:

It is recommended to enter ihrām immediately after a prayer, whether obligatory or voluntary, if it is

---

## Page 12885

a time when voluntary prayer is permitted, according to one of the two views. According to the other, if he is offering an obligatory prayer, he should enter ihrām immediately afterward; otherwise, there is no prayer prescribed specifically for ihrām. This latter view is stronger.

It is recommended to bathe before entering ihrām, even for a woman experiencing postnatal bleeding or menstruation. If any personal grooming is needed, such as clipping the nails, plucking the underarm hair, shaving the pubic hair, or the like, one should attend to it. This is not something specific to ihrām, nor was it mentioned in the reports transmitted by the Companions. Rather, it is prescribed as needed. The same applies to those attending the Friday or Eid prayers.

It is recommended to enter ihrām wearing two clean pieces of cloth; white ones are preferable. One may enter ihrām wearing any permissible fabric, including cotton, linen, and wool. The Sunnah is to wear a waist-wrapper and an upper wrap, whether sewn or unsewn, by agreement of the imams. Entering ihrām in something else is also valid, provided it is permissible to wear. One may wear white or any other permissible colour, even a multicoloured garment.

It is preferable to enter ihrām wearing sandals, if available. A sandal is what is called a tāsūma. If one cannot find sandals, one may wear leather socks without having to cut them below the ankles. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, initially commanded that they be cut, then

---

## Page 12886

later permitted, at ‘Arafāt, those who could not find a waist-wrapper to wear trousers, and those who could not find sandals to wear leather socks. His initial permission concerned leather socks cut below the ankles, because cutting them makes them like sandals.

For this reason, the correct view is that footwear extending no higher than below the ankles may be worn, such as leather socks of the muka‘‘ab type, jumjum and madās footwear, and the like, whether or not sandals are available. If one cannot find sandals or anything that serves in their place, such as jumjum or madās footwear and the like, one may wear leather socks without cutting them. Likewise, if one cannot find a waist-wrapper, one may wear trousers without unseaming them. This is the sounder of the two scholarly views, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, permitted these substitutes at ‘Arafāt, as narrated by Ibn ‘Umar.

One may also wear anything that serves as a waist-wrapper or an upper wrap. Thus, by agreement of the imams, one may wrap oneself in a coat, robe, shirt or similar garment, laying it crosswise over oneself as a covering. One may also wear it upside down, with its lower end uppermost, and cover oneself with a blanket or another covering. However, one must not cover one’s head unless there is a need.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade a person in ihrām to wear a shirt, a hooded cloak, trousers, leather socks or a turban. He also forbade people to cover the head of someone who died in ihrām, and ordered a man who had entered ihrām wearing a robe to remove it. Anything of this kind falls within the scope of what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited. Thus, whatever is equivalent to a shirt

---

## Page 12887

is subject to the same ruling. He may not wear a shirt, whether sleeved or sleeveless, whether or not he puts his arms through it, and whether it is intact or torn. Likewise, he may not wear a robe or a coat into which he puts his arms. The same applies to the shirt called ‘irq jīn and similar garments, by agreement of the imams. As for draping a coat over his shoulders without putting his arms through it, there is disagreement over this.

This is what the jurists mean by the prohibition on wearing such clothing. “Stitched clothing” means garments tailored to fit a part of the body. Likewise, he may not wear anything equivalent to leather socks, such as overboots, socks and similar items. Nor may he wear anything equivalent to trousers, such as short drawers and the like.

He may tie whatever needs tying, such as his waist-wrapper and the money belt holding his expenses. His upper wrap does not need tying, so he should not tie it. If he does need to tie it, there is disagreement, though the more plausible view is that it is then permissible. There is also disagreement over whether the restriction on tying it means that doing so is merely discouraged or strictly forbidden. There is no evidence that it is forbidden except the report that Ibn ‘Umar—may Allah be pleased with him—disliked tying the upper wrap.

Those who follow Ibn ‘Umar have differed: some, such as Abū Hanīfah and others, hold that it is discouraged without being forbidden, while others hold that it is forbidden.

As for his head, he must not cover it with anything, whether stitched or otherwise. Thus, he must not cover it with a turban, a cap, a headscarf, a cloth lying against it, or anything else. He may

---

## Page 12888

take shade beneath a roof or trees, or inside a tent and the like, by their agreement. As for taking shade beneath the canopy of a camel litter while travelling, there is disagreement. It is preferable for a man in ihram to remain exposed to the sun for the sake of the One for whom he entered ihram, as the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and his Companions used to perform Hajj. Ibn ‘Umar once saw a man being shaded and said, “Old man, expose yourself to the sun for the sake of the One for whom you entered ihram.” For this reason, the early generations disliked canopies over camel litters—that is, litters fitted with a roof. Open litters, however, were disliked only by some ascetics. This applies to men.

As for a woman, she is to be covered. She is therefore permitted to wear clothing that conceals her and to take shade beneath the canopy of a camel litter. However, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—forbade her to wear a niqab or gloves. Gloves are coverings made for the hands, like those worn by falconers. If a woman covers her face with something that does not touch it, this is permissible by agreement. If it does touch her face, the correct view is that this too is permissible. She is not required to hold her covering away from her face with a stick, her hand or anything else, for the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—treated her face and hands alike: both are like a man’s body, not his head.

His wives—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—used to lower their coverings over their faces without taking care to keep them away from their skin. No scholar has transmitted from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—that he said:

“A woman’s ihram entails leaving her face uncovered.”

Rather, this is a statement of some of the early generations. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—only forbade her to wear a niqab or gloves.

---

## Page 12889

just as he forbade a man in ihram to wear a shirt or leather socks, even though he may cover his hands and feet by agreement of the imams. The burqa is even more concealing than the niqab, so they unanimously prohibit it. Thus, a woman in ihram must not wear anything made specifically to cover the face, such as a burqa or the like, since it is equivalent to the niqab.

A person in ihram may not wear anything the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—forbade except when there is a need, just as a fasting person may not break his fast except when there is a need. Such a need includes cold that he fears will make him ill if he does not cover his head, or an illness requiring him to cover his head. He may then wear only what he needs, removing it when he no longer needs it.

He must offer compensation: either fasting three days, sacrificing a sheep, or feeding six needy people, giving each half a sāʿ of dates or barley, or one mudd of wheat. Giving bread is also permissible: two Iraqi ratls, approximately half a Damascene ratl, for each person. It should be served with an accompaniment. Feeding him foods such as rusks, thin flatbread, and the like is permissible and is better than giving him wheat or barley. The same applies to all other expiations: providing someone with staple food together with an accompaniment is better than giving him grain alone, if it is not their custom to grind grain and bake bread themselves.

In all these cases, the obligation is what Allah Most High mentions in His words:

“Feeding ten needy people with food of the average standard you feed your own families

---

## Page 12890

or clothing them,” to the end of the verse. Allah Most High thus commanded that the needy be fed with food of the average standard people feed their own families.

The scholars have differed over whether this amount is fixed by Islamic law or determined by customary practice. They have likewise differed over a wife’s maintenance. The stronger view in all these matters is that customary practice should determine it: each community should provide the food they feed their own families. Since Kaʿb ibn ʿUjra and others like him lived on dates, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—commanded him to distribute a firq of dates among six needy people. A firq is sixteen Baghdad ratls.

When a person needs to perform an act prohibited in ihram, he may offer this compensation either before or after performing it. He may slaughter the sacrificial animal before reaching Mecca, and he may fast the three days consecutively or separately, as he wishes. If he has an excuse, he may delay offering the compensation; otherwise, he is to offer it promptly. If he repeatedly wears prohibited clothing without having offered compensation, a single compensation suffices, according to the stronger of the two scholarly opinions.

Section

Upon entering ihram, he should recite the talbiya of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Here I am, O Allah, here I am. Here I am; You have no partner; here I am. All praise, blessings, and sovereignty belong to You. There is no

---

## Page 12891

partner to You.” If he adds such words as “Here I am, O Lord of the ways of ascent,” or “Here I am, ready to serve You,” or similar expressions, that is permissible. The Companions used to make such additions while the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—heard them, and he did not forbid them. He himself consistently recited his own talbiya. One begins reciting the talbiya upon entering ihram, whether one has mounted a riding animal or not. Entering ihram after that is also permissible.

The talbiya is a response to Allah Most High’s call to His creation, when He summoned them to perform Hajj to His House through His close friend Abraham—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The person reciting the talbiya submits and yields to another, just as someone seized by the collar and held at the base of the neck yields. Its meaning is: “We answer Your call, submit to Your wisdom, and obey Your command, time after time, continuing steadfastly in this.”

The talbiya is the distinctive proclamation of Hajj. The finest acts of Hajj are calling aloud and shedding blood: calling aloud means raising the voice in the talbiya, and shedding blood means slaughtering the sacrificial animals. It is therefore recommended for a man to raise his voice in the talbiya without straining himself. A woman raises her voice enough for her female companion to hear her.

It is recommended to recite it frequently as circumstances change: after the prayers, when ascending a rise or descending into a valley, upon hearing someone recite the talbiya, when night or day begins, and when groups of travelers meet. The same applies when one does something forbidden. It has been narrated that whoever recites the talbiya until sunset reaches the evening forgiven.

If, after reciting the talbiya, he supplicates and invokes blessings upon the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—

---

## Page 12892

and asks Allah for His good pleasure and Paradise, and seeks refuge in His mercy from His wrath and the Fire, that is good.

Section

Among the things forbidden to a person in ihram are applying perfume to his body or clothing after entering ihram and deliberately smelling perfume. As for anointing his head or body with olive oil, clarified butter, or similar substances containing no perfume, there is a well-known disagreement concerning this; avoiding it is preferable.

He must not trim his nails or cut his hair. He may scratch his body when it itches and undergo cupping on his head or elsewhere. If this requires shaving some hair, that is permissible, for it is authentically established that:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—underwent cupping on the middle of his head while in ihram.”

This cannot be done without shaving some hair. Likewise, if he bathes and some of his hair falls out as a result, that does not harm him, even if he is certain that it came away through washing. He may undergo bloodletting by opening a vein if he needs it. There is consensus that he may bathe to remove major ritual impurity following sexual activity; he may also bathe for other reasons.

A person in ihram must neither marry nor be given in marriage, nor make a marriage proposal. He must not hunt land game or acquire ownership of it by purchase, acceptance as a gift, or any other means. Nor may he assist in hunting or slaughter game. As for aquatic game, such as fish and the like, he may catch and eat it.

He may cut down trees, but within the Sanctuary itself he must not cut any of

---

## Page 12893

its trees—even if he is not in ihram—nor any of its wild vegetation, except idhkhir grass. Whatever people have planted or sown belongs to them. Dried vegetation may also be gathered. No game may be hunted there, even aquatic game such as fish, according to the correct view. Nor may its game be disturbed, for example by making an animal move so that one can sit in its place.

The same applies to the Sanctuary of the city of Allah’s Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. It lies between the city’s two lava fields. A lābah is a harrah: land covered with black stones. The Sanctuary measures one barīd by one barīd; a barīd is four parasangs. It extends from Mount ʿAyr to Mount Thawr. ʿAyr is a mountain near the miqat that resembles an ʿayr, meaning a donkey. Thawr is a mountain in the direction of Uhud, distinct from Mount Thawr in Mecca.

The game of this Sanctuary must likewise not be hunted, and its trees must not be cut except to meet a need, such as making riding equipment or agricultural implements. As much of its grass as is needed for fodder may be taken. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—permitted this for the people of Medina because they needed it: unlike the Meccan Sanctuary, their surroundings do not provide enough to spare them this need. If game is brought into this Sanctuary, one is not required to release it.

There are no sacred sanctuaries anywhere in the world other than these two—not Jerusalem or any other place. No other place should be called a sanctuary, as the ignorant do when they speak of the “Sanctuary of Jerusalem” and the “Sanctuary of Hebron.” These two places, and all others besides the two sanctuaries, are not sacred sanctuaries, by the consensus of the Muslims. The Sanctuary of Mecca is recognized by consensus. Medina also has a sanctuary according to the majority, as is widely attested

---

## Page 12894

by the hadiths transmitted from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The Muslims have not disputed the status of any third sanctuary except “Wajj,” a valley in al-Tā’if. Some consider it a sanctuary, but the majority do not.

A person in ihram may kill creatures that habitually harm people, such as snakes, scorpions, mice, crows and vicious dogs. He may also defend himself against humans or animals that harm him. If someone attacks him and can only be repelled by fighting, he may fight him. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Whoever is killed defending his property is a martyr; whoever is killed defending his life is a martyr; whoever is killed defending his religion is a martyr; and whoever is killed defending his honour is a martyr.”

If fleas or lice bite him, he may remove or kill them without incurring any liability. Removing them is less serious than killing them. Likewise, he is forbidden to kill animals that come his way, even if they are themselves unlawful to eat, such as lions and cheetahs. If he does kill such an animal, however, no compensation is due according to the stronger of the two scholarly views. As for removing lice when they are not causing discomfort, this is a form of seeking comfort, so he should not do it. If he does, however, he incurs no liability.

Sexual intercourse and the acts leading to it are forbidden to a person in ihram. He must not engage in intercourse with anyone or anything, whether a woman or otherwise. Nor may he seek pleasure through kissing, touching with his hand, or looking with desire. If he has intercourse, his Hajj is invalidated. Whether ejaculation without intercourse invalidates it is disputed. Nor does he invalidate

---

## Page 12895

his Hajj by committing any prohibited act except those of this kind. If he kisses with desire or emits pre-ejaculatory fluid out of desire, he must offer a sacrifice.

Section

When he reaches Mecca, he may enter the city and the Sacred Mosque from any direction. It is preferable, however, to approach from the side facing the front of the Kaaba, following the example of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He entered Mecca from that direction, through its upper quarter, where Bāb al-Ma‘lā stands today. In the Prophet’s time—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—neither Mecca nor Medina had walls or constructed gateways. Rather, he entered through the upper mountain pass, the pass of Kadā’—pronounced with a short a after the k and a long ā—which overlooks the cemetery.

He entered the Sacred Mosque through its main gate, known as Bāb Banī Shaybah, then went to the Black Stone. This is the shortest route to the Black Stone for someone entering through Bāb al-Ma‘lā.

In earlier times, no building in Mecca rose above the House. Nor were there buildings atop al-Safā, al-Marwah or al-Mash‘ar al-Harām (the Sacred Site). There was no mosque in Minā or at ‘Arafāt, nor were there mosques beside the stoning sites. All these were built after the time of the Rightly Guided Caliphs; some were built after the Umayyad dynasty, and others later still. Thus, the House could be seen before entering the Sacred Mosque.

---

## Page 12896

Ibn Jarīr related:

“When the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—saw the House, he would raise his hands and say: ‘O Allah, increase this House in honor, greatness, dignity, awe and goodness, and increase in honor and greatness those who honor and revere it by performing Hajj or ‘Umrah.’”

Whoever sees the House before entering the mosque may do this. Those who recommend this supplication recommend saying it upon seeing the House, even if that is after entering the mosque.

However, upon entering the mosque, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—began with circumambulation. Before that, he offered neither the prayer of greeting the mosque nor any other prayer. Rather, the greeting of the Sacred Mosque is to circumambulate the House. He—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—would also bathe before entering Mecca and spend the night at Dhū Tuwā, near the wells known as Ābār al-Zāhir. Whoever can conveniently spend the night there, bathe and enter Mecca by day should do so; otherwise, none of this is required of him.

Upon entering the mosque, he should begin with circumambulation, starting at the Black Stone. He should face it directly, touch it and kiss it if possible, without harming anyone by jostling to reach it. If he cannot kiss it, he should touch it and kiss his hand; otherwise, he should gesture toward it. He then begins circumambulating, keeping the House on his left. He need not go to the space between the two corners or walk sideways before beginning his circuit; indeed, doing so is not even recommended.

When touching it, he says: “In the name of Allah; Allah is the Greatest.” If he wishes, he may say:

---

## Page 12897

“O Allah, I do this out of faith in You, belief in Your Book, faithfulness to Your covenant, and adherence to the Sunnah of Your Prophet Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”

He keeps the House on his left and makes seven circuits. He must not cut through the Hijr enclosure during his circumambulation, since most of it forms part of the House, and Allah has commanded circumambulation around the House, not within it.

He touches only the two Yemeni corners, not the two Syrian corners. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—touched only those two because they stand on Abraham’s foundations, whereas the other two lie within the House. Thus, the corner containing the Black Stone is touched and kissed; the Yemeni Corner is touched but not kissed; and the other two are neither touched nor kissed. Touching here means passing one’s hand over it.

As for the remaining sides of the House, the Station of Abraham, and all the mosques on earth and their walls, the graves of the prophets and the righteous—such as the chamber of our Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and Abraham’s cave—and the place where our Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—used to pray, as well as other graves of the prophets and the righteous and the Rock in Jerusalem: none of these is to be touched or kissed, by agreement of the imams. Circumambulating them is among the gravest forbidden religious innovations. Whoever adopts this as a religious practice must be called upon to repent; if he repents, well and good; otherwise, he is put to death.

If he places his hand on the shādharwān, the ledge to which the Kaaba’s coverings are fastened, this does not invalidate his circumambulation according to the more correct of the two scholarly opinions. The shādharwān is not part of the House; rather, it was built to support it.

During his first circumambulation, it is recommended that he jog from the Black Stone back to the Black Stone

---

## Page 12898

during the first three circuits. This jogging consists of walking briskly with short steps. If crowding prevents him from jogging, moving to the edge of the circumambulation area and jogging is better than remaining close to the House without jogging. If, however, he can remain close to the House while fully observing the Sunnah, that is preferable. He may circumambulate beyond the dome of Zamzam and the covered porticoes beyond it that adjoin the mosque’s walls. If someone prays in the mosque while people circumambulate in front of him, this is not disliked, whether a man or a woman passes before him. This is one of the rulings specific to Mecca.

It is also recommended that he leave his right shoulder uncovered during this circumambulation. This means exposing his right upper arm by passing the middle of his upper garment beneath his right armpit and placing both ends over his left shoulder. If he omits jogging and uncovering his right shoulder, he owes nothing.

During circumambulation, it is recommended that he remember Allah Most High and supplicate to Him with prescribed supplications. There is no harm in reciting the Quran quietly. No fixed formula of remembrance for circumambulation has been transmitted from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—whether through his commands, words, or instruction. Rather, one may offer any of the prescribed supplications. The particular supplication that many people recite beneath the waterspout, and similar practices, have no basis.

The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—would conclude his circumambulation between the two corners by saying:

“Our Lord, grant us good in this world, and in the Hereafter

---

## Page 12899

good, and protect us from the punishment of the Fire.” He would likewise conclude his other supplications with these words. The imams agree that no particular formula of remembrance is obligatory during circumambulation. Circumambulation of the House is like prayer, except that Allah has permitted speech during it; whoever speaks during it should therefore say only what is good.

For this reason, the person circumambulating is instructed to be free of both minor and major ritual impurity, to cover his private parts, and to avoid the impurities that a person praying must avoid, performing his circumambulation in a state of purity. However, scholars disagree over whether ritual purity is obligatory for circumambulation. No one has reported that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—commanded purification for circumambulation or prohibited someone in a state of ritual impurity from circumambulating. He did, however, circumambulate in a state of purity. It is also established that he prohibited menstruating women from circumambulating.

The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“The key to prayer is purification; its sacred state begins with the declaration ‘Allah is the Greatest’ and ends with the salutation of peace.”

Thus, the prayer for which he required purification is that which begins with the declaration of Allah’s greatness and ends with the salutation of peace. This includes prayers involving bowing and prostration, the funeral prayer, and the two prostrations for forgetfulness. Circumambulation and the prostration of recitation, however, do not fall into this category.

By scholarly agreement, a mosque is required for ritual retreat, but ritual purity is not a condition for it. A woman observing ritual retreat is prohibited from remaining in the mosque while menstruating, although she may remain there in a state of minor ritual impurity.

Ahmad ibn Hanbal said in his son ʿAbd Allāh’s Manāsik al-Hajj (The Rites of Hajj): “We were told by

---

## Page 12900

Sahl ibn Yūsuf, who said: ‘Shuʿba informed us, reporting from Hammād and Mansūr: “I asked them both about a man circumambulating the House without ablution, and neither saw any harm in it.”’”

ʿAbd Allāh said: “I asked my father about this, and he replied: ‘I prefer that he not circumambulate the House without ablution, because circumambulation of the House is prayer.’” Reports from Ahmad differ over whether ritual purity is a condition of validity or an obligation, the latter also being one of the two views within Abū Hanīfa’s school. However, Abū Hanīfa’s school consistently holds that it is not a condition of validity.

Anyone who circumambulates wearing socks or the like to avoid stepping on impurities such as pigeon droppings, or covers his hands to avoid touching a woman, or takes similar measures, has acted contrary to the Sunnah. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—his Companions, and their successors continually circumambulated the House, and pigeons have always been present in Mecca. Taking precautions is good, however, provided it does not conflict with the established Sunnah. When it leads to such a conflict, it is mistaken.

Know that an action involving a departure from the Sunnah is mistaken. An example is removing one’s sandals for an obligatory prayer or a funeral prayer out of fear that they might contain some impurity. This is a mistake contrary to the Sunnah, for the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—used to pray in his sandals and said:

“The Jews do not pray in their sandals, so differ from them.”

He also said:

“When one of you comes to the mosque, let him examine his sandals. If there is any filth on them, let him rub them against the earth, for the earth purifies them.”

Just as it is permissible to pray in one’s sandals, it is likewise permissible to circumambulate in them.

---

## Page 12901

And if he cannot circumambulate on foot and does so riding or being carried, his circumambulation is valid by agreement. The same applies to any obligations of circumambulation that he cannot fulfil. For example, someone who has an impurity that cannot be removed—such as a woman with irregular bleeding or someone with urinary incontinence—may circumambulate without incurring any liability, by agreement of the imams. The same applies if he can only circumambulate naked and therefore does so at night, just as when he can only pray naked.

The same applies to a menstruating woman who cannot perform the obligatory circumambulation except while menstruating because she cannot remain in Mecca. According to one of the two views held by scholars who require ritual purity for circumambulation, if a menstruating woman, a person in a state of major or minor ritual impurity, or someone bearing an impurity circumambulates, the circumambulation is valid without qualification, but a compensatory sacrifice is due: either a sheep, or else a camel in cases of menstruation or major ritual impurity and a sheep in cases of minor ritual impurity.

The prohibition on a menstruating woman circumambulating may be explained by circumambulation’s resemblance to prayer, or by her being barred from the mosque, just as she is barred from observing a devotional retreat there. As Allah, Mighty and Majestic, said to Abraham—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and his son:

“Purify My House for those who circumambulate it, those who remain there in devotion, and those who bow and prostrate.”

He thus commanded him to purify it for these acts of worship, and the menstruating woman was therefore barred from entering it. Scholars agree that circumambulation does not require what prayer requires, such as the prescribed opening and closing formulas, recitation, and other things. Nor is it invalidated by things that invalidate prayer, such as eating, drinking, speaking, and so forth. Thus, the reasoning of anyone who bars a menstruating woman out of regard for the sanctity of the mosque implies that he

---

## Page 12902

does not regard ritual purity as a condition. Indeed, his position implies that she may circumambulate when there is a need, just as she may enter the mosque when there is a need. Allah Most High commanded that it be purified for those who circumambulate, those who remain there in devotion, and those who bow and prostrate. Ritual purity is not a condition for remaining there in devotion, nor is purification from minor ritual impurity obligatory for such a person, by agreement of the Muslims. If a menstruating woman observing a devotional retreat is compelled by need to remain there, she may do so. As for those who bow and prostrate, they are those who pray, and ritual purity is a condition for prayer by agreement of the Muslims. A menstruating woman neither performs the prayers nor makes them up.

There remains the person circumambulating: should this person be classed with someone remaining in devotion, with someone praying, or as a third category between the two? This is a matter for juristic reasoning.

The statement:

“Circumambulating the House is prayer”

is not authentically established from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. It is, however, established from Ibn ʿAbbās, although it has also been narrated as a saying of the Prophet. Some jurists have transmitted that Ibn ʿAbbās said:

“If someone circumambulates the House while in a state of major ritual impurity, he owes a compensatory sacrifice.”

There is no doubt that the intended meaning is that circumambulation resembles prayer in certain respects, not that it is a form of the prayer for which ritual purity is a condition.

The same applies to his saying:

“When one of you comes to the mosque, he should not interlace his fingers, for he is in prayer,”

and his saying:

“A servant of Allah is in prayer for as long as prayer keeps him there, as long as he awaits prayer, and while he is making his way to prayer,”

and similar statements. Thus, a menstruating woman may not circumambulate except in a state of ritual purity, if that is possible for her, by agreement of

---

## Page 12903

the scholars. If a woman arrives while menstruating, she does not circumambulate the House, but she stands at ʿArafah and performs all the other pilgrimage rites while menstruating, except circumambulation. For that, she waits until she becomes ritually pure, if she can, and then circumambulates. If necessity compels her to circumambulate and she does so, it suffices according to the correct one of the scholars’ two opinions.

After completing the circumambulation, the pilgrim prays two rakʿahs for it. It is better to pray them at the Station of Abraham. It is recommended to recite in them the two surahs expressing sincerity of faith:

“Say, O disbelievers”

and

“Say, He is Allah, One.”

After praying them, it is recommended that he touch the Black Stone and then go out to perform saʿy (traversing between al-Safā and al-Marwah). He may also delay this until after tawāf al-ifādah (the circumambulation of return).

There are three circumambulations in Hajj:

The first is performed upon arrival and is called the circumambulation of arrival, entry, or coming.

The second is performed after the standing at ʿArafah. It is called the circumambulation of return or visitation. This is the obligatory circumambulation that cannot be dispensed with, as Allah Most High says:

“Then let them end their untidiness, fulfil their vows, and circumambulate the Ancient House.”

The third is for anyone intending to leave Mecca: the farewell circumambulation. Performing saʿy immediately after any one of these suffices. When going out for saʿy, the pilgrim leaves through the Gate of al-Safā. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would climb al-Safā and al-Marwah, both of which lie at the side of

---

## Page 12904

Mecca’s two mountains. There he would proclaim Allah’s greatness and oneness and supplicate to Allah Most High. Today, two raised platforms have been built upon them. Anyone who reaches the foot of the structure has performed a valid saʿy, even without climbing onto it.

The pilgrim makes seven traversals between al-Safā and al-Marwah, beginning at al-Safā and ending at al-Marwah. It is recommended to run along the valley floor, from one marker to the other—the two landmarks located there. If he does not run along the valley floor but walks at his usual pace throughout the distance between al-Safā and al-Marwah, his saʿy is valid by scholarly agreement, and nothing further is required of him.

No prayer is prescribed after traversing between al-Safā and al-Marwah. Prayer is prescribed only after circumambulating the House, according to the Sunnah of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the agreement of the early generations and the leading scholars.

Once he has traversed between al-Safā and al-Marwah, he leaves ihrām, just as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded his Companions to leave ihrām after traversing between them. The exception is anyone who has brought a sacrificial animal: he does not leave ihrām until he slaughters it. Those performing Hajj alone, or combining Hajj and ʿUmrah in a single ihrām, do not leave ihrām until the Day of Sacrifice. It is recommended that the pilgrim shorten his hair, leaving shaving for Hajj; this is also what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded them to do. Once he leaves ihrām, everything that ihrām had forbidden him becomes permissible again.

Section

When the Day of al-Tarwiyah arrives, he enters ihrām and recites the talbiyah for Hajj, doing as he did at the

---

## Page 12905

designated boundary for ihrām. He may enter ihrām either in Mecca or outside it; this is the correct view. The Companions of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, entered ihrām from al-Bathā’, as he had instructed them. The Sunnah is to enter ihrām wherever one is staying. Likewise, a resident of Mecca enters ihrām from his home, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever lives short of Mecca enters ihrām from his home; even the people of Mecca enter ihrām from Mecca.”

The Sunnah is for the pilgrims to spend the night at Minā, praying Zuhr, ‘Asr, Maghrib, ‘Ishā’ and Fajr there. They do not leave until sunrise, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did. As for lighting fires, this is a reprehensible innovation by scholarly agreement. Lighting fires is confined to Muzdalifah, after returning from ‘Arafah; lighting them at Minā or ‘Arafah is likewise an innovation.

They proceed from Minā to Namirah along the route of Dabb, to the right of the road. Namirah was a village outside ‘Arafāt, on the right-hand side. They remain there until the sun passes its zenith, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did. They then proceed to the valley floor, where the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed Zuhr and ‘Asr and delivered his sermon. This place lies at the boundary of ‘Arafah, in the valley of ‘Uranah. There is a mosque there called the Mosque of Abraham, which was only built at the beginning of the rule of

---

## Page 12906

the Abbasids.

He prays Zuhr and ‘Asr there, shortening them as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did. All the pilgrims, both the people of Mecca and others, pray behind him, shortening and combining the prayers. The imam delivers a sermon to them, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did while mounted on his camel. When he finishes the sermon, the muezzin gives the adhān and the iqāmah, and then he leads the prayer, as established in the Sunnah.

He shortens the prayers at ‘Arafah, Muzdalifah and Minā, and both the people of Mecca and others shorten them. Likewise, they combine the prayers at ‘Arafah, Muzdalifah and Minā, as the people of Mecca used to do behind the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, at ‘Arafah, Muzdalifah and Minā. They also did so behind Abū Bakr and ‘Umar—may Allah be pleased with them both.

Neither the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor his caliphs instructed any of the people of Mecca to perform the prayers in full. Nor did they tell them at ‘Arafah, Muzdalifah or Minā, “Complete your prayers, for we are travellers.” Whoever attributes this to them is mistaken. Rather, what is reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is that he said this during the campaign of the Conquest, when he led them in prayer in Mecca.

During his Hajj, however, he did not stay in Mecca; rather, he stayed outside it, and there he led his Companions in prayer. When he went out to Minā and ‘Arafah, the people of Mecca and others went with him. When he returned from ‘Arafah, they returned with him, and when he prayed at Minā during the days spent there, they prayed with him. He did not tell them, “Complete your prayers, for we are travellers.” Nor did the Prophet define

---

## Page 12907

may Allah bless him and grant him peace, travel by either distance or duration. No one resided at Minā in his time, which is why he said:

“Minā is a camping ground for whoever arrives first.”

However, it has been said that people settled there during the caliphate of ‘Uthmān, and that this was why ‘Uthmān performed the prayers in full: he considered a traveller to be someone who carries provisions and waterskins.

After that, the pilgrim proceeds to ‘Arafāt. This is the Sunnah. Nowadays, however, hardly anyone goes to Namirah or to the place where the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed. Instead, they enter ‘Arafāt by the al-Ma’zimayn route before the sun passes its zenith. Some enter at night and spend the night there before the standing at ‘Arafah. All these practices leave their Hajj valid, but fall short of the Sunnah. The pilgrim should therefore observe as much of the Sunnah as possible, such as combining the two prayers, giving one adhān and a separate iqāmah for each prayer.

Lighting fires at ‘Arafah is a reprehensible innovation. Likewise, lighting fires at Minā is an innovation by scholarly consensus. Fires are to be lit only at Muzdalifah on the return journey.

They remain at ‘Arafāt until sunset and do not leave before the sun sets. Once it has set, they may leave either between the two markers or on either side of them. The first two markers mark the boundary of ‘Arafah, so they must not pass beyond them until sunset. The two posts farther on mark the boundary of Muzdalifah, and the area between them is the valley of ‘Uranah.

The pilgrim should strive earnestly in remembrance of Allah and supplication that afternoon, for Iblīs has never been seen on

---

## Page 12908

any day more diminished, more contemptible, more enraged or more thwarted than on the afternoon of ‘Arafah, because he sees mercy descending and Allah, glorified be He, pardoning grave sins. The only exception was the day of Badr, when he saw Gabriel arranging the angels in battle ranks.

The standing at ‘Arafah is valid for menstruating women and others alike. It may be observed on foot or mounted. Which is preferable depends on the individual. If people need a person and can see him when he is mounted, or if remaining unmounted would cause him hardship, he should observe the standing mounted, for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did so. The same applies to Hajj: for some people, performing it mounted is better, while for others, performing it on foot is better.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not prescribe any particular supplication or remembrance for ‘Arafah. Rather, a person may offer whatever supplications sanctioned by the religion he wishes. He should also proclaim Allah’s greatness and oneness and continue remembering Allah Most High until sunset.

Bathing for ‘Arafah has been mentioned in a hadith reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and has also been reported from Ibn ‘Umar and others. Only three ritual baths during Hajj have been reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions: bathing before entering ihrām, bathing upon entering Mecca, and bathing on the day of ‘Arafah. Other baths—such as those for stoning the pillars, circumambulating the Ka‘bah, or spending the night at Muzdalifah—have no basis in reports from the Prophet

---

## Page 12909

, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or from his Companions. Nor did the majority of the imams recommend them—neither Mālik, Abū Hanīfah nor Ahmad—even though some later followers of Ahmad mentioned them. Rather, such bathing is an innovation, unless there is a reason that makes it recommended, such as having an odor that offends others and bathing to remove it.

All of ‘Arafah is a place for the standing, but one must not stand in the bed of the ‘Uranah valley. As for climbing the mountain there, this is not part of the Sunnah. It is called the Mount of Mercy and is also known as Ilāl, which rhymes with hilāl. Likewise, it is not recommended to enter or pray inside the dome on its summit, known as the Dome of Adam. Circumambulating it is a major sin. Similarly, it is not recommended to enter or pray in any of the mosques near the stoning pillars. As for circumambulating these, the Rock, the chamber of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or anything other than the Ancient House, this is among the gravest forbidden innovations.

Section

When the pilgrim departs from ‘Arafāt, he should go to the Sacred Monument by the route of al-Ma’zimayn, which is the route people use today. The jurists specify “by the route of al-Ma’zimayn” only because there is another route to ‘Arafah, called the route of Dabb, by which the Prophet entered

---

## Page 12910

—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—into ‘Arafāt, and he departed by the route of al-Ma’zimayn. During the pilgrimage rites and on the Eids, he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would go by one route and return by another. Thus, he entered through the upper mountain pass and left through the lower pass. He entered the Sacred Mosque through the Gate of Banū Shaybah and, after the farewell circumambulation, left through what is now the Gate of Hazwarah. He entered ‘Arafāt by the route of Dabb and departed by the route of al-Ma’zimayn. On the day of Eid, he approached Jamrat al-‘Aqabah by the middle road, which leads outside Minā before turning left towards the stoning pillar. Then, when he returned to his place in Minā, where he slaughtered his sacrificial animals and shaved his head, he took the previously mentioned road, which most people use today.

The pilgrim should delay Maghrib until he prays it together with ‘Ishā’ at Muzdalifah. He should not jostle others, but if he finds an open stretch, he should quicken his pace. Upon reaching Muzdalifah, he should pray Maghrib before making the camels kneel, if possible. Once they have made the camels kneel, they should pray ‘Ishā’. There is no harm in delaying ‘Ishā’.

He should spend the night at Muzdalifah. The whole of Muzdalifah is called the Sacred Monument, and it extends from the two passes of ‘Arafah to the bed of the Muhassir valley. Between every two sacred sites lies a boundary area belonging to neither: between ‘Arafah and Muzdalifah lies the bed of the ‘Uranah valley, and between Muzdalifah and Minā lies the bed of the Muhassir valley.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “All of ‘Arafah is a place for the standing, but stay clear of the bed of the ‘Uranah valley; and all of Muzdalifah

---

## Page 12911

is a place for the standing, but stay clear of the bed of the Muhassir valley. All of Minā is a place for slaughter, and all the mountain passes of Mecca are routes.”

The Sunnah is to spend the night at Muzdalifah until dawn, pray Fajr there at the beginning of its time, and then stand at the Sacred Monument until daylight is very bright, before sunrise. Those who are weak, such as women, children, and others like them, may leave Muzdalifah early for Minā once the moon has set. Those who are strong should not leave Muzdalifah until dawn has broken, they have prayed Fajr there, and they have stood there. All of Muzdalifah is a place for the standing, but standing at Quzah is preferable. This is Mount al-Mīqadah, where people stand today. A structure has been built upon it, and this is the particular place that many jurists call the Sacred Monument.

Before sunrise, he should depart from Muzdalifah for Minā. Upon reaching Muhassir, he should quicken his pace for the distance of a stone’s throw. When he reaches Minā, he should throw seven pebbles at Jamrat al-‘Aqabah, raising his hand as he throws. This is the last of the stoning pillars when approaching from Minā and the nearest to Mecca; it is the largest stoning pillar. On the Day of Sacrifice, he should stone no other pillar. He should face it as he throws, with the House on his left and Minā on his right. This is the manner authentically reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concerning it. It is recommended to say “Allah is the Greatest” with each pebble. If he wishes, he may also say:

“O Allah, make this an accepted pilgrimage and a rewarded endeavour, and grant forgiveness of sin.”

And he raises both hands

---

## Page 12912

while throwing. He continues reciting the talbiyah as he travels from one ritual site to another, such as when going to ‘Arafāt and from ‘Arafāt to Muzdalifah, until he stones Jamrat al-‘Aqabah. Once he begins throwing, he stops the talbiyah, for he is then beginning the process of release from ihram.

The scholars hold three views concerning the talbiyah. Some say that he stops reciting it upon reaching ‘Arafāt. Others say that he continues reciting it at ‘Arafāt and elsewhere until he stones the pillar. The third view is that he recites it when departing from ‘Arafāt for Muzdalifah, and when departing from Muzdalifah for Minā, until he stones Jamrat al-‘Aqabah. This is what has been authentically reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Section

As for reciting the talbiyah during the standing at ‘Arafāt and Muzdalifah, this has not been reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. It has, however, been reported that the Rightly Guided Caliphs and others used to recite it at ‘Arafāt.

After stoning Jamrat al-‘Aqabah, he slaughters his sacrificial animal, if he has one with him. It is recommended that camels be slaughtered standing, facing the qiblah, with the left foreleg tethered. As for cattle, sheep, and goats, he lays them on their left sides, facing the qiblah, and says:

“In the name of Allah, and Allah is the Greatest. O Allah, this is from You and for You. O Allah, accept it from me as You accepted it from

---

## Page 12913

Abraham, Your close friend.”

Any animal slaughtered at Minā after being brought into the Sacred Precinct from outside its boundaries is a sacrificial offering, whether a camel, a cow, a sheep, or a goat. It is also called an Eid sacrifice. By contrast, an animal slaughtered outside the Sacred Precinct on the Day of Sacrifice is an Eid sacrifice, but not a sacrificial offering. At Minā, there is no Eid sacrifice that is not also a sacrificial offering, unlike in other towns.

If he buys the sacrificial animal at ‘Arafāt and drives it to Minā, it is a sacrificial offering by scholarly agreement. The same applies if he buys it within the Sacred Precinct and takes it to al-Tan‘īm. However, if he buys the animal at Minā and slaughters it there, the scholars differ. Mālik held that it is not a sacrificial offering, a view also reported from Ibn ‘Umar. The three imams hold that it is a sacrificial offering, a view also reported from ‘Ā’ishah.

He may collect the pebbles wherever he wishes, but he must not use pebbles that have already been thrown. It is recommended that they be larger than a chickpea but smaller than a hazelnut. Breaking stones into pebbles is permissible, though gathering loose pebbles is preferable to breaking them from the mountainside.

He then shaves his head or shortens his hair; shaving is preferable. If he shortens it, he gathers the hair and cuts off a fingertip’s length, or less or more. A woman must not cut off more than that, whereas a man may shorten his hair as much as he wishes.

Once he has done this, he has attained the first stage of release from ihram, by agreement of the Muslims. He may then wear ordinary clothing and trim his nails. According to the sound view, he is also permitted to apply perfume, to marry, and to

---

## Page 12914

hunt. The only remaining restrictions are those concerning women.

After that, he enters Mecca and performs tawāf al-ifādah, the main circumambulation of Hajj, on the Day of Sacrifice if possible. Otherwise, he performs it later, though it should be during the days of Tashrīq, since delaying it beyond those days is a matter of scholarly disagreement. He then performs the sa‘y of Hajj between al-Safā and al-Marwah. A pilgrim performing ifrād (Hajj alone) need only perform sa‘y once. According to the majority of scholars, the same applies to one performing qirān (Hajj and ‘umrah combined in a single ihram). It also applies to one performing tamattu‘ (‘umrah followed by Hajj, with release from ihram between them), according to the soundest scholarly view. This is also the sounder of the two reports from Ahmad: he need only perform sa‘y once. The Companions who performed tamattu‘ with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, only went between al-Safā and al-Marwah once, before the standing at ‘Arafāt.

Thus, if the pilgrim performing tamattu‘ relies on his first sa‘y, that suffices, just as it suffices for those performing ifrād and qirān. ‘Abd Allāh ibn Ahmad ibn Hanbal likewise said:

“My father was asked, ‘How many times does a pilgrim performing tamattu‘ perform sa‘y between al-Safā and al-Marwah?’

“He replied, ‘If he makes the circuits twice—meaning around the House and between al-Safā and al-Marwah—that is better. If he makes them once, there is no harm; but I prefer that he make them twice.’”

Ahmad said: “Al-Walīd ibn Muslim narrated to us: al-Awzā‘ī narrated to us, from ‘Atā’, from Ibn ‘Abbās, that he used to say: ‘For a pilgrim performing ifrād or tamattu‘, one tawāf around the House and one sa‘y between al-Safā and al-Marwah suffice.’”

Scholars have differed concerning what the Companions who performed tamattu‘ with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did upon returning from ‘Arafah, although everyone agrees that they had initially gone around the House and between al-Safā and al-Marwah. One view is that they also performed sa‘y after tawāf al-ifādah.

---

## Page 12915

and another view is that they did not perform sa‘y again. This is what is established in Sahīh Muslim, where Jābir said: “The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions went between al-Safā and al-Marwah only once, on their first occasion.”

It is reported in the hadith of ‘Ā’ishah that they went twice. However, this additional wording has been said to be a statement of al-Zuhrī, not of ‘Ā’ishah. Some have cited it as evidence that performing tawāf around the House twice is recommended, but this is a weak argument. The more evident conclusion is what is stated in Jābir’s hadith. This is further supported by the Prophet’s statement:

“‘Umrah has become incorporated into Hajj until the Day of Resurrection.”

Thus, from the moment a pilgrim performing tamattu‘ enters ihram for ‘umrah, he has entered into Hajj. However, an interval of release from ihram separates the two rites to make matters easier for the pilgrim. The religion most beloved to Allah is the upright monotheistic faith marked by ease.

It is not recommended for a pilgrim performing tamattu‘, or any other pilgrim, to perform the arrival tawāf after the standing at ‘Arafāt. Rather, this tawāf is the prescribed practice for him, as the Companions did with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Once he has performed tawāf al-ifādah, everything becomes lawful for him again, including marital relations and everything else.

There is no Eid prayer at Minā. Rather, stoning Jamrat al-‘Aqabah serves for the pilgrims as the Eid prayer does for the residents of towns. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed neither the Friday prayer nor the Eid prayer while travelling, whether in Mecca or at ‘Arafah. His sermon at ‘Arafah was a sermon concerning the pilgrimage rites, not a Friday sermon, and he did not recite aloud in the prayer at ‘Arafah.

---

## Page 12916

Section

He then returns to Minā, spends the night there, and stones the three jamrahs each day after the sun passes its zenith, beginning with the first jamrah, which is closest to the Mosque of al-Khayf. It is recommended that he walk there and throw seven pebbles at it.

It is recommended that he say “Allah is the Greatest” with each pebble. If he wishes, he may say:

“O Allah, grant me an accepted Hajj, reward my efforts, and forgive my sins.”

After stoning it, it is recommended that he move a little forward to a place where the pebbles will not strike him, then supplicate to Allah Most High, facing the qiblah with his hands raised, for as long as it takes to recite Sūrat al-Baqarah. He then goes to the second jamrah and stones it in the same manner, then moves forward to his left and supplicates as he did at the first. He then stones the third, Jamrat al-‘Aqabah, likewise throwing seven pebbles at it, but does not stop there to supplicate.

On the second of the days at Minā, he stones the jamrahs as he did on the first. Then, if he wishes, he may stone them on the third day, which is preferable. Alternatively, he may himself leave early on the second day, before sunset, as Allah Most High says:

“Whoever hastens to leave in two days

---

## Page 12917

incurs no sin”—and so on to the end of the verse. If the sun sets while he is still in Minā, he must remain until he stones the jamrahs with the people on the third day. The imam who oversees the people’s pilgrimage rites should not leave early; rather, the Sunnah is for him to remain until the third day. It is also the Sunnah for the imam to lead the people in prayer at Minā, and for those attending the pilgrimage to pray behind him.

It is recommended not to miss praying with the imam in the mosque of Minā, namely the Mosque of al-Khayf. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, Abū Bakr and ‘Umar used to lead the people in shortened prayers at Minā without combining them. Everyone praying behind them shortened their prayers, whether they were residents of Mecca or not. It was only when he led them in prayer in Mecca itself that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, is reported to have said:

“O people of Mecca, complete your prayers, for we are travellers.”

If there is no imam leading everyone, a man should lead his companions in prayer. The mosque was built after the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him; it did not exist in his lifetime.

When leaving Minā, it is good to spend the night at al-Muhassab—also known as al-Abtah, the area between the two mountains extending to the cemetery—and then depart, for the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, spent the night there and then left. He did not stay in Mecca after returning from Minā, but bade farewell to the House and said:

“Let no one depart until his last act is a visit to the House.”

Thus, the pilgrim must not leave until he has bidden farewell to the House by performing the farewell tawaf, so that it will be

---

## Page 12918

his last visit to the House. Anyone who remains in Mecca need not perform the farewell tawaf. A person departing from Mecca should delay this tawaf until he has completed all his affairs, and should not engage in trade or similar activities afterwards. However, if he attends to a need, buys something along his way after the farewell tawaf, or returns to his lodging to load his belongings onto his mount, or does something similar in preparation for departure, he need not repeat it. If he stays on after the farewell tawaf, he must repeat it. This tawaf is obligatory according to the majority of scholars, but menstruating women are exempt.

If he wishes, he may go to the Multazam—the area between the Black Stone and the door—and press his chest, face, forearms and palms against it, supplicating and asking Allah Most High for what he needs. He may also do this before the farewell tawaf, for there is no distinction between doing so at the time of departure and at any other time. The Companions used to do this when they entered Mecca. If he wishes, he may offer the supplication transmitted from Ibn ‘Abbās:

“O Allah, I am Your servant, the son of Your servant and Your maidservant. You have carried me on what You have placed at my service among Your creatures and enabled me to travel through Your lands, until, by Your grace, You brought me to Your House and helped me perform my pilgrimage rites. If You are pleased with me, increase Your pleasure with me; otherwise, be pleased with me from this moment, before my dwelling becomes distant from Your House. Now is the time for my departure, if You permit me, neither seeking a substitute for You or Your House nor turning away from You or Your House. O Allah, grant me well-being in my body and sound health in

---

## Page 12919

my body, protection in my religion, and a good return. Enable me to obey You for as long as You keep me alive, and grant me the good of both this world and the Hereafter. Surely You have power over all things.”

If he stands at the door and supplicates there without pressing himself against the House, that is also good. When he turns to leave, he should not stop, look back, or walk backwards. Al-Tha‘labī says in Fiqh al-Lughah (The Study of Language): “Al-qahqarā means walking backwards.” It has even been said that if he sees the House, he should return and bid it farewell. Likewise, after greeting the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he should not turn away or walk backwards; rather, he should leave as people leave mosques after prayer.

The rites of someone combining Hajj and ‘umrah are no more than those of someone performing Hajj alone. However, both the former and someone performing tamattu‘—‘umrah followed by Hajj, with a release from ihram between them—must offer a sacrificial animal: a camel, a cow, a sheep, or a share in a sacrificial animal. Anyone unable to obtain a sacrificial animal must fast three days before the Day of Sacrifice and seven upon returning. According to the strongest scholarly opinion, he may fast the three days from the time he enters ihram for ‘umrah.

Three positions are transmitted from Ahmad on this matter: that he may fast them before entering ihram for ‘umrah; that he may fast them only after entering ihram for Hajj; and that he may fast them from the time he enters ihram for ‘umrah. The last is the strongest view. It has also been said that he may fast them after leaving ihram upon completing ‘umrah, for by then he has embarked upon Hajj. ‘Umrah, however, has been incorporated into Hajj just as ablution is incorporated into the full ritual bath. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“‘Umrah has become part of Hajj until the Day of Resurrection.”

The Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, were performing tamattu‘ with him, and they only

---

## Page 12920

entered ihram for Hajj.

It is recommended to drink Zamzam water to one’s fill and, while drinking, to offer whatever supplications one wishes that accord with Islamic teachings. Bathing with it, however, is not recommended.

As for visiting mosques built in Mecca other than the Sacred Mosque—such as the mosque below al-Safā, those on the slopes of Abū Qubays, and similar mosques built at sites associated with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions, including the Mosque of the Birthplace and others—deliberately seeking out any of these is not part of the Sunnah, nor did any of the imams recommend it. What is prescribed is to visit the Sacred Mosque specifically, along with the sacred sites: ‘Arafah, Muzdalifah, al-Safā, and al-Marwah.

Likewise, seeking out mountains and places around Mecca other than the sacred sites of ‘Arafah, Muzdalifah, and Minā—such as Mount Hirā’, the mountain near Minā where the Dome of Ransom is said to have stood, and similar places—is not part of the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Rather, visiting them is a religious innovation. The same applies to mosques built along the roads at historical sites, and to places said to have such associations: the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not prescribe visiting any of these places in particular, nor visiting any of them at all.

Entering the Ka‘bah is neither obligatory nor an emphatically recommended Sunnah, though entering it is good. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not enter it during Hajj or ‘umrah

---

## Page 12921

—neither the ‘umrah from al-Ji‘rānah nor the make-up ‘umrah. He entered it only in the year of the conquest of Mecca.

Whoever enters it is encouraged to pray inside, proclaim Allah’s greatness, supplicate to Him, and remember Him. Upon entering through the door, one should proceed until three cubits remain between oneself and the wall, with the door behind. That is where the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed. One should enter it only barefoot.

Most of the Hijr enclosure, from where its wall curves, forms part of the House, so whoever enters it is like someone who has entered the Ka‘bah. Entering the Ka‘bah entails no obligations beyond those incumbent upon other pilgrims. Walking barefoot and other things permissible for other pilgrims remain permissible for such a person.

Performing tawāf around the House frequently is a righteous deed. It is better than leaving the Sacred Precinct to perform an additional ‘umrah from Mecca. This was not the practice of the earliest Emigrants and Helpers, nor did the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, encourage his community to do it. Rather, the early generations disapproved of it.

Section

When one enters Medina before or after Hajj, one should go to the Prophet’s Mosque, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and pray there. Prayer there is better than a thousand prayers elsewhere, except in the Sacred Mosque. Journeys are not to be undertaken except to it and to the Sacred Mosque

---

## Page 12922

and to al-Aqsā Mosque. This is established in the two Sahīh collections through the hadiths of Abū Hurayrah and Abū Sa‘īd, and is also transmitted through other chains. His mosque was smaller than it is today, as was the Sacred Mosque, but the Rightly Guided Caliphs and those who came after them expanded both. The extensions have the same status as the original buildings in all rulings.

One should then greet the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his two companions, for he said:

“No man greets me without Allah restoring my soul to me so that I may return his greeting.”

This was narrated by Abū Dāwūd and others. When ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar entered the mosque, he would say: “Peace be upon you, O Messenger of Allah. Peace be upon you, O Abū Bakr. Peace be upon you, O my father.” Then he would leave. This was how the Companions would greet him. According to most scholars, including Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad, one greets him while facing the chamber, with one’s back to the qiblah.

Abū Hanīfah said that one should face the qiblah. Some of his followers said that one should turn one’s back to the chamber, while others said that one should keep it to one’s left. They agreed that one should neither touch nor kiss the chamber, circumambulate it, nor pray facing it.

If one says in greeting him: “Peace be upon you, O Messenger of Allah, O Prophet of Allah, O Allah’s chosen one among His creation, O most honored of creation in the sight of his Lord, O leader of the God-fearing,” all these are among his attributes—may my father and mother be his ransom, and may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Likewise, invoking blessings upon him along with greeting him with peace is something Allah has commanded.

---

## Page 12923

and one should not supplicate there while facing the chamber, for all of this is forbidden by agreement of the imams. Mālik was among the imams most strongly opposed to it. The account claiming that he instructed al-Mansūr to face the chamber when supplicating is falsely attributed to Mālik.

One should not stand at the grave to supplicate for oneself, for this is an innovation. None of the Companions would stand there supplicating for themselves; rather, they would face the qiblah and supplicate in his mosque. For he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“O Allah, do not make my grave an idol that is worshipped.”

He also said:

“Do not make my grave a place of festivity, and do not turn your homes into graves. Invoke blessings upon me wherever you are, for your blessings reach me.”

He also said:

“Invoke blessings upon me abundantly on Friday and the night preceding it, for your blessings are presented to me.”

They asked: “How will our blessings be presented to you when you have decayed?”—meaning, when your body has decomposed. He replied:

“Allah has forbidden the earth to consume the bodies of the prophets.”

He thus stated that he hears blessings and greetings of peace from those nearby, and that these reach him from those far away.

He also said:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians; they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

He was warning against what they had done. ‘Ā’ishah said: “Were it not for that, his grave would have been left exposed, but he disliked its being made a place of worship.” This was recorded in both Sahīh collections.
