The Companions therefore buried him where he had died, in ‘Ā’ishah’s chamber. Her chamber and all the other chambers were outside the mosque, on its southern and eastern sides. However, during the reign of al-Walīd ibn ‘Abd al-Malik, this mosque and others were renovated. His governor in Medina was ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-‘Azīz, and he ordered that the chambers be purchased and that an extension be made

---

## Page 12924

to the mosque. The chamber thus became part of the mosque from that time onward. It was built at an angle to the qiblah, with a pointed shape, so that no one would pray facing it. For he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not sit on graves or pray facing them.”

Muslim narrated this from Abū Marthad al-Ghanawī. And Allah knows best.

Visiting graves is of two kinds: visits prescribed by Islam and visits that constitute religious innovation.

The purpose of a prescribed visit is to greet the deceased with peace and supplicate for him, just as one does when offering the funeral prayer over him. Visiting him after his death is thus akin to offering the funeral prayer over him. The Sunnah is to greet the deceased with peace and supplicate for him, whether he is a prophet or anyone else. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to instruct his Companions, when they visited graves, that each of them should say:

“Peace be upon you, inhabitants of these dwellings, believers and Muslims. We shall, if Allah wills, join you. May Allah have mercy on those among us and you who have gone before and those who come after. We ask Allah for well-being for ourselves and for you. O Allah, do not deprive us of our reward through them, do not put us to trial after them, and forgive us and them.”

This is likewise what one says when visiting the people buried in al-Baqī‘, including the Companions and others, or when visiting the martyrs of Uhud and others. None of the imams of the Muslims considers prayer beside their graves, or anyone else’s graves, recommended. Rather, prayer in mosques that contain no grave of any prophet, righteous person, or anyone else is better than prayer in mosques that contain such graves, by the agreement of the imams of

---

## Page 12925

the Muslims; rather, prayer in mosques built over graves is either forbidden or disliked.

A visit that constitutes religious innovation is one in which the visitor intends to ask the deceased to fulfil his needs, deliberately seeks to supplicate at his grave, or intends to invoke Allah through him. This is not part of the Sunnah of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor did any of the early generations or imams of this Ummah consider it recommended. Rather, it is among the prohibited innovations, by agreement of the early generations and imams of this Ummah.

Mālik and others disliked a person saying, “I visited the grave of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.” This wording has not been transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Rather, the hadiths cited on this subject, such as the saying attributed to him:

“Whoever visits me and my father Abraham in a single year, I guarantee him Paradise from Allah,”

and the saying:

“Whoever visits me after my death, it is as though he visited me during my lifetime; and whoever visits me after my death will be entitled to my intercession,”

and similar reports, are all weak hadiths—indeed, fabricated. They are not found in any of the authoritative hadith collections of Islam, nor were they transmitted by any of the imams of the Muslims, neither the four imams nor others. However, al-Bazzār, al-Dāraqutnī and others like them narrated some of these reports through weak chains of transmission. For it is the practice of al-Dāraqutnī and others like him to include such reports in their Sunan collections so that they may be known; he and others explain the weakness of those reports that are weak.

Thus, if these practices involving associating partners with Allah and religious innovation are forbidden at his grave, although he is the best of creation, then their prohibition at anyone else’s grave is all the more warranted and appropriate.

---

## Page 12926

It is also recommended to go to Qubā Mosque and pray there, for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever purifies himself at home, doing so thoroughly, then comes to Qubā Mosque intending only to pray there, will have a reward like that of an ‘umrah.”

Narrated by Ahmad, al-Nasā’ī and Ibn Mājah. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, also said:

“Prayer in Qubā Mosque is like an ‘umrah.”

Al-Tirmidhī graded it good.

Travelling to al-Aqsā Mosque, praying there, supplicating, remembering Allah, reciting the Quran and observing a devotional retreat are recommended at any time one wishes, whether in the year one performs Hajj or afterwards. Nothing should be done there or in the Mosque of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, except what is done in other mosques. There is nothing in them against which one should rub oneself, or which one should kiss or circumambulate. All these practices are exclusive to the Sacred Mosque. Visiting the Rock is not recommended; rather, what is recommended is to pray in the southern part of al-Aqsā Mosque, which ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb built for the Muslims.

No one should travel to perform the ritual standing anywhere other than ‘Arafāt, nor travel to perform it at al-Aqsā Mosque or at anyone’s grave, whether that of a prophet, a shaykh or anyone else. This is agreed upon by the Muslims. Indeed, the stronger of the two scholarly views is that no one should travel to visit any grave. Rather, graves are to be visited in the prescribed manner by those who are nearby and those passing

---

## Page 12927

by them, just as Qubā Mosque is visited from Madinah. No one may undertake a journey specifically to visit it, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade undertaking such journeys except to the three mosques.

This is because the religion rests upon two foundations: that none be worshipped except Allah alone, without partner, and that He be worshipped only as He has prescribed, not through religious innovations. As the Most High says:

“So whoever hopes to meet his Lord, let him do righteous deeds and associate no one in the worship of his Lord.”

For this reason, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, would say in his supplication:

“O Allah, make all my deeds righteous, make them sincerely for Your sake, and let no one else have any share in them.”

Commenting on the words of the Most High, “To test you as to which of you is best in deeds,” al-Fudayl ibn ‘Iyād said: “The most sincere and the most correct.”

He was asked: “Abū ‘Alī, what does ‘the most sincere and the most correct’ mean?”

He replied: “If a deed is sincere but not correct, it will not be accepted. If it is correct but not sincere, it will not be accepted, until it is both sincere and correct. Sincerity means that it is done for Allah, and correctness means that it accords with the Sunnah.”

Allah Most High has said:

“Or do they have partners who have ordained for them a religion that Allah has not authorized?”

The purpose of all acts of worship is that all devotion should be for Allah alone. Allah is the One worshipped and petitioned, the One who is feared and hoped in, asked and worshipped. All devotion belongs sincerely to Him, and to Him all those in the heavens and the earth have submitted, willingly or unwillingly. The Quran is full of this. As the Most High says:

“The revelation of this Book is from Allah, the Almighty, the All-Wise.”

---

## Page 12928

“Indeed, We have sent down the Book to you in truth, so worship Allah, devoting your religion sincerely to Him.”

“Surely, sincere devotion belongs to Allah alone.”

Up to His words:

“Say: It is Allah I worship, devoting my religion sincerely to Him.”

And up to His words:

“Say: Is it other than Allah that you command me to worship, you ignorant people?”

The Most High also says:

“It is not for any human being to whom Allah has given the Scripture, judgment and prophethood to then say to people: ‘Be servants of mine rather than of Allah’”—this verse and the next.

The Most High also says:

“Say: Call upon those whom you claim besides Him. They have no power to remove harm from you”—this verse and the next.

A group of the early generations said: “Some people used to invoke the angels and the prophets, such as the Messiah and ‘Uzayr, so Allah Most High revealed this verse.”

The Most High also says:

“They say: ‘The Most Merciful has taken a child.’ Glory be to Him! Rather, they are honoured servants.”

“They do not speak before He speaks”—and the subsequent verses.

There is much like this in the Quran. Indeed, this is the Quran’s central purpose and its very essence. It is the purpose of the call of all the messengers, and for this creation was brought into being, as the Most High says:

“I did not create jinn and mankind except to worship Me.”

The Muslim must therefore understand that Hajj belongs to the same category as prayer and similar acts of worship, through which Allah alone, without partner, is worshipped. Funeral prayers and visits to the graves of the dead belong to the category of supplication for them. Supplication for created beings is a form of kindness and benevolence, belonging to the same category as zakat.

The acts of worship Allah has commanded embody worship of Him alone and conformity to the Sunnah, whereas other forms include associating partners with Allah

---

## Page 12929

and religious innovation, as in the worship of Christians and those who resemble them—for example, seeking out a particular site for purposes other than the acts of worship Allah has commanded. This is not part of the religion. Hence, the leading scholars counted travelling to visit the graves of prophets and righteous people among recurring religious innovations. According to the sounder of the two views, this is not prescribed. Some who held this view even explicitly stated that anyone undertaking such a journey may not shorten the prayers during it, because it is a journey undertaken in disobedience to Allah.

Likewise, seeking out a site associated with a created being, such as a grave or a shrine, in order to petition that being, seek his protection, or do anything similar, constitutes associating partners with Allah and religious innovation. This is what Christians and those innovators within this community who resemble them do: they treat Hajj and prayer as belonging to the same category as their practices of associating partners with Allah and introducing religious innovations.

Thus, when some of his wives mentioned to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, a church in Abyssinia, describing its beauty and the images it contained, he said:

“When a righteous man among those people died, they would build a place of worship over his grave and make those images in it. They will be the worst of creation before Allah on the Day of Resurrection.”

For this reason, scholars forbade practices involving worship of anyone other than Allah and petitioning deceased prophets or righteous people. Examples include writing a petition and hanging it beside the grave of a prophet or righteous person, prostrating to a grave, calling upon it, or directing one’s entreaties to it.

They also stated that building mosques over graves is impermissible, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said five nights before he died:

“Indeed, those who came before you used to take

---

## Page 12930

graves as places of worship. Beware! Do not take graves as places of worship, for I forbid you to do so.” Reported by Muslim.

He also said:

“If I were to take anyone on earth as an intimate friend, I would take Abū Bakr as an intimate friend.”

These hadiths are found in the authentic collections. What some people do—eating dates in the mosque or hanging hair in the lamps—is a blameworthy religious innovation.

It is permissible to take some Zamzam water away, as the early generations used to do. As for Sayhānī dates, they have no special merit. Other varieties, such as Barnī and ‘Ajwa, are better. The hadiths transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concern only such varieties, as in the authentic report:

“Whoever eats seven ‘Ajwa dates in the morning will not be harmed that day by poison or sorcery.”

Nothing has been transmitted from him concerning Sayhānī dates. Some people’s claim that they called out to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, stems from ignorance. Rather, they were named for their dryness, for dates are said to have undergone tasawwuh when they have dried out. This resembles the claim of some ignorant people that the spring of al-Zarqā came with him from Mecca. During the Prophet’s lifetime, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, there was no flowing spring in Medina—neither al-Zarqā, nor the springs of Hamza, nor any others. All of these were tapped after his time.

Raising one’s voice in mosques is forbidden. It is established that Umar ibn al-Khattāb

---

## Page 12931

—may Allah be pleased with him—saw two men raising their voices in the mosque and said:

“If I knew that you were residents of this city, I would give you a painful beating. Voices must not be raised in his mosque.”

Thus, what some ignorant members of the public do after prayer—calling out loudly, “Peace be upon you, O Messenger of Allah”—is among the most reprehensible practices. None of the early generations did anything of this kind after concluding the prayer, whether loudly or quietly. Rather, what is prescribed is the worshipper’s saying during the prayer, “Peace be upon you, O Prophet, and the mercy and blessings of Allah.” Likewise, invoking blessings upon him is prescribed at every time and in every place.

It is authentically reported that he said:

“Whoever invokes blessings upon me once, Allah will bless him ten times in return.”

The Musnad reports:

“A man said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, shall I devote a third of my supplication to invoking blessings upon you?’ He replied, ‘Then Allah will take care of a third of your affairs.’ He said, ‘Shall I devote two-thirds of my supplication to invoking blessings upon you?’ He replied, ‘Then Allah will take care of two-thirds of your affairs.’ He said, ‘Shall I devote all my supplication to invoking blessings upon you?’ He replied, ‘Then Allah will take care of whatever concerns you in the affairs of this world and the Hereafter.’”

The Sunan collections report that he said:

“Do not make my grave a place of recurring gatherings. Invoke blessings upon me wherever you may be, for your blessings reach me.”

Abdullāh ibn Hasan, the leading elder among the descendants of al-Hasan in his time, saw a man repeatedly visiting the grave of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to supplicate there. He said: Sir, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Do not make my grave a place of recurring gatherings. Invoke blessings upon me wherever you may be, for

---

## Page 12932

your blessings reach me.” Thus, you and a man in al-Andalus are alike.

For this reason, the early generations frequently invoked blessings and peace upon him at all times and in all places. They did not gather at his grave to recite the entire Quran, light candles, provide food and drink, recite odes, or do anything similar. Rather, these are religious innovations. Instead, they practised in his mosque what is prescribed in all other mosques: prayer, recitation, remembrance of Allah, supplication, devotional retreat, teaching and studying the Quran and religious knowledge, and similar activities.

They knew that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, receives a reward equal to that of every righteous deed performed by his community, for he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever calls others to guidance will receive a reward equal to the rewards of those who follow him, without diminishing their rewards in the least.”

He is the one who called his community to every good. Thus, whatever good anyone in his community does, he receives an equal reward. He therefore had no need for anyone to dedicate to him the reward of a prayer, charity, or recitation, since he receives a reward equal to theirs for what they do, without diminishing their rewards in the least.

The more obedient a person is to him and the more closely he follows him, the closer he is to him in this world and the Hereafter. Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘This is my way: I call to Allah with clear insight—I and those who follow me.’”

And he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The family of Abū so-and-so are not my

---

## Page 12933

allies. My only allies are Allah and the righteous believers.”

He has a greater claim over every believer than the believer has over himself. He is the intermediary between Allah and His creation in conveying His commands, prohibitions, promises, and warnings. What he declares lawful is lawful, what he declares forbidden is forbidden, and religion is what he prescribes. Allah alone is worshipped, petitioned, and called upon for help; He alone is feared, hoped in, and relied upon.

Allah Most High says:

“Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger, fears Allah, and is mindful of Him—it is they who will triumph.”

He thus makes obedience due to both Allah and the Messenger, as He says:

“Whoever obeys the Messenger has indeed obeyed Allah.”

But He reserves reverent fear and mindfulness for Allah alone, who has no partner. Allah Most High says:

“If only they had been content with what Allah and His Messenger gave them and had said, ‘Allah is sufficient for us. Allah will give us of His bounty, and so will His Messenger. To Allah alone we turn in hope.’”

He thus attributes giving to both Allah and the Messenger, as He says:

“Whatever the Messenger gives you, take it; and whatever he forbids you, refrain from it.”

No one may therefore take anything except what the Messenger has permitted, even if Allah has granted it to him through His power and sovereignty. For He grants sovereignty to whomever He wills and takes sovereignty away from whomever He wills. Thus, when standing upright after bowing and after concluding the prayer with the salutation, he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would say:

“O Allah, none can withhold what You give, and none can give what You withhold; nor can worldly fortune avail its possessor against You.”

That is, if You grant someone worldly fortune—good fortune, wealth, and power—nothing will save him from You except faith and piety. As for reliance, it is upon Allah alone, and longing is directed to Him alone, as He says:

---

## Page 12934

exalted is He:

“And they said, ‘Allah is sufficient for us.’”

He did not say, “and His Messenger.” And they said:

“To Allah alone we turn in hope.”

They did not add “and His Messenger” here, as He did when speaking of giving. Rather, this resembles His words:

“So when you have finished, strive diligently, and to your Lord alone turn in longing.”

Allah Most High says:

“Those to whom people said, ‘The people have gathered against you, so fear them,’ but this only increased their faith, and they said, ‘Allah is sufficient for us, and He is the best Trustee.’”

In Sahīh al-Bukhārī, Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have said: “‘Allah is sufficient for us, and He is the best Trustee’ was said by Abraham when he was cast into the fire, and by Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, when people told them, ‘The people have gathered against you, so fear them.’ This only increased their faith, and they said, ‘Allah is sufficient for us, and He is the best Trustee.’”

Allah Most High also says:

“O Prophet, Allah is sufficient for you and for the believers who follow you.”

That is, Allah alone is sufficient for you and for the believers who follow you. Whoever says, “Allah and the believers are sufficient for you,” has gone astray; indeed, his statement is of the kind made by the disbelievers. Allah alone is sufficient for everyone who believes in Him. To be sufficient means to meet every need, as Allah Most High says:

“Is Allah not sufficient for His servant?”

Allah Most High has a right in which no created being shares: worship, sincere devotion, reliance, fear, hope, pilgrimage, prayer, zakāh, fasting, and charity.

The Messenger also has rights: believing in him, obeying him, following his sunnah, befriending those he befriends and opposing those he opposes, and loving him more than one’s family, wealth, and oneself. As he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “By Him in whose hand is my soul,

---

## Page 12935

none of you believes until I am dearer to him than his child, his father, and all mankind.” Indeed, the jihad he commanded must take precedence over all these, as Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘If your fathers, your sons, your brothers, your spouses, your clan, the wealth you have acquired, the trade whose decline you fear, and the homes in which you delight are dearer to you than Allah, His Messenger, and striving in His cause, then wait until Allah brings about His command. Allah does not guide the defiantly disobedient people.’”

Allah Most High also says:

“But Allah and His Messenger have a greater right to be pleased, if they are believers.”

An expanded treatment and explanation of the contents of this brief work may be found elsewhere. Allah, glorified and exalted is He, knows best. May Allah bless our master Muhammad, his family, and his Companions, and grant them peace. All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

---

## Page 12936

He also said—may Allah sanctify his soul:

Section: Hajj

As for Hajj, they followed the established teachings of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concerning its performance and rulings.

It is established through mass-transmitted reports known to specialist hadith scholars, related through numerous channels in the two Sahīh collections and elsewhere, that:

“When he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed the Farewell Pilgrimage, he and the Muslims entered ihrām (ritual consecration) at Dhū al-Hulayfah. He said, ‘Whoever wishes to enter ihrām for ʿumrah may do so; whoever wishes to enter ihrām for Hajj may do so; and whoever wishes to enter ihrām for both ʿumrah and Hajj may do so.’”

When they arrived, circumambulated the House, and went between al-Safā and al-Marwah, he instructed all the Muslims who had performed Hajj with him to leave ihrām and make their pilgrimage an ʿumrah instead, except those who had brought sacrificial animals: they were not to leave ihrām until the sacrificial animals reached their appointed place of sacrifice. Some of them questioned him about this, and he became angry. He said:

“Consider what I have commanded you to do, and do it.”

He himself, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had brought sacrificial animals, so he did not leave ihrām.

---

## Page 12937

And when he saw that some of them were reluctant to leave ihrām, he said:

“Had I known beforehand what I now know, I would not have brought sacrificial animals and would have made it an ʿumrah. Were it not for the sacrificial animals I have with me, I would leave ihrām.”

He also said:

“I have matted my hair and garlanded my sacrificial animals, so I will not leave ihrām until I have slaughtered them.”

All the Muslims therefore left ihrām except the few who had brought sacrificial animals, among them the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib, and Talhah ibn ʿUbayd Allāh.

On the Day of al-Tarwiyah, those who had left ihrām entered ihrām for Hajj as they made their way to Minā. He spent that night with them at Minā and led them there in the Zuhr, ʿAsr, Maghrib, ʿIshāʾ, and Fajr prayers. He then proceeded with them to Namirah by way of Dabb. Namirah lies outside ʿUranah, to its south and west; it is neither within the Sanctuary nor part of ʿArafah. A tent was pitched for him at Namirah, where his rightly guided successors also used to camp after him. There were markets there, and it was where people relieved themselves, ate, and attended to similar needs.

When the sun passed its zenith, he mounted his riding animal, as did those who rode with him, and the Muslims proceeded to the prayer ground in the valley of ʿUranah, where the mosque has been built. It is neither within the Sanctuary nor part of ʿArafah, but rather an intervening strip between the two ritual sites—one outside the Sanctuary and the other within it. From there to the standing-place is about a mile. He delivered the Hajj sermon to them from his mount. It was a Friday. He then dismounted and led them in the Zuhr and ʿAsr prayers, shortening and combining them. He then proceeded, with the Muslims accompanying him, to the standing-place at ʿArafah, beside the mountain known as the Mount of Mercy

---

## Page 12938

, whose name is “Ilāl,” rhyming with hilāl (crescent). This is the mountain that ordinary people call ʿArafah. He and the Muslims continued remembering Allah and supplicating until the sun set. He then led them onwards to Muzdalifah, where he prayed Maghrib and ʿIshā after the twilight had disappeared, before they unloaded their baggage at their stopping-place. He spent the night there until dawn broke, then led the Muslims in Fajr at the very beginning of its time, while it was still darker than when he prayed on other days.

He then took his station at “Quzah,” the mountain of Muzdalifah known as al-Mashʿar al-Harām (the Sacred Monument), although all of Muzdalifah constitutes the Sacred Monument mentioned in the Quran. He remained there with the Muslims until it had grown very light. Then he led them onwards until he reached Minā, beginning there by stoning Jamrat al-ʿAqabah. He then returned to his lodging at Minā and shaved his head. After that, he slaughtered sixty-three of the sacrificial camels he had brought and instructed ʿAlī to slaughter the rest; there were one hundred camels in all. He then proceeded to Mecca and performed the circumambulation of ifādah. He had sent the weaker members of his household ahead from Muzdalifah before dawn, and they had stoned the pillar during the night.

He then stayed with the Muslims for the three days at Minā, leading them in the five daily prayers, shortening but not combining them. Each day, after the sun passed its zenith, he stoned the three pillars, beginning with the first—the smallest, nearest to Minā and farthest from Mecca—and ending with Jamrat al-ʿAqabah. Between the first and second pillars, and between the second and third, he would pause at length, for as long as it takes to recite Surah al-Baqarah, remembering Allah and supplicating. For there are three standing-places: ʿArafah

---

## Page 12939

, Muzdalifah, and Minā. Then, on the last of the days of Tashrīq, after stoning the pillars, he and the Muslims departed and camped at al-Muhassab, near Khayf Banī Kinānah. They spent the night preceding Wednesday there. That night, he sent ‘Ā’ishah with her brother ‘Abd al-Rahmān to perform ‘umrah from al-Tan‘īm, the boundary of the Sacred Precinct closest to Mecca along the road from Medina. A mosque was subsequently built there, which people called the Mosque of ‘Ā’ishah. For none of his Companions ever performed ‘umrah after Hajj while accompanying the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, except ‘Ā’ishah. This was because she had begun menstruating upon her arrival. She had come to perform ‘umrah, but before the standing she had neither circumambulated the House nor walked between al-Safā and al-Marwah.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to her: “Perform all the rites that a pilgrim performs, except that you must not circumambulate the House or walk between al-Safā and al-Marwah.”

He and the Muslims then performed the farewell circumambulation of the House and returned to Medina. He did not stay on after the days of Tashrīq. During his lifetime, no one ever performed an ‘umrah by going out from the Sacred Precinct into the territory beyond it except ‘Ā’ishah alone.

The jurists of hadith, such as Ahmad and others, followed his Sunnah in all of this, although some of them, as well as others, may differ on certain points because of an interpretation that leaves them unaware of

---

## Page 12940

the Sunnah in those matters. Thus, they recommended that Muslims perform Hajj as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed his Companions. Since all the reports agree that he instructed his Companions to leave ihram and make their pilgrimage tamattu‘—performing ‘umrah, leaving ihram, and then performing Hajj—they recommended tamattu‘ for anyone combining the two rites in a single journey and entering ihram during the months of Hajj, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed.

They knew that performing Hajj on its own and then performing ‘umrah from outside the Sacred Precinct—even if they considered it permissible—was something no one had done during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, except ‘Ā’ishah, according to those who hold that she abandoned her ‘umrah and entered ihram for Hajj, as the Kufan scholars maintain. According to the majority of jurists, however, she combined Hajj and ‘umrah under a single ihram; on that view, neither ‘Ā’ishah nor anyone else did this.

Likewise, they knew that someone who had not brought a sacrificial animal should not combine the two rites under a single ihram, even though most of them—such as Ahmad and others—held it permissible. No one had done this during the lifetime of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, except ‘Ā’ishah, according to those who hold that she combined the two rites under a single ihram.

The leading hadith authorities—both jurists and scholars, such as Ahmad and others—did not disagree that the Prophet himself, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, neither performed Hajj on its own nor performed tamattu‘ in a manner involving release from ihram. Those among Ahmad’s followers who say that he performed tamattu‘

---

## Page 12941

and left ihram are mistaken. Likewise, anyone who says that he did not perform ‘umrah during his Hajj is mistaken.

As for those jurists who imagine that he performed ‘umrah after his Hajj—as is done by those who prefer performing Hajj on its own when they undertake both rites—none of those knowledgeable about his Hajj, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, has ever narrated or maintained this. They agree that neither he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor any of his Companions performed ‘umrah after Hajj except ‘Ā’ishah. Hence, the place for entering ihram for ‘umrah is known only as the Mosques of ‘Ā’ishah, since she alone went from the Sacred Precinct to the area outside it to enter ihram for ‘umrah.

Nor did he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, combine Hajj and ‘umrah under a single ihram in a manner involving two sets of circumambulation and two sets of traversals between Safā and Marwah. All the authentic reports explicitly state that, before standing at ‘Arafāt, he circumambulated the House and traversed between Safā and Marwah only once each. Any follower of Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, or Ahmad who maintains any of these views is therefore mistaken.

The source of this error is the use of expressions with more than one meaning in the accounts of the Companions who related the Prophet’s Hajj, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. For the authentic hadith collections establish, on the authority of several Companions—including ‘Ā’ishah, Ibn ‘Umar, and others—that “he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed tamattu‘, combining ‘umrah with Hajj.” It is also established on their authority that “he performed Hajj on its own.”

---

## Page 12942

and it is established that most of those reported to have said that “he performed Hajj on its own” also said that “he performed tamattu‘, combining ‘umrah with Hajj.” It is established that Anas ibn Mālik said:

“I heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say: ‘Here I am, answering Your call for ‘umrah and Hajj.’”

It is also established that ‘Umar was informed that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Someone came to me from my Lord—meaning in the valley of al-‘Aqīq—and said: ‘Say: An ‘umrah combined with Hajj.’”

No one other than ‘Umar and Anas related the actual words with which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, entered ihram. Hence Imam Ahmad said: “I have no doubt that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, combined Hajj and ‘umrah under a single ihram.”

As for the Companions’ wording, tamattu‘—combining ‘umrah with Hajj—describes anyone who performs ‘umrah during the months of Hajj and performs Hajj that same year, whether he combines them under a single ihram or leaves ihram between them. This broader sense of tamattu‘ includes qirān, combining both rites under a single ihram. Accordingly, most jurists hold that someone performing qirān must make a sacrificial offering, including him within the general scope of Allah’s words:

“Whoever combines ‘umrah with Hajj must offer whatever sacrificial animal he can afford.”

Nevertheless, the term “tamattu‘” may be used specifically for someone who performs ‘umrah and then enters ihram for Hajj after completing his ‘umrah. Thus, those Companions who said that “he performed tamattu‘, combining ‘umrah with Hajj” did not mean that he had left ihram. Rather, they meant that he combined the two rites during his pilgrimage, performing ‘umrah during the months of Hajj. They did not, however, specify whether he entered ihram for ‘umrah before circumambulating the House and traversing between the two hills

---

## Page 12943

or entered ihram for Hajj after that?

If he entered ihram before circumambulating the House and traversing between the two hills, then he was unquestionably performing qirān. If, however, he entered ihram for Hajj only after circumambulating the House and traversing between the two hills, without having left his ihram, then he is called a performer of tamattu‘ because he performed ‘umrah before entering ihram for Hajj. He is also called a performer of qirān because he entered ihram for Hajj before leaving his ihram for ‘umrah. Accordingly, some of our fellow scholars call him “a performer of tamattu‘,” others call him “a performer of qirān,” and others apply both names to him, which is the most correct approach. This concerns tamattu‘ in its restricted sense; in its broader sense, it unquestionably includes him.

Nevertheless, the correct view is what Ahmad affirmed decisively: that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, entered ihram for Hajj before circumambulating the House, because he said:

“Here I am, answering Your call for ‘umrah and Hajj.”

Even if it were from the moment one enters ihram for ‘umrah, together with His words—glory be to Him:

“Then fast three days during Hajj.”

For ‘umrah has become part of Hajj, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, stated.

Since the ‘umrah of someone performing tamattu‘ is part of his Hajj, the sacrificial animal brought along must not be slaughtered until he completes his prescribed rites, as Allah Most High says:

“Then let them complete their prescribed rites and fulfil their vows.”

This refers to the sacrificial animal brought along, for it is a vowed offering. Thus, if it becomes incapacitated before reaching its appointed place, it must be slaughtered. For its slaughter is to take place only when it reaches its appointed place, and it reaches that place only when its owner reaches the point at which he may leave ihram, since it follows his status. Its owner reaches that point only on the Day of Sacrifice, because before then he cannot leave ihram unconditionally: he is required to perform Hajj. This differs from someone who performs ‘umrah on its own, for he leaves ihram unconditionally.

---

## Page 12944

As for the practices established in the Sunnah of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—staying at Minā on the Day of Tarwiyah, spending the night there before the Day of ‘Arafah, then staying at ‘Uranah, between the Sacred Monument and ‘Arafah, until the sun passes its zenith, and proceeding from there towards ‘Arafah, delivering the sermon and offering the two prayers along the way in the valley of ‘Uranah—these are virtually matters of consensus among the jurists. Nevertheless, many authors do not clearly distinguish these details, and most people are unfamiliar with them because newly introduced customs have become so prevalent.

It was also the Sunnah of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to lead all the Muslims together in combining the noon and afternoon prayers at ‘Arafah, and the sunset and night prayers at Muzdalifah. With him were many people from Mecca and its surroundings whose homes lay less than the distance permitting the shortening of prayers. He did not instruct those living near the Sacred Mosque to offer each prayer separately at its prescribed time. Nor did he instruct the Meccans and others like them to withdraw, refrain from offering the afternoon prayer with him, and instead pray it separately within its own time, apart from the other Muslims. Anyone who examines the hadith reports knows beyond doubt that no such thing occurred.

This is the view of Mālik and a group of followers of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad, and Ahmad’s own statements support it. Some followers of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad overlooked this and applied their analogy concerning the combining of prayers across the board. They believed that he combined the prayers only because he was travelling, and that combining prayers on account of travel is permitted only for someone who travels sixteen parasangs, whereas the residents of Mecca do not live that far from ‘Uranah.

---

## Page 12945

But this is not correct. Had he combined the prayers because he was travelling, he would have combined them before and after that day as well. He stayed at Minā during the days of Tashrīq without combining the prayers. Moreover, only once is he reported to have combined prayers while travelling when he had stopped to stay somewhere. Ordinarily, he combined prayers during travel only when pressing on with his journey. Here, he combined them for purposes such as the standing at ‘Arafah, so that this standing would not be interrupted by prayer or anything else.

As Ahmad stated, prayers may be combined because of such an engagement that prevents them from being offered separately. Those followers of Ahmad who make travel a condition for combining prayers in this case are even further removed from his principles than are the followers of al-Shāfi‘ī. Ahmad permits combining prayers for many reasons other than travel. Indeed, al-Qādī Abū Ya‘lā and others, explaining Ahmad’s statement, said that prayers may be combined for any reason that permits absence from congregational prayer. Combining prayers is therefore not exclusive to travel. This differs from shortening prayers, which is prescribed only for travellers.

For this reason, most jurists, including al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad, held that shortening prayers at ‘Arafah, Muzdalifah and Minā, and during the days of Tashrīq, is permissible only for travellers who meet their criteria for shortening prayers. They applied their analogy consistently and believed that, unlike combining prayers, shortening them was solely on account of travel. Ahmad and others even directed that the governor of Mecca should not preside over the Hajj rites, on account of the shortening of prayers. A different view was held by groups from Medina and elsewhere—including Mālik and a group

---

## Page 12946

of the followers of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad, such as Abū al-Khattāb in his work on the five acts of worship—namely, that Meccans and others alike may shorten their prayers, and that shortening them there is on account of the pilgrimage rites.

The evidence supports this latter view: it has not been established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed the Meccans who prayed behind him at ‘Arafah, Muzdalifah and Minā to complete their prayers in full, as he instructed them when he led them in prayer in Mecca during the conquest, saying:

“Complete your prayers in full, for we are travellers.”

For if the Meccans had stood up after praying Zuhr behind him and completed four rak‘ahs, then stood up after praying ‘Asr and completed four rak‘ahs, then stood up after praying ‘Ishā’ behind him and completed four rak‘ahs, and had continued completing their prayers in full behind him throughout his stay at Minā, the Companions would not have failed to report something like this.

Another matter in which people may err is the belief held by some that offering the Eid prayer at Minā on the Day of Sacrifice is recommended. Indeed, even some engaged in jurisprudence perform it, relying on general textual statements or generalizations drawn by analogy. This betrays an obvious disregard for the Sunnah, for neither the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor his caliphs ever offered an Eid prayer at Minā. Rather, the equivalent of the Eid prayer at Minā is the stoning of Jamrat al-‘Aqabah. For those attending the pilgrimage, stoning Jamrat al-‘Aqabah takes the place of the Eid prayer for others. This is why Ahmad recommended that people in the towns offer their prayer at the time when sacrifices are slaughtered at Minā. This is also why the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, delivered a sermon on the Day of Sacrifice

---

## Page 12947

after the stoning of the Jamrah, just as he would deliver a sermon outside Mecca after the Eid prayer. Stoning the Jamrah is the greeting for Minā, just as tawāf is the greeting for the Sacred Mosque.

A similar example is the view expressed by a group of scholars—including Ibn ‘Aqīl—that when a pilgrim in ihrām enters the Sacred Mosque, it is recommended that he offer the mosque-greeting prayer, as in other mosques, and then perform the arrival tawāf or the like.

The imams and the great majority of jurists, among both Ahmad’s followers and others, reject this. First, it contradicts the Sunnah established through mass transmission concerning the practice of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his caliphs. When they entered the mosque, they invariably began with tawāf, followed by prayer. Second, the greeting for the Sacred Mosque is tawāf, just as the greeting for other mosques is prayer.

More objectionable still is the recommendation made by some followers of al-Shāfi‘ī that anyone who performs sa‘y between al-Safā and al-Marwah should pray two rak‘ahs on al-Marwah afterwards, by analogy with prayer after tawāf. The other scholars among al-Shāfi‘ī’s followers, as well as those of all the other schools, rejected this and regarded it as a plainly reprehensible innovation. For the established Sunnah is that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his caliphs performed tawāf and prayed, just as Allah mentioned tawāf and prayer together. They then performed sa‘y and did not pray afterwards. Thus, recommending

---

## Page 12948

prayer after sa‘y is like recommending it at the Jamrahs or at the standing place at ‘Arafāt, or making Fajr four rak‘ahs by analogy with Zuhr.

Consistently refraining from an act is Sunnah, just as consistently performing an act is Sunnah. This differs from an act omitted because there was no occasion calling for it, a prerequisite was absent, or an impediment existed, when circumstances later arose—an occasion or prerequisite becoming present, or an impediment being removed—under which Islamic law indicated that it should be performed. Examples include compiling the Quran into a single volume, gathering people behind one imam for the Tarāwīh prayers, learning Arabic and the names of those who transmitted religious knowledge, and other things needed in religion without which prescribed obligations or recommended acts cannot be fulfilled. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, refrained from such things only because a prerequisite was absent or an impediment existed.

As for acts of worship that he omitted, although he would have performed or authorized them had they been prescribed, and the caliphs and Companions after him would have performed them, one must conclude with certainty that performing them is innovation and misguidance. Analogy is inadmissible in such cases, even though it may be permissible in the first category. An example is drawing an analogy between “the two Eid prayers, the prayer for rain, and the eclipse prayer” and the five daily prayers, so as to institute an adhān and an iqāmah for them, as some of the Marwānids did for the two Eid prayers.

Another example is drawing an analogy between his chamber, and other such burial places of prophets, and the House of Allah with regard to touching and kissing. Such analogies resemble that of those whom Allah quoted as saying:

“Trade is only like usury.”

The hadith jurists—such as al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, and others—adopted, together with the jurists of

---

## Page 12949

Kufa—the position held by the majority of the Companions and early generations concerning the talbiyah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. It is established that he continued reciting the talbiyah until he stoned Jamrat al-‘Aqabah. A group of the early generations, including Companions, Successors, and scholars of Medina such as Mālik, held that the talbiyah ceases upon reaching the standing place at ‘Arafāt, because it is a response to a call and therefore ceases upon reaching the destination. Yet it is the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that must be followed.

As for the rationale, although someone who reaches ‘Arafāt has arrived at this standing place, he is then called onward to another standing place: Muzdalifah. Once he has completed his standing at Muzdalifah, he is called onward to the Jamrah. When he begins stoning it, the summons has come to an end: no further place remains to which he is called while in ihrām. He may shave his head and slaughter his sacrificial animal wherever he wishes within the Sacred Precinct, while the circumambulation of ifādah takes place after the first release from ihrām.

For this reason, they also adopted what is established from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: that he recited the talbiyah for ‘umrah until he touched the Black Stone, although Ibn ‘Umar and those scholars of Medina who followed him, such as Mālik, said that one recites it only until reaching the Sacred Precinct. For even upon reaching it, one is still called onward to the House.

---

## Page 12950

This reasoning does, however, imply that one recites the talbiyah only while travelling, not while standing at ‘Arafāt and Muzdalifah or spending the night there. This is a matter on which hadith scholars have differed. As for reciting the talbiyah while travelling from ‘Arafāt to Muzdalifah and from Muzdalifah to Minā, those who have gathered the authentic hadiths agree on it.

Scholars have held three views concerning a person in ihrām eating the meat of game hunted and ritually slaughtered by someone not in ihrām.

One group of the early generations said that it is forbidden, following their understanding of the words of Allah Most High:

“Land game is forbidden to you as long as you are in ihrām.”

They also relied on the established report that “the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, refused game meat when it was presented to him as a gift.”

Others, including Abū Hanīfah, said that it is permissible without qualification, acting upon the hadith about “Abū Qatādah, who hunted a wild ass, offered its meat to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and informed him that he had not hunted it for him,” as related in the authentic hadiths.

The third group, which includes jurists versed in hadith, said that it is permissible for a person in ihrām, provided that the person in ihrām has neither hunted it for him nor slaughtered it for his sake. This reconciles the hadiths, as Jābir reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “

---

## Page 12951

The meat of land game is lawful for you while you are in ihrām, provided you have not hunted it yourselves and it has not been hunted for you.” Al-Shāfiʿī said: “This is the best hadith on this subject and the most consistent with legal analogy.”

This is the position of Mālik, Ahmad, al-Shāfiʿī and others. They differed only over game hunted for a particular person in ihrām: is it permissible for others in ihrām to eat it? There are two views on this, both represented as juristic positions within the school of Ahmad, may Allah Most High have mercy on him.

---

## Page 12952

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about circumambulation by a menstruating woman, a person in a state of major ritual impurity, and a person in a state of minor ritual impurity.

He replied:

It is established that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“A menstruating woman performs all the pilgrimage rites except circumambulation of the House.”

He also said to ʿĀʾishah—may Allah be pleased with her:

“Do everything the pilgrims do, except circumambulate the House.”

When he was told that Safiyyah had begun menstruating, he asked, “Will she hold us back?” He was told, “She has already performed the circumambulation of ifāḍah.” He replied, “Then she will not.”

It is also authentically reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, sent Abū Bakr in the year 9 AH, when he appointed him to lead the pilgrimage, to proclaim: “No one is to circumambulate the House naked.” Yet no one has reported that he instructed those performing circumambulation to perform ablution or to keep themselves free of physical impurities, as he instructed those offering prayer to perform ablution.

His prohibition on a menstruating woman circumambulating the House may therefore relate to the mosque: either because she is forbidden to remain there, and circumambulation involves remaining there, or because she is forbidden to enter it at all, whether to pass through or to remain. Alternatively, it may be because circumambulation itself is forbidden during menstruation, just as prayer and fasting are forbidden to a menstruating woman by explicit scriptural texts and scholarly consensus; touching a copy of the Quran is likewise forbidden according to the great majority of scholars, as is reciting the Quran according to one of

---

## Page 12953

the two scholarly views.

Those who prohibit her from reciting—including Ahmad, according to the well-known position reported from him, as well as al-Shāfi‘ī and Abū Hanīfah—differ over whether she and a woman with postnatal bleeding may recite the Quran after the bleeding has ceased but before performing the ritual bath. There are three views:

The first permits recitation for both women following menstruation and women following postnatal bleeding. This was the position chosen by al-Qādī Abū Ya‘lā, who said that it was the apparent meaning of Ahmad’s statements.

The second prohibits recitation for both.

The third permits it for women following postnatal bleeding, but not for women following menstruation. This was the position chosen by al-Khallāl, one of Ahmad’s followers.

The prohibition may therefore rest on each of the two grounds independently, or on their combination, such that neither alone would entail prohibition. If it is prohibited on the first ground, then it is not forbidden to her in cases of necessity, since she may remain in the mosque out of necessity—for example, if she fears someone will kill her unless she enters the mosque, if the cold is severe, or if she has no shelter other than the mosque.

It is authentically reported in Sahīh Muslim and elsewhere from ‘Ā’ishah—may Allah be pleased with her—that she said:

“The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said to me, ‘Hand me the prayer mat from the mosque.’ I replied, ‘I am menstruating.’

---

## Page 12954

He said, ‘Your menstruation is not in your hand.’”

Maymūnah, the Prophet’s wife, said:

“The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, would rest his head in the lap of one of us and recite the Quran while she was menstruating. One of us would also take his prayer mat to the mosque and spread it out while she was menstruating.”

This was narrated by al-Nasā’ī. Abū Dāwūd narrated from ‘Ā’ishah that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“I do not permit a person in a state of major ritual impurity or a menstruating woman to be in the mosque.”

Ibn Mājah narrated it from Umm Salamah. The authenticity of both reports has been questioned. Most scholars, including al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, and others, therefore distinguished between passing through the mosque and remaining there, reconciling the hadiths. Some, such as Abū Hanīfah and Mālik, prohibited a menstruating woman from both remaining there and passing through.

Others did not prohibit her from being in the mosque. They may cite as evidence the words of Allah Most High:

“Nor in a state of major ritual impurity, except when passing through.”

Ahmad and others permitted a person in a state of major ritual impurity to remain there after performing ablution, based on what he and others narrated from ‘Atā’ ibn Yasār, who said:

“I saw men among the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, sitting in the mosque while in a state of major ritual impurity, once they had performed ablution as for prayer.”

The reason for this—and Allah knows best—is that the mosque is the dwelling place of the angels, and the angels do not enter a house in which there is a person in a state of major ritual impurity, as reported from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, in the Sunan collections. For this reason, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, forbade a person in a state of major ritual impurity to sleep until

---

## Page 12955

he has performed ablution. Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd narrated from Hishām ibn ‘Urwah, who said: My father related to me from ‘Ā’ishah that she used to say:

“If any of you has intercourse with his wife and then wishes to sleep, he should not sleep until he has performed ablution as for prayer, for he does not know—his soul may be taken during his sleep.”

Another hadith states:

“For if he dies, the angels will not attend his funeral.”

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, instructed a person in a state of major ritual impurity to perform ablution before eating, drinking, or having intercourse again. This indicates that, when he performs ablution, major ritual impurity is removed from the parts of the body washed in ablution. His major ritual impurity thus no longer remains in full, although some ritual impurity remains, just as someone in a state of minor ritual impurity has an impurity less than major ritual impurity. Although his remaining impurity is greater than minor ritual impurity, he is still in a lesser state than someone whose major ritual impurity remains in full. The angels therefore do not refrain from being present with him, and for this reason he may sleep and remain in the mosque. This indicates that major ritual impurity is divisible: it may be removed from some parts of the body while remaining in others, as the majority of scholars hold.

As for a menstruating woman, her ritual impurity is continuous, and she cannot purify herself in a way that stops it from continuing. She is therefore excused with regard to remaining in the mosque, sleeping, eating, and other such activities. When she needs to do these things, she is not barred from them as a person in a state of major ritual impurity is. Hence, the stronger of the two scholarly views is that she is not prohibited from reciting the Quran when she needs to do so. This is Mālik’s position and one of the two views within al-Shāfi‘ī’s school; it is also mentioned as a view reported from Ahmad. She needs to recite it and cannot purify herself as a person in a state of major ritual impurity can, even though her ritual impurity is more severe than

---

## Page 12956

that of a person in a state of major ritual impurity, in that she cannot fast until the bleeding stops, whereas such a person can fast, and in that she is barred from prayer whether she has purified herself or not. A man is also prohibited from having intercourse with her. This means that the grounds for prohibition are stronger in her case. Yet when she needs to perform an act, necessity permits her to do what is otherwise prohibited, even though the grounds for its prohibition remain.

Likewise, necessity permits other forbidden things, such as blood, carrion, and pork, whereas things less strictly prohibited—such as wearing silk or drinking from gold and silver vessels—remain impermissible in the absence of need.

Similarly, praying in a direction other than the qiblah, with the parts of the body that must be covered exposed, and with impurity on one’s body and clothing, is more strictly prohibited than other acts. Yet it becomes permissible, indeed obligatory, when there is a need. Other acts, though less strictly prohibited, such as reciting the Noble Quran, remain impermissible in the absence of need.

If we suppose that someone remains in a state of major ritual impurity and is unable either to perform the full ritual bath or to purify himself with clean earth, he has the same concession as a menstruating woman, although such a case is rare. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, instructed menstruating women to go out on Eid, witness the good and the Muslims’ supplications, and join the people in proclaiming Allah’s greatness.

Likewise, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, instructed menstruating women and women experiencing postpartum bleeding to enter ihrām and recite the talbiyah, with the remembrance of Allah these involve; to be present at ‘Arafāt, remembering Allah and supplicating; to perform the ritual stoning while remembering Allah; and other

---

## Page 12957

such acts. These are not disliked for her; rather, they are obligatory for her. For a person in a state of major ritual impurity, however, they are disliked until he performs the full ritual bath, because he is able to purify himself, unlike a menstruating woman.

This is a fundamental principle concerning these and similar questions: the gravity of the harm that warrants prohibition should not be considered without also considering the need that warrants permission—or even makes the act recommended or obligatory. Whenever a condition normally makes prayer forbidden, praying under that condition becomes obligatory in cases of need if prayer is otherwise impossible. Praying under such conditions is less grave than abandoning prayer. Thus, if someone prayed after purifying himself with clean earth despite being able to use water, that prayer would be forbidden. But if he were unable to use water, praying after purification with clean earth would be obligatory within the prescribed time. The same applies to praying naked, facing a direction other than the qiblah, with impurity present, or without recitation; and to performing an obligatory prayer seated or without completing the bowing and prostration. These and similar ways of praying are forbidden when one is able to fulfil the requirements, but obligatory when one is unable to do so.

Likewise, eating carrion, blood, and pork is forbidden when one has no need of them, but obligatory in cases of necessity, according to the four imams and the majority of scholars.

Masrūq said: “Whoever is compelled by necessity but refuses to eat until he dies will enter the Fire.”

This is because he has contributed to killing himself by refraining from eating food that he was able to eat and was permitted to eat in those circumstances. He is thus like someone who kills himself, unlike one who risks his life in jihad, or one who

---

## Page 12958

speaks the truth before a tyrannical ruler. Such a person is killed while striving in Allah’s cause, and his death benefits the religion of Allah Most High.

As for explaining the prohibition on a menstruating woman’s circumambulation by reference to the sanctity of the mosque, I have seen some Hanafis offer this explanation. Abū Hanīfah’s position is that purification is a duty (wājib) for circumambulation, but not a strict obligation (fard) within it or a condition of its validity. However, this explanation accords with the view that circumambulation in a state of minor ritual impurity is not forbidden. This was the position of Mansūr ibn al-Mu‘tamir and Hammād ibn Abī Sulaymān, as Ahmad reported from them.

‘Abd Allah said in his Manāsik (Rites of Pilgrimage):

“My father narrated to me: Sahl ibn Yūsuf narrated to us: Shu‘bah informed us, reporting from Hammād and Mansūr: ‘I asked them about a man circumambulating the House without having performed ablution, and neither saw any harm in it.’”

‘Abd Allah said:

“I asked my father about this, and he replied: ‘I prefer that he circumambulate the House having performed ablution, because circumambulation is prayer.’”

Two positions are explicitly transmitted from Ahmad concerning purification: is it a condition of circumambulation or not? His statements likewise imply two positions concerning whether purification is obligatory for circumambulation.

Some Hanafis similarly said that purification is not obligatory for circumambulation but is recommended, while also holding that omitting it requires a compensatory sacrifice. Thus, anyone who holds that a person in a state of minor ritual impurity may circumambulate, unlike a menstruating woman or someone in a state of major ritual impurity, can explain the prohibition by reference to the sanctity of the mosque rather than to circumambulation itself. Speaking, eating and drinking are permitted during circumambulation, so it is not like prayer. Moreover, purification is the key to prayer; its opening declaration, “Allah is Most Great,” brings its restrictions into effect, and its closing salutation lifts them. Circumambulation is not like that.

He would also say that naked people were prohibited from circumambulating only because others could see them, and because of the sanctity of the mosque as well.

---

## Page 12959

Those who hold this view say:

The place of circumambulation is the noblest of mosques and is scarcely ever without someone circumambulating. Allah Most High has said:

“Take your adornment at every mosque.”

Thus, He commanded people to wear their adornment when entering the mosque. Prayer is different: a person praying must cover himself for the sake of prayer itself, and prayer may be performed anywhere. Even if he prayed alone in a dark room, he would still have to observe the covering prescribed for prayer. Circumambulation, by contrast, must take place in the Sacred Mosque, while spiritual retreat must take place in a mosque.

According to those who hold this view, neither a person in a state of major ritual impurity nor a menstruating woman is forbidden to circumambulate when compelled by necessity, just as, in their view, a person in a state of minor ritual impurity is never forbidden to circumambulate. This is because entering the mosque is not forbidden to either of them in such circumstances. When compelled by necessity, they have a stronger claim to permission than someone in a state of minor ritual impurity whom these scholars permit to circumambulate without any excuse.

Do you not see that someone in a state of minor ritual impurity is forbidden to pray or touch a copy of the Quran when able to purify himself, whereas someone in a state of major ritual impurity may do so after performing dry ablution? If he is unable to perform dry ablution, he prays without either a full ritual bath or dry ablution, according to one of the two scholarly views. This is the well-known position in the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, as it is established in an authentic report that the Companions prayed in a state of major ritual impurity before the verse concerning dry ablution was revealed.

A menstruating woman has been forbidden to fast, for she has no need to fast during menstruation: she can fast another month outside Ramadan. Thus, if a traveler and a sick person

---

## Page 12960

are allowed to fast another month despite being able to fast, then a menstruating woman, who is forbidden to fast, has even greater reason to fast another month. When she is commanded to make up the fasts, she is commanded to fast only one month; thus, no more is required of her than is required of others. For this reason, if she experiences non-menstrual bleeding, she fasts despite it, since such bleeding cannot be avoided: it may also occur when she is making up her fasts.

Prayer, however, recurs five times every day and night, and menstruation is a bar to prayer. If it were said that she should pray during menstruation because of need, menstruation would never constitute a bar to prayer at all, and fasting and circumambulating the House would possess greater sanctity than prayer. That is not the case. Rather, it is out of reverence for prayer that she does not pray during menstruation, since praying during her periods of purity suffices her without praying during menstruation.

If she is forbidden to circumambulate solely because of the mosque, then clearly there are stronger grounds for permitting this when there is a valid excuse than for permitting her to touch a copy of the Quran when there is a valid excuse. If she had a copy of the Quran and could only safeguard it by touching it—for example, if a thief or a disbeliever wanted to take it, or someone wanted to plunder it or receive it from her as a gift—and she could only prevent him by touching it, she would be permitted to do so. Yet someone in a state of minor ritual impurity may not touch a copy of the Quran, although he may enter the mosque.

It is therefore clear that the sanctity of a copy of the Quran is greater than that of the mosque. If she is permitted to touch a copy of the Quran because of need, then entering the mosque, whose sanctity is less than that of a copy of the Quran, is even more deserving of permission.

---

## Page 12961

Section

If, however, the prohibition of circumambulation is due to something intrinsic to circumambulation itself, just as she is prohibited from other acts, or is due both to that and to the sanctity of the mosque, each constituting an independent ground for prohibition, then we say:

If necessity compels her to do so, such that she cannot perform Hajj without circumambulating while menstruating because she is unable to remain until she becomes pure, then the choice is between her circumambulating while menstruating and suffering harm incompatible with the Sharia. The Sharia does not require her to remain when doing so threatens her safety and property and leaves her unable to return to her family; nor does it require her to remain in Mecca when she is unable to do so and would suffer harm as a result.

The position of most scholars is that Hajj is not obligatory for someone who can perform it but cannot return to his family. There is a weak opinion that it is obligatory if he can remain there. However, where remaining entails harm that threatens his life, or where he is unable to earn a living, no scholar requires him to remain. Thus, she is not obliged to undertake a Hajj that would require her to reside in Mecca.

Many women, if they do not return with those with whom they performed Hajj, cannot return thereafter. Even supposing that she could return later, it cannot be required of her that

---

## Page 12962

intercourse with her remain forbidden after she returns to her family, and continue to be forbidden until she comes back to Mecca. This, too, is among the gravest hardships, the like of which Allah does not impose, for it is more burdensome than requiring two pilgrimages, whereas Allah Most High has required only one. Those obliged to make up their Hajj, such as someone who invalidates it, are required to do so only because of their negligence in invalidating it. Hence, according to the stronger of the two scholarly opinions, someone prevented from completing Hajj is not required to make it up, since there has been no negligence. Those who require someone who misses Hajj to make it up do so because they consider that person negligent.

If it is said concerning this woman, “Rather, she should leave ihram just as someone prevented from completing Hajj does,” this does not discharge her obligation. She would therefore still need to perform a second Hajj, during which she would fear the same thing she feared during the first. Moreover, someone prevented from completing Hajj may leave ihram only because of a tangible impediment, whether an enemy, illness, poverty, or imprisonment.

A legal prohibition alone, however, does not make anyone a person prevented from completing Hajj. Anyone able to reach the House is not considered prevented under the Sharia. These, then, are the possible courses of action: she must either remain in Mecca, return home while still in ihram, or leave ihram. Yet the Sharia rules out all these options for someone in her circumstances.

If it is said that Hajj is waived for a woman in these circumstances, just as it is waived for a woman who can perform Hajj only with someone who would engage in illicit intercourse with her, because circumambulating while menstruating is forbidden just as illicit intercourse is forbidden,

---

## Page 12963

the reply is: This is contrary to the fundamental principles of the Sharia, for the Sharia is founded upon Allah Most High’s words:

“Fear Allah as much as you are able,”

and upon the words of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him:

“When I command you to do something, do as much of it as you can.”

It is well known that if a woman cannot fulfil an obligation of prayer, fasting, or anything else except by engaging in illicit intercourse, she is not permitted to do so. Allah Most High has not commanded His servants to do anything that can only be accomplished through illicit intercourse. Necessity does not make illicit intercourse permissible as it makes eating carrion permissible. However, if she is forcibly subjected to intercourse and cannot resist it, she herself has performed no act. If, however, she acts under coercion, there are two views, both reported from Ahmad. The first is that coercion makes only utterances permissible, not deeds.

The second, which is the view of the majority, is that a woman coerced into illicit intercourse or drinking wine is pardoned, because Allah Most High says:

“If anyone compels them, then after their compulsion Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

As for a man who engages in illicit intercourse, there are two views within Ahmad’s school and elsewhere, depending on whether coercion prevents an erection. Abū Hanīfah and Ahmad, according to the latter’s explicitly reported ruling, hold that a man cannot be coerced into illicit intercourse.

But if a servant is able to fulfil some obligations and not others, then he

---

## Page 12964

is commanded to do what he can, while what he cannot do remains waived. Thus, he is commanded to pray naked, with ritual impurity present, or facing a direction other than the qiblah if that is all he can manage. Likewise, circumambulation while riding or being carried is permissible for a valid excuse, according to explicit scriptural evidence and scholarly agreement; without such an excuse, it is disputed.

Similarly, a sick person may perform an obligatory prayer sitting or riding, although this is not permissible in an obligatory prayer without an excuse. Praying in a direction other than the qiblah, praying naked, praying without cleansing oneself after relieving oneself, and praying in an impure garment are all forbidden in both obligatory and voluntary prayers. Nevertheless, performing the obligatory prayer despite these prohibited circumstances is better than abandoning it. The same applies to the prayer in times of fear, which may involve considerable movement, turning one’s back to the qiblah, separating from the imam during the prayer, making up what one has missed before the closing salutation, and other things that are impermissible without an excuse.

If it is objected: “Circumambulating during menstruation is like praying or fasting during menstruation, neither of which is permissible under any circumstances.”

The reply is: There is never any need to fast during menstruation, since she is obliged to fast one month, and fasting outside Ramadan can take its place. If she may not perform the obligatory fast during menstruation, then voluntary fasting is all the more impermissible, since she has the alternative of fasting when free of menstruation. Likewise, someone offering voluntary prayers at prohibited times has the alternative of offering them at other times. Thus, she never needs to fast during menstruation, and this harm is not permitted when it can be avoided, just as it is impermissible to offer a prayer

---

## Page 12965

that is voluntary and has no specific occasion during the prohibited times. Prayers tied to specific occasions are different: of the two scholarly views, the one better supported by the evidence is that they are permissible because of the need to perform them. If one does not perform them then, the opportunity to do so and their benefit will be lost. This is unlike unrestricted voluntary prayer, whose opportunity is not lost. Fasting falls into this latter category: there is no fast she might observe that cannot be observed during days when she is free of menstruation. This is why a woman experiencing non-menstrual bleeding is permitted to fast and pray.

As for prayer, if it were permitted during menstruation, menstruation would never prevent prayer at all. Menstruation is something women regularly experience, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said to ‘Ā’ishah:

“This is something Allah has decreed for the daughters of Adam.”

Had the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, permitted women to pray while menstruating, prayer during menstruation would have become like prayer when free of menstruation. If all other acts of worship were then permitted, menstruation would cease to be an impediment, even though major and minor ritual impurity remained impediments. This would be a grave contradiction. If acts of worship lesser than prayer were forbidden while prayer itself were permitted, this too would be contradictory.

Moreover, she has no need to pray during menstruation, since the prayers she offers when free of menstruation—which is most of the time—suffice her without praying during her menstrual days. She has, however, been granted permission to do what she needs, such as reciting the talbiyah (pilgrimage invocation), remembering Allah, and supplicating.

Nevertheless, she has been instructed to bathe, just as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, instructed Asmā’ to bathe upon entering the consecrated state for pilgrimage, when she had given birth to Muhammad ibn Abī Bakr. He also instructed women generally to do this, and instructed ‘Ā’ishah, when she began menstruating at Sarif,

---

## Page 12966

to bathe and enter the consecrated state for Hajj. Thus, he instructed her to bathe despite menstruating in order to enter that state. Yet he permitted a menstruating woman to recite the talbiyah, stand at ‘Arafah, supplicate, and remember Allah without bathing or performing ablution. Doing so is not disliked for her, whereas it is disliked for someone in major ritual impurity to do so without purification. This is because she needs to do these things, and neither bathing nor ablution has any effect on her continuing state of ritual impurity. Her bath upon entering the consecrated state is different: it is a bath for cleanliness, like the bath taken for Friday prayer.

For this reason, should dry purification be performed in place of such baths when water is unavailable? There are two views within Ahmad’s school. Likewise, there are two views on whether dry purification should be performed for the deceased when washing the body is not possible. This is unlike bathing to remove major ritual impurity or performing ablution to remove minor ritual impurity. Nevertheless, she was not instructed to bathe upon entering Mecca or standing at ‘Arafah. Since she was forbidden to pray while menstruating, but was permitted to remember Allah without this even being disliked, the distinction between what she needs and what she does not need becomes clear.

If it is said:

“Other forms of remembrance are permissible for those in major or minor ritual impurity, so there is no prohibition in that.”

The response is:

A person in major ritual impurity is prohibited from reciting the Quran. It is disliked for him to give the call to prayer or deliver a sermon while in that state, to sleep without performing ablution, or to perform the pilgrimage rites without purification when he is able to purify himself. Likewise, someone in minor ritual impurity is encouraged to purify himself before remembering Allah Most High, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said: “I disliked mentioning

---

## Page 12967

Allah except in a state of purity.” None of these forms of purification is recommended for a menstruating woman, nor does any scholar consider it disliked for her to remember Allah without them, given the mass-transmitted Sunnah concerning this.

Their disagreement concerns only recitation of the Quran. There is no basis whatsoever in the Sunnah for prohibiting her from reciting it. The report,

“Neither a menstruating woman nor a person in major ritual impurity may recite any of the Quran,”

is a weak hadith, as the scholars of hadith unanimously agree. It was narrated by Ismā‘īl ibn ‘Ayyāsh from Mūsā ibn ‘Uqbah, from Nāfi‘, from Ibn ‘Umar. Ismā‘īl frequently makes mistakes in his narrations from the people of the Hijaz. This report has no basis from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. Nor was it narrated from Ibn ‘Umar, Nāfi‘, or Mūsā ibn ‘Uqbah by their associates known for transmitting the Sunnah from them.

Women menstruated during the time of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him. Had recitation been forbidden to them, as prayer was, this would have been something the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, explained to his community, something the Mothers of the Believers knew, and something they transmitted to the people. Since no one has transmitted any prohibition concerning this from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, it is impermissible to declare it forbidden when it is known that he did not prohibit it. Since he did not prohibit it, despite menstruation being so common in his time, it is known that it is not forbidden.
