This is like our argument that, had semen been ritually impure, he would have commanded the Companions to remove it from their bodies and clothes, for it inevitably comes into contact with people’s bodies and clothes

---

## Page 12968

during wet dreams. Since no one has transmitted that he commanded its removal, whether by washing or rubbing, despite how frequently it comes into contact with bodies and clothes—in his time and until the Day of Resurrection—it is known that he did not command this. It is inconceivable that removing it should be obligatory without his commanding it, given how commonly this occurs, just as he commanded cleansing after defecation and urination, and instructed a menstruating woman to remove menstrual blood from her garment.

The same applies to ablution after touching women or after ritually impure substances emerge from somewhere other than the two private passages. He did not command Muslims to perform ablution for these things, despite their frequent occurrence. Had it been obligatory, he would have had to command it; and had he commanded it, Muslims would necessarily have transmitted it, since there were ample reasons and motives for transmitting such a command.

His commands to perform ablution after touching the penis and after eating food cooked over fire indicate recommendation. Thus, in these cases, it is all the more appropriate that ablution should be no more than recommended.

If the established Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, grants a menstruating woman concessions not granted to someone in major ritual impurity because she needs them and cannot purify herself, and forbids her only what she has no need to do—just as she is prohibited from fasting because of the ritual impurity of menstruation and because she has no need to fast then, and is prohibited from prayer with even greater reason, since her prayers during purity take the place of those during menstruation—then she is prohibited from circumambulating the Ka‘bah if she is able to do so in a state of purity. For circumambulation resembles prayer in some respects, but not in every respect.

---

## Page 12969

As for the hadith narrated by al-Nasā’ī from Ibn ‘Abbās, in which the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Circumambulating the House is prayer, except that Allah has permitted speech during it. Whoever speaks during it should therefore say only what is good,”

it has been said that these are the words of Ibn ‘Abbās himself. Whether they are the words of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, or those of Ibn ‘Abbās, they do not mean that circumambulation is a form of prayer like the Friday prayer, the prayer for rain, or the eclipse prayer. Allah has distinguished prayer from circumambulation in His saying, Exalted is He:

“Purify My House, both of you, for those who circumambulate it, those who stand in prayer, and those who bow and prostrate.”

Scholars have discussed which is better for a newly arrived visitor: prayer or circumambulation. They unanimously agree that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, circumambulated the House and prayed two units of prayer behind the Station of Abraham. Mass-transmitted reports from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, the Companions, the Successors, and the other scholars establish the distinction between what is called prayer and what is called circumambulation. Thus, circumambulation cannot be classified as a form of prayer.

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“The key to prayer is purification; its sacred state begins with declaring Allah’s greatness and ends with the salutation of peace.”

Circumambulation, however, does not begin its sacred state with declaring Allah’s greatness or end it with the salutation of peace. The early generations and those who came after them disagreed over whether ablution is obligatory for circumambulation after entering a state of minor ritual impurity. By contrast, the obligation of ablution for prayer is an elementary and indisputable teaching of Islam, and whoever denies it is a disbeliever. Nothing has been transmitted from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, requiring ablution for circumambulation. Prohibiting a menstruating woman from circumambulation does not entail

---

## Page 12970

prohibiting someone in a state of minor ritual impurity from doing so.

Scholars hold two views concerning purification from menstruation: is it obligatory for circumambulation, or is it a condition of its validity? They have not, however, disputed that purification is a condition for the validity of prayer. Moreover, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“There is no prayer without the Opening Chapter of the Quran.”

Yet Quranic recitation during circumambulation is not obligatory, by scholarly agreement. Indeed, scholars hold two views on whether it is disliked.

He also said:

“Allah introduces whatever commands He wills, and among the commands He has introduced is that you must not speak during prayer.”

Thus, he prohibited speech during prayer without qualification. During circumambulation, however, speech is permitted that is not permitted in prayer. This also makes clear the distinction between circumambulation and the funeral prayer. The latter has a prescribed entry into and exit from its sacred state; speech is prohibited during it; and it is performed behind an imam, in rows. All of this is agreed upon. Quranic recitation during it is established in the Sunnah of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him; this is the more correct of the two scholarly views.

As for “the prostration of recitation,” scholars have disputed whether it counts as prayer for which purification is a condition, even though it consists of prostration—the greatest of prayer’s physical pillars—and one does not speak while prostrating. Rather, one declares Allah’s greatness when prostrating and when rising, and, according to one of the two scholarly views, also concludes with the salutation of peace. This is according to those who accept that purification is obligatory for a prostration performed on its own, such as the prostration of recitation. Those who reject this requirement maintain that it is permissible without ablution. They say that a prostration performed on its own does not fall within the meaning of prayer; rather, prayer denotes only that which has a prescribed entry into and exit from its sacred state. Regarding this prostration, it has not been reported from

---

## Page 12971

the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, that he required purification for it. Rather, it is established in the authentic hadith collection that:

“When the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, recited Sūrat al-Najm, the Muslims and polytheists, jinn and humans, prostrated with him. Pharaoh’s sorcerers also prostrated without being in a state of ritual purity.”

It is also established that Ibn ‘Umar performed the prostration of recitation without ablution. No Companion is reported to have required purification for it. Likewise, no one has reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, concluded it with the salutation of peace. Most of the early generations held that no salutation of peace is prescribed for it. This is also one of the two views reported from Ahmad, who stated that he had heard no report prescribing a salutation of peace. Those who prescribe it have established it through a faulty analogy, treating this prostration as prayer when that is precisely the point in dispute.

As for the funeral prayer, some have held that purification is not a condition for its validity. This, however, is a weak view, for it has a prescribed entry into and exit from its sacred state and is therefore prayer. Circumambulation is not like any of these acts, nor does a menstruating woman need to perform them. Since she does not perform prayers that are an individual obligation, this applies all the more to communal obligations and voluntary prayers. Her supplication for the deceased and her seeking forgiveness for him achieve the intended purpose as far as possible, just as her attendance at Eid and remembrance of Allah Most High alongside the Muslims achieve the intended purpose as far as possible.

Although circumambulation has a distinction over the other rites by its very nature, by taking place in the mosque, and by being prescribed both as an independent act of worship and as part of ‘umrah

---

## Page 12972

and Hajj—whereas entering ihrām, going between al-Safā and al-Marwah, and shaving the head are prescribed only as part of Hajj or ‘umrah, while the remaining rites, such as standing at ‘Arafah and Muzdalifah and stoning the pillars, are prescribed only as part of Hajj—this indicates that Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has made circumambulation easy for people. He has enabled them to draw near to Him through it both outside and within the state of ihrām, during the two pilgrimages and apart from them. Thus, He has not imposed upon it the requirements He imposed upon prayer, nor prohibited during it what He prohibited during prayer.

It is therefore clear that prayer is of greater consequence, and circumambulation must not be treated as equivalent to it.

Those scholars who say that circumambulation is better than prayer in the mosque for visitors from distant lands say so only because they can pray in other cities, whereas circumambulation is possible only in Mecca. An otherwise less excellent deed takes precedence over a more excellent one at its appropriate place and time, not because its category is superior. Thus, supplication at the end of prayer takes precedence over remembrance and recitation, and remembrance while bowing and prostrating takes precedence over recitation, for the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“I have been forbidden to recite the Quran while bowing or prostrating.”

Likewise, recitation, remembrance, and supplication take precedence at times when prayer is prohibited. Responding to the muezzin takes precedence over prayer and recitation, because the opportunity for the former will pass, whereas that for the latter will not. Similarly, when the eclipse prayer coincides with another prayer, whichever is liable to be missed takes precedence.

Circumambulation therefore takes precedence because a visitor from distant lands will lose the opportunity to perform it once he leaves. It is given precedence for this reason, not because circumambulation as a category is superior to prayer, or even equal to it, for this is not

---

## Page 12973

a view held by anyone. Hajj in its entirety cannot be compared to prayer, which is the pillar of the religion; how, then, can any of its individual rites be compared to it? Allah has prescribed Hajj for every Muslim only once in a lifetime and has not required any of its rites to be performed twice. Rather, He has prescribed only one circumambulation and one standing at ‘Arafah. The same applies to going between al-Safā and al-Marwah: according to the more explicit of the two reports from Ahmad, a pilgrim performing tamattu‘ is required to perform this rite only once, either before standing at ‘Arafah or afterwards, following circumambulation.

For this reason, most scholars hold that ‘umrah is not obligatory. This is the position of Mālik and Abū Hanīfah, and one of the two positions in the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad. It is also the position more clearly supported by the evidence. Allah has required only pilgrimage to the House; He has not required ‘umrah. He has, however, required anyone who begins Hajj or ‘umrah to complete it, because ‘umrah is the lesser pilgrimage. It must therefore be completed, just as a voluntary Hajj must be completed.

Allah has required only what is denoted by the term Hajj; He has not required two pilgrimages, a greater and a lesser one. That requirement is fulfilled by the greater pilgrimage, which is what the term Hajj means when used without qualification. Nothing beyond that is therefore obligatory. Nor do the rites of ‘umrah contain anything additional to the rites of Hajj. If ‘umrah were obligatory, this would merely require the same rites to be performed twice, contrary to what Allah has prescribed for Hajj.

The point here is this: if Hajj is obligatory only once, how can it be compared to something obligatory five times every day and night?

---

## Page 12974

This is one distinction between a menstruating woman’s circumambulation and her prayer. She needs to perform the circumambulation required of her once in a lifetime, having undertaken a long journey, with camels carrying her burdens to a land people could reach only with great hardship. How can her need for this circumambulation be compared to her need for prayer, which she can forgo during menstruation, since the prayers she performs when not menstruating suffice her? It has already been explained that a menstruating woman is not prohibited from reciting the Quran because she needs to do so; her need for this circumambulation is greater still.

If someone says, “She may recite the Quran while in a state of minor ritual impurity, whereas circumambulation requires purification,” the response is: “This is a well-known matter of disagreement among earlier and later scholars. You must therefore provide evidence that minor ritual purification is obligatory for circumambulation.”

Invoking the statement,

“Circumambulation of the House is prayer,”

is a weak argument. At most, it likens circumambulation to prayer in certain rulings; something likened to another is not identical to it in every respect. What is meant is simply that circumambulation resembles prayer in requiring avoidance of prohibited acts that are also forbidden outside prayer. As for the things that invalidate prayer—speaking, eating, drinking, and excessive movement—none of these invalidates circumambulation. They are, however, disliked when there is no need for them, because they distract from its purpose, just as such things are disliked during Quran recitation, supplication, and remembrance of Allah. This is comparable to the Prophet’s saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“A servant remains in prayer as long as he is waiting for prayer,”

and his saying: “When one of you sets out for the mosque, he should not interlace his fingers, for he is in prayer.

---

## Page 12975

and this is why he said, “Except that Allah has permitted you to speak during it.” It is well known that eating and drinking are also permitted during circumambulation. Eating, drinking, and excessive movement are prohibited acts that invalidate prayer, yet none of them invalidates circumambulation. At most, they are disliked during it when done without need, just as fidgeting is disliked during prayer. If someone interrupts circumambulation for an obligatory prayer or a funeral prayer that is commencing, he may resume his circumambulation from where he stopped.

Prayer, however, is not interrupted for such things. Thus, the prohibitions of prayer do not apply to circumambulation, nor do its obligatory acts, such as the formal acts of concluding and commencing prayer. How, then, can it be said that circumambulation is like prayer in what it requires and what it prohibits? Whoever holds that minor ritual purification is obligatory for circumambulation must provide evidence from the revealed law, and I know of none that makes it obligatory.

Upon further reflection, it became clear to me that purification from ritual impurity is neither a condition for circumambulation nor obligatory for it, without doubt. Minor ritual purification is, however, recommended for it. The evidence of the revealed law indicates only that it is not obligatory, and nothing in the revealed law establishes that minor ritual purification is obligatory for circumambulation.

Accordingly, we do not concede that circumambulation, as a category of worship, is superior to Quran recitation as a category. Rather, recitation is superior: it is the finest of the words spoken in prayer, while prostration is the finest of its physical acts. Circumambulation, by contrast, contains no obligatory remembrance of Allah.

If it is said, “Some circumambulation has been made obligatory,” the response is: “Recitation has been made obligatory in every prayer; no prayer is valid without recitation. How, then, can circumambulation be compared

---

## Page 12976

with prayer?” If recitation is superior, and a menstruating woman may recite when she needs to, according to the stronger of the two scholarly views, then circumambulation is all the more deserving of being permitted when needed.

If it is said, “You concede that circumambulation is ordinarily prohibited for a menstruating woman and is permitted only out of necessity,” the response is:

Those who attribute the prohibition to the mosque do not concede that the act of circumambulation itself is intrinsically prohibited. Those who do concede this say: Some Quran recitation is likewise prohibited for a menstruating woman, namely, recitation during prayer. Most scholars also prohibit her from reciting outside prayer without a need; it is permitted only when needed. If recitation is permitted when needed, circumambulation is all the more deserving of that permission.

Moreover, touching a copy of the Quran requires purification from both major and minor ritual impurity according to the vast majority of scholars, as indicated by the Quran and Sunnah. This is also established from Salmān, Saʿd, and other Companions. The sanctity of a copy of the Quran is greater than that of mosques. Nevertheless, if someone in a state of major or minor ritual impurity, or a menstruating woman, must touch it out of necessity, they may do so. If someone must circumambulate out of necessity, then this is all the more deserving of being permitted, since no evidence from the revealed law establishes any obligation of ritual purification for it.

If it is said, “Some circumambulation is obligatory,” the response is:

Touching a copy of the Quran may also become obligatory in certain circumstances: when it is needed to fulfil the obligation of safeguarding it, reciting from it, or carrying it, if that obligation cannot be fulfilled without touching it.

And his statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “A menstruating woman performs all the pilgrimage rites

---

## Page 12977

except circumambulation around the House,” is analogous to his saying:

“Allah does not accept the prayer of any of you who has invalidated his ablution until he performs ablution,”

and his saying:

“Allah does not accept the prayer of a woman who has reached puberty unless she wears a headcovering,”

and his saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“I do not permit the mosque for anyone in a state of major ritual impurity or for a menstruating woman.”

Indeed, requiring ablution for prayer and a headcovering for a woman in prayer, and prohibiting prayer without these, are more stringent requirements than prohibiting circumambulation during menstruation. If he prohibited the mosque for someone in a state of major ritual impurity and for a menstruating woman, yet permitted a menstruating woman to hand him the small prayer mat from the mosque, telling her, “Your menstruation is not in your hand,” then it is clear that menstruation is located in the private parts, which do not come into contact with the mosque. This reasoning would entail allowing a menstruating woman unrestricted access to it. But if he also said, “I do not permit the mosque for anyone in a state of major ritual impurity or for a menstruating woman,” then these statements must be reconciled, and everything that has come from Allah must be believed.

If neither statement abrogates the other, then one is general and lacks detail, while the other is specific and permits passing through the mosque. This is an exception to that prohibition, even though there is no necessity for it. Thus, permitting circumambulation out of necessity does not conflict with its prohibition in that text. It is like permitting a woman to pray without a headcovering out of necessity, or permitting prayer without ablution out of necessity, using dry purification instead. Indeed, necessity may permit prayer without either ablution or dry purification, as the Companions did when they had no water before the verse was revealed. It is also like permitting prayer without recitation out of necessity, despite his saying:

“There is no prayer without the Opening Chapter of the Quran.”

Likewise, necessity permits prayer and circumambulation despite the presence of ritual impurities, notwithstanding his saying:

“Scrape off the blood, then rub the spot with your fingertips, then pray in the garment.”

Necessity also permits prayer on an impure place, despite his saying:

“Every pure tract of earth has been made for me a place of prayer and a means of purification.”

---

## Page 12978

Indeed, the prohibition of consuming blood and pork is among the most stringent, yet consuming them has been permitted out of necessity. What the Sunnah establishes is that circumambulation is an act of worship occupying an intermediate position between prayer and the other pilgrimage rites. It is superior to the other rites, as menstruating women are forbidden to perform it, while prayer is more complete than it. This is because circumambulation resembles prayer more closely than the other rites do, and because it is confined to the mosque. Because of these two aspects of its sanctity, a menstruating woman is prohibited from performing it. Yet no Sunnah report prohibits someone without ablution from performing it. Whatever is not forbidden to someone without ablution is, with all the more reason, not forbidden to a menstruating woman in cases of necessity, such as reciting the Quran or observing a devotional retreat in the mosque. Even if something is forbidden to her while ritually impure, this does not entail that it remains forbidden in cases of necessity, as with touching a copy of the Quran and other such acts.

Whoever makes circumambulation identical to prayer in its obligations and prohibitions has contradicted the scriptural texts and consensus. No one may invoke anyone’s opinion as proof in disputed matters. Proof consists only of scriptural texts, consensus, and arguments derived from them whose premises are established by scriptural evidence, not by the statements of individual scholars. Scholars’ statements must themselves be supported by scriptural evidence; they cannot be used as evidence against it.

Someone brought up following a particular school, who has become accustomed to it and believes its teachings, but lacks a sound understanding of scriptural evidence and scholarly disagreements, does not distinguish between what has come from the Messenger and been accepted by the Muslim community—and must therefore be believed—and what individual scholars have said. Establishing the proof against such a person is difficult or impossible. Whoever cannot distinguish between these two is not equipped to discuss religious knowledge as scholars do

---

## Page 12979

but is merely a follower of scholarly authority who transmits the opinions of others, like someone reporting another person’s account. A witness who testifies about another is not a judge, and someone who merely transmits opinions is a reporter, not a mufti.

This woman’s situation admits only those three possibilities, this view, or the view that performing tawāf al-ifādah (the principal circumambulation) before standing at Arafat suffices when performing it afterwards is impossible. A similar opinion is reported within Mālik’s school concerning someone who forgets the principal circumambulation until he has returned to his homeland: his arrival circumambulation suffices. Yet this offers her no relief, for her menstruation may continue from the time she enters Mecca until the pilgrims depart.

Moreover, this entails performing circumambulation before its appointed time, as established by the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus. Pilgrimage rites performed before their appointed times are not valid.

If the choice is between her performing the principal circumambulation in a state of ritual impurity and not performing it at all, then performing it in a state of ritual impurity is preferable. Whether ritual purity is a condition of validity is a well-known point of disagreement. Many scholars, such as Abū Hanīfah and Ahmad, according to one of the two reports from him, hold that even when she is able to observe ritual purity, circumambulation performed while menstruating is valid, though she owes a sacrificial offering. They nevertheless maintain that she sins by doing so. Were she to perform it before standing at Arafat, however, it would not be valid. This position is well known and widely recognised. Thus, it is clear that circumambulation while menstruating is preferable to circumambulation before its appointed time.

Those who hold this view say that ritual purity is an obligation in circumambulation, not a condition of its validity, and that all obligations are waived when one is unable to fulfil them. Hence the view of Abū Hanīfah and others

---

## Page 12980

among the scholars is that whatever is obligatory in some circumstances but not others is not an indispensable requirement; rather, an indispensable requirement is something binding upon everyone in every circumstance.

Thus they argued as follows:

When the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) waived the farewell circumambulation for menstruating women, this showed that it is not an essential pillar; rather, its omission can be compensated for by a sacrificial offering. Likewise, when he waived the overnight stay at Minā for those responsible for supplying water to the pilgrims, this showed that it is not an indispensable requirement, but an obligatory duty whose omission can be compensated for by a sacrificial offering. Similarly, when he allowed herdsmen and those supplying water to postpone the stoning from one appointed time to another, this showed that performing it at that particular time is not an indispensable requirement. Likewise, when he permitted the weak to depart from Jam‘ during the night, this showed that remaining at Muzdalifah after dawn is not an indispensable requirement, but an obligatory duty whose omission can be compensated for by a sacrificial offering.

This is an argument supporting these scholars among the followers of Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, and others. Followers of Abū Hanīfah, such as al-Tahāwī and others, have cited it.

If, then, their position is that ritual purity is neither an indispensable requirement for circumambulation nor a condition of its validity, but an obligatory duty whose omission can be compensated for by a sacrificial offering, this indicates that it is not binding upon everyone in every circumstance. For, according to them, whatever is binding upon everyone in every circumstance is an indispensable requirement that must be fulfilled and cannot be compensated for by a sacrificial offering.

Accordingly, if ritual purity is obligatory in some circumstances but not others, its obligation is waived when one is unable to fulfil it, just as all other obligatory duties are waived when one is unable to fulfil them, such as the farewell circumambulation, and just as it is permissible

---

## Page 12981

for a person in ihrām to use items that meet a general human need, such as trousers and leather socks, without owing any expiation according to most scholars, including al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, and the other hadith jurists. This differs from what is needed only in particular circumstances, which is permissible only with expiation. Abū Hanīfah, however, requires expiation in all these cases.

Accordingly, the most that can be said concerning this woman who needs to perform circumambulation is that she owes a sacrificial offering, as is the position of Abū Hanīfah and one of the two views within Ahmad’s school. According to those who regard ritual purity as obligatory, she owes a sacrificial offering when she has no excuse. When she is unable to fulfil this duty, however, even if such an offering is deemed obligatory, that is the utmost that can be required of her. The view more consistent with legal analogy is that she owes no sacrificial offering in a case of necessity.

But to regard this as an obligatory duty whose omission can be compensated for by a sacrificial offering, while maintaining that its obligation is not waived by necessity, contradicts the foundational principles of Islamic law.

This makes it clear that, since some Muslim scholars rule that circumambulation performed by a menstruating woman is valid with a sacrificial offering even when she is not compelled by necessity, the Muslim community has not reached a consensus that, in all circumstances, only circumambulation performed in a state of purity is valid for her. Thus, the opposing scholar who maintains this has neither a scriptural text, nor consensus, nor analogical reasoning to support his position. We have already explained that this position necessarily entails the permissibility of doing so when there is a need. We have also explained that scholars disagree over whether purification from ritual impurity is obligatory for her, and that the view of those who deny its obligation is stronger. The Muslim community has therefore reached no consensus either that ritual purity is obligatory in all circumstances or that any form of purification is a condition for the validity of circumambulation.

As for the point on which I know of no disagreement, it is that she may not perform circumambulation while menstruating if

---

## Page 12982

she is able to perform circumambulation in a state of purity. I know of no one who disputes that doing so is forbidden for her and that she sins thereby. Scholars have differed, however, over whether it fulfils her obligation.

According to Abū Hanīfah’s school, it does fulfil her obligation, and this is also one view within Ahmad’s school. In one transmitted report, Ahmad explicitly stated that if a person in a state of major ritual impurity performs circumambulation forgetfully, it fulfils his obligation. Some of his followers restricted this ruling to cases of forgetfulness. Others said that this indicates that ritual purity is not obligatory, for if it were obligatory, it would not be waived through forgetfulness: it belongs to the category of commanded acts, not prohibited acts, just like purification from ritual impurity for prayer.

This differs from avoiding physical impurity during prayer. The apparent position of Ahmad’s school is that someone who prays having forgotten such impurity, or being unaware of it, need not repeat the prayer. This is because it belongs to the category of prohibited acts. If someone does such an act forgetfully or unknowingly, he incurs no sin, and its occurrence is therefore treated as though it had not occurred.

Some of Ahmad’s followers then said that this indicates that, according to this report, he does not regard ritual purity as an integral requirement of circumambulation, but rather as an obligatory duty whose omission is compensated for by a sacrificial offering. They related two reports concerning the validity of a menstruating woman’s circumambulation: according to the first, it is invalid; according to the second, it is valid, and she compensates for the omission with a sacrificial offering. Abū al-Barakāt and others mentioned this.

Likewise, several of his followers explicitly stated that this disagreement concerns purification from menstruation and major ritual impurity, as in Abū Hanīfah’s school. According to this view, the obligation is waived when one is unable to fulfil it, like all other obligations.

---

## Page 12983

Other followers of Ahmad related three reports from him: according to one, circumambulation performed forgetfully in a state of major ritual impurity fulfils one’s obligation, and no sacrificial offering is due; according to another, a sacrificial offering is due; and according to the third, such circumambulation does not fulfil one’s obligation.

Some people suppose that the disagreement within Ahmad’s school concerns only those in a state of major or minor ritual impurity, not menstruating women. This is not so. Several of his followers explicitly stated that the disagreement concerns menstruating women as well as others. Ahmad’s own words indicate this. It is clear that he withheld judgment regarding circumambulation by both a menstruating woman and someone in a state of major ritual impurity. He would mention the views of the Companions, the Successors and others on this matter.

In al-Shāfī, Abū Bakr ‘Abd al-‘Azīz related from al-Maymūnī:

“I asked Ahmad about someone who performed sa‘y (the passage between Safā and Marwah) and the obligatory circumambulation without ritual purity, then had intercourse with his wife. He replied, ‘This is a matter over which people differ.’ He mentioned Ibn ‘Umar’s view, what ‘Atā’ said and the concessions he allowed in this matter, and what al-Hasan said. He also mentioned the case of ‘Ā’ishah: when she menstruated, the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

‘Do what the pilgrim does, but do not circumambulate the House. This is something Allah has decreed for the daughters of Adam.’

“She was afflicted with it; it befell her through no doing of her own.”

Al-Maymūnī said:

“I said, ‘Some people say that he must perform Hajj again.’ He replied, ‘Yes, that is what I have generally understood.’ ‘And some hold that he owes a sacrificial offering?’ Abū ‘Abd Allāh said, both at the outset and at the end, ‘This is an unclear matter requiring examination. Give me time to consider it.’

“Some people say, ‘Even if he has returned to his homeland, he must come back and perform circumambulation.’

“I asked, ‘What about forgetfulness?’ He replied, ‘The ruling in cases of forgetfulness is far more lenient.’” He meant that it is more lenient than the ruling concerning someone who deliberately performs circumambulation without ritual purity.

---

## Page 12984

Abū Bakr ‘Abd al-‘Azīz said:

“We have already explained, in the rulings on circumambulation, the two views concerning circumambulation of the House”—meaning Ahmad’s two views.

“According to one view, if a man circumambulates without ritual purity out of forgetfulness, his circumambulation fulfils his obligation. According to the other, it does not fulfil his obligation unless he is ritually pure. If he has intercourse after having circumambulated without ritual purity out of forgetfulness, there are likewise two views, corresponding to those concerning circumambulation. Whoever considers circumambulation without ritual purity valid says that his Hajj is complete. Whoever considers it valid only with ritual purity requires him to return from wherever he remembers and perform circumambulation.”

He said: “This is the view I adopt.”

Thus, Abū Bakr and other followers of Ahmad hold that, according to one of the two reports from him, circumambulation fulfils one’s obligation when there is an excuse, and no sacrificial offering is due. Ahmad’s words are clear on this point. His answer quoted above shows that he understood the disagreement to include circumambulation by a menstruating woman as well as by others. Leniency in this matter has been reported from Ibn ‘Umar, ‘Atā’ and others.

Among the views transmitted from ‘Atā’ on this matter is that if a woman begins menstruating during circumambulation, she should complete it. This is an explicit statement from ‘Atā’ that being free of menstruation is not a condition for circumambulation, and Ahmad took his view into account. Ahmad also cited the hadith of ‘Ā’ishah, explaining that the Prophet’s words (may Allah bless him and grant him peace),

“This is something Allah has decreed for the daughters of Adam,”

show that she was afflicted with something that befell her through no doing of her own, and she is therefore excused. For this reason, she is excused if she begins menstruating while in devotional retreat, and her retreat is not invalidated; rather,

---

## Page 12985

she remains in the mosque’s courtyard. If compelled to stay inside the mosque, she stays there. Likewise, if she menstruates during the two-month fast, this does not interrupt its continuity, by scholarly consensus.

It follows that there is nothing disliked in her attending the pilgrimage rites or joining the Muslims for Eid. She may also supplicate and remember Allah. Such actions are disliked for someone in a state of janābah (major ritual impurity), because he can purify himself, whereas she cannot and is therefore excused. Similarly, those who permit her to recite the Quran excuse her, unlike someone in janābah who can purify himself.

A menstruating woman therefore has a stronger claim to being excused than someone who circumambulated while in janābah: he can purify himself, whereas she cannot. Her excuse of inability and necessity is stronger than his excuse of forgetfulness. Since purification is required for prayer, someone who forgets it is required to undertake it when he remembers. Likewise, someone who forgets to purify himself for prayer must purify himself and pray when he remembers.

This differs from the case of someone unable to fulfil a condition, such as someone unable to purify himself with water: that requirement is waived for him. The same applies to someone unable to perform the other essential components of prayer—for example, someone unable to recite, stand, bow and prostrate fully, or face the direction of prayer. Whatever he is unable to do is waived for him. Allah has not required anyone to perform obligations of worship that are beyond his ability.

Thus, if this woman cannot circumambulate in a state of ritual purity, whatever she cannot fulfil is waived for her. Her inability to fulfil an essential component or obligation of circumambulation does not waive circumambulation itself, which she is able to perform, just as in prayer and other acts of worship. Allah Most High has said:

“So fear

---

## Page 12986

Allah as much as you are able.” The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“When I command you to do something, do as much of it as you can.”

This woman can do no more than this. She has feared Allah as much as she can, so nothing further is required of her.

It is well known that someone who deliberately circumambulates without ritual purity is sinful. Ahmad mentioned both opinions: must he offer a sacrifice, or must he return and circumambulate? His discussion of this disagreement makes clear that his hesitation concerning someone who circumambulates without ritual purity includes a menstruating woman and someone in a state of janābah who do so deliberately. It also shows that the case of someone who forgets is far less serious. Someone unable to purify himself has a stronger excuse than someone who forgets.

Abū Bakr ‘Abd al-‘Azīz said in al-Shāfī:

(Chapter on Circumambulating the House without Ritual Purity. Abū ‘Abd Allāh said, as reported by Abū Tālib: “No one should circumambulate the House except in a state of ritual purity. There is greater leniency regarding voluntary circumambulation. Nor should anyone attend the pilgrimage rites except in a state of ritual purity.”

As reported by Muhammad ibn al-Hakam, he said: “If someone performs the visitation circumambulation, having forgotten to purify himself, and has already returned, nothing is required of him.” He preferred that the person circumambulate in a state of ritual purity. “If he has had intercourse, his Hajj remains valid, and nothing is required of him.”

This explicit statement from Ahmad clearly establishes that ritual purity is not a condition of validity, and that someone who circumambulates having forgotten to purify himself owes nothing—neither a sacrifice nor anything else. It also establishes that if he subsequently has intercourse, his Hajj remains valid and nothing is required of him. Likewise, when he distinguished between voluntary circumambulation

---

## Page 12987

and other circumambulation with regard to ritual purity, enjoining it for circumambulation and all the other pilgrimage rites, this indicated that he did not consider ritual purity a condition of validity. Here, therefore, he stated unequivocally that nothing is required of someone who forgets.

He also said, as reported by Abū Tālib:

“If someone circumambulates the House without ritual purity, he should perform ablution and repeat the circumambulation. If he circumambulates in a state of major ritual impurity, he should take a full ritual bath and repeat the circumambulation.”

As reported by Abū Dāwūd, he said:

“Sufyān narrated to us, from Ibn Jurayj, from ‘Atā’: ‘If someone circumambulates without ablution, let him repeat his circumambulation.’”

Abū Bakr ‘Abd al-‘Azīz said:

“Chapter on Circumambulation in a Ritually Impure Garment. Abū ‘Abd Allāh said, as reported by Abū Tālib: ‘If a man circumambulates in a ritually impure garment, al-Hasan used to dislike this. He should not circumambulate except in a ritually pure garment.’”

These words of Ahmad show that, in his view, circumambulation does not have the same conditions of validity as prayer. For the most he said about circumambulating in a ritually impure garment was that al-Hasan disliked it, and that one should not circumambulate except in a ritually pure garment. Such wording is also used for something strongly recommended. This differs from ritual purity in prayer. The position of Abū Hanīfah and others is that if someone circumambulates with ritual impurity on him, his circumambulation is valid and nothing is required of him.

In sum, are the conditions of validity for prayer also required for circumambulation? There are two opinions within Ahmad’s school and among others:

The first is that they are required, as held by Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī and others.

---

## Page 12988

The second is that they are not required. This is the view of most of the early generations and the position of Abū Hanīfah and others. This is the correct view, for those who require the same conditions for circumambulation as for prayer have no evidence other than the saying attributed to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Circumambulation of the House is prayer.”

Even if this were established as a statement of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, it would not support their position, as explained earlier. Indeed, the scriptural evidence indicates otherwise.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not require those circumambulating to maintain ritual purity or avoid impure substances. Rather, he said:

“The key to prayer is purification; its consecration is the proclamation of Allah’s greatness, and its release is the salutation of peace.”

Circumambulation is not like this. What is obligatory in prayer is not obligatory in circumambulation, nor is what is forbidden in prayer forbidden in circumambulation. Thus, the claim that it is equivalent to prayer is untenable.

They have also argued by analogy that circumambulation is an act of worship connected to the House, and therefore ritual purity and the other conditions are required for it, just as for prayer. This analogy is invalid. The response is: We do not concede that the effective cause of the ruling in the original case is its connection to the House, and they have provided no evidence for this. A sound analogy is one in which the feature shared by the original case and the new case is shown to be the effective cause of the ruling, or an indicator of that cause.

Furthermore, ritual purity is required because the act is prayer, whether or not it is connected to the House. Do you not see that when they prayed towards the Rock, ritual purity was still a condition of prayer, although it was not connected to the House? Likewise, when someone prays

---

## Page 12989

without facing the qiblah (direction of prayer), as in voluntary prayer while travelling or the prayer of fear while mounted, ritual purity remains a condition, although the prayer is not connected to the House. Moreover, looking at the House is an act of worship connected to it, yet neither ritual purity nor the other conditions are required for it.

There are also acts of worship that require being in a mosque but do not require ritual purity, such as devotional retreat. Allah Most High says:

“Purify My House for those who circumambulate it, those who stand in worship, and those who bow and prostrate.”

There is no stronger basis for comparing someone circumambulating the House to someone bowing and prostrating than for comparing him to someone observing devotional retreat. Indeed, he more closely resembles the latter, since being in the mosque is a condition of both circumambulation and devotional retreat, but not of prayer.

If it is said, “Someone who circumambulates must offer the two units of prayer afterwards, and prayer requires ritual purity,” the response is:

Whether the two units of prayer following circumambulation are obligatory is disputed. Even assuming that they are obligatory, they need not follow the circumambulation immediately. Their connection to circumambulation is no closer than that of the Friday prayer to its sermon. It is well known that if someone delivers the sermon in a state of minor ritual impurity, then performs ablution and leads the Friday prayer, this is valid. All the more, then, may someone circumambulate in a state of minor ritual impurity, then perform ablution and offer the two units of prayer. This is a situation people frequently encounter when they forget to ensure ritual purity before delivering the sermon or circumambulating: they may purify themselves afterwards and pray. It has also been expressly stated that delivering the sermon while in a state of major ritual impurity is valid.

Once it is clear that ritual purity is not a condition, the remaining question is whether

---

## Page 12990

it is obligatory or recommended. Both views were held by the early generations and are found in the schools of Ahmad and others, as well as in the school of Abū Hanīfah. However, those among Abū Hanīfah’s followers who regard it as recommended nevertheless say that she must offer a compensatory sacrifice.

Ahmad, however, says that she incurs no penalty—neither a sacrificial offering nor anything else—as he expressly stated concerning someone who circumambulates in a state of major ritual impurity out of forgetfulness. If she deliberately circumambulates while menstruating, there is a basis for saying that she must offer a compensatory sacrifice.

When she is unable to do otherwise, the most that can be said is that she must offer a compensatory sacrifice. The more fitting view, however, is that no sacrifice is required, because this is a duty she is commanded to fulfil when able, not when unable. A compensatory sacrifice becomes obligatory only for omitting something commanded, and in this situation she has neither omitted anything commanded nor committed any of the acts prohibited during ihrām. This is not one of the prohibitions of ihrām, since circumambulation is performed both by those outside ihrām and by those in it. Rather, the prohibition here is of the same kind as the prohibition on remaining in the mosque, a menstruating woman observing devotional retreat in the mosque, touching a copy of the Quran, or reciting the Quran. Such acts are permissible when needed, without a compensatory sacrifice. Moreover, the principal circumambulation of Hajj is permissible only after the first release from ihrām, at which point she is allowed to do what was previously prohibited, except intercourse.

If it is objected:

Had it been possible for her to circumambulate while menstruating, she would have been commanded to perform the arrival and farewell circumambulations. Yet the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, waived the farewell circumambulation for menstruating women. He also instructed ‘Ā’ishah, who had arrived intending to perform tamattu‘ and then began menstruating, to set aside the rites of

---

## Page 12991

‘umrah and enter ihrām for Hajj. This shows that she cannot perform circumambulation.

The answer is:

Circumambulation during menstruation is prohibited out of respect for the sanctity of the mosque, for circumambulation itself, or for both. Prohibited acts become permissible only in cases of necessity. She has no compelling need to perform the farewell circumambulation, since it is not part of Hajj. Thus, someone residing in Mecca does not perform it; only someone departing from Mecca does so, making the House the last place they visit.

Likewise, she has no compelling need to perform the arrival circumambulation. Indeed, if a pilgrim arrives with little time remaining, he proceeds first to ‘Arafah without performing the arrival circumambulation. Thus, if the Prophet commanded those capable of performing these two circumambulations to do so, his command made both or one of them obligatory, or was a recommendation. Scholars hold differing views on this. According to the established Sunnah and the consensus of scholars, neither is an essential pillar required of every pilgrim.

The obligatory circumambulation is different: she has a compelling need to perform it, because Hajj is not valid without it. Similarly, she may enter the mosque out of necessity, but not for prayer or devotional retreat, even a retreat she has vowed to observe. Rather, if a woman menstruates during devotional retreat, she leaves the mosque and pitches a tent for herself in its forecourt.

This also indicates that prohibiting a menstruating woman from circumambulation is like prohibiting her from observing devotional retreat inside the mosque: it is out of respect for the mosque’s sanctity. Menstruation itself does not invalidate her retreat, for she has no choice in the matter. Rather, she is barred from the mosque, not from devotional retreat, since she has no compelling need to remain inside the mosque. If she were permitted to do so throughout her menstruation, this would amount to allowing women to remain in the mosque while menstruating. Circumambulation, however, can only be performed in the mosque

---

## Page 12992

—the Sacred Mosque—for circumambulation is restricted to a particular place. It differs from devotional retreat, since someone observing retreat may leave the mosque for necessities, such as relieving himself, eating, or drinking. He remains in retreat while outside the mosque and may not have sexual relations during that time. This accords with Allah’s words:

“Do not have sexual relations with them while you are in devotional retreat in the mosques.”

The phrase “in the mosques” modifies “in devotional retreat,” not “have sexual relations with them.” Sexual relations in the mosque are forbidden both to those observing retreat and to others. Rather, someone observing retreat in the mosque may not have sexual relations even when he leaves it for a necessity.

Since this resembles devotional retreat, and a menstruating woman leaves out of necessity, menstruation does not interrupt her retreat. Allah, glorified be He, mentioned retreat, circumambulation, and prayer together when commanding that His House be purified, saying:

“Purify My House for those who circumambulate it, those who observe devotional retreat, and those who bow and prostrate.”

Keeping menstruating women out of it is therefore part of maintaining its purity. Circumambulation resembles devotional retreat, not prayer: prayer is permissible anywhere on earth and is not restricted to a mosque. Certain things are required for prayer, and certain things are forbidden during it that are not forbidden during devotional retreat or circumambulation.

The essential point is that circumambulation is an act of worship performed both by those outside ihrām and by those in ihrām; it is not exclusive to the state of ihrām. Hence, the obligatory circumambulation becomes due only after the first release from ihrām. The pilgrim thus performs the circumambulation mentioned in the words of Allah Most High:

---

## Page 12993

“Then let them put an end to their unkempt state, fulfill their vows, and circumambulate the Ancient House.”

Thus, the pilgrims circumambulate after being released from ihrām, having completed their Hajj, with nothing remaining forbidden to them except sexual relations. Hence, if one of them has intercourse in this state, his pilgrimage is not invalidated, by agreement of the imams. If circumambulation is an act of worship, then it is one restricted to the Sacred Mosque, just as devotional retreat is restricted to mosques generally. Allah Most High has commanded that His House be purified for those who circumambulate it, those who observe devotional retreat, and those who bow and prostrate. Circumambulation is not a form of prayer. If someone omits one of its obligatory elements, it may be said that he has omitted something from his rites, and whoever omits something from his rites must offer a sacrificial animal.

If, however, he omits an obligatory requirement concerning how circumambulation is performed because he is unable to fulfill it, this is a matter for juristic judgment: should he be treated like someone who has omitted part of his rites? Or should it be said that this ruling concerns someone who omits an independent rite, omits it despite being able to perform it and without an excuse, or omits something specific to Hajj and umrah?

As for saying that this woman, who is unable to circumambulate while menstruating, must return home still in ihrām, be treated as someone prevented from completing the pilgrimage, or be exempted from Hajj or from the obligatory circumambulation—all these views conflict with the fundamental principles of Islamic law. Moreover, I know of no imam who has explicitly stated any of them concerning this particular situation.

Rather, the statements of the early generations and the imams that she must offer a sacrificial animal, return home still in ihrām, or the like are general statements encompassing those who did this in their time. In their day, a woman could remain until she became pure and circumambulated. They used to instruct the leaders to wait until the menstruating women became pure

---

## Page 12994

and circumambulated. For this reason, Mālik and others required the man from whom she had hired her mount to remain with her until she became pure and circumambulated.

His followers, however, said:
“He is not obliged in these times to remain with her, because of the harm this would cause him.”

It is thus clear that the imams’ rulings that purity from menstruation is a condition or an obligation concerned a woman who was able to circumambulate in a state of purity, not one who was unable to do so—unless, of course, some of them held that it was a condition or an obligation in both circumstances. In that case, the disagreement is with whoever held that view.

And Allah Most High knows best. May Allah bless Muhammad.

---

## Page 12995

Shaykh al-Islām was also asked:

About the difficulty caused by menstruation, which affects half the women performing Hajj, and the many different circumstances in which it occurs: some are menstruating when they enter ihrām, while others begin menstruating during the days of Tashrīq.

The first question:
A woman menstruates at the beginning of the month and has no opportunity to circumambulate except while menstruating. When standing at ʿArafah, she notices some of the yellowish and brownish discharge that she sees after the white discharge. What is the ruling concerning this?

The second question:
A woman begins menstruating sometime between the fifth and ninth, and her menstruation continues until the seventeenth or later. She stood at ʿArafah, performed the stoning, and performed the circumambulation of ifādah, all while menstruating, and was unable to perform ʿumrah.

The third question:
A woman stood at ʿArafah and stoned the pillars, intending to perform the circumambulation of ifādah. However, she began menstruating before the circumambulation, so she did not perform it and concealed the matter. She had also intended to perform ʿumrah but did not do so. She returned without performing either the circumambulation or ʿumrah, and without offering a sacrificial animal.

He replied—may Allah have mercy on him:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. As for the “first question,”

---

## Page 12996

a menstruating woman performs all the pilgrimage rites while menstruating, except circumambulation, according to the established Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) and the agreement of the imams. He (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“A menstruating woman performs all the rites except circumambulation of the House.”

“He instructed Asmā’ bint Abī Bakr, when she experienced postnatal bleeding at Dhū al-Hulayfah, to bathe and enter ihrām.”

“He instructed ‘Ā’ishah, when she began menstruating at Sarif, to bathe and enter ihrām for Hajj, and not to circumambulate before the standing at ‘Arafah.”

Thus, this woman who arrived in Mecca while menstruating, before the standing at ‘Arafah, must not circumambulate the House. She does, however, stand at ‘Arafah even while menstruating. How much more so, then, if she is merely seeing some yellowish and brownish discharge?

Concerning yellowish and brownish discharge, jurists in Ahmad’s school and elsewhere hold three opinions: that it is menstruation in all cases; that it is not menstruation in any case; and the third opinion—which is correct—that if it occurs during the usual menstrual period alongside black and red blood, it is menstruation; otherwise, it is not.

This is because women used to send ‘Ā’ishah a small container holding cotton, and she would tell them:

“Do not be hasty until you see the white discharge.”

Others did likewise, regarding whatever preceded the white discharge as menstruation. Umm ‘Atiyyah said:

“We did not regard yellowish and brownish discharge after becoming pure as anything.”

None of the pilgrimage rites requires ritual purity except circumambulation, for ritual purity is required for circumambulating the House by agreement of the scholars. As for going between al-Safā and al-Marwah,

---

## Page 12997

there is disagreement, though the majority hold that ritual purity is not required for it. For all other rites, ritual purity is not required, by agreement of the scholars.

The scholars then differed over whether ritual purity is a condition for the validity of circumambulation, as it is for prayer, or an obligation whose omission is compensated for by offering a sacrifice, like failing to enter ihrām at the designated boundary, omitting the stoning of the pillars, or similar omissions. There are two well-known opinions, both reported from Ahmad.

The better-known report from him—which is also the position of Mālik and al-Shāfi‘ī—is that ritual purity is a condition for its validity. Thus, anyone who circumambulates in a state of major or minor ritual impurity, or while menstruating, out of forgetfulness or ignorance, must repeat the circumambulation upon becoming aware.

The second opinion is that ritual purity is obligatory, so anyone who does this must compensate by offering a sacrifice. According to Abū Hanīfah, however, someone in a state of major ritual impurity or a menstruating woman must offer a badanah (a large sacrificial animal), whereas someone in a state of minor ritual impurity must offer a sheep or goat.

Ahmad required a sacrifice without specifying a badanah. He explicitly addressed the case of someone who circumambulated forgetfully while in a state of major ritual impurity, saying in this report: “He must offer a sacrifice.” Some jurists of his school therefore restricted the two reported opinions to those with an excuse, such as forgetfulness. Others applied both reports without restriction to those who acted forgetfully, deliberately, and in similar circumstances.

Those who made ritual purity a condition argued that circumambulating the House is like prayer

---

## Page 12998

as reported by al-Nasā’ī and others from Ibn ‘Abbās, who related that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“Circumambulating the House is prayer, except that Allah has permitted you to speak during it. Whoever speaks should therefore say only what is good.”

It has, however, been said that these are the words of Ibn ‘Abbās himself, rather than the Prophet.

It is authentically reported that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“No one may circumambulate the House naked.”

Allah Most High said:

“Wear your adornment at every place of prayer.”

This was revealed when people used to circumambulate the House naked, except for al-Hums, who would circumambulate in their clothes. Others would not circumambulate in their own clothes, saying, “These are clothes in which we have disobeyed Allah.” If someone found a garment belonging to a member of al-Hums, he would circumambulate wearing it; otherwise, he would circumambulate naked. If he circumambulated in his own clothes, he would discard them, and they were therefore called “castoffs.”

This was among the practices the polytheists had introduced into circumambulation. They had also introduced prohibitions on certain foods while in ihrām. Allah therefore revealed:

“Wear your adornment at every place of prayer, and eat and drink, but do not be excessive. Indeed, He does not love those who are excessive.”

“Say, ‘Who has forbidden the adornment Allah has provided for His servants and the good things He has provided for their sustenance?’”

And His words:

“And when they commit an indecency”—such as circumambulating the House naked—“they say, ‘We found our forefathers doing it, and Allah has commanded us to do it.’ Say, ‘Allah does not command indecency. Do you say about Allah what you do not know?’”

Thus, the obligation of ritual purity and covering the body during circumambulation, as established by the revealed texts, is agreed upon. What is disputed is whether it necessarily follows that these are conditions for its validity, as they are for prayer.

---

## Page 12999

Those who hold that this is not a condition argue that Hajj includes obligatory acts whose omission is compensated for by a sacrifice, but which are not conditions for its validity. If a pilgrim omits them, deliberately or inadvertently, he compensates for them with a sacrifice, unlike prayer.

As for prayer, are there obligatory acts whose omission does not invalidate it under any circumstances, or are there not? Or does their omission leave it valid only when they are forgotten? This is a well-known matter of disagreement. Abū Hanīfa considers certain acts obligatory in prayer without their omission invalidating it under any circumstances, such as reciting al-Fātiha and remaining still in the prayer postures. Ahmad takes a similar position according to one of the two views in his school: he considers congregational prayer obligatory but does not make it a condition for the prayer’s validity. According to the well-known view reported from Ahmad, some obligatory acts in prayer, if omitted inadvertently, are compensated for by the two prostrations for forgetfulness, while others require no compensation, such as keeping free of impurities, according to the well-known view reported from him. Likewise, Mālik considers keeping free of impurities and similar matters obligatory in prayer, yet holds that someone who omits them must repeat the prayer within its prescribed time, but not after that time has passed. These positions are well known in their respective schools.

As for the “second question”: if a woman menstruates and becomes pure before the Day of Sacrifice, she is excused from the arrival circumambulation and performs the circumambulation of ifādah on the Day of Sacrifice or afterward, while she is pure. Likewise, if she performs the circumambulation of ifādah while pure, then menstruates and does not become pure before departing, she is excused from the farewell circumambulation. This accords with the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), who granted this concession to a woman who performed circumambulation while pure and then menstruated, excusing her from the farewell circumambulation:

“His wife Safiyya, the Mother of the Believers, began menstruating on the Day of Sacrifice. He asked, ‘Will she hold us back?’ They replied, ‘She

---

## Page 13000

has already performed the circumambulation of ifādah.’ He said, ‘Then she will not hold us back.’”

If she menstruates before the circumambulation of ifādah, she must remain until she becomes pure and performs it, if that is possible. Those accompanying her must also remain for her sake if they can. When the roads were safe in the time of the early generations, and people travelled to and from Mecca throughout the year, a woman could remain with her mahram (a male relative permanently forbidden to her in marriage) and the man providing her hired transport until she became pure, then perform circumambulation. The scholars therefore used to instruct women to do this. Sometimes they would instruct the leader to wait for menstruating women until they became pure, just as the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said, “Will she hold us back?”

Abū Hurayra—may Allah be pleased with him—said: “A leader, yet not a leader: a woman accompanying a group who begins menstruating before the circumambulation of ifādah, so they wait for her until she becomes pure and performs circumambulation”—or words to that effect.

Nowadays, however, many women, or most of them, cannot remain behind after the caravan leaves. The caravan departs one, two or three days after the days of Tashrīq, whereas she may have begun menstruating on the eve of the Day of Sacrifice and may not become pure for seven days or more. She cannot remain in Mecca until she becomes pure, either because she lacks the means to support herself or because she has no travelling companions who will stay with her and return with her. She cannot stay in Mecca because she lacks one or the other, or because she fears harm to herself and her property if she stays and returns after the caravan.

As for her travelling companions, sometimes they cannot wait for her, either because they are unable to stay and make the return journey on their own, or because they fear harm to themselves

---

## Page 13001

and their property. At other times, they can do so but will not; she therefore remains excused. This, then, is the “issue” that has become so widespread.

If such a woman performs circumambulation while menstruating and makes compensation with a sacrificial offering or a badana (a camel or cow offered in sacrifice), this suffices according to those who hold that ritual purity is not a condition of validity, as previously explained concerning the position of Abū Hanīfa and one of the two positions reported from Ahmad. This applies all the more readily to her, since she has an excuse. Whether this excuse makes circumambulation permissible for her, however, requires consideration.

Likewise, according to those who regard ritual purity as a condition of validity: is this condition waived when she cannot fulfil it, so that her circumambulation is valid? This is what people need to know.

A tenable answer is that she need only fulfil those obligations she can perform, while those beyond her ability are waived; she may therefore perform circumambulation. She should take a ritual bath—even though she is menstruating—just as she does before entering ihrām, and with even greater reason here. She should also secure a cloth to contain the bleeding, as a woman with non-menstrual bleeding does, and with even greater reason. This is supported by several considerations:

First: only one of five courses is possible in her case. Either it is said that she must remain until she becomes pure and performs circumambulation, even if she has neither the means to support herself nor a place to stay in Mecca, even if she cannot return to her homeland, and even if remaining in Mecca exposes her to someone who forces her into illicit intercourse and takes her money, if she has any.

Or it is said that she should return without circumambulating the House and remain subject to the remaining restrictions of her ihrām until she can return; and if she cannot, she remains in ihrām

---

## Page 13002

until she dies.

Or it is said that she should leave ihrām just as someone prevented from completing the pilgrimage does, while completing Hajj remains an obligation for which she must return. This is like anyone prevented from reaching the House by an excuse: he leaves ihrām, but the obligation is not waived; rather, it remains incumbent upon him by scholarly agreement. If, however, he entered ihrām for a voluntary Hajj or ʿumrah and was then prevented from completing it, must he make it up? There are two well-known views, both reported from Ahmad. The better-known report from him is that he need not make it up; this is also the view of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī. The second is that he must make it up; this is the view of Abū Hanīfa.

Both sides cited ʿUmrat al-Qadiyya as evidence. Those who require it to be made up said, “The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, made it up.” The others said, “Those who had been prevented along with him did not make it up. They numbered more than fourteen hundred, whereas those who performed ʿUmrat al-Qadiyya with him the following year were far fewer.” They also said, “It was called ʿUmrat al-Qadiyya because he negotiated an agreement concerning it with the polytheists, not because he was making it up. Rather, it was an independent ʿumrah.”

Or it is said that a woman who fears she will menstruate and thus be unable to perform circumambulation in a state of ritual purity should not be instructed to perform Hajj, whether as an obligation or a recommended act. Half of women, or nearly half, begin menstruating either on the tenth or a few days beforehand, and their menstruation continues until one, two, or three days after the days of Tashrīq. Thus, in these times, in many or most years, these women cannot perform tawāf al-ifāda—the principal circumambulation of Hajj—in a state of ritual purity

---

## Page 13003

so they should not perform Hajj. If, nevertheless, one of them does perform Hajj, she must follow one of the three courses mentioned above, unless she is permitted to perform circumambulation while menstruating. It is clear that she may not be instructed to follow the first course, for it entails such harm to her religious and worldly affairs that it is self-evident that Allah forbids it, let alone commands it.

The second course is likewise impermissible, for three reasons.

First: Allah has not commanded anyone to remain in ihrām until death. Someone prevented from proceeding by an enemy may leave ihrām by scholarly agreement. As for someone prevented by illness or poverty, there is a well-known disagreement. Those who permit him to leave ihrām present no difficulty here. Those who prohibit him from doing so argue that leaving ihrām does not remove the harm caused by illness or poverty, unlike detention by an enemy, since leaving ihrām enables him to return to his homeland. They permit him to do whatever he needs to do of the acts normally prohibited in ihrām. Then, if he misses Hajj, he leaves ihrām by performing an ʿumrah on account of having missed it. Once the sick person recovers, he can proceed; and the poor person’s need for resources to complete his Hajj journey is the same as his need for them to return home. This, then, is their reasoning for not allowing him to leave ihrām. They say that he gains nothing by leaving it.

If this reasoning is sound, it stands; otherwise, the correct view is the first: that he may leave ihrām. This reasoning entails agreement among the imams that whenever remaining in ihrām causes harm that would be removed by leaving it, the person may leave ihrām.

---

## Page 13004

It is well known that if this woman remains in ihrām, she will remain permanently barred from sexual intercourse. Indeed, according to one of their two opinions, she is also barred from sexual foreplay, and even from contracting marriage, using perfume and hunting, according to those who maintain these prohibitions. Our Sharia does not prescribe anything of this kind.

Even supposing that some who hold that a person prevented from proceeding by illness or lack of funds must remain in ihrām were to maintain such a view—in the case of a sick person with no hope of recovery, or a poor person who can stay where he is but cannot travel—their position would be refuted by the fundamental principles of the Sharia. No jurist would say that Allah has commanded a person incapacitated by illness, with no hope of recovery, to remain in ihrām until death. The most that could be said is that he must appoint someone to perform Hajj on his behalf, as al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad held regarding the basic obligation of Hajj.

They therefore held Hajj to be obligatory for an incapacitated person if he possesses sufficient wealth for someone else to perform it on his behalf, since, in their view, the basis of the obligation is possession of provisions and a mount. According to Mālik, it is physical ability, however achieved; according to Abū Hanīfah, it is the combination of both. According to Ahmad, either of these two conditions independently establishes the obligation, so Hajj becomes obligatory in either case. None of the imams of the Muslims has said that an incapacitated person must perform Hajj or ʿumrah in person. How, then, could he remain in ihrām, obliged to complete Hajj until he dies?

Second: If this woman is able to return and does so, the same thing that happened on her first visit may happen on her second, if she can return only with the pilgrim company: she may menstruate throughout their stay in Mecca.

---

## Page 13005

Third: This would require a person to make two complete journeys for Hajj without any negligence or transgression on their part. This is contrary to the fundamental principles, for Allah has required people to perform Hajj only once. If someone who invalidates his Hajj is required to make it up, that is because he has violated his ihrām. If someone who misses Hajj is required to make it up, that is because of his negligence: standing at Arafāt has a prescribed time, which a person can ordinarily avoid missing. His delay therefore results from ignorance of the route or of how much time remains, or from failing to travel at the customary pace. All of this constitutes negligence on his part. The menstruating woman, by contrast, has not been negligent. This is why the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, exempted her from the farewell tawāf and the arrival tawāf, as in the hadiths of Ā’ishah and Safiyyah.

As for the third possibility—namely, that she may leave ihrām as someone prevented from completing the pilgrimage does—this is a stronger view, and a group of scholars have upheld it. Her fear prevents her from remaining until she can perform tawāf, just as an enemy in Mecca might prevent her from performing tawāf itself without preventing her from remaining there, according to the view that permits this. However, this alone does not discharge her obligation to perform Hajj, nor is a Muslim instructed to undertake a Hajj during which he will be prevented from completing it. Whoever believes that, if he undertakes Hajj, he will be prevented from reaching the House is not obliged to undertake it. Indeed, an unobstructed and safe route, together with sufficient time, is a condition for being obliged to set out, by the agreement of the Muslims.

They disagreed only over whether this is a condition for the obligation itself: if someone possesses provisions and a mount, but the route is unsafe or time is insufficient, does Hajj become obligatory for him, such that Hajj is to be performed

---

## Page 13006

on his behalf if he dies? Or is it not obligatory for him at all? There are two well-known views on this.

Those who allow her no concession other than that granted to someone prevented from completing the pilgrimage must therefore accept the fourth view: that she is not instructed to perform Hajj; indeed, it is neither obligatory nor recommended for her. On this reasoning, Hajj would remain unprescribed for many women—or most of them—in most of these circumstances, even though they can perform all its rites, merely because they cannot fulfil some of the obligations associated with tawāf.

It is well known that this contradicts the fundamental principles of the Sharia. When an act of worship is prescribed as obligatory or recommended, a person’s inability to fulfil some of its requirements does not release him from performing what he can. Rather, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“When I command you to do something, do as much of it as you can.”

This accords with Allah’s words, Exalted is He:

“So fear Allah as much as you are able.”

It is well known that prayer and other acts of worship greater than tawāf are not waived because a person cannot fulfil some of their conditions or essential elements. How, then, could Hajj be waived because a person cannot fulfil some of the conditions or essential elements of tawāf?

A similar view would be to say that she is exempted from tawāf al-ifādah. This, too, contradicts the fundamental principles, for Hajj consists of standing at Arafāt and performing tawāf, and tawāf is the better and more exalted of these two pillars. Hence, it is prescribed as part of Hajj, as part of ʿumrah, and as an independent act of worship. Moreover, it has conditions that are not required for standing at Arafāt. How, then, could Hajj be valid with standing at Arafāt but without tawāf?

---

## Page 13007

But a more plausible view than that would be to say that her tawāf al-ifādah before standing at Arafāt suffices. It might thus be said that, if she can perform tawāf after standing at Arafāt, she should do so; otherwise, she should perform it beforehand. Yet we know of no imam who has held this view in any circumstances or deemed such a tawāf sufficient, except for what the scholars of Basra reported from Mālik concerning someone who performed tawāf and saʿy before standing at Arafāt, then returned home out of forgetfulness or ignorance: that this fulfils the requirement of tawāf al-ifādah.

It has been suggested that, on this basis, the same could be said of a menstruating woman if she can only perform tawāf before standing at Arafāt. However, I know of no one who holds this view.

Regarding the case reported from Mālik, it might be argued that forgetfulness and ignorance constitute an excuse, and that requiring the person to return would entail great hardship; the prescribed order is therefore waived on account of this excuse. A similar view is held concerning ritual purity in one of the two juristic positions based on one of the two reports from Ahmad within his school: if someone forgetfully performs tawāf in a state of ritual impurity and then travels far away, he is excused and compensates for it with a sacrifice.
