But if he can fulfil most of the obligations, how can the act itself be waived because he is unable to fulfil some of them? Moreover, it has been held that a menstruating woman’s tawāf suffices without qualification, though she must offer a compensatory sacrifice.

As for deliberately performing the obligatory tawāf before standing at Arafāt, it does not suffice, without any disagreement. The requirement to make up missed prayers in their proper order is waived in cases of forgetfulness according to most

---

## Page 13008

scholars, but according to most of them it is not waived because one cannot fulfil some of the conditions of prayer or because time is short.

Furthermore, if a woman experiencing non-menstrual bleeding, someone suffering from urinary incontinence, or anyone in a similar situation could perform tawāf in a state of ritual purity before standing at Arafāt, but only in this state of ritual impurity afterward, they would perform tawāf only after standing at Arafāt. Likewise, a woman may not fast before Ramadan because she will menstruate during Ramadan; rather, she fasts after fasting has become obligatory.

Moreover, the established principles agree that whenever the choice is between failing to observe the prescribed time of an act of worship and failing to fulfil some of its conditions or essential components, the latter takes precedence. For example, if someone could pray before the prescribed time in a state of ritual purity, properly covered, facing the qiblah and free of physical impurity, but could not do so within the prescribed time, he must pray within that time as best he can, rather than beforehand. This is established by the Quran, the Sunnah and scholarly consensus.

Likewise, one must not delay an act of worship beyond its prescribed time, but must perform it within that time to the best of one’s ability. A person with a valid excuse is permitted to combine prayers only because there are two kinds of prescribed time: a time specific to each prayer for those without an excuse, and a shared time for those with valid excuses. Someone who combines two prayers has offered both within the prescribed time, neither missing either prayer nor offering it before the time at which it is valid, by scholarly consensus.

---

## Page 13009

The same applies to standing at Arafāt. Suppose someone could stand there before or after its prescribed time, but not within it: standing outside that time would not suffice, by scholarly consensus. Tawāf al-ifādah (the obligatory circumambulation) is prescribed after standing at Arafāt, and its time is the Day of Sacrifice and thereafter. Whether it suffices after midnight on the eve of the Day of Sacrifice is a well-known point of disagreement.

Once these four alternatives have been shown to be invalid, the remaining alternative is (the fifth): she does whatever she can, and whatever she cannot do is waived. This is what the scriptural texts applicable to this case and analogous established principles indicate. It does not contradict those principles. The texts indicating that ritual purity is obligatory, such as the Prophet’s saying (peace and blessings be upon him),

“A menstruating woman performs all the pilgrimage rites except tawāf around the House,”

merely establish the obligation in general terms. They are like his sayings:

“If any of you breaks his state of ritual purity, he must not pray until he performs ablution.”

“Allah does not accept the prayer of any of you until he performs ablution.”

“Allah does not accept the prayer of a woman who has reached puberty unless she wears a head covering.”

“Scrape it off, then rub it with your fingertips, then wash it, then pray in it.”

“No one may perform tawāf around the House naked.”

The same applies to similar texts.

It is known that all these obligations are conditional upon ability, as Allah Most High says:

“So fear Allah as much as you are able.”

And the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“When I command you to do something, do as much of it as you can.”

This exhausts the possible alternatives. Once it is clear that she cannot be ordered to remain when she is unable to do so and would suffer harm to herself

---

## Page 13010

and her religion and property, and that she cannot be ordered to remain in ihrām and return despite her inability, with repeated journeys and continuing harm through no negligence on her part. Nor is release from ihrām sufficient, nor does it discharge the obligation.

The same applies to the other conditions, such as covering oneself and avoiding impurity, which are more emphatically required in prayer. At most, tawaf may be likened to prayer. Unlike prayer, however, there is no scriptural text denying the acceptance of tawaf in the absence of ritual purity or bodily covering. There are, nevertheless, texts indicating that these are obligatory.

For this reason, scholars have disagreed over whether this is a condition of validity or an obligation that is not a condition of validity. They have not disputed that it is a condition for the validity of prayer, and that this entails ordering her to forgo Hajj. Yet she cannot be ordered to forgo Hajj except on the basis we have mentioned. This is what was to be established.

The second proof:

It may be said that, at most, ritual purity is a condition for tawaf. It is well known that its status as a condition for prayer is more firmly established than its status as a condition for tawaf. It is also well known that ritual purity is comparable to covering oneself and avoiding impurity. Indeed, covering oneself during tawaf is more emphatically obligatory than tawaf itself, because covering the private parts is obligatory both during tawaf and outside it, and because exposing them is among the practices of the polytheists that Allah and His Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him) prohibited in general terms. Furthermore, a woman experiencing non-menstrual bleeding, a person suffering from urinary incontinence, and others in similar circumstances may perform tawaf and pray by the consensus of the Muslims, and the ritual impurity affecting them is of the same kind as that affecting

---

## Page 13011

others, with nothing distinguishing the two cases except a valid excuse. Since this is so, and the conditions of prayer are waived when one is unable to fulfil them, there is all the more reason for the conditions of tawaf to be waived in such circumstances. A person may pray unclothed, or with ritual impurity and physical impurity, as in the case of a woman experiencing non-menstrual bleeding and others. A person may also pray with the major ritual impurity resulting from sexual activity or menstruation after performing dry ablution—or, according to the majority, even without dry ablution when neither water nor earth is available.

A menstruating woman, however, does not pray, because she has no need to pray during menstruation: the obligation is waived for her without any substitute being required. Prayer recurs day after day, so her prayers on the remaining days suffice without her having to make up those she missed. This is why she is commanded to make up missed fasts but not missed prayers. Fasting is prescribed for only one month each year; if she cannot fast in a state of ritual purity during Ramadan, she fasts outside Ramadan. Her obligation is thus not multiplied; rather, it is transferred from one time to another.

If, however, she were permanently unable to fast, like an elderly man or woman, or a sick person with no hope of recovery, the obligation would be waived for her. According to the majority, including Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, it is replaced by compensation consisting of feeding one needy person for each day. According to Mālik, it is waived without any substitute.

As for prayer, one cannot be incapable of all its essential elements; rather, one performs whatever one can of them. If a person were unable to make any outward movements with either his head or his body, the obligation of prayer would be waived according to one of the two scholarly views. This is the view of Abū Hanīfah and of Ahmad in one of

---

## Page 13012

the two reports from him, as well as one of the two views in Mālik’s school. According to the other view, he gestures with his eyes and calls the actions to mind. This is the view of al-Shāfiʿī and of Ahmad in one of the two reports from him. The first view is more consistent with transmitted evidence and sound reasoning.

As for Hajj, the case under discussion is one in which she can perform it only in this manner. If she cannot do otherwise, this is the utmost she is capable of, just as someone might be able to circumambulate only while riding or with physical impurity on him.

If it is said: “There are two questions here. First, why is a menstruating woman not treated like a physically incapacitated person, appointing a proxy unless she expects to be able to perform Hajj and circumambulate? Second, since the Lawgiver does not permit her to pray during menstruation, although He permits a person in a state of major ritual impurity following sexual activity to pray after dry ablution, and permits a woman experiencing non-menstrual bleeding to pray, this shows that worship cannot be valid during menstruation under any circumstances.”

The reply is: As for the first question, a physically incapacitated person is someone unable to reach Mecca. Someone who can reach Mecca but is unable to fulfil some of the obligations is not considered physically incapacitated in this sense. This is like someone who can reach Mecca but cannot avoid physical impurity, such as a woman experiencing non-menstrual bleeding, a person with urinary incontinence, or others in similar circumstances: such a person is obliged to perform Hajj by consensus.

---

## Page 13013

and whatever requirements of purification he cannot fulfil are waived for him. The same applies to someone who can circumambulate only while riding or being carried, or who cannot stone the pillars, and similar cases: he appoints a proxy for what he cannot perform and undertakes Hajj in person.

As for prayer during menstruation, a woman has no need to perform it, since her prayers on the remaining days suffice. Thus, when she experiences non-menstrual bleeding, she is commanded to pray despite that bleeding and despite the possibility that she may be praying during menstruation. Were it not for her valid excuse, the discharge of that blood and the physical impurity it causes her would invalidate her prayer. The Lawgiver has therefore distinguished in this matter between someone with a valid excuse and someone without one. Accordingly, if a woman experiencing non-menstrual bleeding can purify herself and pray when the bleeding stops, she must do so. Prayer while the bleeding continues has been permitted only out of necessity.

If it is said:

“The obligation to pray could have been waived for someone in a state of major ritual impurity following sexual activity, a woman experiencing non-menstrual bleeding, and others in similar circumstances, just as it was waived for a menstruating woman, with their prayers on the remaining days sufficing. Since the Lawgiver commanded a woman experiencing non-menstrual bleeding to pray, but not a menstruating woman, this shows that menstruation is incompatible with prayer under all circumstances. Likewise, it is incompatible with circumambulation, which is akin to prayer.”

The reply is:

For someone in a state of major ritual impurity following sexual activity, and others in similar circumstances, the condition necessitating purification does not persist. Rather, he is like a menstruating woman whose bleeding has ceased, and he is able to perform one of the two forms of purification. As for a woman experiencing non-menstrual bleeding, if the obligation to pray were waived for her, it would necessarily be waived forever. Since her state of ritual impurity is continuous, she can pray only while in that state, so the obligation of purification is waived for her. This is therefore evidence that

---

## Page 13014

when an act of worship cannot be performed except in conjunction with something prohibited, performing it in that manner is preferable to abandoning it. All the established principles support this. Someone in a state of major ritual impurity following sexual activity who has neither water nor earth must also pray, according to the better-known of the two scholarly views, because he is unable to purify himself. Menstruation, however, is incompatible with prayer under all circumstances, because there is no need to pray during menstruation: the recurrence of equivalent prayers suffices.

By contrast, the obligation to perform Hajj and its circumambulation does not recur. If it were invalid despite a valid excuse, it would necessarily be invalid altogether. The established principles show that when an act of worship can only be performed with a valid excuse, it is valid and discharges the obligation despite that excuse, unlike when it is performed without such an excuse. It has already become clear that a menstruating woman has no valid excuse for praying during menstruation, since the recurrence of equivalent prayers outside her menstrual days suffices her. Circumambulation is different: if she can perform it only during menstruation, she has no equivalent act that would render it unnecessary. She is therefore permitted to perform it in that state, just as other conditions of worship are waived when she cannot fulfil them.

The third proof:

It may be argued that this is one form of purification, so its requirement is waived when one is unable to fulfil it, just as with other forms of purification. If she were experiencing non-menstrual bleeding and could circumambulate only in a state of continuous ritual impurity, she would circumambulate by scholarly agreement. There is a well-known disagreement among scholars as to whether ablution is obligatory for her. This involves praying both in a state of ritual impurity and while bearing physical impurity. Likewise, if someone in a state of major ritual impurity following sexual activity, or someone in a state of minor ritual impurity, has no access to either water or earth, he prays and circumambulates according to the stronger of the two scholarly views.

The fourth proof is to say:

This is a condition of circumambulation, and so it is waived

---

## Page 13015

when one is unable to fulfil it, like other conditions. If a person could circumambulate only while naked, doing so would be less objectionable than praying naked. Yet praying in that state is obligatory by consensus; all the more, then, must he circumambulate naked if that is his only option. Scholars have discussed this little only because it is rare: hardly anyone in Mecca is unable to obtain something with which to cover himself during circumambulation. But suppose that someone were stripped of his clothes and his caravan were departing, and he could not remain behind. He would then be obliged to perform whatever circumambulation he could while naked, just as a woman experiencing non-menstrual bleeding and someone with urinary incontinence perform circumambulation, even though the prohibition against circumambulating naked is clearer and better known in the Quran and Sunnah than the prohibition against a menstruating woman circumambulating.

What I have stated follows from the explicitly stated general principles that encompass this case in both wording and meaning. It also follows from reasoning and analogy with comparable established principles. Anyone opposing this has merely found no statement by the recognized scholarly authorities concerning this particular situation, just as he has found no statement by them concerning someone who can circumambulate only while naked. Situations that did not arise in their times need not necessarily have occurred to them such that they would have been obliged to discuss them. These two situations either never arose in their times or arose only very rarely.

Their statements on this subject are unqualified and general. That would imply general applicability were the particular case not distinguished by considerations requiring it to be treated differently and separately. This particular case may not have been in mind when such general wording was used by one of

---

## Page 13016

the scholarly authorities, because such a situation did not arise in their time. Their followers reported the statements they found from them. Thus, Mālik and others required the man she had hired for transport to wait for her, provided the roads were safe and remaining behind with her would cause him no harm. In the time of the Companions and others, the leader would wait for menstruating women. Later scholars among Mālik’s followers waived the farewell circumambulation for the hired carrier. The obligation to stay overnight was also waived for those providing water and tending livestock because of their inability to fulfil it.

Their inability necessitates remaining behind with her in these times. There is no doubt that anyone who holds that ritual purity is obligatory for circumambulation, but is not a condition of its validity, would consequently be bound to say that ritual purity is not obligatory in such circumstances, since it cannot be attained. For he holds that someone who circumambulates in a state of ritual impurity and then travels far from Mecca is not obliged to return, owing to the hardship involved. How, then, could this woman be required to do something she can accomplish only with even greater hardship?

In the former case, however, some maintain that a compensatory sacrifice is required. In this woman’s case, there are grounds for holding that no compensatory sacrifice is required, because someone who omits an obligation without negligence owes no compensatory sacrifice, unlike someone who omits it through forgetfulness or ignorance. It may nevertheless be argued that she owes a compensatory sacrifice because this situation is rare.

A comparable case is that of someone whom an enemy prevents from stoning the pillar, so that he cannot do so until he returns to Mecca; or whom an enemy prevents from remaining at Arafat until nightfall; or whom an enemy prevents from performing the farewell circumambulation, when he cannot remain until he can perform it.

---

## Page 13017

It is established in an authentic report that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, exempted menstruating women from the farewell circumambulation. As for those who maintain that ritual purity is obligatory for circumambulation and a condition of its validity, its status as a condition is no greater here than in prayer. It is well known that the conditions of prayer are waived when one is unable to fulfil them; there is therefore all the more reason to waive the conditions of circumambulation when one is unable to fulfil them.

This is the conclusion that appears sound to me on this issue. There is no power or strength except through Allah, the Most High, the Magnificent. Were it not for people’s pressing need for guidance on this issue, both in understanding and in practice, I would not have undertaken to discuss it, since I have found no discussion of it by anyone else. Exercising independent legal reasoning when necessity demands it is among the things Allah has commanded us to do.

If what I have said is correct, then it is the ruling of Allah and His Messenger, and all praise is due to Allah. If what I have said is mistaken, then it is from me and from Satan. Allah and His Messenger are free of error, even if the person who errs is pardoned.

Allah, glorified and exalted is He, knows best. All praise is due to Allah alone. May Allah bless Muhammad and his family and grant them peace.

---

## Page 13018

He was also asked—may Allah sanctify his soul—about a woman whose menstrual period began before the main circumambulation of Hajj (tawāf al-ifādah) and had not ended by the time the pilgrims departed, while she could not remain behind until it ended:

May she perform circumambulation in this condition out of necessity? If this is permissible, must she make a sacrificial offering? Is it recommended for her to take a ritual bath nonetheless?

If a woman knows from her usual menstrual cycle that her period will not end before the pilgrims depart, and she cannot remain behind, is Hajj still obligatory for her? If it is not obligatory, is it recommended for her to proceed and perform circumambulation? Give us a ruling; may you be rewarded.

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah. Scholars hold two well-known views on whether ritual purity is a condition for the validity of circumambulation.

The first is that it is a condition. This is the position of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī, and one of the two positions reported from Ahmad.

The second is that it is not a condition. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and the other position reported from Ahmad.

---

## Page 13019

According to the latter view, if someone performs circumambulation while in a state of major or minor ritual impurity, or while carrying something ritually impure, his circumambulation is valid, but he must make a sacrificial offering. Ahmad’s followers differed, however, over whether this applies without qualification to someone with an excuse, such as a person who forgot that he was in a state of major ritual impurity. Abū Hanīfah requires the offering to be a badanah (large sacrificial animal) if the woman is menstruating or in a state of major ritual impurity.

A woman who cannot perform circumambulation except while menstruating has an even stronger claim to be excused. Hajj is obligatory for her, and no scholar has said that menstruation exempts a woman from Hajj. It is not a position recognized in Islamic law that obligatory acts cease to be binding because one cannot fulfil some of their requirements—as when someone is unable to attain ritual purity for prayer.

If she can remain in Mecca until her menstruation ends and then perform circumambulation, she must undoubtedly do so. If that is not possible, however, requiring her to return a second time would impose two journeys for Hajj upon her through no fault of her own. This is contrary to Islamic law. Moreover, she can travel only with the caravan, and her menstruation occurs each month as usual; thus, she cannot perform circumambulation in a state of ritual purity at all.

The principles of Islamic law establish that a person is excused from fulfilling any condition of worship that is beyond their ability. This applies, for example, when someone praying cannot cover the parts of the body that must be covered, face the direction of prayer, or avoid ritual impurities. Likewise, if someone cannot perform circumambulation independently, whether riding or walking, he is carried around to perform it.

As for those who hold that her circumambulation without ritual purity is valid when she has no excuse

---

## Page 13020

provided she makes a sacrificial offering, as some followers of Abū Hanīfah and Ahmad hold, it is all the more fitting and appropriate for them to affirm its validity when she does have an excuse. As for bathing, it is good if she does so, just as women who are menstruating or experiencing postnatal bleeding bathe before entering ihrām. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

What should a woman do if her menstruation begins at the time of circumambulation?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

A menstruating woman performs all the pilgrimage rites except circumambulation of the House. She must make every effort to circumambulate the House only when ritually pure. If she cannot do so and cannot remain behind after the caravan departs until she becomes pure and performs circumambulation, then, according to one of the two scholarly views, her circumambulation of visitation while menstruating is valid.

Abū Hanīfah and others held that it is valid even if she has no excuse, but he required her to offer a badanah (large sacrificial animal). Ahmad, however, required someone who omitted ritual purification out of forgetfulness to make a sacrificial offering consisting of a sheep. As for this woman who cannot perform circumambulation while ritually pure, offering a sacrifice is the more cautious course; otherwise, it is not clear that she owes anything. For Allah does not burden any soul beyond its capacity.

And He, Most High, said:

“So fear Allah as much as you are able.”

And the Prophet said:

---

## Page 13021

(may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“When I command you to do something, do as much of it as you can.”

This woman can fulfill all the requirements except this one. Prayer is more important than circumambulation, yet if someone cannot fulfill its conditions—ritual purification, covering the parts of the body that must be concealed, or facing the qiblah—he prays as his circumstances allow. This concession applies all the more to circumambulation.

If she were experiencing non-menstrual bleeding and could only circumambulate with the impurity of blood upon her, she would pray and circumambulate in that condition, by agreement of the Muslims, provided she performed ablution, cleansed herself, and did whatever she could. When a menstruating woman circumambulates, she should bathe and secure a cloth to contain the bleeding—that is, take protective measures—just as she does when entering ihrām.

The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) waived the farewell circumambulation for menstruating women. He also waived the overnight stay at Minā for those responsible for supplying water and for herdsmen because of their need, and did not require them to make a sacrificial offering, since they had an excuse, unlike others.

Likewise, anyone unable to stone the pillars himself because of illness or a similar reason appoints someone to do so on his behalf and owes nothing further. Someone who omits an obligation because he is unable to fulfill it is not like someone who omits it for another reason. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 13022

He was asked:

About a woman who performed Hajj, entering ihrām for ‘umrah and Hajj together. She entered Mecca, circumambulated the Ka‘bah, performed the sa‘y, and proceeded to Minā, then to ‘Arafah, where she stood. She then returned to Minā, and the sacrificial offering required of her was slaughtered on her behalf. She stoned the pillars on one day, entered Mecca, and circumambulated the Ka‘bah. When she came to the Sacred Mosque, she began menstruating. She returned to Minā and concealed what had happened, convinced that her Hajj was complete. She returned to her homeland, and two years later disclosed what had happened to her. She was told, “You must return,” but her husband would not allow her to do so under these circumstances.

He replied:

If she performed the obligatory circumambulation while menstruating, with the intention of performing it, then under these circumstances her Hajj was valid according to one of the two scholarly opinions. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and of Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him. The most she owes according to Abū Hanīfah is a large sacrificial animal, while according to Ahmad she owes a sacrificial offering consisting of a sheep.

If, however, she did not perform that circumambulation, she has attained the initial release from ihrām. She may therefore use perfume, cover her face, and do other such things, but her husband must not have intercourse with her until she performs the obligatory circumambulation. If she cannot return, the furthest concession that might be granted her is that she

---

## Page 13023

may be treated as a woman prevented from completing her pilgrimage and come out of ihrām by means of a sacrificial offering. However, the more cautious course is for her to send it to Mecca to be slaughtered—for example, on the Day of Sacrifice. Once it is slaughtered there, she comes out of ihrām where she is, and her husband may then have intercourse with her. If she arranges with someone to slaughter it there on a specified day, she comes out of ihrām on that day.

If she is subsequently able to go to Mecca, she should enter it in ihrām for ‘umrah and perform the circumambulation that she still owes. Then, if she wishes, she may perform Hajj from there. If she remains unable to do so until she dies, “Allah does not burden a soul beyond its capacity.” If someone can be sent after her death to perform this on her behalf, that should be done.

If her husband had intercourse with her before this circumambulation, that does not invalidate her Hajj, although it invalidates what remains. She must still perform the obligatory circumambulation, as all the imams agree, as mentioned above. However, according to Mālik and Ahmad, she must enter ihrām for ‘umrah, as reported from Ibn ‘Abbās. According to the well-known position of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfi‘ī, performing it without entering ihrām anew suffices. This applies if she is still there.

If, however, she has returned to her homeland and her husband has had intercourse with her, then upon returning she must enter ihrām for ‘umrah at the prescribed boundary, for entering Mecca in ihrām for Hajj or ‘umrah is either obligatory or recommended, except for those who need to enter repeatedly and similar cases.

---

## Page 13024

Abū al-‘Abbās was asked:

Which is better for someone in Mecca: circumambulating the House, or going outside the sacred precincts to enter ihrām for ‘umrah and then returning? Is someone in Mecca encouraged to perform ‘umrah frequently, during Ramadan or at other times, or to circumambulate the House instead?

Likewise, is frequently performing ‘umrah recommended for someone who is not a Meccan? Do the Prophet’s ‘umrah from al-Ji‘rānah and his ‘umrah at al-Hudaybiyah provide a basis for someone to perform ‘umrah starting from Mecca, as does his instruction to ‘Ā’ishah to perform ‘umrah from al-Tan‘īm? And when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, “An ‘umrah in Ramadan is equivalent to a Hajj,” does this refer to the ‘umrah of someone coming from afar, or does it also include a Meccan who goes outside the sacred precincts to perform ‘umrah in Ramadan?

He answered:

For anyone in Mecca—whether a permanent resident, a temporary resident, a newly arrived visitor, or anyone else—circumambulating the House is better than performing ‘umrah. This applies whether, for that ‘umrah, he goes to the nearest point outside the sacred precincts, namely al-Tan‘īm, where the mosques known as “‘Ā’ishah’s Mosques” were subsequently built, or far beyond the sacred precincts in any direction, whether towards al-Ji‘rānah, al-Hudaybiyah, or elsewhere. This is agreed upon among the early generations of the Muslim community, and I know of no imam of Islam who disagreed concerning ‘umrah undertaken from Mecca.

---

## Page 13025

As for ‘umrah from the mīqāt (the designated boundary for entering ihrām)—where someone goes to the mīqāt and enters ihrām there, or returns to his homeland and then sets out from there on a journey for ‘umrah—this is not an ‘umrah undertaken from Mecca. Rather, it is a complete ‘umrah, and it is not what is being discussed here.

There is disagreement concerning this: is remaining in Mecca better than undertaking such an ‘umrah, or is returning to one’s homeland or to the mīqāt better? The statements of some who preferred remaining in Mecca to circumambulate the House over returning to perform ‘umrah from the mīqāt will be cited later.

The dispute is only over whether it is disliked for a Meccan to go outside the sacred precincts to perform ‘umrah; whether it is disliked for someone for whom ‘umrah is prescribed, such as someone coming from afar, to perform it more than once a year; and whether performing ‘umrah frequently is recommended.

As for circumambulating the House being better than performing ‘umrah for someone in Mecca, no one knowledgeable about the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the practice of his caliphs, and the reports from the Companions, the early generations of the Muslim community, and its imams would doubt this. Circumambulating the House is among the finest acts of worship and means of drawing near to Allah that He, Exalted is He, has prescribed in His Book and through His Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. It is among the greatest acts of worship performed by the people of Mecca—by which I mean anyone in Mecca, whether permanently resident or not—and among their regular, ongoing acts of worship that distinguish them from the inhabitants of all other cities. Throughout the time of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, his caliphs and his Companions—may Allah be pleased with them—the people of Mecca would circumambulate the House at every

---

## Page 13026

time, and would do so frequently.

Likewise, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed the custodians of the House not to prevent anyone from doing so at any time. Jubayr ibn Mut‘im narrated that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“O descendants of ‘Abd Manāf, do not prevent anyone from circumambulating this House and praying there at whatever hour of the night or day he wishes.”

[Reported by Muslim in his Sahīh] (*), and by the other compilers of the Sunan collections, such as Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī, al-Nasā’ī, Ibn Mājah and others.

Allah, Exalted is He, said to His close friend, the leader of the pure monotheists, whom He commanded to build the House and who called people to perform pilgrimage to it:

“Purify My House for those who circumambulate it, those who remain there in devotion, and those who bow and prostrate.”

In the other verse, He says:

“And those who stand in prayer.”

Thus, He mentioned three kinds of worship: circumambulation, remaining in devotion, and bowing together with prostration. He placed them in order, beginning with the most restricted in scope, for circumambulation is prescribed only around the Ancient House, by the consensus of the Muslims. Hence, they agree that whoever circumambulates anything else is misguided—for example, someone who circumambulates the Rock, the chamber of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the mosques built at ‘Arafah, Minā or elsewhere, or the grave of a shaykh or a member of the Prophet’s household, as many ignorant Muslims do.

Circumambulating anything other than the Ancient House is impermissible by the consensus of the Muslims. Moreover, anyone who believes this to be part of religion and a means of drawing near to Allah is informed that, by the consensus of the Muslims, it is not part of religion, and that this is something necessarily known in the religion of Islam. If he persists in treating it as part of religion, he is put to death.

> **Footnotes:**
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd (p. 266) said: “Muslim did not narrate it.”

---

## Page 13027

As for “remaining in devotion,” it is prescribed in mosques alone. Bowing together with prostration, however, is prescribed throughout the earth, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The earth has been made for me a place of prayer and a means of purification. Thus, whenever the time for prayer comes upon any man of my community, he has at hand his place of prayer and his means of purification.”

All of this is agreed upon among Muslims, although prayer is prohibited in certain places because of some incidental characteristic, such as impurity, or their being a cemetery, a latrine, or the like.

The point here is that He, Glorified and Exalted is He, mentioned these acts in order, beginning with the most restricted in terms of location. He mentioned circumambulation first because it is specific to the Sacred Mosque, then remaining in devotion because it may take place there and in the mosques where Muslims perform the prescribed prayers—the five daily prayers in congregation—and then prayer, because the places where it may be performed are more extensive.

Among the distinctive features of circumambulation is that it is prescribed as an independent act of worship, as part of ‘umrah, and as part of Hajj. No pilgrimage rite is prescribed independently of Hajj or ‘umrah except circumambulation. The pilgrimage rites fall into three categories:

Some take place only during Hajj: standing at ‘Arafah and the associated rites at Muzdalifah.

Some take place only during Hajj or ‘umrah: entering the state of consecration and being released from it,

---

## Page 13028

and going back and forth between the two hills, as the Exalted says:

“Indeed, al-Safā and al-Marwah are among the symbols of Allah. So whoever makes Hajj to the House or performs ‘umrah, there is no blame upon him for going between them.”

Some rites take place during Hajj and ‘umrah and may also be performed independently: namely, circumambulation. Circumambulation is also the most frequently performed rite during Hajj. The arrival circumambulation is prescribed for the arriving pilgrim, and the farewell circumambulation is prescribed for the pilgrim departing after Hajj. These are in addition to the obligatory circumambulation, known as tawāf al-ifādah, which takes place after standing at ‘Arafah. Circumambulation is also recommended during one’s stay at Minā, and is generally recommended throughout the year.

As for someone in Mecca performing ‘umrah by going outside the Sacred Precinct, no one ever did this during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, except ‘Ā’ishah during the Farewell Pilgrimage. Even then, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not instruct her to do so; rather, he permitted it after she repeatedly appealed to him, as we shall explain, if Allah the Exalted wills.

As for his Companions who performed the Farewell Pilgrimage with him, not a single one of them went out, either before or after that pilgrimage, to al-Tan‘īm, al-Hudaybiyah, al-Ji‘rānah, or anywhere else, for the purpose of performing ‘umrah. Likewise, none of the permanent residents of Mecca went outside the Sacred Precinct to perform ‘umrah. This is agreed upon and known to all scholars acquainted with his Sunnah and his revealed law.

---

## Page 13029

Likewise, none of his Companions who resided in Mecca from the time of its conquest in Ramadan of 8 AH until his death performed ‘umrah by setting out from Mecca. None of them went outside the Sacred Precinct to enter ihrām there. Nor did the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ever perform ‘umrah while based in Mecca, whether from al-Hudaybiyah, al-Ji‘rānah, or anywhere else. Rather, he performed four ‘umrahs: three independently and one together with his Hajj. In every one of them, he was arriving in Mecca, not leaving it to go outside the Sacred Precinct.

As for the ‘umrah of al-Hudaybiyah, he and his Companions—who pledged allegiance to him beneath the tree during that ‘umrah—entered ihrām at Dhū al-Hulayfah, the designated point for the people of Medina to enter ihrām. When the polytheists prevented them from reaching the House, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concluded an agreement with them to perform ‘umrah the following year and made the well-known peace treaty with them. He and his Companions then left the state of ihrām for ‘umrah at al-Hudaybiyah, without entering Mecca that year. Concerning this, Allah the Exalted revealed Sūrat al-Fath and His words:

“And complete Hajj and ‘umrah for Allah. But if you are prevented, then offer whatever sacrificial animal is available”—and the remainder of the verse.

Al-Shāfi‘ī and others have reported a consensus that this verse was revealed that year. Then, in the following year, 7 AH, after conquering Khaybar—which took place shortly after his return from al-Hudaybiyah—he and those with him performed ‘umrat al-qadiyyah, also called “‘umrat al-qadā’.” This ‘umrah took place in Dhū al-Qa‘dah of 7 AH, while the preceding one, the ‘umrah of al-Hudaybiyah, had also taken place in Dhū al-Qa‘dah.

---

## Page 13030

and the ‘umrah of al-Ji‘rānah also took place in Dhū al-Qa‘dah. All his ‘umrahs took place in Dhū al-Qa‘dah, the middle month of the Hajj months. Through this, he demonstrated to the Muslims that performing ‘umrah during the Hajj months is permissible. When he and those with him performed ‘umrat al-qadiyyah, they likewise entered ihrām at Dhū al-Hulayfah, entered Mecca, and stayed there for three days. That year, he married Maymūnah bint al-Hārith.

Then, in 8 AH, the people of Mecca broke the treaty, so the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, marched against them in Ramadan with approximately ten thousand men in the campaign of the Conquest. He entered Mecca without being in ihrām, wearing a headguard, and circumambulated the House. He stayed in Mecca for seventeen nights and did not perform ‘umrah during this visit. When he learned that Hawāzin had mustered against him, he marched against them in the campaign of Hunayn. After that, he besieged al-Tā’if but did not conquer it. He distributed the spoils of Hunayn at al-Ji‘rānah and then set out from there to perform ‘umrah. Thus, in that ‘umrah, he was arriving in Mecca; he had not gone out from Mecca to al-Ji‘rānah.

Anyone setting out to perform Hajj or ‘umrah from a place on the Meccan side of the designated points of ihrām must enter ihrām at that place. As related in the two Sahīh collections, Ibn ‘Abbās said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, designated Dhū al-Hulayfah for the people of Medina, al-Juhfah for the people of Syria, Qarn al-Manāzil for the people of Najd, and Yalamlam for the people of Yemen. These points are for their people and for anyone from elsewhere who passes through them intending to perform Hajj or ‘umrah. Anyone living nearer Mecca than these points enters ihrām from his home. Likewise, the people of Mecca enter ihrām from Mecca.”

---

## Page 13031

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, entered ihrām at al-Ji‘rānah because he began his ‘umrah there, after having arrived there in connection with the campaign and the spoils. It is thus clear that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not enter ihrām at al-Hudaybiyah, either when approaching Mecca or after leaving it. Rather, it was where he ended his ihrām for ‘umrah when the polytheists prevented him from proceeding. As for al-Ji‘rānah, he entered ihrām there for an ‘umrah that he began from that place. All of this is agreed upon and known through mass transmission; no two people with even the slightest knowledge of the Prophet’s life and Sunnah dispute it.

Anyone who imagines that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, left Mecca and then performed ‘umrah from al-Hudaybiyah or al-Ji‘rānah has committed a gross and outrageous error. Such a claim could only come from someone utterly unfamiliar with the Prophet’s Sunnah and life, although groups of the most eminent scholars have erred in citing this as evidence for performing ‘umrah from Mecca.

It has thus become clear that neither the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor any of his Companions performed ‘umrah from Mecca during his lifetime after its conquest and its becoming a land of Islam, except ‘Ā’ishah. Likewise, no one performed ‘umrah from it before the Conquest, when it was a land of unbelief, even though some of the Prophet’s Companions were there both before and after his migration to Medina. They would circumambulate the House, but none of them went beyond the sanctuary’s boundaries to perform ‘umrah from there, for circumambulating the House had remained a prescribed act of worship from the very beginning of the mission of

---

## Page 13032

the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Indeed, it had remained so since the time of Abraham, and even before Abraham. Whenever the Muslims were in Mecca, from the beginning of the Prophet’s mission until his death, they did not perform ‘umrah from Mecca. Rather, they circumambulated the House and performed Hajj year after year, and they would circumambulate at any time without performing ‘umrah. This establishes with self-evident certainty that the prescribed practice for the people of Mecca is to circumambulate the House, and that this is better for them than going out to perform ‘umrah. For it is inconceivable that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and all his Companions during his lifetime would consistently observe the less meritorious practice and abandon the better one, with none of them undertaking the better practice and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, never encouraging them to do so. No believer would maintain such a thing.

This is further clarified by the following:

Muslims have differed over whether ‘umrah is obligatory, as Hajj is, with two well-known scholarly positions on the matter. Disagreement over this has also been reported from the Companions: the view that it is obligatory is reported from ‘Umar, Ibn ‘Abbās and others, while the view that it is not obligatory is reported from Ibn Mas‘ūd.

The first is the well-known position of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad.

The second is also one of the two positions reported from each of them, and is the position of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik. Nevertheless, the explicit reports from those Companions and Successors who held ‘umrah to be obligatory show that they did not consider it obligatory for the people of Mecca.

Ahmad ibn Hanbal said:

Ibn ‘Abbās held that ‘umrah was obligatory, yet would say: “People of Mecca, ‘umrah is not obligatory for you; your ‘umrah is simply your circumambulation of the House.”

And ‘Atā’ ibn Abī Rabāh—the most knowledgeable of the Successors regarding the pilgrimage rites

---

## Page 13033

and the leading authority on them—said: “Everyone among Allah’s creation who is able to undertake them is obliged to perform Hajj once and ‘umrah once, except the people of Mecca. They are obliged to perform Hajj, but not ‘umrah, because they circumambulate the House, and this suffices for them.”

Tāwūs said: “‘Umrah is not obligatory for the people of Mecca.” This was narrated by Ibn Abī Shaybah.

The statements of these early scholars and others imply that they did not regard ‘umrah as recommended for the people of Mecca, let alone obligatory. Thus, Abū Bakr ibn Abī Shaybah narrated in his major work, al-Musannaf:

Ibn Idrīs narrated to us, from Ibn Jurayj, from ‘Atā’, who said: “‘Umrah is not obligatory for the people of Mecca.”

Ibn ‘Abbās said: “People of Mecca, there is no ‘umrah for you; your ‘umrah is simply circumambulating the House. But whoever puts a valley floor between himself and the Sacred Precinct must not enter Mecca except in a state of ihrām (ritual consecration).”

He said: “I asked ‘Atā’, ‘Does Ibn ‘Abbās mean a valley outside the Sacred Precinct?’ He replied, ‘The floor of a valley outside the Sacred Precinct.’”

He also said: Sufyān ibn ‘Uyaynah narrated to us, from ‘Amr ibn Dīnār, from Ibn Kaysān, who said: “I heard Ibn ‘Abbās say: ‘It does you no harm, people of Mecca, not to perform ‘umrah. But if you insist, put a valley floor between yourselves and the Sacred Precinct.’”

He also said: Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd al-Qattān narrated to us, from Ibn Jurayj, from Khalaf ibn Muslim, from Sālim, who said: “If I were one of the people of Mecca, I would not perform ‘umrah.”

He also said: ‘Ubayd Allāh ibn Mūsā narrated to us, from ‘Uthmān, from ‘Atā’, who said: “‘Umrah is not obligatory for the people of Mecca. ‘Umrah is performed by those who visit the House to circumambulate it, whereas the people of Mecca circumambulate whenever they wish.”

---

## Page 13034

Ahmad explicitly stated in several places that ‘umrah is not obligatory for the people of Mecca, although he held it to be obligatory for others. Thus, the accurate formulation of his position, when he holds ‘umrah to be obligatory, is that it is obligatory for everyone except the people of Mecca. Some of his followers, however, have treated this distinction as a third position reported from him, considering his position affirming its obligation to apply universally. Others have interpreted his words to mean that the people of Mecca need not perform ‘umrah alongside Hajj, because they perform it beforehand, outside the time of Hajj. This, however, contradicts Ahmad’s explicit statements making this distinction.

Some followers of al-Shāfi‘ī likewise hold that ‘umrah is obligatory for the people of Mecca. This is an exceedingly weak view, contrary to the established Sunnah and the consensus of the Companions. Had it been obligatory for them, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would have commanded them to perform it, and they would have done so. Yet it is known that the people of Mecca did not perform ‘umrah at all during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Indeed, no one can produce a report of anyone setting out from Mecca to perform ‘umrah during his lifetime except ‘Ā’ishah.

For this reason, when the compilers of Sunan collections wished to mention the reports in the Sunnah concerning ‘umrah undertaken from Mecca, they had nothing but the account of ‘Ā’ishah. It is well known that even matters of lesser significance attract ample interest and incentive to transmit them. Thus, if all the people of Mecca—nay,

---

## Page 13035

even some of them—had gone beyond the sanctuary during the lifetime of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to undertake ‘umrah from there, this would have been transmitted, just as their departure for ‘Arafāt during Hajj was transmitted. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed the Farewell Pilgrimage, and the people of Mecca went out with him to ‘Arafāt. Yet no one, whether from Mecca or elsewhere, performed ‘umrah from the nearest point outside the sanctuary, either before or after that pilgrimage, except ‘Ā’ishah. This remained the practice throughout the time of the Rightly Guided Caliphs.

Eventually, as people grew further removed from the age of prophethood, Ibn ‘Abbās, followed by ‘Atā’ and others, said:

“O people of Mecca, ‘umrah is not incumbent upon you; your ‘umrah is simply to circumambulate the House.”

It is well known that, had the people of Mecca performed ‘umrah during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and been commanded to do so, such a practice would not have been unknown to Ibn ‘Abbās, the leading scholar of Mecca and the most knowledgeable person in the Muslim community in his time concerning the pilgrimage rites and other matters.

The same applies to ‘Atā’, who succeeded him as the leading scholar of Mecca—indeed, the foremost authority among all people on the pilgrimage rites. Thus, it used to be said of the four leading scholars among the Successors, who were the authorities of the major cities:

“Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyab was the leading scholar of Medina; ‘Atā’ ibn Abī Rabāh, the leading scholar of Mecca; Ibrāhīm al-Nakha‘ī, the leading scholar of Kufa; and al-Hasan al-Basrī, the leading scholar of Basra. The most knowledgeable of them concerning the lawful and unlawful was Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyab; concerning the pilgrimage rites, ‘Atā’; and concerning prayer, Ibrāhīm. Al-Hasan was the most comprehensive in his learning.”

Moreover, both Hajj and ‘umrah entail journeying to the House of

---

## Page 13036

Allah, which is surrounded by the sanctuary of Allah Most High. For this reason, a pilgrim must include both the area outside the sanctuary and the sanctuary itself in his rites, so that he approaches the sanctuary from outside it. This gives expression to the meaning of seeking Allah and turning toward His House and sanctuary. Whoever lives outside the sanctuary thus approaches the sanctuary and the House from outside its boundaries.

As for those within the sanctuary, such as the people of Mecca, they must go out to ‘Arafāt during Hajj, and ‘Arafāt lies outside the sanctuary. When they depart from ‘Arafāt, they then approach the House from outside the sanctuary. This is why the obligatory circumambulation takes place only after the standing at ‘Arafāt. This journey from outside the sanctuary to the Ka‘bah constitutes the very essence of Hajj, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Hajj is ‘Arafāt.”

Accordingly, Hajj is secured by attending the standing at ‘Arafāt and is missed when its allotted time expires at dawn on the Day of Sacrifice, following the day of the standing at ‘Arafāt. The essence of Hajj is therefore attainable for the people of Mecca just as it is for others, since the rites preceding the standing at ‘Arafāt, such as the arrival circumambulation, are not indispensable.

When ‘Ā’ishah informed the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that she had begun menstruating—having entered ihrām for ‘umrah with the intention of subsequently performing Hajj—he instructed her to unbraid her hair, comb it, enter ihrām for Hajj, and leave aside ‘umrah. Most jurists say that he thereby made her combine ‘umrah and Hajj in a single state of ihrām and exempted her from the arrival circumambulation.

---

## Page 13037

so waiving it for someone performing Hajj alone is all the more warranted. This is the view of Abū Hanīfah. Others say that he instructed her to abandon her ‘umrah; this is the view of Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad. They disagreed, however, over whether the arrival circumambulation is waived for someone without a valid excuse. On either view, this indicates that, whether she was performing Hajj alone or combining it with ‘umrah, waiving the arrival circumambulation because she was menstruating would be even more warranted than waiving ‘umrah and its circumambulation.

This differs from the obligatory circumambulation after ‘Arafāt. When the Prophet was told, “Safiyyah bint Huyayy has begun menstruating,” he said:

“What misfortune! Is she going to delay us?”

He was told, “She has already performed the obligatory circumambulation.” He replied, “Then no.”

Similarly, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed that no one should depart until his final act was a visit to the House—namely, the farewell circumambulation—but permitted a menstruating woman to depart without it. Whatever is waived because of a valid excuse is thereby known not to be among the indispensable pillars of Hajj. Thus, the people of Mecca are required to perform neither an arrival circumambulation nor a farewell circumambulation, since the purpose of these does not apply to them: they are neither arriving in Mecca nor bidding it farewell while they remain there. It is therefore clear that Hajj, whose foundation is the standing at ‘Arafāt followed by circumambulation, is prescribed for them, since its essential reality applies to them.

As for ‘umrah, its essence consists of circumambulating the House and passing between al-Safā and al-Marwah. All this takes place within the sanctuary itself, where the Meccan is always present. The passage between al-Safā

---

## Page 13038

and al-Marwah is a subsidiary rite in ‘umrah. Hence it is performed only after circumambulation and is not repeated within either Hajj or ‘umrah. The principal purpose of ‘umrah is therefore circumambulation, which the people of Mecca can perform without leaving the sanctuary; they have no need to leave it. Moreover, circumambulation and remaining there in worship are the purpose of coming to Mecca, and the people of Mecca are already able to do both. Whoever can attain the intended goal without resorting to a means is not commanded to abandon that goal and occupy himself with the means.

Furthermore, it is well known that walking around the House in circumambulation is itself the intended act of worship, whereas walking there from outside the sanctuary is merely a means and a route to it. Whoever abandons walking for this intended purpose—which is itself worship—and occupies himself with the means instead is misguided and ignorant of the true nature of religion. His ignorance is worse than that of someone living beside the mosque on a Friday who could go early and pray there, but instead goes somewhere far away so that he can set out for the mosque from there, thereby depriving himself of the prayer he could have performed in the mosque—the very purpose of going there.

This is further clarified by the fact that i‘timār (performing ‘umrah) follows the ifti‘āl pattern, derived from the verb ‘amara–ya‘muru, with ‘umrah as the corresponding noun. Allah Most High says:

“So whoever performs Hajj to the House or performs ‘umrah”

And He Most High says:

“Have you made the provision of water for pilgrims and the maintenance of the Sacred Mosque”

Maintaining mosques consists in worshipping in them and going to them for that purpose, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When you see a man regularly attending the mosque, bear witness to his faith.”

For Allah says:

---

## Page 13039

“Only those who believe in Allah and the Last Day, establish prayer, give zakah, and fear none but Allah maintain the mosques of Allah.”

One who remains at the House more fully embodies the meaning of ‘imārah (maintaining it through worship) than one who travels to it. Hence it has been said that ‘umrah means a visit, since the person performing ‘umrah must enter from outside the sanctuary, and that constitutes a visit. The former activity, however, is called ‘imārah. The term ‘imārah is better than the term ‘umrah, and the additional letters reflect additional meaning. Thus it is recorded in the Sahīh:

“One of the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘After embracing Islam, I would not mind doing no other deed than maintaining the Sacred Mosque.’ Another said, ‘After embracing Islam, I would not mind doing no other deed than providing water for the pilgrims.’ ‘Alī said, ‘Jihad in Allah’s cause is better than what you have mentioned.’

“‘Umar then said, ‘Do not raise your voices beside the pulpit of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. When the Friday prayer is over, Allah willing, I will go in to him and ask him.’ Then Allah Most High revealed: ‘Have you made the provision of water for pilgrims and the maintenance of the Sacred Mosque’—the verse.”

Since this is so, a person who remains at the House, circumambulating it and maintaining it through worship, has fulfilled the meaning embodied by someone performing ‘umrah more completely and has attained the purpose of ‘umrah. It is therefore not recommended for him to abandon this by leaving off maintaining the mosque through worship, only to return afterward to maintaining it; for he has exchanged what is better for what is inferior.

---

## Page 13040

Section

What we have mentioned as evidence that circumambulation is superior also shows that setting out from Mecca to perform ‘umrah, thereby abandoning circumambulation, is not recommended. Rather, what is recommended is circumambulation, not performing ‘umrah. Indeed, performing ‘umrah in these circumstances is an innovation: the early generations did not practise it, neither the Quran nor the Sunnah commands it, and no scriptural evidence establishes that it is recommended. Anything of this kind is a reprehensible innovation, by scholarly agreement.

For this reason, the early generations and the leading scholars used to discourage it. Sa‘īd narrated in his Sunan that Tāwūs, one of Ibn ‘Abbās’s foremost students, said:

“Those who perform ‘umrah from al-Tan‘īm—I do not know whether they are rewarded for it or punished.”

He was asked, “Why would they be punished?”

He replied, “Because a person leaves off circumambulating the House, goes four miles away, and returns. By the time he returns from those four miles away, he could have completed two hundred circuits. Every circuit he makes around the House is better than walking to no purpose.”

Abū Tālib reported that Ahmad ibn Hanbal was asked, “What do you say about the muhrim’s ‘umrah?”

He replied, “What merit is there in it? In my view, ‘umrah is that for which you set out from your home. Allah said:

---

## Page 13041

‘And complete Hajj and ‘umrah for Allah.’

And ‘Ā’ishah said, ‘The reward for ‘umrah is commensurate with what it entails’—meaning, with the exertion and expense.”

He also cited the report from ‘Alī and ‘Umar:

“Completing it means entering ritual consecration for it from your family home.”

Abū Tālib said:

“I said to Ahmad, ‘Tāwūs said: “Those who perform ‘umrah from al-Tan‘īm—I do not know whether they are rewarded for it or punished.” He was asked, “Why would they be punished?” He replied, “Because a person leaves off circumambulating the House and goes four miles away. In the time he spends going out and returning from those four miles, he could have completed two hundred circuits. Every circuit he makes around the House is better than walking to no purpose.”’”

Thus Ahmad endorsed this statement of Tāwūs, which Abū Tālib cited in support of Ahmad’s statement. Abū Bakr narrated this in al-Shāfī.

‘Abd al-Razzāq related, with his chain of transmission, that Mujāhid said:

“‘Alī, ‘Umar and ‘Ā’ishah were asked about performing ‘umrah on the night of al-Hasbah. ‘Umar said, ‘It is better than nothing.’ He also said, ‘It is better than an atom’s weight.’ ‘Ā’ishah said, ‘The reward for ‘umrah is commensurate with the expense?’”

It is also reported that ‘Ā’ishah said:

“I would rather fast for three days or give charity to ten needy people than perform the ‘umrah that I performed from al-Tan‘īm.”

Tāwūs said:

“As for those who perform ‘umrah after Hajj, I do not know whether they are punished for it or rewarded.”

‘Atā’ ibn al-Sā’ib said:

“We performed ‘umrah after Hajj, and Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr reproached us for doing so.”

Others permitted it, but did not practise it. It is reported from Umm al-Dardā’ that she was asked

---

## Page 13042

by someone about performing ‘umrah after Hajj, and she instructed him to do so.

‘Atā’ was asked about ‘umrah from al-Tan‘īm and said, “It is complete and valid.”

Al-Qāsim ibn Muhammad said, “The ‘umrah of one in ritual consecration is complete.”

‘Abd al-Razzāq narrated in his Musannaf: “Someone who heard ‘Atā’ informed me that he said, ‘Seven circuits are better for you than travelling to Medina.’ The man asked, ‘Should I go to Jeddah, then?’ He replied, ‘No. What you have been commanded to do is circumambulate.’ He said, ‘I asked, “Should I go out to al-Shajarah and begin ‘umrah from there?” He replied, “No.”’

“He said, ‘One of the scholars said, “My feet have not moved since I arrived in Mecca.”’ He said, ‘I asked, “Then do you prefer going back and forth to passing through?” He replied, “No; rather, going back and forth.”’”

‘Abd al-Razzāq said: “My father informed me: ‘I said to al-Muthannā, “I intend to go to Medina.” He replied, “Do not do so. I heard a man ask ‘Atā’, who told him, ‘Seven circuits around the House are better for you than travelling to Medina.’”’”

Abū Bakr ibn Abī Shaybah narrated in al-Musannaf: “Wakī‘ narrated to us from Sufyān, from Aslam al-Minq arī, who said: ‘I said to ‘Atā’, “Should I go out to Medina and enter ritual consecration for ‘umrah from the Prophet’s appointed boundary, may Allah bless him and grant him peace?” He replied, “Your circumambulation of the House is preferable to me to your travelling to Medina.”’”

He also said: “Wakī‘ narrated to us: ‘Umar ibn Zarr narrated to us from Mujāhid, who said, ‘Your circumambulation of the House is preferable to me to your travelling to Medina.’”

He also said: “Ismā‘īl ibn ‘Abd al-Malik narrated to us from ‘Atā’, who said, ‘Circumambulating the House is preferable to me to going out to perform ‘umrah.’”

---

## Page 13043

Section

As for frequently performing ‘umrah during Ramadan, whether by residents of Mecca or others, there are three questions to consider in order: performing ‘umrah more than once a year; then performing ‘umrah for those who are not residents of Mecca; and finally, frequently performing ‘umrah for residents of Mecca.

As for “frequently performing ‘umrah in the prescribed manner”—as when someone travels from his own home and enters ritual consecration for ‘umrah at the appointed boundary, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions used to do—this was a form of ‘umrah well known among them. The scholars disagreed over whether performing ‘umrah more than once a year is disliked. Some held it to be disliked, including al-Hasan and Ibn Sīrīn, and this is Mālik’s position.

Ibrāhīm al-Nakha‘ī said, “They used to perform ‘umrah only once a year.”

This is because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions used to perform only one ‘umrah: they did not perform it twice in a single year. Thus, going beyond what they did is disliked, just as entering ritual consecration before reaching the appointed boundary and other such practices are disliked. Another reason is that “in the letter which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, wrote to ‘Amr ibn Hazm, it is stated that ‘umrah is the lesser pilgrimage.” This is also indicated by

---

## Page 13044

the Quran, in the words of the Most High:

“On the day of the greater pilgrimage.”

Hajj is prescribed only once a year, and the same therefore applies to ‘umrah. Others, however, permitted this, including the Meccan scholars ‘Atā’, Tāwūs, and ‘Ikrimah. This is also the position of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad, and it is reported from such Companions as ‘Alī, Ibn ‘Umar, Ibn ‘Abbās, Anas, and ‘Ā’ishah.

For ‘Ā’ishah performed ‘umrah twice in one month at the command of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: the ‘umrah she performed together with Hajj, and the ‘umrah she performed from al-Tan‘īm at his command on the night of al-Hasbah, which follows the days spent at Minā—the night of the fourteenth of Dhū al-Hijjah. This is according to the majority, who hold that she did not abandon her ‘umrah but combined it with Hajj in a single state of ritual consecration.

Moreover, in the two Sahīh collections and elsewhere, Abū Hurayrah reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“One ‘umrah expiates the sins committed between it and the next, and an accepted Hajj has no reward but Paradise.”

Besides being unrestricted and general in its wording, this implies a distinction between ‘umrah and Hajj. If ‘umrah could be performed only once a year, it would be like Hajj, and the wording would have been, “One Hajj to the next.”

Furthermore, this is supported by the statements of the Companions. Al-Shāfi‘ī narrated from ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib that he said, “Once every month,” and from Anas that whenever the hair on his head had begun to grow back after shaving

---

## Page 13045

he would go out and perform ‘umrah. Wakī‘ narrated from Isrā’īl, from Suwayd ibn Abī Nājiyah, from Abū Ja‘far, who said:

‘Alī said, “I perform ‘umrah several times a month if I am able.”

Sa‘īd ibn Mansūr narrated from Sufyān, from Ibn Abī Husayn, from one of Anas’s children:

“When Anas was in Mecca and his hair had begun to grow back, he would go out to al-Tan‘īm and perform ‘umrah.”

This—and Allah knows best—is the ‘umrah of the Sacred Precinct, for they would remain in Mecca until Muharram and then perform ‘umrah.

This indicates that performing ‘umrah from Mecca is prescribed in principle. There is no dispute about this, and the leading scholars agree that it is permissible. This is the meaning of the well-known report transmitted by Ibn Sīrīn directly from the Prophet, without mentioning a Companion:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, designated al-Tan‘īm as the point of entering ritual consecration for the people of Mecca.”

‘Ikrimah said:

“A person may perform ‘umrah once his hair has grown enough to be shaved with a razor. If he wishes, he may perform ‘umrah twice each month.”

In another report from him: “He may perform ‘umrah several times a month.”

Furthermore, ‘umrah has no fixed period after which its time has passed, as Hajj does. Since it may be performed at any time throughout the year, it is not restricted to once a year as Hajj is.

Section

The Second Issue: Performing ‘Umrah Frequently and in Close Succession:

---

## Page 13046

For example, someone living near the Sacred Precinct might perform ‘umrah every day or every other day; someone living near the prescribed points of entering ritual consecration, two days’ journey from Mecca, might perform five or six ‘umrahs a month, or the like; or someone who considers ‘umrah from Mecca legitimate might perform it once or twice every day. This is disliked by consensus of the early generations of the Muslim community. None of them practised it; rather, they agreed that it was disliked. Although some jurists of the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad recommended it, they have no evidence whatsoever for this beyond a general analogy—that it increases acts of worship—or reliance on general texts concerning the merit of ‘umrah and the like.

Those who allowed more than one ‘umrah a year went no further than saying that a person may perform ‘umrah once his hair has grown enough to be shaved with a razor, or twice a month, or the like. This is what Imam Ahmad stated.

Ahmad said:

“When someone performs ‘umrah, he must shave his head or shorten his hair, and after ten days the head can be shaved.”

What Imam Ahmad stated accords with the practice of Anas ibn Mālik, as reported by al-Shāfi‘ī:

“When his hair began to grow back, he would go out and perform ‘umrah.”

This is because the rites are completed by shaving the head or shortening the hair, which is either obligatory or recommended as part of them. Whoever reports from Ahmad or others like him that this is merely permissible, rather than recommended, is mistaken. Thus, the time needed for the hair to grow back is the shortest interval in which the rites can be completed. This is not contradicted by ‘umrah

---

## Page 13047

immediately after Hajj, from the nearest point outside the Sacred Precinct, by someone who has performed Hajj alone; this is prescribed because of the need to perform ‘umrah. Even so, the early generations did not practise it, nor did anyone do so during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him. Rather, what is established through mass-transmitted reports concerning the Prophet’s Farewell Hajj is that he commanded all his Companions, after circumambulating the House and passing between al-Safā and al-Marwah, to leave their state of consecration and make their pilgrimage an ‘umrah. The exception was anyone who had brought sacrificial animals: he was not to leave consecration until the Day of Sacrifice, when the sacrificial animals reached their appointed place. He said:

“‘Umrah has been incorporated into Hajj until the Day of Resurrection.”

Thus, during the Farewell Hajj, the ‘umrah of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and that of all his Companions, acting on his command, was included within their Hajj. The only difference between them was that most—those who had no sacrificial animals with them—left their state of consecration, whereas those who had sacrificial animals remained consecrated. They called all these forms tamattu‘ (combining ‘umrah with Hajj), as is widely attested in authentic hadiths. These show that qirān—combining the two rites in a single state of consecration—is a form of tamattu‘, just as is leaving consecration after ‘umrah and then performing Hajj.

Thus, anyone who performs ‘umrah during the months of Hajj and performs Hajj that same year is performing tamattu‘ in the language of the Companions, in whose tongue the Quran was revealed. A person is performing qirān if he enters consecration for both ‘umrah and Hajj from the outset. He is also performing qirān if he enters consecration for ‘umrah and then adds Hajj to it before circumambulation, by agreement of the four imams and others. If someone performing tamattu‘ does not leave his state of consecration because he has brought sacrificial animals, and then enters consecration for Hajj, his consecration for Hajj takes effect, and some jurists of Ahmad’s school call him

---

## Page 13048

a pilgrim performing qirān—as do other jurists—because he has not left consecration. Others say that he is not called a pilgrim performing qirān because, in their view, he must perform another traversal between al-Safā and al-Marwah after the obligatory circumambulation, unlike someone performing qirān.

Two positions are reported from Ahmad on this question. He recommended that someone performing tamattu‘ undertake the traversal a second time, yet he also explicitly stated in several places that the first traversal suffices, as established in the authentic report from ‘Ā’ishah and others:

“The Companions who performed tamattu‘ with the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, circumambulated the House and passed between al-Safā and al-Marwah only once—their initial round.”

Thus, when Allah Most High required anyone combining ‘umrah with Hajj to offer whatever sacrificial animal he could afford, this obligation applied both to someone who entered consecration for Hajj after leaving consecration for an ‘umrah begun during the months of Hajj, and to someone who combined ‘umrah with Hajj, whether from the outset of his consecration for Hajj or during that consecration.

Accordingly, someone who brings sacrificial animals while consecrated for the ‘umrah of tamattu‘, and enters consecration for Hajj only after circumambulating and traversing between al-Safā and al-Marwah, may be called a pilgrim performing qirān by those who distinguish qirān from tamattu‘ in its narrower sense, because he entered consecration for Hajj before leaving consecration for ‘umrah. They may also call him a pilgrim performing tamattu‘—the more common designation—because he did not enter consecration for Hajj until after completing ‘umrah. This is a dispute over terminology that does not affect the ruling in any way, except for the question already mentioned of whether a second traversal is obligatory, and the view of those who may recommend that a pilgrim performing tamattu‘ undertake the arrival circumambulation after returning from ‘Arafah, before the circumambulation of ifādah. Although this has been reported from Ahmad

---

## Page 13049

and adopted by a group of his followers, the correct view, held by the vast majority of scholars, is that it is not recommended, because the Companions did not do this with the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. This is also the final position in Ahmad’s school. Accordingly, both the reports that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, performed tamattu‘ by combining ‘umrah with Hajj and those that he joined them in qirān are correct and convey the same meaning.

The same applies to those who reported that he performed Hajj alone, such as Ibn ‘Umar, ‘Ā’ishah and others: they meant that he performed a single set of Hajj rites. Indeed, these very narrators also reported that he performed a single set of Hajj rites, without separating the two pilgrimages by leaving consecration, as someone performing tamattu‘ does upon completing his ‘umrah. Nor did his rites involve anything beyond those performed by someone undertaking Hajj alone. This differs from someone performing tamattu‘ who leaves consecration, thereby separating the ‘umrah of his tamattu‘ from his Hajj.

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, did not perform ‘umrah after his Hajj, nor did any of the Companions who performed Hajj with him, except ‘Ā’ishah. This is universally agreed upon and established by mass transmission, known to all scholars familiar with his Hajj. They do not dispute that he performed no ‘umrah after his Hajj, whether from the nearest point outside the Sacred Precinct—namely al-Tan‘īm, where the mosques commonly called “the Mosques of ‘Ā’ishah” were later built—or from anywhere other than al-Tan‘īm.

---

## Page 13050

For this reason, they agreed that the established hadiths in the Sahīh collections and elsewhere—which state that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, performed four ‘umrahs: the ‘umrah of al-Hudaybiyyah, the ‘umrah of al-Qadiyyah, the ‘umrah of al-Ji‘rānah, and the ‘umrah accompanying his Hajj—mean that he performed ‘umrah as a pilgrim undertaking tamattu‘ who had brought his sacrificial animals with him. Such a pilgrim is also performing qirān. Thus, describing him as performing tamattu‘ or qirān amounts to the same thing when he has entered consecration for both ‘umrah and Hajj: he is performing tamattu‘ and is also performing qirān.

Accordingly, any jurist who mistakenly says that he entered consecration for Hajj alone and did not join an ‘umrah to it, either before it or together with it, or who says that he entered consecration without specifying a rite and subsequently followed it with Hajj, is denying that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, performed ‘umrah along with his Hajj. His position necessarily entails rejecting these authentic hadiths which clearly state that he performed four ‘umrahs, since the Muslims agree that neither he nor any of his Companions, except ‘Ā’ishah, performed ‘umrah after Hajj.

This therefore constitutes decisive proof of a matter over which the four imams and the vast majority of jurists did not disagree: a pilgrim performing tamattu‘ by combining ‘umrah with Hajj has thereby fulfilled both Hajj and ‘umrah, whether ‘umrah is considered obligatory or strongly recommended rather than obligatory. This is because {the Companions who performed Hajj with the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, did precisely this at his command, and the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, told them that ‘umrah had become part of Hajj until the Day of Resurrection.

They asked him, “Is this ‘umrah of ours for this year alone, or for all time?”

He replied, “Rather, for all time. ‘Umrah has become part of Hajj until the Day of Resurrection.”}

He said:

---

## Page 13051

And those Companions who reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, performed Hajj alone intended thereby to explain that he did not come out of consecration after his ‘umrah of tamattu‘, as he instructed most of his Companions to do—namely, those who had not brought sacrificial animals with them. All the established, mass-transmitted hadiths agree that, when his Companions arrived in Mecca and circumambulated the House and went between al-Safā and al-Marwah, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, instructed them to come out of consecration and make their pilgrimage an ‘umrah. The exception was anyone who had brought sacrificial animals: he instructed such a person to remain in consecration until the Day of Sacrifice, when the sacrificial animals would reach their appointed place, acting in accordance with the meaning of Allah’s words:

“And do not shave your heads until the sacrificial offering reaches its place of slaughter.”

No scholar has disputed this account of how the Farewell Hajj took place.

Many Companions also reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, performed tamattu‘ by combining ‘umrah with Hajj.
