Some people consequently assumed that they meant he had come out of consecration after ‘umrah and then entered consecration for Hajj, as he had instructed those Companions who had not brought sacrificial animals to do. Some of these same Companions also reported that he performed Hajj alone, in order to dispel the assumption that he had come out of consecration. They explained that he had not come out of consecration; rather, he acted as one performing Hajj alone would act, remaining in consecration and performing the same rites. Thus, the Companions’ accounts agree on this.

Every Companion reported to have narrated that he performed Hajj alone also narrated that he performed tamattu‘. They explained tamattu‘ as qirān and explicitly related that he said:

“Here I am at Your service

---

## Page 13052

for ‘umrah and Hajj,” and that he said:

“A messenger from my Lord came to me in this blessed valley and said, ‘Say: an ‘umrah within a Hajj.’”

Thus, the correct view is that qirān is preferable for someone who brings sacrificial animals. For someone who does not bring sacrificial animals, combines the two pilgrimages in one journey, and arrives during the months of Hajj, tamattu‘ in its specific sense is preferable. If, however, he arrives for ‘umrah in Ramadan or earlier, that is preferable to tamattu‘. Likewise, performing Hajj on one journey and ‘umrah on another is preferable to tamattu‘ alone. This differs from someone who makes a separate journey for ‘umrah and then returns during the months of Hajj to perform tamattu‘: he performs two ‘umrahs and one Hajj, which is preferable. Such was the case of the Companions who performed the ‘umrah of fulfilment with the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and then performed tamattu‘ with him during the Farewell Hajj, combining ‘umrah with Hajj. This is the best way to complete the rites.

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, likewise performed ‘umrah first, then combined ‘umrah and Hajj in his pilgrimage because he had brought sacrificial animals. However, he did no more than someone performing Hajj alone: he did not perform a fourth circumambulation for ‘umrah. This is why it was said that he performed Hajj alone.

Then, during the time of Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, people saw how easy this was and began restricting their ‘umrah to the months of Hajj, neglecting the remaining months. They would not travel from their towns to perform ‘umrah at those other times, so the House was left without ‘umrah pilgrims from those towns for the rest of the year. ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb therefore instructed them to do what was more complete for them: to perform ‘umrah outside the months of Hajj, so that the House would be visited and filled with worshippers during

---

## Page 13053

the months of Hajj and outside them. What ‘Umar chose for them is preferable even according to those who hold that tamattu‘ is preferable to performing Hajj alone or to qirān, such as Imam Ahmad and others.

Imam Ahmad maintains that performing ‘umrah outside the months of Hajj is preferable to delaying it until those months, whether a person arrives in Mecca before the months of Hajj, performs ‘umrah, and remains there until performing Hajj that same year, or performs ‘umrah and then returns to his hometown or his region’s designated place for entering ihram before entering ihram for Hajj.

This is clear: if someone travelling to Mecca arrives, for example, in Ramadan and performs ‘umrah during that month, he attains what the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, described when he said:

“An ‘umrah in Ramadan is equivalent to a Hajj.”

If he arrives earlier to perform ‘umrah and remains in Mecca, all of that is better for him, for he performs circumambulation and observes spiritual retreat there throughout that period until entering ihram for Hajj. If he returns to his hometown and then comes back and enters ihram for Hajj, he has devoted a separate journey to ‘umrah and another to Hajj. This is a more complete way of performing both, as ‘Alī said concerning the words of Allah Most High:

“Complete Hajj and ‘umrah for Allah.”

“Completing them means entering ihram for them from your family home.”

That is, beginning the journey for them from your family home.

As for someone who performs ‘umrah before the months of Hajj, then returns to his hometown and comes back a second time during the months of Hajj to perform tamattu‘, combining ‘umrah with Hajj, this is better than someone who confines himself to Hajj alone on his second journey, if he also performs ‘umrah after Hajj. This is because the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, performed ‘umrah together with Hajj as a form of tamattu‘ that was qirān, as they have explained, and because whoever

---

## Page 13054

performs a separate ‘umrah and another together with Hajj is better off than someone who performs only one ‘umrah and one Hajj. Moreover, an ‘umrah performed as part of tamattu‘ is preferable to an ‘umrah undertaken from Mecca immediately after Hajj.

Thus, the course ‘Umar chose for the people is the preferred practice according to most jurists, including Imams Ahmad, Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and others. Abū Hanīfah’s companions likewise reported this from Muhammad ibn al-Hasan. No disagreement is known among the scholars concerning its preferability. Since this was the better and more favorable course, and since people would neglect it unless instructed to follow it, turning away from what benefited them more in their religion, ‘Umar’s independent judgment and concern for his subjects led him to require it of them, just as a caring father requires his child to do what is best for him. This also benefited the people of Mecca.

This, however, was a matter open to independent judgment. ‘Alī, ‘Imrān ibn Husayn, and other Companions disagreed with him over it. They did not consider that people should be compelled to follow this course. Rather, they should be left free: whoever wished could perform ‘umrah before the months of Hajj, and whoever wished could perform it during them, even though the former was more complete.

The disagreement intensified during “the caliphate of ‘Uthmān,” to the point that it is authentically reported in the two Sahīh collections that ‘Uthmān used to forbid tamattu‘. When ‘Alī saw this, he entered ihram for both Hajj and ‘umrah and said:

“I would not abandon the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, on account of anyone’s statement.”

‘Uthmān’s prohibition was intended to favor the preferable practice, not to declare tamattu‘ religiously disliked. Then, when division arose within the Muslim community following the killing of ‘Uthmān and the people split into

---

## Page 13055

two factions—one inclined toward ‘Uthmān and his supporters, the other toward ‘Alī and his supporters—some Umayyad governors began forbidding tamattu‘, punishing those who performed it, and allowing no one to perform ‘umrah during the months of Hajj. This involved a measure of ignorance and injustice.

When the learned Companions, such as ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Abbās, ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar, and others, saw this, they began denouncing it and instructing people to perform tamattu‘ in accordance with the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him. They told people that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, had instructed his Companions to perform it during the Farewell Pilgrimage.

Some people then began disputing with them on the basis of what they mistakenly attributed to Abū Bakr and ‘Umar. They would say to ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar, “Your father used to forbid it.” He would reply, “My father did not mean that, nor did he have people beaten for it,” and make similar statements. He thus explained to them that ‘Umar intended to direct people toward the preferable practice, not to prohibit the less preferable one. ‘Umar had only instructed them to perform ‘umrah outside the months of Hajj.

If, however, the suggestion is that ‘Umar or any other Companion chose for people to perform Hajj on its own during its months and also perform an ‘umrah from Mecca during that time, then no Companion ever prescribed or preferred this at all. Certainly, no one did so during the lifetime of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. I consider it most likely that no Companion did so after the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, or instructed others to do so.

A group of scholars have interpreted ‘Umar’s prohibition as referring to tamattu‘ through converting one’s ihram for Hajj into ‘umrah.

---

## Page 13056

and these scholars maintain that converting one’s ihram for Hajj into ‘umrah was permissible only for those who were with the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. This matter has been discussed at length elsewhere. It is clear that the early generations and the scholars disagreed over this conversion.

Ibn ‘Abbās, his followers, and many of the Zāhirīs and Shī‘ah hold that this conversion is obligatory and that no one may perform Hajj except by performing tamattu‘. Many scholars of the early and later generations, however, hold that although tamattu‘ is permissible, someone who has entered ihram for Hajj alone, or for Hajj and ‘umrah combined, may not make this conversion. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, and al-Shāfi‘ī.

Many jurists of the hadith tradition and others, such as Ahmad ibn Hanbal, hold that this conversion is preferable, but that performing Hajj alone, or combining Hajj and ‘umrah without making the conversion, is permissible. As for someone who has brought sacrificial animals with him, he must not make this conversion; there is no disagreement on this point. The conversion is permissible so long as he has not stood at ‘Arafah, regardless of whether he intended his circumambulation as the arrival circumambulation or otherwise, and regardless of whether, upon entering ihram, he intended to combine Hajj and ‘umrah, to perform Hajj alone, or entered ihram without specifying a particular rite.

According to these scholars, it is preferable for everyone who has not brought sacrificial animals to leave ihram by completing an ‘umrah as part of tamattu‘, just as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, instructed his Companions to do during the Farewell Pilgrimage. He may not leave ihram by performing an ‘umrah if his intention is to perform Hajj that same year, thereby performing tamattu‘. As for converting one’s ihram into ‘umrah alone, no scholar permits it, nor may someone who

---

## Page 13057

combines ‘umrah and Hajj in a single journey perform Hajj during the months of Hajj and then perform ‘umrah from Mecca immediately afterward. Rather, they agree that this is not the recommended practice prescribed by the Sunnah. This is therefore better than limiting oneself to Hajj alone on one’s second journey, or performing ‘umrah during it. It is thus established that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, performed ‘umrah with Hajj as tamattu‘, combining the two in one ihram, as explained above. Moreover, performing a separate ‘umrah as well as an ‘umrah together with one’s Hajj is better than performing only one ‘umrah and one Hajj. An ‘umrah of tamattu‘ is better than an ‘umrah performed in Mecca after Hajj in addition to that Hajj, even though they permit the latter.

When ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar explained to them what ‘Umar meant, they would dispute it with him. He would then say:

“Suppose, then, that ‘Umar forbade it. Which has the greater claim to your obedience: the command of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, or ‘Umar?”

Likewise, when ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Abbās explained to them the Prophet’s Sunnah concerning his tamattu‘, they would counter it with what they mistakenly attributed to Abū Bakr and ‘Umar. He would say to them:

“Stones may soon rain down upon you from heaven! I tell you, ‘The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said,’ and you say, ‘Abū Bakr and ‘Umar said’!”

He was explaining to them that no one may oppose the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, with anyone else’s words, even though those who raised these objections were also misrepresenting Abū Bakr and ‘Umar. Whether they correctly understood the position of Abū Bakr and ‘Umar or wrongly attributed it to them, no one may reject what is established of the Sunnah of

---

## Page 13058

the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, on the strength of anyone else’s words. Rather, anyone’s statements may be accepted or rejected, except those of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him. This is agreed upon by the scholars and imams of the Muslim community. Only ignorant people among the Rāfidah and extremist ascetics have disputed it—those who believe that certain members of the Prophet’s household or certain shaykhs are infallible, or virtually so.

Ibn ‘Abbās advocated tamattu‘ so strongly that he regarded it as obligatory and also considered conversion of the pilgrimage rite obligatory. This is the view of Abū Hanīfah and a group among the Zāhirīs and the Shia. He held that anyone who had performed tawaf and sa‘y had thereby left ihram and become a pilgrim performing tamattu‘, whether he intended tamattu‘ or not. A group among the Shia and others likewise adopted the view that tamattu‘ is obligatory. This stands in direct opposition to those among the Umayyads and others who prohibited it and punished people for performing it.

As for the leading jurists, they permit both options, but disagree over conversion of the rite and whether it is recommended. Whoever performs Hajj as tamattu‘, beginning at the mīqāt, fulfils his obligation of Hajj by scholarly consensus. Other cases are disputed when a person arrives during the months of Hajj, whether he performs Hajj alone, combines Hajj and ‘umrah in one ihram, or converts his rite. The exception is someone who combines Hajj and ‘umrah in one ihram and has brought along his sacrificial offering: all agree that his Hajj also fulfils his obligation. As for someone who arrives for ‘umrah before the months of Hajj and stays until he performs Hajj, then in his case

---

## Page 13059

too, I know of no disagreement. Thus, the recommended forms of tamattu‘, qirān (combining Hajj and ‘umrah in one ihram), and ifrād (performing Hajj alone) are those which, by their consensus, fulfil the obligation. Because of the different meanings borne by the same terms in the reports, differences of scholarly judgment concerning practice, and other factors, many jurists have misunderstood “the manner of the Farewell Pilgrimage.” Some followers of Ahmad and others suppose that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, performed tamattu‘ in the sense that he left his ihram for ‘umrah and subsequently entered ihram for Hajj. This is undoubtedly mistaken.

Imam Ahmad said:

“I have no doubt that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, performed qirān, but I prefer tamattu‘.”

He meant for someone who had not brought along a sacrificial offering. His position is consistent: for someone who combines Hajj and ‘umrah in a single journey, arrives during the months of Hajj, and has not brought along a sacrificial offering, this form of tamattu‘ is preferable. Indeed, it is the practice prescribed by the Sunnah, because the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, instructed his Companions to do this.

As for someone who has brought along a sacrificial offering, is qirān preferable for him, or tamattu‘? Two positions have been transmitted from Ahmad. What he explicitly stated in the report transmitted by al-Marwadhī is that qirān is preferable for him, because this is how the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, performed Hajj—a matter undisputed among scholars and hadith specialists.

For someone who has brought along a sacrificial offering, tamattu‘ and qirān differ only in whether he enters ihram earlier or later. Thus, whenever he enters ihram for Hajj together with ‘umrah, or couples his ihram with ‘umrah—or with an additional sa‘y, according to those who hold that view—and before his tawaf and sa‘y, according to those who hold that view,

---

## Page 13060

he is performing qirān, which is a form of tamattuʿ, without dispute. If, however, he enters ihram for Hajj only after completing the tawaf and saʿy, while still remaining in ihram, then he is performing tamattuʿ. According to Abū Hanīfah and Ahmad, he must remain in ihram if he has brought along a sacrificial offering. According to Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī, he leaves ihram only if he has not brought along a sacrificial offering; in that case, they all agree that he leaves his ihram for ʿumrah. If he enters ihram for Hajj before leaving his ihram for ʿumrah, however, there is disagreement. Those among Ahmad’s followers who permit this also describe him as “performing qirān,” since he did not leave his ihram before entering ihram for Hajj.

Must someone performing tamattuʿ undertake another saʿy after tawaf al-ifādah, besides the first saʿy performed after the tawaf of ʿumrah? There are two opinions on this within Ahmad’s school and elsewhere. Ahmad explicitly stated on more than one occasion that a single saʿy suffices for someone performing tamattuʿ, just as it suffices for someone performing qirān. On this basis, the two differ only in whether ihram for Hajj is entered earlier or later. If that is so, then entering ihram for Hajj earlier is clearly preferable to delaying it, because it is more complete.

This is what is established through an authentic and explicit report from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. Anas said:

“I heard him say, ‘Here I am, responding to Your call for ʿumrah and Hajj.’”

Likewise, in the hadith of ʿUmar recorded in the Sahīh—Sahīh al-Bukhārī—it is reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said, while at al-ʿAqīq:

“Someone came to me tonight from my Lord and said:

---

## Page 13061

‘Pray in this blessed valley and say, “ʿUmrah combined with Hajj.”’” No one has transmitted from the Prophet himself, peace and blessings be upon him, any wording whatsoever that contradicts these two statements. Indeed, no wording declaring his entry into ihram has been transmitted from him other than this.

Likewise, ʿĀ’ishah said in the hadith recorded by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim:

“We set out with the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, for the Farewell Pilgrimage and entered ihram for ʿumrah. Then the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said, ‘Whoever has brought a sacrificial offering should enter ihram for Hajj together with ʿumrah.’”

As for the Prophet’s statement, peace and blessings be upon him:

“Had I known beforehand what I now know, I would not have brought the sacrificial offering, and I would have made it ʿumrah,”

this also shows that, having brought the sacrificial offering, he would not make it ʿumrah; he would have made it ʿumrah only if he had not brought the offering. His Companions whom he instructed to leave ihram—those who had not brought sacrificial offerings—were reluctant to leave ihram during the months of Hajj, because they were not accustomed to interrupting their ihram during those months. To put their hearts at ease, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, indicated that he would have done as they did, explaining that, had he known beforehand what he later came to know—that is, “Were I entering ihram now, I would not bring a sacrificial offering, and I would enter ihram for ʿumrah, after which I would leave ihram.” All these reports are established as authentic from him without dispute.

This makes clear that the preferred course for someone arriving during the months of Hajj is one of two things: either to bring along a sacrificial offering, or to perform tamattuʿ in the manner of someone performing qirān; or not to bring along a sacrificial offering and to perform tamattuʿ by undertaking ʿumrah and then leaving ihram.

---

## Page 13062

What should be said, then, is that what Allah chose for His Prophet is the better of the two courses. As for his statement, peace and blessings be upon him:

“Had I known beforehand what I now know, I would not have done that,”

this is a judgment dependent upon a condition, and a conditional judgment does not apply when its condition is absent. He did not know beforehand what he later came to know. Allah Most High chose for him what he did, and chose that he should not know beforehand what he later came to know. Something being preferable under a particular assumption does not mean that it is preferable without qualification.

This is like his statement:

“Had I not been sent among you, Umar would have been sent among you.”

This does not indicate that Umar would have been the best of them had the Messenger not been sent, nor does it indicate that he is the best with the Messenger having been sent. Rather, Abu Bakr is better than him in the latter circumstances. But this shows that following the same practice is preferable to varying practices when such variation causes division and disunity. Such variation encompasses the choice of the better course by someone capable of the less excellent course, as well as the case of someone incapable of the less excellent course—as when someone able to bring a sacrificial offering chose the better course, alongside someone unable to bring one. This variation is preferable when there is no division, or when division is followed by harmony.

Some followers of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and others also erred concerning “the manner of his Hajj.” They thought that he performed Hajj alone, meaning that he entered ihram for Hajj alone and did not

---

## Page 13063

perform Umrah alongside it at all. This, too, contradicts the established authentic hadiths and what has been transmitted through numerous independent reports concerning his Sunnah. Some of their later followers make a further mistake, supposing that he also performed Umrah starting from Mecca. For this reason, no one whose opinion carries weight has reported this.

They did not dispute that he instructed those of his Companions who had not brought sacrificial animals to perform Umrah followed by Hajj, coming out of ihrām between them. For his community, following his command takes precedence over following his own practice, especially since he explained that his remaining in ihrām was solely because he had brought sacrificial animals. This has been transmitted from him through numerous independent reports. In the two Sahih collections, Hafsah is reported to have asked him:

“Why have the people come out of ihrām, while you have not come out of ihrām for your Umrah?”

He replied:

“I have matted my hair and garlanded my sacrificial animals, so I will not come out of ihrām until I slaughter them.”

This does not contradict his having entered ihrām for both Umrah and Hajj, as reported by Anas, Umar, and others. This too is called Umrah, since its rites, taken by themselves, are those of someone performing Umrah. Moreover, he had instructed them to come out of ihrām and make their pilgrimage an Umrah, so she likened his case to theirs.

Some followers of Abū Hanīfah and others also erred concerning “the manner of his Hajj.” They believed that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, combined Umrah and Hajj in the sense that he first performed a tawāf (circumambulation of the Kaaba) and a sa‘y (the ritual passage between Safā and Marwah) for Umrah, then performed a second tawāf and sa‘y for Hajj before the standing at Arafat. Anyone who examines the established hadiths transmitted through numerous independent reports from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, will know that he did not perform two tawāfs or two sa‘ys. Nor did he instruct those of his Companions who had brought sacrificial animals, whom he ordered to remain in ihrām, to do so, let alone

---

## Page 13064

those whom he instructed to come out of ihrām.

As for reports that Ali and others instructed people to perform two tawāfs and two sa‘ys, several hadith scholars have judged them weak. None of the hadith collections reports that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, performed two tawāfs and two sa‘ys during his Hajj. This is found only in certain works of juristic opinion whose authors transmit many weak hadiths. They did not deliberately lie, but they heard these hadiths from people who did not transmit them accurately.

The same applies to choosing the preferable form of pilgrimage. Although the jurists regard all three forms as permissible, many of them may err in deciding which is preferable. The soundest position—the one most faithful to the Sunnah and best supported by transmitted reports and reason—is what we have already stated: whoever arrives during the months of Hajj intending to perform both Umrah and Hajj on that journey should, according to the Sunnah, combine Umrah with Hajj (tamattu‘).

If he has brought sacrificial animals, he does not come out of ihrām. However, it is better for him to enter ihrām for Hajj together with Umrah at the outset, before tawāf and sa‘y, than to delay entering ihrām for Hajj until after tawāf and sa‘y. If he has not brought sacrificial animals, he comes out of ihrām. This is better for him than performing an Umrah after Hajj.

As for someone who performs the two rites separately during one journey, performing Umrah before the months of Hajj and remaining until Hajj, this is better than tamattu‘. This is the position of the Rightly Guided Caliphs, and it is

---

## Page 13065

the position of Imam Ahmad and others, as well as those followers of Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and others who hold this view. Preferring tamattu‘ is the position of the hadith scholars, the jurists of Mecca among the Companions and Successors, and Banū Hāshim. Thus, the scholars of the Prophet’s Sunnah, the people of his city, and his household agreed in preferring it.

Although Mālik prefers performing Hajj on its own (ifrād), he does not prefer this for someone who performs Umrah immediately after Hajj. Rather, Umrah is to be performed outside the months of Hajj, such as in Muharram. In one of his opinions, al-Shāfi‘ī prefers tamattu‘; in another, entering ihrām without specifying the form of pilgrimage; and in another, ifrād. However, I do not recall his position concerning someone who performs Umrah immediately after Hajj. Although some of his followers regard this as preferable, many followers of Ahmad likewise suppose that his position is that tamattu‘ is preferable to performing Umrah during the months of Hajj.

There are many mistaken representations of both the Sunnah and the imams’ positions on this subject. How could anyone with even the slightest knowledge of the Sunnah doubt that none of the Prophet’s Companions performed Umrah immediately after Hajj? And how could any Muslim doubt that what they did at the command of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, was best for them and for anyone whose circumstances resembled theirs?

What we have stated makes it clear that, even if performing Umrah from Mecca immediately after Hajj is permitted for someone who performed Hajj on its own, no one did this during the lifetime of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, nor was it prescribed by him—or by any of his caliphs or any of his Companions

---

## Page 13066

or by the Successors and their imams—as a preferred practice. All of this leaves the case for performing Umrah from Mecca exceedingly weak.

Section

As for the third question, we say:

The Sunnah and the agreement of the early generations of this community, as we have explained, establish that performing Umrah repeatedly in close succession is not recommended but is, rather, disliked for someone who enters ihrām at the prescribed boundary. Clearly, then, doing so from Mecca, whether in Ramadan or otherwise, is even more deserving of being considered disliked, since it combines two objectionable practices.

The first is performing Umrah from Mecca. They have agreed that choosing this instead of circumambulating the Kaaba is disliked.

The second is performing Umrah repeatedly in close succession. They have agreed that this is not recommended. Indeed, to my knowledge, it should be considered disliked without qualification even for someone who does not substitute circumambulation for it; this is more consistent with legal analogy. How much more so, then, for someone who can substitute circumambulation for it!

Frequent circumambulation, by contrast, is recommended and enjoined, especially for those visiting Mecca. The majority of scholars hold that their circumambulation of the House is better for them than prayer in the Sacred Mosque, despite the great merit of prayer in the Sacred Mosque.

---

## Page 13067

Section

As for performing Umrah in Ramadan:

The two Sahihs and the Sunan collections report from ʿAtāʾ: I heard Ibn ʿAbbās relate to us:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to a woman of the Ansār—whom Ibn ʿAbbās named, but whose name I have forgotten—‘What prevented you from performing Hajj with us?’

“She replied, ‘We had only two camels used for drawing water. The father of her child performed Hajj on one and left us the other to draw water with.’

“He said, ‘When Ramadan comes, perform Umrah, for an Umrah in Ramadan is equivalent to a Hajj.’”

The two Sahihs also report from Ibn ʿAbbās that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“An Umrah in Ramadan is equivalent to a Hajj.”

The two Sahihs also report:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to Umm Sinān, a woman of the Ansār, ‘An Umrah in Ramadan is equivalent to a Hajj with me.’”

Al-Bukhārī also related this hadith through Jābir, omitting the beginning of its chain of transmission. Umm Maʿqil also reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“An Umrah in Ramadan is equivalent to a Hajj.”

Ibn Mājah and al-Tirmidhī related it, and al-Tirmidhī graded it a good hadith.

Yūsuf ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām reported from his grandmother, Umm Maʿqil, who said:

“When the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed the Farewell Hajj, we had a camel which Abū Maʿqil had dedicated to the cause of Allah. Illness afflicted us, Abū Maʿqil died, and there set out

---

## Page 13068

the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. When he had completed his Hajj, I came to him, and he said, ‘Umm Maʿqil, what prevented you from performing Hajj?’

“She replied, ‘We had made preparations, but Abū Maʿqil died. We had a camel on which we would perform Hajj, but Abū Maʿqil bequeathed it to the cause of Allah.’

“He said, ‘Why did you not set out on it? Hajj is part of the cause of Allah.’”

Abū Dāwūd related it. Ahmad also related in his Musnad:

“Umm Maʿqil al-Asadiyyah reported that her husband had dedicated a young male camel to the cause of Allah. She wished to perform Umrah and asked her husband for the camel, but he refused. She went to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and told him about this. He instructed her husband to give it to her. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Hajj and Umrah are part of the cause of Allah.’”

These hadiths show that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, meant the Umrah familiar to those he was addressing: a person travelling to Mecca to perform Umrah. As for a Meccan going outside the Sacred Precinct to begin an Umrah, this was something they neither knew nor practised nor enjoined. How, then, could this have been intended by the hadith? Moreover, this woman was in Medina, the Prophet’s city, and her Umrah could only have begun at the prescribed boundary; it was not an Umrah originating in Mecca.

How could he have encouraged them to perform an Umrah originating in Mecca during Ramadan, yet they failed to perform an act carrying such an immense reward, despite their intense desire for goodness and eagerness to pursue it? And why did the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, not tell the residents of Mecca about this, so that they could perform Umrah every year during Ramadan? Rather, he told this only to someone who was

---

## Page 13069

in Medina, when that person told him of an obstacle that had prevented them from travelling for Hajj. He explained that Hajj is part of the cause of Allah and that an Umrah in Ramadan is equivalent to a Hajj. This is clear: if someone who performs Umrah in Ramadan returns home, they will have made a complete journey for Umrah, outward and back, during the revered month of Ramadan. They thus combine the sanctity of Ramadan with the sanctity of Umrah. The distinction of both time and place makes it fitting that this should be equivalent to Hajj, which likewise enjoys the distinction of its time—the months of Hajj—and its place. Nevertheless, the two things being compared are not alike in every respect, especially in this particular instance, as Muslims unanimously agree.

If, instead, that person remains in Mecca until performing Hajj that year, they will also have gained a rite that expiates sins. This differs from someone who performs tamattuʿ (Umrah followed by Hajj) during the months of Hajj: such a person is solely a Hajj pilgrim, even though performing tamattuʿ. Hence, they are engaged in Hajj from the moment they enter ihram for Umrah.

This is clarified by one version of the hadith in the Sahīh, in which he said to the woman:

“An Umrah in Ramadan is equivalent to a Hajj with me.”

It is clear that he meant, “Your Umrah in Ramadan is equivalent to a Hajj with me.” She had intended to perform Hajj with him but had been unable to do so, so he told her what would take its place. The same applies to any of the Companions whose circumstances were like hers.

No reasonable person would say what some ignorant people imagine: that an Umrah performed by one of us, whether beginning at the prescribed boundary or in Mecca, is equivalent to a Hajj with him. It is self-evident that a complete Hajj is superior to an Umrah in Ramadan. Even if one of us performed the obligatory Hajj, it would not be like performing Hajj with him—so how could an Umrah be? The most the hadith establishes is that an Umrah performed by one of us in

---

## Page 13070

Ramadan, beginning at the prescribed boundary, is equivalent to a Hajj. It may be said that this applies to someone who intended to perform Hajj but was unable to do so: their intention to perform Hajj, together with their Umrah in Ramadan, would then be equivalent to a Hajj—not either one alone. Likewise, when a person does as much of a deed as they can, and would have completed it had they been able, they receive the same reward as someone who performed it in full.

Thus, in the two Sahīhs, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When a servant falls ill or travels, the deeds they used to perform while healthy and resident are recorded for them.”

It is also reported from him in the Sahīh that he said:

“Whoever calls others to guidance will receive a reward equal to the rewards of those who follow him, without their rewards being diminished in the least.”

He said the same concerning misguidance. There is abundant evidence supporting this principle.

A similar example is his statement in the hadith narrated by Ibn Masʿūd from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Perform Hajj and Umrah in succession, for they remove poverty and sins just as the bellows removes dross from iron, gold, and silver. An accepted Hajj has no reward but Paradise.”

It was narrated by al-Nasā’ī and al-Tirmidhī, who said: “This is a sound and authentic hadith.”

His words:

“Perform Hajj and Umrah in succession”

did not mean performing Umrah from Mecca. Had he meant that, the Companions would have obeyed his command, whether it conveyed an obligation or a recommendation. Nor can it be supposed of the Companions

---

## Page 13071

and the Successors that they abandoned following his Sunnah and that none of them sought to do so, until this practice arose after their time. Since they did not perform Umrah from Mecca, it is clear that this is not what the hadith means. Rather, it means the Umrah they knew and practised: the Umrah performed by someone arriving in Mecca.

This is illustrated by the fact that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not instruct Ā’ishah to perform Umrah from the nearest point outside the Sacred Precinct, although this would have constituted performing Hajj and Umrah in succession if an Umrah from Mecca were intended. When she requested this of him, he instructed her to be content with what she had already done, saying:

“Your circumambulation of the House and your passage between al-Safā and al-Marwah suffice for your Hajj and Umrah.”

When she pressed him and persisted in her request, he permitted her to do so. Had this been something he had commanded, he would not initially have instructed her to forgo it and be content with less, when she was seeking something everyone desired.

In the two Sahīhs, the Sunan of Abū Dāwūd and al-Nasā’ī, and other collections, it is related from Ā’ishah that she said:

“We set out with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, for the Farewell Hajj and entered ihram for Umrah. Then the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Whoever has a sacrificial animal with him should enter ihram for Hajj together with Umrah, and should not leave ihram until he has completed both.’ I then arrived in Mecca while menstruating, so I did not circumambulate the House or pass between al-Safā and al-Marwah. I complained to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and he said, ‘Unbraid your hair, comb it, enter ihram for Hajj, and leave Umrah.’ She did so. When we had completed Hajj, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, sent me with Abd al-Rahmān ibn Abī Bakr to al-Tan‘īm, and I performed Umrah. He then said:

---

## Page 13072

‘This is in place of your Umrah.’” She said:

“Those who had entered ihram for Umrah circumambulated the House and passed between al-Safā and al-Marwah, then left ihram. After returning from Minā, they performed another circumambulation for their Hajj. As for those who had combined Hajj and Umrah, they performed only one circumambulation.”

The two Sahīhs and the Sunan also relate from Ā’ishah that she said:

“We entered ihram for Hajj. When we reached Sarif, I began menstruating. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, came in while I was weeping and asked, ‘What makes you weep, Ā’ishah?’ I replied, ‘I have begun menstruating. I wish I had not come for Hajj.’ He said, ‘Glory be to Allah! This is simply something Allah has decreed for the daughters of Adam.’ He then said, ‘Perform all the rites, but do not circumambulate the House.’

“When we entered Mecca, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘Whoever wishes to make it an Umrah may do so, except someone who has brought a sacrificial animal.’ The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, sacrificed cattle on behalf of his wives on the Day of Sacrifice. When the night at al-Bathā’ came and Ā’ishah had become free of menstruation, she said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, will my companions return with Hajj and Umrah while I return with Hajj alone?’ The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed Abd al-Rahmān ibn Abī Bakr to take her to perform Umrah from al-Tan‘īm, and she performed Umrah.”

The two Sahīhs and the Sunan of Abū Dāwūd and al-Nasā’ī relate from Jābir that he said:

“We came with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, having entered ihram for Hajj alone, while Ā’ishah had entered ihram for Umrah. When she reached Sarif, she began menstruating. Then, when we

---

## Page 13073

arrived, we circumambulated the Kaaba and passed between al-Safā and al-Marwah. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed those of us who had not brought sacrificial animals to leave ihram. We asked, ‘To what extent are we released from ihram?’ He replied, ‘Completely.’ So we had relations with our wives, applied perfume, and put on our ordinary clothes, although only four nights remained before the day at Arafat. Then we entered ihram on the Day of al-Tarwiyah.

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, then went in to Ā’ishah and found her weeping. He asked, ‘What is the matter?’ She replied, ‘I have begun menstruating. The people have left ihram, but I have not, nor have I circumambulated the House, and now they are setting out for Hajj.’ He said, ‘This is something Allah has decreed for the daughters of Adam. Bathe, then enter ihram for Hajj.’ She did so and attended the prescribed stations. Once she became free of menstruation, she circumambulated the House and passed between al-Safā and al-Marwah. He then said, ‘You have now left ihram for both your Hajj and your Umrah.’

“She said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, I still feel troubled that I did not circumambulate the House when I performed Hajj.’ He said, ‘Take her, Abd al-Rahmān, and have her perform Umrah from al-Tan‘īm.’ That was on the night of al-Hasbah.”

In Muslim’s version: “The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was accommodating: when she desired something, he would go along with her wishes. So he sent her with Abd al-Rahmān, and she entered ihram for Umrah from al-Tan‘īm.”

Muslim also related in his Sahīh, through Tāwūs, from Ā’ishah:

“She entered ihram for Umrah and arrived, but did not circumambulate the House because she had begun menstruating. She performed all the rites, having entered ihram for Hajj. On the day of departure, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to her, ‘Sufficient for you is

---

## Page 13074

your circumambulation for your Hajj and your Umrah.’ She declined, so he sent her with Abd al-Rahmān to al-Tan‘īm, and she performed Umrah after Hajj.” Muslim also related through Mujāhid,

“From Ā’ishah, that she began menstruating at Sarif and purified herself at Arafat. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to her, ‘Your circumambulation of the House and your passage between al-Safā and al-Marwah suffice for both your Hajj and your Umrah.’”

This, then, is the account of Ā’ishah.

The jurists hold two well-known views concerning the Umrah she performed:

The first is the view of the majority of jurists among the scholars of hadith and those of the Hijaz, such as Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, and others. She had intended to perform Umrah followed by Hajj, leaving ihram between them. When she began menstruating and was thus prevented from performing the circumambulation for Umrah, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed her to enter ihram for Hajj while remaining in her existing state of ihram. She thereby combined Umrah and Hajj. A person combining the two is one who either enters ihram for both together at the outset or enters ihram for Umrah and then adds Hajj before performing its circumambulation.

They said: The hadiths indicate that someone combining Hajj and Umrah performs no additional rites beyond those performed by someone undertaking Hajj alone, except for the sacrificial offering. Hence, when she left ihram, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to her:

“You have now left ihram for both your Hajj and your Umrah.”

The second view, held by Abū Hanīfah and those who agree with him, is that when she began menstruating, he instructed her to abandon Umrah and switch from it to Hajj, without separating

---

## Page 13075

the two; rather, she remained in ihram for Hajj alone. They said: When she left ihram, she did so for Hajj only, and she still owed an Umrah to make up for the one she had abandoned. According to these scholars, the Umrah she subsequently performed was obligatory because it made up for the one she had abandoned. According to the majority, however, it was not obligatory, but permissible.

The ruling concerning any woman who arrives intending to perform Umrah followed by Hajj, leaving ihram between them, but begins menstruating before circumambulation, follows these first two views: should she be instructed to enter ihram for Hajj, thereby combining Hajj and Umrah, or should she abandon Umrah in favour of Hajj?

There is a third view, reported from Ahmad: she had combined Hajj and Umrah, but the Umrah performed as part of this combination does not discharge the obligation of Umrah. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, therefore instructed her to perform her obligatory Umrah.

A fourth view, mentioned by some Mālikī scholars, holds that she was prevented from performing the arrival circumambulation because of menstruation, and that this Umrah was her obligatory Umrah. This is the weakest of the views for several reasons; the preceding view is the next weakest.

Underlying this disagreement is the following: according to the latter group, someone combining Hajj and Umrah must first circumambulate the House and perform the passage between al-Safā and al-Marwah for Umrah, then circumambulate and perform that passage for Hajj. On their view, menstruation prevents her from performing the rites of someone combining Hajj and Umrah, just as it prevented her from performing Umrah followed by Hajj with a release from ihram between them. According to the first group, however, someone combining Hajj and Umrah need only perform one circumambulation and one passage between al-Safā and al-Marwah, just like someone performing Hajj alone. Thus, if she is menstruating, the arrival circumambulation is waived for her, and she postpones the passage between al-Safā and al-Marwah until she performs it

---

## Page 13076

after the principal circumambulation of Hajj; nothing else is required of her.

Those who hold the second view had received the report established in the authentic collection that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to her:

“Abandon your Umrah.”

They believed that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had instructed her to perform Umrah from al-Tan‘īm. They therefore believed that this Umrah had become obligatory to make up for the abandoned Umrah, and that abandoning Umrah meant leaving it by entering ihram for Hajj alone.

Those who hold the first view, however, had received knowledge that had not reached the others. The account of ‘Ā’ishah was transmitted through several routes, from her and from others, including Jābir. Consider what she said and what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to her:

“You have now left ihram for both your Hajj and your Umrah.”

He also told her:

“Your passage between al-Safā and al-Marwah and your circumambulation count for both your Hajj and your Umrah.”

Another version states:

“Your passage between al-Safā and al-Marwah suffices for both your Hajj and your Umrah.”

This explicitly establishes that she was performing both Hajj and Umrah, not Hajj alone, and that a single circumambulation sufficed for her: she did not need two.

Furthermore, authentic and explicit reports of the Sunnah establish that, when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and those of his Companions who had brought sacrificial animals arrived, they circumambulated the House and passed between al-Safā and al-Marwah only once.

---

## Page 13077

Moreover, when he told her that, she said to him, “I feel troubled that I did not circumambulate the House when I performed Hajj.” He said, “Take her, O ‘Abd al-Rahmān, and have her perform Umrah from al-Tan‘īm.” Likewise, she said to him, “Are my companions to return with Hajj and Umrah, while I return with Hajj alone?” So he instructed ‘Abd al-Rahmān, who took her to al-Tan‘īm.

This shows that he did not instruct her to perform Umrah on his own initiative; rather, he granted her request when she was reluctant to return without performing an Umrah. Her companions had undertaken Umrah before Hajj, with a release from ihram between the two: they had first circumambulated the House and passed between al-Safā and al-Marwah, whereas she did not perform either until after standing at ‘Arafāt. Thus, they had performed more rites than she had, because the initial circumambulation had been waived for her on account of menstruation.

---

## Page 13078

He—may Allah be pleased with him and make him content—was asked:

About someone who stands at ‘Arafāt but cannot go to the House for fear of being killed or losing his property: does this fulfill his obligation of Hajj, or not?

And about someone who suffers from an ailment affecting his body or head, so he wears ordinary clothing and covers his head: must he offer expiation, or not? What is the expiation? If someone can find only a camel that it is unlawful for him to use, does performing Hajj on it fulfill his obligation? What are the ifrād, qirān, and tamattu‘ forms of Hajj, and which is best? Is the Hajj of someone who does not know these things valid, or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. After standing at ‘Arafāt, one must perform tawāf al-ifādah, the obligatory circumambulation. If one does not circumambulate the House, one’s Hajj is incomplete, by the consensus of the Muslim community. If an enemy blocks his access to the House and fear prevents him from performing the circumambulation, he is to release himself from ihram by slaughtering a sacrificial animal and coming out of ihram. He must perform the circumambulation afterward if this was his obligatory Hajj; he should enter Mecca to perform an Umrah in compensation for that.

He may not cover his head without a need, nor wear a shirt, robe, or similar clothing except out of necessity. If he fears that severe cold will make him ill, he may wear such clothing, but must also offer expiation and seek Allah’s forgiveness for his sins.

---

## Page 13079

The expiation when there is a valid excuse is to slaughter a sheep and distribute its meat among the poor, fast for three days, or give charity to six poor people, giving each half a sā‘ of dates. Giving each of them one ratl of bread is also permissible. It is not permissible to perform Hajj on a camel that it is unlawful for one to use.

For someone who brings a sacrificial animal with him, it is preferable to perform qirān, combining Umrah and Hajj. If he does not bring a sacrificial animal and wishes to combine Umrah and Hajj, tamattu‘ is preferable. If he performs Hajj on one journey and Umrah on another, ifrād is preferable for him.

If he enters ihram without specifying its form, and none of these matters occurs to him, his Hajj is valid provided he performs it as the Muslims do. Allah—glorified and exalted is He—knows best what is correct.

---

## Page 13080

Chapter: Hajj Sacrifices, Eid Sacrifices, and Sacrifices for a Newborn

He—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

Offering an Eid sacrifice, a sacrifice for a newborn, or a Hajj sacrifice is preferable to giving its price in charity. If someone has money with which he wishes to draw closer to Allah, he may use it to offer an Eid sacrifice. Eating from the Eid sacrifice is preferable to giving it away in charity, and offering a Hajj sacrifice in Mecca is preferable to giving charity there.

If someone has bound himself by a vow to offer a sacrificial animal, then purchases one on credit, and it is sold before slaughter, he must replace it with a sheep. If, however, he purchases a sacrificial animal and it develops a defect before slaughter, he should slaughter it according to one of the two scholarly opinions. If it develops a defect during slaughter, it suffices in both cases.

---

## Page 13081

He—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

Offering an Eid sacrifice is part of reasonable expenditure. Thus, a sacrifice may be offered on behalf of an orphan using the orphan’s own funds. A woman may take from her husband’s money enough to offer a sacrifice on behalf of the household, even without his permission. A debtor may offer a sacrifice if repayment has not been demanded of him, and a person may borrow to offer a sacrifice if he has the means to repay.

He was asked:

Should someone who cannot afford an Eid sacrifice borrow money for it?

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. If he has the means to repay and borrows money to offer a sacrifice, that is good, but he is not obliged to do so. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 13082

He—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

Section

An Eid sacrifice may be offered on behalf of the deceased, just as Hajj may be performed and charity given on their behalf. The sacrifice should be offered at home; no animal should be slaughtered at a grave, whether as an Eid sacrifice or otherwise. Sunan Abī Dāwūd reports:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade slaughtering animals at graves.”

Ahmad even disapproved of eating meat from animals slaughtered at graves, because this resembles what is slaughtered on sacrificial altars.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians; they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

He was warning against what they had done.

It is also authentically reported from him in the Sahīh that he said:

“Do not sit on graves or pray towards them.”

He also said:

“The whole earth is a place of prayer except graveyards and bathhouses.”

Thus, he forbade praying at graves, lest this resemble praying to them. Likewise, slaughtering animals at graves resembles slaughtering them as offerings to those buried there. The polytheists used to slaughter animals as offerings to those in the graves and present offerings to them. During the pre-Islamic age of ignorance, when a prominent person among them died, they would slaughter horses, camels, and other animals at his grave to honour the deceased. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade all of this.

---

## Page 13083

Even if someone vowed to do this, he would not be permitted to fulfil his vow. If the founder of an endowment stipulated it, that stipulation would be invalid. Likewise, the scholars disapproved of giving charity at a grave, and an endowment founder’s stipulation requiring this would be invalid. Still more objectionable is placing food and drink on a grave for people to take, for this and similar practices belong to the unbelievers among the Turks, not to the Muslims.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

Section

It is permissible to offer a pregnant animal as an Eid sacrifice. If its young comes out dead, the mother’s lawful slaughter suffices for it as well, according to al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others, whether or not it has developed hair. If it comes out alive, it must be slaughtered. Mālik’s position is that if it has developed hair, it is lawful to eat; otherwise, it is not. According to Abū Hanīfah, it is not lawful to eat unless it is lawfully slaughtered after coming out. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 13084

He—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

Section

Regarding an animal that has lost some of its teeth, termed “al-hatmāʾ,” there are two opinions within Ahmad’s school. The sounder opinion is that it is valid as an Eid sacrifice. As for an animal with no upper teeth, it is valid by agreement. A white animal is preferable to a black one. If it has black markings around its eyes and mouth and on its legs, it resembles the Prophet’s sacrificial animal—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

He was asked:

What should be said when slaughtering the Eid sacrifice? How should it be slaughtered, and how should it be divided?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

The sacrificial animal should be turned towards the qiblah and laid on its left side. The person slaughtering it should say:

“In the name of Allah; Allah is the Greatest. O Allah, accept this from me just as

---

## Page 13085

You accepted it from Abraham, Your close friend.”

When slaughtering it, he should say:

“I have turned my face towards Him who created the heavens and the earth, inclining to true faith, and I am not one of the polytheists.”

“Say, ‘Indeed, my prayer, my sacrifice, my living and my dying are for Allah, Lord of the worlds.

He has no partner. This I have been commanded, and I am the first of the Muslims.’”

He should give one-third of it in charity and another third as gifts. If he eats most of it, gives it away as a gift, eats it, or cooks it and invites people to partake, that is permissible. He should pay the butcher from his own funds. As for its hide, he may use it himself or give it in charity, as he wishes. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—also said:

Section

The animal to be slaughtered, whether for the Eid sacrifice or otherwise, should be laid on its left side. The person slaughtering it should place his right foot on its neck, as is established in an authentic report from the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He should invoke Allah’s name and proclaim His greatness, saying:

“In the name of Allah; Allah is the Greatest. O Allah, this is from You and for You. O Allah, accept this from me just as You accepted it from Abraham, Your close friend.”

---

## Page 13086

And anyone who lays it on its right side, places his left foot on its neck, and awkwardly crosses his hands to slaughter it is ignorant of the Sunnah and torments both himself and the animal. Nevertheless, its meat is lawful to eat. Laying it on its left side is more comfortable for the animal, makes it easier to end its life, and facilitates slaughter. This is the Sunnah practised by the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and the practice followed by Muslims and all peoples. It is also prescribed to turn the animal towards the direction of prayer.

If a person sacrifices one sheep on behalf of himself and his household, that suffices according to the stronger of the two scholarly opinions. This is the position of Mālik, Ahmad, and others, for the Companions used to do so. It is established in an authentic report that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—sacrificed two sheep and said concerning one of them:

“O Allah, this is from Muhammad and the family of Muhammad.”

He was asked:

A man named Abū Bakr became a soldier, changed his name, and adopted a name of the kind used by the Mamluks. Is there any sin upon him?

He replied:

If he adopts a Turkish name for some benefit it brings him, there is no sin upon him

---

## Page 13087

and he will then have two names, just as someone has a name given to him by his parents and is later given an honorific by people, such as “So-and-so of the Faith.”

He was asked:

What about the titles commonly accepted among people?

He replied:

As for titles, the practice of the early generations was to use personal names and kunyahs (parental bynames). When calling someone Abū So-and-so, they sometimes named him after his child. They also gave kunyahs to those who had no children, with reference to the person’s own name, his father’s name, the son of his namesake, or something associated with him. Thus, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—gave ‘Ā’ishah a kunyah after her sister’s son, ‘Abd Allāh. Likewise, they would call a man named Dāwūd “Abū Sulaymān” because he bore the name of David—peace be upon him—whose son was named Solomon. Similarly, Ibrāhīm would be given the kunyah Abū Ishāq. They also called ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Abbās “Abū al-‘Abbās,” and the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—called Abū Hurayra by that name after a kitten he had with him.

This remained the practice throughout the first three generations. Then, when non-Arab rule gained ascendancy under the Umayyads, they began to . . . (1)

Later, they introduced titles incorporating “of the Faith” and greatly expanded their use. There is no doubt that, whenever possible, the proper course is to follow the early generations’ customary forms of

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space in the original.

---

## Page 13088

address and bynames. Whoever can follow this practice should not depart from it if he must address someone, especially since names implying self-praise have been forbidden. Thus, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—changed the name Barra to Zaynab, so that she would not thereby praise herself. When referring to someone by these newly introduced names for fear that abandoning them might lead to harm, one should confine oneself to what is necessary. Such titles were bestowed because they were merely proper names, without regard to their descriptive meaning, like words transferred into use as personal names, such as Asad (lion), Kalb (dog), and Thawr (bull).

There is no doubt that these innovations, introduced by non-Arabs and then increasingly embellished with titles such as “Glory of the Religious Community and the Faith” and “Glory of the Religious Community, the Truth, and the Faith,” very often involve blatant falsehood: the person so described may more rightly deserve the opposite description. Those who seek these things out of pride and arrogance are punished by Allah with the opposite of what they intended: He humiliates them and gives their enemies power over them. But those who fear Allah and fulfil His commands to worship and obey Him, He honours and grants victory.

As He, Most High, says:

“Indeed, We shall support Our messengers and those who believe, both in the life of this world and on the Day when the witnesses stand forth.”

And He, Most High, says:

“Honour belongs to Allah, His Messenger, and the believers, but the hypocrites do not know.”

Allah knows best. May Allah bless Muhammad and his family and grant them peace.
